# SECTION JavaScript Fundamentals | The language questions that open almost every full-stack interview — answer them like an engineer who has debugged them in production.

### What is the difference between `var`, `let` and `const`?
@short
`var` is function-scoped and hoisted as `undefined`; `let` and `const` are block-scoped and live in the temporal dead zone until initialised. `const` prevents reassignment of the binding, not mutation of the value. In modern code I default to `const`, use `let` only when I genuinely reassign, and never use `var`.
@deep
Scope is the visibility boundary. `var` attaches to the nearest function (or global) scope, so a `var` declared inside an `if` block leaks out of it. `let`/`const` attach to the nearest `{}` block, which is why they behave correctly inside loops and conditionals.

Hoisting is the second difference. All three declarations are hoisted to the top of their scope, but only `var` is initialised to `undefined`. `let`/`const` are hoisted uninitialised — touching them before the declaration line throws `ReferenceError: Cannot access 'x' before initialization`. That window is the **temporal dead zone (TDZ)**.

The third difference is the global object: a top-level `var` in a script becomes a property of `globalThis`; `let`/`const` do not.
@code js The classic loop-closure bug, and why `let` fixes it
// var: one binding shared by all three callbacks -> 3, 3, 3
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) setTimeout(() => console.log(i));

// let: a fresh binding is created per iteration -> 0, 1, 2
for (let j = 0; j < 3; j++) setTimeout(() => console.log(j));

const user = { name: 'Ada' };
user.name = 'Grace';       // legal — the object is mutable
// user = {};              // TypeError — the binding is not reassignable
@end
@senior
The `let` loop fix is not a special case in the timer — the spec creates a new environment record per iteration and copies the value forward, which is why `let` in a `for` loop costs marginally more than `var`. It never matters at application scale, but knowing *why* it works signals you have read the semantics rather than memorised a rule. In a code review I enforce `const` by default with ESLint `prefer-const`, because an immutable binding removes a whole class of "who reassigned this?" bugs during debugging.
@followup
If `const` doesn't make an object immutable, how do you actually get immutability?
@answer
`Object.freeze()` gives a shallow freeze — nested objects are still mutable, so a deep freeze needs recursion. In practice I do not freeze at runtime; I enforce it at the type level with TypeScript `readonly` / `as const`, and in React state I always produce new objects instead of mutating, because mutation breaks the reference-equality checks that `React.memo`, `useMemo` and Redux selectors depend on.
@red
- Saying "`const` makes the value constant" — it makes the binding constant.
- Claiming `var` is "just older syntax" with no semantic difference.
- Not being able to explain the loop-closure output.

### Explain hoisting and the temporal dead zone.
@short
Hoisting means declarations are registered in the scope before any code runs. Function declarations are fully hoisted and callable early; `var` is hoisted and initialised to `undefined`; `let`, `const` and `class` are hoisted but uninitialised, which creates the temporal dead zone where any access throws.
@deep
When the engine enters a scope it does a creation phase before the execution phase: it walks the code, allocates bindings in the environment record, and only then starts executing statements. That two-phase model is what "hoisting" describes — nothing physically moves.

Function *declarations* have their whole body bound in the creation phase, so `foo()` works above `function foo(){}`. Function *expressions* assigned to `var` do not — the variable exists as `undefined`, so you get `TypeError: foo is not a function`, which is a genuinely different error from the TDZ `ReferenceError`. Being able to name which error you get is a strong signal.
@code js Three declarations, three different failure modes
sayHi();              // ✅ works — function declaration fully hoisted
function sayHi(){ console.log('hi'); }

console.log(a);       // undefined  (var: hoisted + initialised)
var a = 1;

console.log(b);       // ReferenceError (TDZ)
let b = 2;

notYet();             // TypeError: notYet is not a function
var notYet = () => {};
@end
@senior
The practical payoff is debugging speed. `undefined` in production usually means a `var`/import-order problem; a TDZ `ReferenceError` usually means circular imports in an ES-module graph, where a module reads a `const` from a partner module that has not finished evaluating. On the Node side I have seen this exactly once with a circular `models/index.js` → `models/user.js` import, and the fix was to break the cycle with a lazy getter rather than reorder imports and hope.
@followup
Are ES module imports hoisted?
@answer
Yes — `import` bindings are hoisted and live, so you can call an imported function above the import statement. But they are live *bindings*, not copies: with circular dependencies the binding can exist while still being in its TDZ, which is why circular ESM imports fail loudly rather than silently giving you `undefined` like CommonJS does.
@red
- Saying "the code is physically moved to the top".
- Confusing the `undefined` case with the `ReferenceError` case.

### What is a closure, and where do you actually use one?
@short
A closure is a function together with the lexical environment it was created in, so it keeps access to those variables after the outer function has returned. I use closures for private state, for factory functions like rate limiters and memoisers, and every React hook that captures props or state is a closure.
@deep
When a function is created, it stores a reference to its defining scope chain. As long as the function is reachable, the garbage collector cannot free the variables it closes over — that is both the feature and the leak risk.

The React consequence is the "stale closure": an effect or callback captures the value of `count` at the render it was created in. If the dependency array does not include `count`, later invocations still see the old value. The functional-updater form `setCount(c => c + 1)` avoids the capture entirely because the current value is supplied by React.
@code js A closure-based per-user rate limiter (no globals, no leaks by key)
function createRateLimiter({ limit, windowMs }) {
  const hits = new Map();                      // private — no external access

  return function allow(key) {
    const now = Date.now();
    const bucket = (hits.get(key) ?? []).filter(t => now - t < windowMs);
    if (bucket.length >= limit) return false;
    bucket.push(now);
    hits.set(key, bucket);
    return true;
  };
}

const allow = createRateLimiter({ limit: 5, windowMs: 60_000 });
app.post('/api/otp', (req, res, next) =>
  allow(req.ip) ? next() : res.status(429).json({ error: 'Too many requests' }));
@end
@senior
The senior addition is memory. That `Map` above grows forever unless you sweep it — an in-process closure cache is a memory leak with a nice API. In real services I either bound it (LRU) or move the state to Redis so it survives restarts and works across the several PM2/container replicas that sit behind the load balancer. "Closure state does not survive horizontal scaling" is the sentence interviewers want.
@followup
How would you make that rate limiter work across four Node instances?
@answer
Move the counter into Redis and make the increment atomic: `INCR` the key, and if the reply is `1`, set the TTL — or do both in one Lua script so it is a single round trip and cannot half-apply. For smoother behaviour I would use a sliding window with a sorted set (`ZADD` + `ZREMRANGEBYSCORE` + `ZCARD`) or a token bucket, and I would fail *open* on a Redis outage for a login endpoint but fail *closed* for an expensive AI endpoint.
@red
- Defining a closure as "a function inside a function" and stopping there.
- Not mentioning that closures retain memory.

### How does `this` work in JavaScript?
@short
In a normal function, `this` is decided at call time by how the function is called: as a method, the object before the dot; standalone, `undefined` in strict mode; with `new`, the new instance; with `call`/`apply`/`bind`, whatever you pass. Arrow functions have no `this` of their own — they inherit it lexically from where they were defined.
@deep
The precedence order is: `new` binding, then explicit binding (`call`/`apply`/`bind`), then implicit binding (the object before the dot), then the default (`globalThis`, or `undefined` under strict mode and inside ES modules).

The classic bug is losing the receiver by extracting a method: `const f = obj.method; f()` — the implicit binding was in the call site, not the function, so it is gone. Passing a method as a callback (`setTimeout(obj.method, 0)`, `arr.map(this.transform)`) is the same bug wearing a different hat.
@code js Losing and restoring `this` in an Express controller
class OrderController {
  constructor(service) { this.service = service; }

  // ❌ passed as a bare reference -> `this` is undefined at call time
  async list(req, res) { res.json(await this.service.findAll()); }

  // ✅ class field + arrow: `this` is captured lexically, safe to pass around
  detail = async (req, res) => {
    res.json(await this.service.findById(req.params.id));
  };
}

const c = new OrderController(orderService);
router.get('/orders', c.list.bind(c));   // explicit bind fixes the first one
router.get('/orders/:id', c.detail);     // already bound by the arrow field
@end
@senior
Because arrows take `this` lexically, they are wrong for object literal methods and for prototype methods you intend to be dynamically dispatched — and they are wrong as Mocha test callbacks, which need `this.timeout()`. The rule I use: arrow for anything that will be *passed*, normal function for anything that will be *called on a receiver*. In TypeScript I additionally turn on `strictBindCallApply` and use the `this` parameter type so a lost receiver becomes a compile error instead of a 3am `Cannot read properties of undefined`.
@followup
What is the difference between `call`, `apply` and `bind`?
@answer
`call` and `apply` invoke immediately and differ only in how arguments are passed — `call(thisArg, a, b)` versus `apply(thisArg, [a, b])`. `bind` invokes nothing; it returns a new function permanently bound to that `this`, and it also supports partial application. `bind` is the one you use for callbacks and event handlers.
@red
- "Arrow functions bind `this` to the parent object" — they bind to the enclosing lexical scope, which is not always an object.
- Saying `this` is determined by where a function is defined (that is only true for arrows).

### Explain the JavaScript event loop, and the difference between microtasks and macrotasks.
@short
JavaScript runs on a single call stack. The event loop takes work from the queues and pushes it onto that stack whenever it empties. Promise callbacks go on the microtask queue, which is drained completely after every task and before the next render or timer; `setTimeout`, I/O and UI events are macrotasks, so one microtask flood can starve them.
@deep
Concretely, a tick looks like: run the current task to completion → drain the entire microtask queue (including microtasks queued by microtasks) → in a browser, run `requestAnimationFrame` and paint → take the next macrotask.

That "drain completely" is the key property: `Promise.resolve().then(...)` always runs before a `setTimeout(..., 0)` queued earlier, and an infinite microtask chain freezes the page permanently, whereas an infinite `setTimeout` chain does not. In Node the event loop has named phases — timers, pending callbacks, poll, check (`setImmediate`), close — and microtasks (plus `process.nextTick`, which runs even earlier) drain between each phase.
@code js Predict the output — this exact snippet is asked constantly
console.log('1 sync');
setTimeout(() => console.log('2 timeout (macrotask)'), 0);
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('3 promise (microtask)'));
queueMicrotask(() => console.log('4 queueMicrotask'));
process.nextTick?.(() => console.log('5 nextTick — Node only, highest priority'));
console.log('6 sync');

// Node output: 1 sync, 6 sync, 5 nextTick, 3 promise, 4 queueMicrotask, 2 timeout
@end
@senior
The production consequence is that "async" does not mean "non-blocking". `await` on a CPU-bound loop, a huge `JSON.parse`, a synchronous `bcrypt.hashSync`, or an unbounded regex still blocks the single thread and every other request on that process. That is why real CPU work goes to `worker_threads` or a queue, and why I watch event-loop lag (`perf_hooks.monitorEventLoopDelay`) as a first-class metric next to p99 latency — rising loop lag explains a latency spike that CPU and memory graphs alone will not.
@followup
So how does Node do file and network I/O without blocking?
@answer
Network I/O uses the OS's own non-blocking primitives (epoll/kqueue) through libuv, so it never needs a thread. File system calls, DNS lookups via `getaddrinfo`, and the crypto functions like `pbkdf2` and `bcrypt` are the exception — those go to libuv's thread pool, which defaults to only four threads. Saturating that pool with parallel `fs` or hashing work makes unrelated requests queue, and the fix is either to raise `UV_THREADPOOL_SIZE` or to move the work off the process entirely.
@red
- Saying JavaScript is multi-threaded.
- Claiming `setTimeout(fn, 0)` runs immediately.
- Not knowing microtasks beat macrotasks.

### Callbacks vs Promises vs async/await — what actually changed?
@short
All three describe the same asynchronous machinery; what changed is composability and error handling. Callbacks nest and lose stack traces, Promises make async values first-class and chainable, and `async/await` is syntax over Promises that lets you use ordinary `try/catch` and normal control flow. Under the hood `await` is still a `.then()`.
@deep
A Promise is a state machine — pending → fulfilled or rejected, settled once, immutable after. That single-settlement guarantee is what callbacks lack: a badly written callback can fire twice, or never, and nothing stops it.

`async` functions always return a Promise; `await` suspends the function and schedules the continuation as a microtask. Crucially it does not block the thread — other tasks run while you are suspended.

The most common performance mistake is sequential awaits inside a loop when the operations are independent. `Promise.all` parallelises them; `Promise.allSettled` does the same but does not reject the whole batch when one item fails; `Promise.any` resolves on the first success.
@code js Sequential vs parallel — the same code, an order-of-magnitude apart
// ❌ 40 users × 120 ms = ~4.8 s of wall clock
const out = [];
for (const id of ids) out.push(await fetchUser(id));

// ✅ ~120 ms — but unbounded fan-out will melt your DB pool
const all = await Promise.all(ids.map(fetchUser));

// ✅✅ bounded concurrency: fast AND safe for a 20-connection pool
import pLimit from 'p-limit';
const limit = pLimit(10);
const safe = await Promise.all(ids.map(id => limit(() => fetchUser(id))));

// Partial failure tolerated (e.g. enriching a list from three services)
const results = await Promise.allSettled(ids.map(fetchUser));
const ok = results.filter(r => r.status === 'fulfilled').map(r => r.value);
@end
@senior
`Promise.all` with an unbounded array is one of the most common self-inflicted outages: 5,000 IDs becomes 5,000 concurrent queries, the connection pool is exhausted in milliseconds, healthy endpoints start timing out, and the health check fails so the orchestrator restarts a process that was never broken. Bounded concurrency (`p-limit`, or a batched `IN (...)` query, which is better still) is the senior answer. Also: every `await` outside a `try/catch` in an Express handler without `express-async-errors` is an unhandled rejection, which in Node 15+ terminates the process by default.
@followup
What is the difference between `Promise.all`, `allSettled`, `race` and `any`?
@answer
`all` rejects on the first rejection and gives you all values on success — use it when you need everything. `allSettled` never rejects and gives you a status per item — use it for best-effort fan-out. `race` settles with the first settled promise, success or failure — that is how you implement a timeout. `any` resolves with the first *fulfilled* promise and only rejects if all fail — that is how you implement a fallback across replicas.
@red
- Saying async/await is "faster" than Promises — it is the same machinery.
- Awaiting inside a loop for independent work and not noticing.
- Forgetting that a rejected Promise with no catch can crash Node.

### `==` vs `===`, and how does type coercion work?
@short
`===` compares type and value with no conversion. `==` applies the abstract equality algorithm, converting operands first, which produces genuinely surprising results. I use `===` everywhere, with one deliberate exception: `x == null` as a compact check for "null or undefined".
@deep
The coercion rules worth remembering: `null == undefined` is true but neither equals anything else; comparing a number to a string converts the string to a number; comparing anything to a boolean converts the boolean to a number first, which is why `'0' == false` is true; objects are converted via `valueOf`/`toString`, which is why `[] == false` is true.

Falsy values are exactly `false`, `0`, `-0`, `0n`, `''`, `null`, `undefined`, `NaN` — everything else, including `[]` and `{}` and `'0'`, is truthy.
@code js The bug this actually causes in an API handler
// ❌ page 0, quantity 0 and empty string all silently become the default
const page = req.query.page || 1;
const qty  = body.quantity || 1;

// ✅ nullish coalescing only falls back on null/undefined
const page2 = req.query.page ?? 1;
const qty2  = body.quantity ?? 1;

// Coercion trivia that shows up in quizzes
'' == 0            // true
'0' == false       // true
null == undefined  // true
null == 0          // false  (null only loosely equals undefined)
NaN === NaN        // false  -> use Number.isNaN() or Object.is()
[] + {}            // '[object Object]'
@end
@senior
The `||` versus `??` distinction is the one that bites in real code, and it is a genuine correctness bug rather than trivia: a `discount || 10` silently ignores a legitimate zero discount, and a `retries || 3` ignores an explicit "do not retry". I lint for it. On the validation side I never rely on coercion at all at the boundary — request bodies go through Zod, which converts and validates once at the edge so the rest of the handler works with real types.
@followup
Why is `NaN !== NaN`?
@answer
IEEE-754 defines NaN as unordered — it is not equal to anything, including itself, because it represents "the result of an invalid operation", and two invalid operations are not necessarily the same value. Practically you test with `Number.isNaN(x)`, or `Object.is(x, NaN)` if you also want to distinguish `+0` from `-0`.
@red
- "Always use `===`" with no explanation of why, or of the `== null` idiom.
- Not knowing `[]` is truthy but `[] == false`.

### Explain prototypal inheritance and the prototype chain.
@short
Every JavaScript object has an internal link to another object, its prototype. When you read a property that the object does not have, the engine walks that chain until it finds it or reaches `null`. `class` syntax is sugar over exactly this — methods live on `Constructor.prototype`, so all instances share one copy.
@deep
`obj.__proto__` (properly `Object.getPrototypeOf(obj)`) is the link; `Fn.prototype` is the object that will *become* the prototype of instances created with `new Fn()`. Those are two different things with confusingly similar names, and interviewers test exactly that.

`new` does four things: create an empty object, set its prototype to `Fn.prototype`, call `Fn` with `this` bound to it, and return it unless the constructor returns an object explicitly.

`instanceof` walks the prototype chain looking for `Fn.prototype`, which is why it breaks across iframes and across two copies of the same npm package — a real cause of "this Error is not an Error" bugs in monorepos.
@code js The chain, and why methods live on the prototype
class Animal {
  constructor(name) { this.name = name; }     // own property, per instance
  speak() { return `${this.name} makes a sound`; }   // shared, on the prototype
}
class Dog extends Animal {
  speak() { return `${super.speak()} — a bark`; }
}

const d = new Dog('Rex');
Object.getPrototypeOf(d) === Dog.prototype;            // true
Object.getPrototypeOf(Dog.prototype) === Animal.prototype; // true
d.hasOwnProperty('speak');                              // false — inherited
d.hasOwnProperty('name');                               // true  — own

// Chain: d -> Dog.prototype -> Animal.prototype -> Object.prototype -> null
@end
@senior
The performance point: putting methods on the prototype means one function object for a million instances, whereas assigning arrow-function class fields creates a new closure per instance. That is a real memory difference in hot paths — but arrow fields are still the right choice when the method must survive being detached. The other senior note is that mutating a prototype at runtime (`Object.setPrototypeOf`, or monkey-patching `Array.prototype`) de-optimises V8's inline caches and slows down every site that touches the object, so it is effectively forbidden in production code.
@followup
When would you use composition instead of inheritance?
@answer
Almost always. Deep inheritance chains couple unrelated concerns and force you to reason about the whole ancestry to understand one method. I compose behaviour by injecting collaborators — a service takes a repository, a repository takes a client — which keeps each unit independently testable and swappable. I reserve inheritance for genuine "is-a" specialisation with a stable interface, such as a base `AppError` class that framework error middleware can `instanceof`.
@red
- Confusing `__proto__` with `prototype`.
- Saying JavaScript classes are "real classes like Java".

### What is the difference between a shallow copy and a deep copy?
@short
A shallow copy duplicates the top level and shares every nested reference, so mutating a nested object mutates both. A deep copy recursively clones everything. Spread and `Object.assign` are shallow; `structuredClone` is the modern built-in deep copy; `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(x))` is the old trick and it silently destroys `Date`, `Map`, `Set`, `undefined` and `NaN`.
@deep
This matters most in React and Redux, where state updates must produce new references at every level you changed. If you spread the top level but mutate `state.user.address.city`, the nested object is the same reference as before — `React.memo` and `useMemo` see no change and your UI does not update, which presents as "state changed but nothing re-rendered".
@code js Correct nested immutable update, and the copy options
const state = { user: { name: 'Ada', address: { city: 'London' } }, tags: ['a'] };

const shallow = { ...state };
shallow.user.address.city = 'Paris';       // ⚠️ also changed `state`

// ✅ new reference at every level that changed
const next = {
  ...state,
  user: { ...state.user, address: { ...state.user.address, city: 'Paris' } },
};

const deep = structuredClone(state);       // Node 17+, all modern browsers
// keeps Date/Map/Set/ArrayBuffer; throws on functions, DOM nodes, class identity
@end
@senior
For anything deeper than two levels I stop hand-spreading and use Immer (which is what Redux Toolkit uses internally): you write mutable-looking code against a Proxy and it produces a correct immutable result with structural sharing, so unchanged subtrees keep their identity and memoisation still works. Structural sharing is the point — a naive deep clone breaks memoisation everywhere by giving every subtree a new reference.
@followup
Why is `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify())` risky?
@answer
It is lossy and slow. `Date` becomes an ISO string, `Map`/`Set` become `{}`, `undefined` and functions are dropped from objects and become `null` in arrays, `NaN`/`Infinity` become `null`, `BigInt` throws, and circular references throw. It is also fully synchronous, so cloning a large object blocks the event loop.
@red
- Saying spread is a deep copy.
- Not knowing `structuredClone` exists.

### `map`, `filter`, `reduce`, `find`, `some`, `every` — when do you use each?
@short
`map` transforms each element into a new array of the same length; `filter` selects a subset; `reduce` folds a collection into a single value of any shape; `find` returns the first match; `some`/`every` return booleans and short-circuit. They are all immutable except that they do not deep-clone the elements themselves.
@deep
The rule I apply in review is: use the method whose *name* states the intent. `forEach` with a `push` is a `map` written badly; a `filter().length > 0` is a `some` that scans the whole array; a `filter()[0]` is a `find` that does the same. Choosing correctly makes the code both faster and self-documenting.

`reduce` is the general case — `map` and `filter` can both be expressed with it — but it is also the easiest to make unreadable. I use it for grouping, indexing and summing, and reach for something clearer when the accumulator logic outgrows a few lines.
@code js Grouping and indexing — the two reduce patterns worth memorising
// Index by id: turns O(n) lookups into O(1) — the single most useful one
const byId = users.reduce((acc, u) => (acc[u.id] = u, acc), {});

// Group by a key
const byRole = users.reduce((acc, u) => {
  (acc[u.role] ??= []).push(u);
  return acc;
}, {});

// Modern equivalents (Node 21+ / ES2024)
const grouped = Object.groupBy(users, u => u.role);
const mapped  = Map.groupBy(users, u => u.role);

// Chaining reads top-to-bottom and is O(n) per pass — fine until n is huge
const revenue = orders
  .filter(o => o.status === 'paid')
  .map(o => o.total)
  .reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0);
@end
@senior
The performance nuance is that chaining creates an intermediate array per step. At a few thousand items that is irrelevant and readability wins; at hundreds of thousands, in a hot request path, a single loop or a generator pipeline avoids the allocations and the extra GC pressure. But the real senior answer is usually "do not do this in JavaScript at all" — a `SUM` with a `GROUP BY` in Postgres beats pulling 200k rows into Node to reduce them, both in latency and in memory.
@followup
What is the time complexity of that `byId` lookup pattern versus `find` in a loop?
@answer
Building the index is O(n) once, then each lookup is O(1), so enriching m items against n is O(n + m). Calling `.find()` inside a loop is O(n × m) — with 2,000 orders and 2,000 users that is four million comparisons instead of four thousand. This is the single most common accidental quadratic I find in code review.
@red
- Using `reduce` where `map`/`filter` would be clearer, to look clever.
- Mutating the accumulator's source array during iteration.
- Not knowing `some`/`every` short-circuit.

### What is the difference between debounce and throttle?
@short
Debounce waits for a pause: it fires once, after the events stop for N milliseconds — right for search-as-you-type and autosave. Throttle enforces a rate: it fires at most once per N milliseconds regardless of how many events arrive — right for scroll, resize, mousemove and drag.
@deep
Both are closures over a timer. Debounce resets the timer on every call, so a continuous stream of events produces exactly one invocation at the end. Throttle records the last execution time (or holds a "cooling" flag) and drops or defers calls in between, so a continuous stream produces a steady drip.

The variants matter: a leading-edge debounce fires immediately then suppresses, which feels more responsive for buttons; a trailing throttle guarantees the final event is not lost, which matters for a scroll position you must persist.
@code ts Both implementations, with cancel — TypeScript, generic-safe
export function debounce<A extends unknown[]>(fn: (...a: A) => void, ms = 300) {
  let t: ReturnType<typeof setTimeout>;
  const wrapped = (...args: A) => { clearTimeout(t); t = setTimeout(() => fn(...args), ms); };
  wrapped.cancel = () => clearTimeout(t);
  return wrapped;
}

export function throttle<A extends unknown[]>(fn: (...a: A) => void, ms = 200) {
  let last = 0, timer: ReturnType<typeof setTimeout> | null = null;
  return (...args: A) => {
    const now = Date.now(), remaining = ms - (now - last);
    if (remaining <= 0) { last = now; fn(...args); }
    else if (!timer) {                       // trailing edge — never lose the last event
      timer = setTimeout(() => { last = Date.now(); timer = null; fn(...args); }, remaining);
    }
  };
}
@end
@senior
In React, a debounce created inline in the component body is recreated every render, so the timer is never actually shared and the debounce silently does nothing — it has to live in a `useMemo`/`useRef`, and it must be cancelled in the effect cleanup or it fires after unmount and sets state on a dead component. Beyond that, debouncing the *input* is only half the fix for a search box: you also need to cancel the in-flight request (`AbortController`) so a slow earlier response cannot overwrite a fast later one. TanStack Query handles that ordering for me, which is why I prefer it over hand-rolled fetching.
@followup
Does debouncing on the client remove the need for server-side rate limiting?
@answer
No — client-side throttling is a UX optimisation, not a control. Anyone can call the API directly with curl. The server still needs its own rate limit, ideally in Redis so it is shared across instances, plus per-user quotas on anything expensive such as OTP sends or LLM calls.
@red
- Swapping the two definitions.
- Creating the debounced function inside the render body.
- Forgetting cleanup on unmount.

### What are pure functions and why does immutability matter?
@short
A pure function returns the same output for the same input and has no side effects — no I/O, no mutation of arguments, no reading of clocks or randomness. Purity makes code trivially testable, cacheable and safe to parallelise, and immutability is what makes purity possible when you are working with objects.
@deep
Impurity is not wrong; it is where the value is. The architectural move is to push side effects to the edges — a "functional core, imperative shell". Your pricing rules, validation and transformations are pure and unit-testable with no mocks; your database calls, HTTP calls and file writes sit in a thin outer layer that you integration-test.

Immutability underpins React's rendering model (reference equality decides re-render), Redux time-travel debugging, and safe caching — you can hand a frozen object to ten callers without defensive copying.
@code ts Functional core, imperative shell — pricing logic you can test with no mocks
// pure — no DB, no clock, no network. 100% unit-testable.
export function priceOrder(items: Item[], coupon?: Coupon, taxRate = 0.18) {
  const subtotal = items.reduce((s, i) => s + i.price * i.qty, 0);
  const discount = coupon ? Math.min(subtotal * coupon.pct, coupon.maxOff) : 0;
  const taxable  = subtotal - discount;
  return { subtotal, discount, tax: taxable * taxRate, total: taxable * (1 + taxRate) };
}

// impure shell — the only place that touches the world
export async function checkout(orderId: string) {
  const [items, coupon] = await Promise.all([repo.items(orderId), repo.coupon(orderId)]);
  const totals = priceOrder(items, coupon);            // pure call
  await repo.saveTotals(orderId, totals);
  return totals;
}
@end
@senior
The test-suite consequence is the argument that lands: pure pricing logic needs zero mocks, so the tests are fast, deterministic and do not break when you swap Sequelize for a raw query. Mock-heavy tests are usually a symptom of business logic tangled with I/O. I would also mention memoisation — you can only safely cache a pure function, which is why `useMemo`, React Server Component caching and HTTP caching all assume purity and misbehave loudly when you violate it.
@followup
Is `Math.random()` or `Date.now()` allowed in a pure function?
@answer
No — both make the function non-deterministic. I inject them: pass `now` as a parameter, or take a `clock`/`idGenerator` dependency. That is not academic purity, it is what lets me test "this coupon expires at midnight" without freezing the system clock.
@red
- Claiming immutability is only about performance.
- Not connecting immutability to React re-rendering.

### Explain destructuring, spread/rest, and optional chaining.
@short
Destructuring pulls values out of objects and arrays into named bindings with defaults. Spread (`...`) expands an iterable or an object's own enumerable properties; rest collects the remainder. Optional chaining `?.` short-circuits to `undefined` instead of throwing when an intermediate value is null or undefined, and `??` supplies a fallback only for null/undefined.
@deep
Together these three eliminate most defensive boilerplate. `const { data: { items = [] } = {} } = response` handles a missing body, a missing `data` and a missing `items` in one line — though nesting that deeply is usually a sign the response should be validated instead.

`?.` also works for calls (`fn?.()`) and index access (`arr?.[0]`), which is how you safely invoke an optional callback prop in React.
@code ts Idiomatic use in a real handler
// Destructure with rename + default, and collect the rest
const { page = 1, limit = 20, sort = '-createdAt', ...filters } = req.query;

// Omit a sensitive field without mutating the original
const { password, refreshToken, ...safeUser } = user;
res.json(safeUser);

// Optional chaining across an uncertain payload
const city = order?.customer?.address?.city ?? 'unknown';
onUploadProgress?.(percent);            // optional callback prop
const first = rows?.[0]?.id;

// Swap, and function-parameter destructuring with a default object
let [a, b] = [1, 2]; [a, b] = [b, a];
function createUser({ name, role = 'user' } = {}) { /* ... */ }
@end
@senior
Two cautions I raise in review. First, `?.` used everywhere hides a modelling problem — if you need five levels of optional chaining, the real fix is to validate and normalise the payload at the boundary with Zod so downstream code has guaranteed shapes. Second, object spread copies only *own enumerable* properties, so spreading a class instance drops its prototype methods; that is why spreading a Sequelize model instance gives you a lifeless object and you want `.get({ plain: true })` or `.toJSON()` instead. That is a bug I have actually shipped and fixed.
@followup
What is the difference between `?.` and `&&` chaining?
@answer
`a && a.b && a.b.c` treats every falsy value as absent, so a legitimate `0` or `''` in the middle breaks the chain. `a?.b?.c` short-circuits only on `null` and `undefined`, which is almost always what you meant. It is also shorter and does not evaluate `a` repeatedly.
@red
- Not knowing `?.` short-circuits the rest of the chain including calls.
- Using `?.` on the left of an assignment (it is not allowed).

### What is the difference between ES modules and CommonJS?
@short
CommonJS is Node's original synchronous `require`/`module.exports` system, resolved at runtime. ES modules use `import`/`export`, are statically analysable, hoisted, asynchronously loaded and give you live bindings. Static structure is what enables tree-shaking, which is why every modern bundler and my Vite setup targets ESM.
@deep
`require` executes the module and returns a snapshot of `module.exports` at that moment. `import` creates a live binding: if the exporting module later reassigns the value, the importer sees the new one. That difference is what makes circular ESM imports fail loudly with a TDZ error rather than silently handing you a half-built object.

In Node, `.mjs` or `"type": "module"` selects ESM; `.cjs` or the default selects CommonJS. ESM has top-level `await` and no `__dirname`/`require` (you reconstruct them from `import.meta.url`). CommonJS cannot `require` an ESM module synchronously — that is the interop wall people hit when a dependency goes ESM-only.
@code js The interop details you will actually be asked about
// package.json: { "type": "module" }
import { readFile } from 'node:fs/promises';
import { fileURLToPath } from 'node:url';
import path from 'node:path';

const __filename = fileURLToPath(import.meta.url);
const __dirname  = path.dirname(__filename);       // ESM has no __dirname

const config = await readFile('./config.json', 'utf8');   // top-level await: ESM only

// Loading a CJS-only dependency from ESM: default import works
import legacy from 'some-cjs-package';
// Loading ESM from CJS: must be dynamic and async
const { default: esmOnly } = await import('some-esm-package');
@end
@senior
The bundle-size argument is the one that matters commercially: ESM's static imports let Rollup/esbuild prove which exports are unused and drop them, so `import { debounce } from 'lodash-es'` ships one function while `require('lodash')` ships the whole library. That, plus dropping Babel for esbuild, is exactly the kind of change behind a large build-time and bundle-size reduction on a Vite migration. On the server I would also flag that `"sideEffects": false` in `package.json` is what lets the bundler be aggressive, and getting it wrong silently drops CSS imports.
@followup
Why can't you `require()` an ES module?
@answer
Because `require` is synchronous and ESM resolution and evaluation are asynchronous — the module graph may need network or async hooks before it can be evaluated. The escape hatch is dynamic `import()`, which returns a Promise and works from CommonJS. Recent Node versions can `require()` a fully synchronous ESM graph, but I would not rely on that in a library that has to support older runtimes.
@red
- Saying they are just different syntax for the same thing.
- Not knowing tree-shaking depends on static analysis.

### How do you handle errors correctly in async JavaScript?
@short
Wrap awaited calls in `try/catch`, always reject with `Error` objects rather than strings so you keep a stack, never swallow an error silently, and make sure every Promise has a rejection handler — an unhandled rejection terminates a modern Node process. In Express I funnel everything into one error middleware through an async wrapper.
@deep
The three failure modes are: a `.then` chain with no `.catch`; an `async` function called without `await` or `.catch` (fire-and-forget); and a `throw` inside a callback passed to something that does not understand Promises, such as an old-style `EventEmitter` handler — that throw escapes to the top level.

For the API layer, the pattern that scales is a typed error hierarchy plus one central handler: domain code throws `new NotFoundError('order')`, the middleware maps error classes to status codes, logs with a request id, and returns a stable JSON shape. Clients then get consistent errors and you get one place to add observability.
@code ts Typed errors + async wrapper + central Express handler
export class AppError extends Error {
  constructor(message: string, public status = 500, public code = 'INTERNAL', public expose = false) {
    super(message);
    this.name = new.target.name;
    Error.captureStackTrace?.(this, new.target);
  }
}
export class NotFoundError extends AppError {
  constructor(what: string) { super(`${what} not found`, 404, 'NOT_FOUND', true); }
}

// removes try/catch from every handler; Express 5 does this natively
export const ah = (fn: RequestHandler): RequestHandler =>
  (req, res, next) => Promise.resolve(fn(req, res, next)).catch(next);

app.get('/orders/:id', ah(async (req, res) => {
  const order = await repo.find(req.params.id);
  if (!order) throw new NotFoundError('Order');
  res.json(order);
}));

app.use((err, req, res, _next) => {
  const status = err instanceof AppError ? err.status : 500;
  logger.error({ err, reqId: req.id, path: req.path, userId: req.user?.id });
  res.status(status).json({
    error: { code: err.code ?? 'INTERNAL', message: err.expose ? err.message : 'Something went wrong', requestId: req.id },
  });
});

process.on('unhandledRejection', (r) => { logger.fatal({ r }); process.exit(1); });
@end
@senior
Two senior points. First, never leak internal messages to clients — a raw Postgres error string exposes your schema, so I gate it behind an `expose` flag and always return a request id the user can quote to support. Second, on `uncaughtException` and `unhandledRejection` the correct action is to log and exit, not to continue: the process is in an undefined state, and the supervisor (PM2, ECS, Kubernetes) will restart it cleanly. Graceful shutdown means stopping the listener, draining in-flight requests, closing the pool, then exiting.
@followup
Should you retry a failed operation automatically?
@answer
Only if it is idempotent and the failure is transient — a network timeout, a 429, a 5xx. Retrying a non-idempotent `POST /payments` can double-charge, which is why payment APIs use idempotency keys. When I do retry, it is with exponential backoff plus jitter and a cap, and behind a circuit breaker, because synchronised retries from many clients are how a small blip becomes a full outage.
@red
- `catch (e) { console.log(e) }` and continuing as if nothing happened.
- Throwing strings instead of `Error` instances.
- Trying to keep the process alive after an `uncaughtException`.

### What are generators and iterators, and when have you used them?
@short
An iterator is any object with a `next()` returning `{ value, done }`; anything implementing `Symbol.iterator` is iterable and works with `for...of` and spread. A generator function (`function*`) is the ergonomic way to build one — it pauses at each `yield` and resumes on demand. I use them for lazy pagination and for streaming large datasets without loading everything into memory.
@deep
The value of a generator is laziness: values are produced on request, so an infinite sequence is fine and a 10-million-row export never materialises in memory. Async generators (`async function*` + `for await...of`) extend this to asynchronous sources, which is exactly the shape of paginated APIs, Kafka consumers and — very relevantly — token-by-token LLM streaming.
@code ts Async generator that paginates an API and streams to the client
async function* paginate(url: string) {
  let cursor: string | undefined;
  do {
    const res = await fetch(`${url}?cursor=${cursor ?? ''}`).then(r => r.json());
    yield* res.items;                    // yield each item, lazily
    cursor = res.nextCursor;
  } while (cursor);
}

// Memory stays flat regardless of total size
for await (const item of paginate('/api/transactions')) {
  await writeCsvRow(item);
}

// Same shape for SSE token streaming from an LLM
async function* tokens(prompt: string) {
  const stream = await llm.stream(prompt);
  for await (const chunk of stream) yield chunk.delta ?? '';
}
@end
@senior
Streaming is the difference between an export endpoint that works at 100 rows and one that works at 5 million. The naive version buffers the whole result set in Node's heap and OOMs the container; the generator version, piped into a CSV transform and then into the response, keeps memory flat and starts delivering bytes immediately, which also improves perceived latency. The thing to add is backpressure — if you `yield` faster than the socket drains, you rebuild the buffer in a different place, so the pipeline must respect the writable's `drain` signal, which `stream.pipeline` does for you.
@followup
How does this relate to Node streams?
@answer
They solve the same problem with different ergonomics, and they interoperate: `Readable.from(asyncGenerator)` turns a generator into a stream, and a stream is itself async-iterable, so `for await (const chunk of readable)` works. Streams give you backpressure, piping and error propagation for free; generators give you readable business logic. I write the logic as a generator and let `stream.pipeline` handle the plumbing.
@red
- Saying generators are "just old async/await".
- Not being able to name a real use case.

### What is the difference between `Map`/`Set` and plain objects/arrays?
@short
`Map` allows any value as a key, preserves insertion order, has a real `size`, and is optimised for frequent additions and deletions; a plain object only takes string and symbol keys and inherits from `Object.prototype`. `Set` gives O(1) membership and automatic de-duplication, versus `Array.includes` which is O(n).
@deep
The prototype issue is a security concern, not a style one: a plain object used as a lookup table inherits keys like `constructor` and `__proto__`, which is the basis of prototype-pollution attacks against a user-supplied key. `Object.create(null)` or a `Map` avoids it entirely.

`WeakMap` and `WeakSet` hold their keys weakly, so an entry disappears when the key object is garbage-collected. That is the correct structure for per-object metadata caches, because it cannot leak.
@code js Choosing the right one — and the performance difference
// ❌ O(n) per check -> O(n·m) overall
const seen = [];
if (!seen.includes(id)) seen.push(id);

// ✅ O(1) per check
const seenSet = new Set();
if (!seenSet.has(id)) seenSet.add(id);

const dedup = [...new Set(orderIds)];              // one-line de-duplication

// Map: object keys, ordered, size, safe against prototype pollution
const cache = new Map();
cache.set(reqObject, computed);
cache.size;

// WeakMap: entry is collected when the key object dies — cannot leak
const meta = new WeakMap();
meta.set(socket, { userId, joinedAt: t });
@end
@senior
The rule I use: a `Map` when keys are dynamic or non-string data, a plain object when the shape is fixed and known (because objects are still faster for monomorphic property access and they serialise to JSON for free — `JSON.stringify(new Map())` gives you `{}`, which surprises people at the API boundary). In a WebSocket server the connection registry is a `Map` from user id to socket set, and per-socket metadata is a `WeakMap`, so a dropped connection cannot leave a leaked entry behind. Leaks in long-lived Node processes almost always trace back to an unbounded `Map` or array that nobody sweeps.
@followup
How would you cap a `Map` used as a cache?
@answer
Use an LRU: on every `get`, delete and re-set the key so it moves to the end of the insertion order, and when `size` exceeds the limit, delete the first key from `map.keys().next().value`. That is about ten lines and relies on `Map`'s guaranteed insertion ordering. For anything shared across processes I would use Redis with a TTL rather than in-process memory.
@red
- Using an array with `includes` for membership on large collections.
- Not knowing `Map` preserves insertion order.

### What is currying and partial application, and where is it useful?
@short
Currying transforms a function of N arguments into a chain of N single-argument functions; partial application fixes some arguments now and leaves the rest for later. Both are ways of pre-configuring behaviour, which is why they show up in middleware factories, selectors and logger builders.
@deep
In practice you rarely need true currying — partial application via a closure or `Function.prototype.bind` covers most needs. The pattern that earns its keep is the **configurable middleware factory**: a function that takes options and returns the actual handler, so the same middleware can be mounted with different rules on different routes.
@code ts Middleware factories — the pattern that actually pays off
// Partial application: config now, request later
const requireRole = (...roles: Role[]): RequestHandler => (req, res, next) =>
  roles.includes(req.user?.role) ? next() : res.status(403).json({ error: 'Forbidden' });

router.delete('/users/:id', requireRole('admin', 'owner'), deleteUser);

// Curried logger — context accumulates without repeating yourself
const log = (service: string) => (level: string) => (msg: string, meta = {}) =>
  logger[level]({ service, msg, ...meta });

const payLog = log('payments');
payLog('error')('webhook signature invalid', { provider: 'stripe' });

// Reusable curried helper for data pipelines
const pluck = (key: string) => (obj: Record<string, unknown>) => obj[key];
const totals = orders.map(pluck('total'));
@end
@senior
The trade-off I raise is readability and stack traces: heavily curried code produces anonymous frames that make production stack traces useless, and it can defeat TypeScript inference so you end up annotating everything by hand. I use it deliberately — middleware factories, selector factories, per-tenant configured clients — and avoid it as a general style. Named intermediate functions cost nothing and make an incident at 2am survivable.
@followup
How does this relate to dependency injection?
@answer
It is the functional form of it. `createUserService({ repo, mailer, clock })` returning an object of functions is partial application of dependencies, and it gives you exactly what constructor injection gives you — swap the repo for a fake in tests, swap the mailer per environment — without any framework or decorators. In Go I express the same idea by returning a struct with the dependencies as fields.
@red
- Reciting the definition with no use case.
- Claiming currying improves performance.

### What is memoisation and how do you implement it?
@short
Memoisation caches a function's result keyed by its arguments so repeated calls with the same input skip the work. It is only valid for pure functions, and in production the two things that matter are the key function and the eviction policy — an unbounded memo cache is a memory leak.
@deep
The naive implementation is a `Map` from `JSON.stringify(args)` to result. That key function is where the bugs live: object key order is not guaranteed to match, `undefined` disappears, and stringifying large arguments can cost more than the computation you are avoiding.

In React the equivalents are `useMemo` (cache a value), `useCallback` (cache a function reference) and `React.memo` (skip re-render if props are shallow-equal). All three are trades: you pay comparison cost and memory to save render cost, and they are only worth it when the saved work is genuinely expensive or when the identity is needed downstream.
@code ts Memoise with a TTL and a size cap — safe for a long-lived server
function memoize<A extends unknown[], R>(
  fn: (...a: A) => Promise<R>,
  { ttlMs = 60_000, max = 500, key = (...a: A) => JSON.stringify(a) } = {}
) {
  const cache = new Map<string, { v: Promise<R>; exp: number }>();
  return async (...args: A): Promise<R> => {
    const k = key(...args), now = Date.now();
    const hit = cache.get(k);
    if (hit && hit.exp > now) { cache.delete(k); cache.set(k, hit); return hit.v; }  // LRU touch
    const v = fn(...args).catch(e => { cache.delete(k); throw e; });   // never cache a rejection
    cache.set(k, { v, exp: now + ttlMs });
    if (cache.size > max) cache.delete(cache.keys().next().value);     // evict oldest
    return v;
  };
}

const getFxRate = memoize(fetchFxRate, { ttlMs: 300_000 });
@end
@senior
Two details separate a real implementation from a toy. First, cache the *Promise*, not the resolved value — that gives you request coalescing, so fifty concurrent callers for the same key trigger one upstream call instead of fifty. Second, never cache a rejection, or one transient blip is pinned for the whole TTL. And in a multi-instance deployment an in-process memo means N copies with N different expiry times, so anything correctness-sensitive belongs in Redis with an explicit invalidation path.
@followup
When is `useMemo` a bad idea in React?
@answer
When the computation is cheap. `useMemo` itself allocates, stores the dependency array and runs a comparison every render, so memoising `a + b` is strictly slower and adds noise. I reach for it when profiling shows an expensive calculation, when a referentially stable object or array is needed by a memoised child or an effect dependency, and otherwise I leave it out. The React Compiler is now automating most of these decisions, which is worth mentioning as awareness of where the ecosystem is going.
@red
- Memoising an impure function.
- Unbounded cache with no TTL or eviction.
- Memoising everything in React "for performance".

# SECTION TypeScript Fundamentals | You migrated a production codebase to TypeScript — expect these to be asked as "why did you bother?" as much as "what is a generic?".

### What problems does TypeScript actually solve, and what does it not solve?
@short
TypeScript gives you a compile-time contract: the shape of every value, checked before the code runs, plus editor-grade autocomplete and safe refactoring. It does not exist at runtime — types are erased — so it does nothing about untrusted input, and a `JSON.parse` typed as `User` is a lie the compiler happily believes.
@deep
The value shows up in three places. First, refactoring: renaming a field on a widely used entity becomes a compiler task instead of a grep-and-pray exercise. Second, self-documentation: a function signature tells the next engineer what is optional, what is nullable and what the error shape is. Third, whole classes of bugs — `undefined is not a function`, wrong argument order, forgotten `await` — become build failures.

What it does not do is validate at runtime. Every boundary — HTTP request bodies, query params, third-party webhooks, environment variables, database rows from a raw query — is `unknown` in reality, and typing it otherwise just moves the crash further from its cause.
@code ts The boundary pattern: validate once, then trust the type everywhere inside
import { z } from 'zod';

const CreateOrder = z.object({
  items: z.array(z.object({ sku: z.string().min(1), qty: z.number().int().positive() })).min(1),
  couponCode: z.string().optional(),
  currency: z.enum(['INR', 'USD', 'EUR']),
});
export type CreateOrder = z.infer<typeof CreateOrder>;   // one source of truth

app.post('/orders', (req, res) => {
  const parsed = CreateOrder.safeParse(req.body);
  if (!parsed.success) {
    return res.status(422).json({ error: 'VALIDATION', details: parsed.error.flatten() });
  }
  return createOrder(parsed.data);      // fully typed AND actually verified from here on
});

// Same idea for env — fail at boot, not at 2am on the one code path that reads it
export const env = z.object({
  DATABASE_URL: z.string().url(),
  JWT_SECRET: z.string().min(32),
  PORT: z.coerce.number().default(3000),
}).parse(process.env);
@end
@senior
When I am asked about my TypeScript migration, the answer that lands is about *sequencing*, not syntax: enable `allowJs` and rename files incrementally, start with `strict: false` to get the build green, then turn on `strictNullChecks` first because it finds the highest density of real bugs, and ban `any` in new code with `@typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any` while leaving legacy `any` behind a `// TODO` so you can measure the burn-down. The measurable outcome is fewer runtime type errors in Sentry and faster review, not "the code is prettier".
@followup
How do you keep frontend and backend types in sync?
@answer
By generating rather than duplicating. Options in increasing strength: share a Zod schema package in a monorepo and infer both sides from it; generate a client from OpenAPI with `openapi-typescript`; or use an end-to-end typed layer like tRPC where the client type is literally the server's inferred type. Hand-written duplicate interfaces on both sides always drift, and the drift only shows up in production.
@red
- Claiming TypeScript prevents runtime errors.
- Casting request bodies with `as` and calling it validation.
- Saying "we use TypeScript" but every model is `any`.

### `interface` vs `type` — which do you use and why?
@short
They overlap almost entirely for object shapes. `interface` supports declaration merging and reads better in `implements` clauses; `type` can express unions, tuples, mapped and conditional types, which interfaces cannot. My rule: `interface` for object contracts that others may extend, `type` for everything else — especially unions.
@deep
`interface` is open — declaring it twice merges the members, which is how libraries augment `Express.Request` or the global `Window`. That openness is a feature for extension points and a hazard for closed domain models.

`type` is closed and far more expressive. Only a `type` can do `type Status = 'draft' | 'paid' | 'refunded'`, tuple types, template literal types or conditional types. Union types are the real reason to reach for it, and discriminated unions are TypeScript's single most valuable modelling tool.
@code ts Declaration merging vs discriminated unions
// interface: merging — how you attach `req.user` after auth middleware
declare global {
  namespace Express {
    interface Request { user?: { id: string; role: 'admin' | 'user' }; requestId: string; }
  }
}

// type: a discriminated union the compiler can narrow exhaustively
type PaymentResult =
  | { status: 'succeeded'; chargeId: string; amount: number }
  | { status: 'requires_action'; clientSecret: string }
  | { status: 'failed'; code: string; declineReason: string };

function handle(r: PaymentResult) {
  switch (r.status) {
    case 'succeeded':       return markPaid(r.chargeId);       // amount/chargeId available
    case 'requires_action': return sendToSca(r.clientSecret);  // only clientSecret available
    case 'failed':          return recordFailure(r.code);
    default: {
      const _exhaustive: never = r;      // adding a 4th variant becomes a COMPILE error
      throw new Error(`Unhandled: ${_exhaustive}`);
    }
  }
}
@end
@senior
The `never` exhaustiveness check is the detail that signals experience. Model your payment states, socket message types and job types as discriminated unions, and adding a new variant becomes a compile error at every site that must handle it — the compiler generates your to-do list. That is far stronger than a string status field plus an `if/else` chain, and it is exactly the safety you want in a payment lifecycle where an unhandled state means a customer is charged with no order.
@followup
Can an interface extend a type, or vice versa?
@answer
Yes in both directions, as long as the type is an object shape — `interface A extends SomeObjectType {}` works, and `type B = SomeInterface & { extra: string }` works. It breaks only when the type is a union, because an interface cannot represent a union. Note also that `extends` and `&` behave differently on conflicting members: `extends` errors, while an intersection quietly produces `never` for the conflicting property, which is a nastier failure.
@red
- "Always use interface" or "always use type" with no reasoning.
- Not knowing interfaces merge.

### Explain generics with a real example.
@short
Generics let a function or type work over many types while preserving the relationship between input and output — instead of `any`, which discards it. The classic case is a typed API client: `get<User>('/users/1')` returns `Promise<User>`, so every downstream property access stays checked.
@deep
Constraints (`extends`) are what make generics useful rather than merely generic: `<T extends { id: string }>` says "any object that has an id", and `<K extends keyof T>` ties a key parameter to the object you passed, so a typo in the key name is a compile error.

Defaults (`<T = unknown>`) keep call sites clean, and inference means you almost never write the type argument explicitly — you only annotate when inference cannot see it, such as at an I/O boundary.
@code ts A typed repository and a typed fetch wrapper — the two everyday generics
// Constrained generic: T must at least have an id
interface Entity { id: string }

class Repository<T extends Entity> {
  constructor(private model: ModelStatic<T>) {}
  findById(id: string): Promise<T | null> { return this.model.findByPk(id); }
  async pluck<K extends keyof T>(id: string, key: K): Promise<T[K] | undefined> {
    return (await this.findById(id))?.[key];       // return type follows the key
  }
}

const users = new Repository<User>(UserModel);
const email = await users.pluck('u_1', 'email');   // string | undefined
// await users.pluck('u_1', 'emial');              // ❌ compile error — typo caught

// Generic API client: one function, correct types at every call site
async function api<T>(path: string, init?: RequestInit): Promise<T> {
  const res = await fetch(`${BASE}${path}`, init);
  if (!res.ok) throw new ApiError(res.status, await res.text());
  return res.json() as Promise<T>;                  // ⚠️ an assertion — validate if untrusted
}
const order = await api<Order>('/orders/123');
@end
@senior
Two things I would add. First, that `as Promise<T>` is an unchecked assertion — for an internal service I accept it, for a third-party API I run the response through Zod, because a silently changed upstream field otherwise surfaces as a crash three layers away. Second, generics have a compile-time cost: deeply recursive conditional types are a real cause of slow builds and "type instantiation is excessively deep" errors, so I keep the clever type-level programming to library boundaries and keep application code boring.
@followup
What is the difference between `unknown` and `any`?
@answer
`any` disables checking entirely and infects everything it touches. `unknown` is the type-safe top type: you can assign anything *to* it, but you cannot use it until you narrow it with a type guard, `typeof`, or a schema parse. `unknown` is the correct type for a `catch` variable, a `JSON.parse` result and any external payload — it forces the check instead of skipping it.
@red
- Using `any` and calling it a generic.
- Not knowing about `extends` constraints.

### What are utility types and which do you use most?
@short
Utility types are built-in type transformations: `Partial`, `Required`, `Pick`, `Omit`, `Record`, `Readonly`, `ReturnType`, `Awaited`, `NonNullable`. They let you derive types from one source of truth instead of hand-maintaining near-duplicate interfaces that drift apart.
@deep
The derivation habit is the point. If `User` is the entity, then `Omit<User, 'passwordHash'>` is the API response, `Pick<User, 'email' | 'name'>` is the update payload, and `Partial<User>` is the patch body. Change `User` once and every derived type follows.

`Record<K, V>` is the everyday map type. `ReturnType<typeof fn>` and `Awaited<T>` let you extract types from existing functions instead of restating them — very useful with Zod's `z.infer` and with Sequelize/Prisma model types.
@code ts Deriving an entire API surface from one entity
interface User {
  id: string; email: string; name: string; role: 'admin' | 'user';
  passwordHash: string; createdAt: Date;
}

type PublicUser  = Omit<User, 'passwordHash'>;                 // safe response
type CreateUser  = Pick<User, 'email' | 'name' | 'role'> & { password: string };
type UpdateUser  = Partial<Pick<User, 'name' | 'role'>>;       // PATCH body
type UsersById   = Record<string, PublicUser>;
type Frozen      = Readonly<PublicUser>;

// Extract types instead of re-declaring them
const buildOrder = async (id: string) => ({ id, total: 10, items: [] as Item[] });
type Order = Awaited<ReturnType<typeof buildOrder>>;

// Template literal + mapped type: typed event names, checked at compile time
type Entity = 'order' | 'user' | 'payment';
type Event  = `${Entity}.${'created' | 'updated' | 'deleted'}`;   // 'order.created' | ...
const handlers: Record<Event, () => void> = { /* compiler demands all 9 keys */ } as any;
@end
@senior
The senior framing is "derive, never duplicate". I have seen a bug where a `PublicUser` interface was hand-written, someone added `passwordResetToken` to `User`, and the serialiser spread the whole entity — the token leaked in an API response. Deriving with `Omit` plus an explicit allow-list serialiser would have made that structurally impossible. `Omit` is convenient but it is a deny-list; for anything security-sensitive I prefer `Pick`, because a new field is then excluded by default rather than included by default.
@followup
What is the difference between `Omit` and `Exclude`?
@answer
`Omit<T, K>` removes *properties* from an object type. `Exclude<U, M>` removes *members* from a union type. So `Omit<User, 'id'>` gives a user without the id field, while `Exclude<'a' | 'b' | 'c', 'c'>` gives `'a' | 'b'`. `Extract` is the complement of `Exclude`, and `NonNullable<T>` is just `Exclude<T, null | undefined>`.
@red
- Hand-writing duplicate interfaces for every DTO.
- Not knowing `Omit` does not error on a key that does not exist (unless you constrain it).

### What are type guards and narrowing?
@short
Narrowing is how TypeScript refines a broad type into a specific one inside a branch, using `typeof`, `instanceof`, `in`, truthiness, equality on a discriminant, or a user-defined type-guard function returning `x is T`. It is the mechanism that makes union types safe to work with.
@deep
The control-flow analyser tracks narrowing per branch, and it resets when it cannot prove the value did not change — for instance after an `await` in some closure scenarios, or for a mutable property accessed twice. That is why narrowing a local `const` works reliably and narrowing `obj.maybe` sometimes appears to "forget".

User-defined guards (`function isX(v: unknown): v is X`) are an assertion you are making to the compiler, so a wrong guard silently breaks type safety. That is why schema-based narrowing (Zod's `safeParse`) is stronger — the check and the type come from the same declaration.
@code ts Every narrowing tool, in one realistic handler
type Ok  = { ok: true; data: Buffer };
type Err = { ok: false; error: string };

function isOk(r: Ok | Err): r is Ok { return r.ok; }         // user-defined guard

function handle(v: unknown, r: Ok | Err) {
  if (typeof v === 'string') v.trim();                        // typeof narrowing
  if (v instanceof Error) console.log(v.stack);               // instanceof narrowing
  if (v && typeof v === 'object' && 'id' in v) console.log(v.id);   // `in` narrowing
  if (isOk(r)) upload(r.data); else logger.warn(r.error);     // guard narrowing
}

// The correct way to type a catch block (it is `unknown` under useUnknownInCatchVariables)
try { await pay(); }
catch (e: unknown) {
  if (e instanceof StripeError)      return retryable(e);
  if (e instanceof z.ZodError)       return badRequest(e.flatten());
  const msg = e instanceof Error ? e.message : String(e);
  logger.error({ msg });
  throw e;
}
@end
@senior
The point I make is that assertion functions and `as` casts are *promises you make to the compiler*, and the compiler cannot check them. In a codebase I would rather see one Zod schema at the boundary than fifteen hand-written type guards, because guards drift from reality the moment the upstream payload changes and nothing tells you. For error handling specifically, `useUnknownInCatchVariables` (part of `strict`) is worth turning on precisely because it forces you to narrow before you touch `e.message` — the single most common source of "Cannot read properties of undefined" *inside the error handler*.
@followup
What does the `satisfies` operator do?
@answer
`satisfies` checks a value against a type without widening it to that type, so you keep the literal inference *and* get the validation. `const config = { port: 3000, env: 'prod' } satisfies Config` verifies the shape but keeps `env` as the literal `'prod'` rather than `string`. It replaced the old trick of a helper function purely for inference, and it is the right tool for typed config objects and route tables.
@red
- Using `as` to silence an error instead of narrowing.
- Writing a type guard whose body does not actually check the shape.

### What do `strict` mode and `strictNullChecks` change?
@short
`strict` turns on a family of checks; the most valuable is `strictNullChecks`, which stops `null` and `undefined` from being assignable to every type. Without it, `string` silently includes `null` and the compiler cannot warn you about the most common runtime crash in JavaScript.
@deep
`strict` includes `noImplicitAny`, `strictNullChecks`, `strictFunctionTypes`, `strictBindCallApply`, `strictPropertyInitialization`, `useUnknownInCatchVariables`, `alwaysStrict` and `noImplicitThis`. Beyond `strict` I also enable `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, which makes `arr[0]` return `T | undefined` — pedantic, but it catches real off-by-one and empty-array bugs.

On a migration you do not enable everything at once. The order that works is: get it compiling with `strict: false`, enable `noImplicitAny`, then `strictNullChecks` (the big one, and the one that produces the most errors), then the rest.
@code json A pragmatic strict tsconfig for a Node service
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    "target": "ES2022",
    "module": "NodeNext",
    "moduleResolution": "NodeNext",
    "strict": true,
    "noUncheckedIndexedAccess": true,
    "noImplicitOverride": true,
    "exactOptionalPropertyTypes": true,
    "noUnusedLocals": true,
    "noFallthroughCasesInSwitch": true,
    "skipLibCheck": true,
    "isolatedModules": true,
    "sourceMap": true,
    "outDir": "dist"
  },
  "include": ["src"]
}
@end
@senior
`skipLibCheck: true` is worth being able to defend: it skips type-checking `.d.ts` files in `node_modules`, which cuts build time substantially and avoids being blocked by a dependency's broken types — the trade is that a genuinely wrong library type will not be caught. And `isolatedModules` matters if you use esbuild/SWC via Vite, because those transpile file-by-file and cannot do whole-program analysis; turning it on makes the compiler reject the patterns that would silently break, such as re-exporting a type without `export type`.
@followup
Does TypeScript slow down your build?
@answer
Type-checking does; transpiling does not have to. The standard setup is to let esbuild or SWC strip types for speed and run `tsc --noEmit` in parallel as a separate check in CI and in the editor. That is how Vite gives you instant HMR and still full type safety — and it is a large part of why a Webpack-plus-`ts-loader` build measured in minutes drops to seconds after migrating.
@red
- Running a production TypeScript project with `strict: false` and no plan.
- Not knowing what `strict` actually enables.

### What is the difference between `enum`, union of literals, and `as const`?
@short
A string-literal union is the lightest and my default — zero runtime cost, exhaustively checkable. `as const` gives you a frozen object plus a derived union, which is best when you need both the values and the type. A TypeScript `enum` emits real runtime code and numeric enums are not type-safe, so I avoid them.
@deep
A numeric `enum` accepts any number at some positions and reverse-maps values to names in the emitted object, which is surprising. `const enum` is inlined and has no runtime object, but it does not work with `isolatedModules`/esbuild, which rules it out of most modern build setups.

The `as const` pattern gives you the best of both: an iterable array or object for runtime use (validation lists, dropdown options, DB check constraints) and a union type derived from it, so the two can never disagree.
@code ts The pattern I actually ship
export const ORDER_STATUS = ['pending', 'paid', 'shipped', 'refunded'] as const;
export type OrderStatus = typeof ORDER_STATUS[number];   // 'pending' | 'paid' | ...

// runtime use — validation, dropdowns, seeding — from the same source
const schema = z.enum(ORDER_STATUS);
ORDER_STATUS.forEach(s => console.log(s));

// Object form when you need labels or codes as well
export const ROLE = { Admin: 'admin', Manager: 'manager', User: 'user' } as const;
export type Role = typeof ROLE[keyof typeof ROLE];       // 'admin' | 'manager' | 'user'

// Compare: an enum emits runtime code and needs an import everywhere it is used
enum Bad { Admin = 'admin' }      // extra bundle weight, awkward with plain JSON payloads
@end
@senior
The deciding argument for me is the boundary: your database stores `'paid'`, your JSON API sends `'paid'`, and a string-literal union *is* `'paid'` — no conversion, no import needed by consumers, and it serialises for free. A TypeScript `enum` introduces a nominal type that requires importing the enum just to pass a value, which is friction across a monorepo and impossible for an external API consumer. The one time I would keep an enum is a large legacy codebase where they are already ubiquitous, because consistency beats correctness on a style question.
@followup
Is TypeScript's type system nominal or structural?
@answer
Structural — compatibility is decided by shape, so any object with the right members is assignable, regardless of its declared name. That is usually convenient but it means `UserId` and `OrderId`, both `string`, are interchangeable. When that matters I use branded types: `type UserId = string & { readonly __brand: 'UserId' }`, created only through a factory function, which gives you nominal-style safety for ids and money amounts.
@red
- Using numeric enums for API values that go over the wire.
- Not knowing `as const` exists.

### How do you type an async function and handle its errors in TypeScript?
@short
An `async` function's return type is always a Promise, so I annotate the resolved type and let the compiler add the wrapper — `Promise<User | null>`. TypeScript cannot type what a function *throws*, which is its one real gap in error handling, so for expected failures I return a discriminated result type instead of throwing.
@deep
Because throws are untyped, a caller cannot know from the signature whether to expect a `NotFoundError`. The two mitigations are: a typed error hierarchy narrowed with `instanceof` at the catch site, or a `Result<T, E>` union returned from functions where failure is an ordinary outcome rather than an exception.

My rule: throw for *exceptional* conditions (database down, programmer error), return a `Result` for *expected* ones (invalid coupon, insufficient stock, payment declined). Expected failures are business outcomes and deserve to be in the type signature.
@code ts Result type for expected failures, exceptions for the rest
type Result<T, E extends string = string> =
  | { ok: true; value: T }
  | { ok: false; error: E; detail?: string };

async function applyCoupon(
  orderId: string, code: string
): Promise<Result<Order, 'NOT_FOUND' | 'EXPIRED' | 'MIN_VALUE'>> {
  const coupon = await repo.coupon(code);
  if (!coupon)               return { ok: false, error: 'NOT_FOUND' };
  if (coupon.expiresAt < new Date()) return { ok: false, error: 'EXPIRED' };
  const order = await repo.order(orderId);                 // throws if the DB is down — correct
  if (order.total < coupon.minValue) return { ok: false, error: 'MIN_VALUE' };
  return { ok: true, value: await repo.apply(order, coupon) };
}

const r = await applyCoupon(id, code);
if (!r.ok) {
  // the compiler forces you to handle every error string you declared
  return res.status(422).json({ error: r.error });
}
res.json(r.value);
@end
@senior
The senior point is that this changes what shows up in your alerting. If invalid coupons throw, they land in Sentry alongside real incidents and your error rate is meaningless; if they are typed results, your exception stream only contains things that are actually broken. That distinction — expected failure versus exceptional failure — is what makes on-call sustainable, and it is a stronger answer than any amount of `try/catch` syntax detail.
@followup
What happens if you forget to `await` a Promise?
@answer
The function continues immediately with a `Promise` object rather than the value, and any rejection becomes an unhandled rejection that can kill the process. TypeScript will usually catch the type mismatch, but not when the result is discarded — which is exactly the dangerous case. The linter rules `@typescript-eslint/no-floating-promises` and `no-misused-promises` catch it, and I treat them as errors, not warnings; the second one also catches passing an `async` function where a `void` callback is expected, which is how unhandled rejections sneak into Express handlers.
@red
- Typing a function as `Promise<any>`.
- Throwing for ordinary business validation failures.

### What are decorators and where have you seen them?
@short
Decorators are functions that annotate and modify classes, methods, properties or parameters at definition time. They are the backbone of NestJS and TypeORM — `@Controller`, `@Injectable`, `@Entity`, `@Column` — and they work by writing metadata that a framework reads at runtime through reflection.
@deep
The old TypeScript implementation (`experimentalDecorators`) is what NestJS and TypeORM use, together with `emitDecoratorMetadata` and `reflect-metadata` to record parameter types for dependency injection. The ES2022 standard decorators are a different, incompatible design, which is why you must be careful which one a library expects.

Mechanically, a method decorator receives the target, the key and the property descriptor, and can wrap the original function — that is how `@Cacheable` or `@Transactional` or `@Log` are implemented.
@code ts A caching decorator — the canonical example
function Cacheable(ttlMs = 30_000) {
  return function (_target: unknown, key: string, desc: PropertyDescriptor) {
    const original = desc.value;
    const cache = new Map<string, { v: unknown; exp: number }>();
    desc.value = async function (...args: unknown[]) {
      const k = `${key}:${JSON.stringify(args)}`;
      const hit = cache.get(k);
      if (hit && hit.exp > Date.now()) return hit.v;
      const v = await original.apply(this, args);
      cache.set(k, { v, exp: Date.now() + ttlMs });
      return v;
    };
  };
}

class PricingService {
  @Cacheable(60_000)
  async getFxRate(from: string, to: string) { return http.get(`/fx/${from}/${to}`); }
}
@end
@senior
The trade-off I raise is debuggability and testability: decorators hide control flow, so a `@Transactional` that silently opens a transaction means a reader cannot see where the boundary is, and stack traces point at the wrapper rather than the code. They are excellent for genuinely cross-cutting, uniform concerns — validation, auth guards, tracing — and poor for anything with business meaning. Given the choice, I keep transaction boundaries explicit in the service layer, because "where does this transaction start and end" is a question I want answered by reading, not by convention.
@followup
How does NestJS dependency injection actually work?
@answer
`emitDecoratorMetadata` makes the compiler record each constructor parameter's type into `design:paramtypes` metadata. At bootstrap, Nest reads that metadata, resolves each type from its provider registry (respecting module scope), instantiates in dependency order and caches singletons. That is also why Nest needs a class token rather than an interface — interfaces are erased at compile time, so injecting one requires an explicit string or symbol token.
@red
- Saying decorators are "just annotations" with no runtime effect.
- Not knowing they require `reflect-metadata` for DI.

### How do you type React components and hooks?
@short
