I type props with an interface, let the return type be inferred, and avoid `React.FC` because it adds an implicit `children` and complicates generics. For hooks I type the state explicitly when the initial value cannot express the full type, and I always type `useRef` with its element type.
@deep
The awkward spots are: `useState` with an initial `null` (`useState<User | null>(null)`), a reducer where the action must be a discriminated union, a ref that starts `null` (`useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null)`), and generic components where you want the prop type to flow to the callback.

Event types come from React's synthetic event namespace — `React.ChangeEvent<HTMLInputElement>`, `React.FormEvent<HTMLFormElement>`, `React.MouseEvent<HTMLButtonElement>` — and getting them right is what gives you autocomplete on `e.target.value`.
@code tsx The patterns you will be asked to write on a whiteboard
interface TableProps<T> {
  rows: T[];
  columns: { key: keyof T; label: string; render?: (row: T) => React.ReactNode }[];
  onRowClick?: (row: T) => void;
  children?: React.ReactNode;
}

// Generic component: `row` in the callback is fully typed at every call site
export function Table<T extends { id: string }>({ rows, columns, onRowClick }: TableProps<T>) {
  return (
    <table>
      <tbody>
        {rows.map(r => (
          <tr key={r.id} onClick={() => onRowClick?.(r)}>
            {columns.map(c => <td key={String(c.key)}>{c.render ? c.render(r) : String(r[c.key])}</td>)}
          </tr>
        ))}
      </tbody>
    </table>
  );
}

const [user, setUser] = useState<User | null>(null);          // union needs the annotation
const inputRef = useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null);              // null-initialised DOM ref
const timer = useRef<ReturnType<typeof setInterval>>();       // mutable non-DOM ref

type Action = { type: 'add'; item: Item } | { type: 'clear' };  // discriminated reducer actions
const reducer = (s: Item[], a: Action): Item[] =>
  a.type === 'add' ? [...s, a.item] : [];
@end
@senior
The senior note is about *where* types come from. Hand-typing an API response in the component is duplication that will drift; I infer it from the Zod schema or the generated OpenAPI client, so a backend field rename becomes a frontend compile error. That is the strongest single argument for a typed monorepo, and it is the thing that actually prevents the "works on my machine, 500s in prod" class of bug in a full-stack team.
@followup
Why avoid `React.FC`?
@answer
Historically it implicitly added `children` to every component, which meant a component that should not accept children silently did. It also makes generic components awkward — you cannot easily write `React.FC<Props<T>>` with inference. React 18's types removed the implicit children, so it is less harmful now, but a plain function with a typed props parameter is simpler, works with generics, and is what the React team's own docs use.
@red
- Typing props as `any` or `object`.
- Using `useRef<HTMLInputElement>()` without `null` and then fighting the type error.

### What is module augmentation and when do you need it?
@short
Module augmentation lets you add types to an existing module or global namespace using `declare module` or `declare global`. In practice you need it the first time you attach `req.user` in Express auth middleware, or extend a library's config type.
@deep
It works because interfaces merge. Re-declaring `Express.Request` inside `declare global { namespace Express { ... } }` adds members to the existing interface rather than shadowing it. The file must be included in `tsconfig`'s `include`, and if it has no imports or exports it is treated as a script rather than a module, which changes how `declare global` behaves — that is the usual reason "my types are not being picked up".
@code ts Typing `req.user`, and typing an untyped third-party package
// types/express.d.ts
import 'express';                       // makes this file a module

declare module 'express-serve-static-core' {
  interface Request {
    user?: { id: string; role: 'admin' | 'user'; tenantId: string };
    requestId: string;
  }
}

// Shim for a package that ships no types
declare module 'legacy-payment-sdk' {
  export function charge(amount: number, token: string): Promise<{ id: string }>;
  const _default: { charge: typeof charge };
  export default _default;
}

// Typed env via NodeJS.ProcessEnv
declare global {
  namespace NodeJS {
    interface ProcessEnv { JWT_SECRET: string; DATABASE_URL: string; NODE_ENV: 'development' | 'production' | 'test' }
  }
}
@end
@senior
I would flag that augmenting `ProcessEnv` is a *lie* unless you also validate at boot — the type says `JWT_SECRET: string` but the value can genuinely be `undefined`, and you have just disabled the compiler's warning about the exact case you care about. The honest pattern is a validated `env` object from Zod that throws at startup, and to leave `process.env` alone. Same with `req.user`: typing it as optional (`user?`) is correct, and the middleware that guarantees it should narrow it or the route should use a typed `AuthedRequest`, rather than everyone writing `req.user!`.
@followup
What is the difference between `declare module 'x'` and `declare global`?
@answer
`declare module 'x'` augments a specific module's exported types — you use it to extend a library. `declare global` reaches the global scope from inside a module — you use it for `window`, `globalThis`, `NodeJS.ProcessEnv` and the `Express` namespace. If the file has no top-level import or export it is already global, and `declare global` is then unnecessary and actually an error.
@red
- Using `(req as any).user` throughout the codebase.
- Putting the `.d.ts` outside `include` and wondering why it does not apply.

### How does TypeScript compilation work, and what is `tsc --noEmit`?
@short
`tsc` does two independent jobs: type-check the program, and emit JavaScript by erasing types and downlevelling syntax. `--noEmit` runs only the checking, which is how you use TypeScript as a linter while a faster tool such as esbuild or SWC produces the actual output.
@deep
Erasure means types have zero runtime footprint — no type information exists in the emitted JS, which is why you cannot do `if (x instanceof MyInterface)`. `target` controls how far modern syntax is downlevelled; `lib` controls which built-in APIs the checker believes exist; `module` and `moduleResolution` control import resolution and must match your runtime (NodeNext for modern Node, Bundler for Vite).

Type-checking is whole-program and single-threaded, so it is the slow part on a large codebase. Project references (`composite: true`) let you split a monorepo so unchanged packages are not re-checked.
@code bash The build split that makes CI fast and the editor accurate
# Fast transpile — no type checking, milliseconds
esbuild src/index.ts --bundle --platform=node --target=node22 --outfile=dist/index.js

# Type check separately, in parallel, in CI and as a pre-push hook
tsc --noEmit                      # whole-program correctness
tsc --noEmit --watch              # editor-grade feedback in a terminal

# Incremental / monorepo
tsc --build --incremental         # reuses .tsbuildinfo, only rechecks what changed
@end
@senior
The reason this matters commercially is build feedback loops. A Webpack + `ts-loader` pipeline type-checks on every rebuild, so HMR gets slower as the codebase grows; Vite + esbuild strips types instantly and defers checking to a parallel process, so dev-server start and hot reload become near-instant. That split — transpile fast, check separately — is the actual mechanism behind a 60%+ build-time reduction, and it is the answer to give if someone asks how you achieved one. The trade is that a type error no longer breaks the dev build, so you must enforce `tsc --noEmit` in CI or broken types reach main.
@followup
Does the emitted JavaScript differ between `tsc` and esbuild?
@answer
Slightly, and in ways worth knowing. esbuild does not type-check, does not support `const enum` or legacy decorators fully, and handles `emitDecoratorMetadata` only via a plugin or SWC. It also transpiles file-by-file, so anything requiring cross-file knowledge — like knowing whether an import is a type — needs `isolatedModules` and `import type` to be explicit. For a NestJS app that relies on decorator metadata, SWC is the safer fast option; for a plain Node or React app, esbuild is fine.
@red
- Believing types exist at runtime.
- Shipping without any type-check step because the bundler "does not care".

# SECTION React Fundamentals | The screening round. Every answer here should end with a sentence about what it costs in a real application.

### What is the Virtual DOM and why does React use it?
@short
The Virtual DOM is a lightweight JavaScript description of the UI. On a state change React builds a new tree, diffs it against the previous one, and applies only the minimal set of real DOM operations. The point is not raw speed — it is a declarative programming model where you describe the end state and React works out the transitions.
@deep
Direct DOM manipulation is expensive mainly because reads of layout properties force synchronous reflow, and because manual updates are easy to get wrong. React batches changes and commits them in one pass, so a state update that touches ten nodes causes one layout pass rather than ten.

The diffing algorithm is O(n) rather than the O(n³) of a general tree diff, because React makes two assumptions: elements of different types produce different trees (so it unmounts and remounts rather than trying to reconcile), and keys tell it which children are stable across renders.
@code jsx Why keys matter — the classic index-key bug
// ❌ index keys: deleting the first item makes React reuse the wrong DOM node,
//    so the input's uncontrolled value stays attached to the wrong row
{items.map((item, i) => <Row key={i} item={item} />)}

// ✅ stable identity: React moves the DOM node instead of recreating it
{items.map(item => <Row key={item.id} item={item} />)}
@end
@senior
The honest senior framing is that the Virtual DOM is not automatically faster than well-written imperative DOM code — Svelte and Solid compile away the diff entirely and beat React on raw benchmarks. What React buys is a predictable model that scales to a large team and a large component tree, plus the ability to retarget the renderer (React Native, react-three-fiber). And the diff is only cheap if you help it: stable keys, and not recreating the whole tree by remounting because a `key` on a parent changed or a component is defined inside another component's body.
@followup
When would you deliberately use `key` to force a remount?
@answer
When you want to reset all internal state of a subtree — for example a form that must clear when the selected record changes: `<EditForm key={recordId} … />`. Changing the key unmounts the old instance and mounts a fresh one, which resets state, refs and effects. It is much cleaner than an effect that manually resets six pieces of state whenever a prop changes.
@red
- "The Virtual DOM is faster than the DOM" with nothing behind it.
- Using array index as key on a reorderable or filterable list.

### Explain the React component lifecycle in function components.
@short
With hooks there are three moments: mount, update and unmount. `useEffect` covers all three — the body runs after paint, the returned cleanup runs before the next effect and on unmount, and the dependency array controls when it re-runs. `useLayoutEffect` is the same but runs synchronously before paint.
@deep
The dependency array is the whole game. Empty means run once on mount; omitted means run after every render; populated means run when any listed value changes by `Object.is` comparison. Objects and functions declared in the component body get a new identity on every render, so including them without memoisation causes an infinite loop.

Cleanup is not optional bookkeeping. Every subscription, timer, WebSocket, event listener and `AbortController` must be torn down in the cleanup, or you leak memory and get "setState on an unmounted component" behaviour.
@code jsx A correct data-fetching effect with cleanup and race protection
useEffect(() => {
  const controller = new AbortController();
  let active = true;

  (async () => {
    try {
      const res = await fetch(`/api/orders/${id}`, { signal: controller.signal });
      const data = await res.json();
      if (active) setOrder(data);          // ignore a response that arrived after a change
    } catch (e) {
      if (e.name !== 'AbortError' && active) setError(e);
    }
  })();

  return () => { active = false; controller.abort(); };   // cleanup: cancel + ignore
}, [id]);
@end
@senior
Two senior points. First, in React 18 StrictMode every effect runs, cleans up and runs again in development — this is deliberate, and it surfaces exactly the missing-cleanup bugs above. Complaining that "StrictMode calls my API twice" signals you have a real bug. Second, I would not hand-write this at all in production: TanStack Query gives you cancellation, deduplication, caching, retry and stale-while-revalidate for free, and effect-based fetching is now considered an anti-pattern by the React team for anything beyond trivial cases.
@followup
When do you need `useLayoutEffect` instead of `useEffect`?
@answer
When you must read layout and mutate the DOM before the browser paints — measuring an element to position a tooltip, or synchronising scroll position — because `useEffect` runs after paint and the user would see a flicker. The cost is that it blocks painting, so it must be fast, and it does not run on the server, which produces a warning in SSR. In Next.js the common workaround is `useIsomorphicLayoutEffect`, which falls back to `useEffect` on the server.
@red
- Omitting the dependency array "to make it work".
- Forgetting cleanup for subscriptions and timers.
- Blaming StrictMode double-invocation instead of fixing the effect.

### `useState` vs `useReducer` — when do you choose which?
@short
`useState` for independent, simple values. `useReducer` when the next state depends on the previous one in non-trivial ways, when several fields must change together atomically, or when the update logic is worth unit-testing separately from the component.
@deep
The signal that you have outgrown `useState` is a cluster of `useState` calls that always change together — `loading`, `error`, `data`, `page` — because updating them separately allows impossible intermediate states such as `loading: true` with `error` still set from last time.

A reducer makes the state machine explicit: one dispatch, one atomic transition, and the reducer is a pure function you can test with no rendering at all. It also gives you a stable `dispatch` identity, which never changes and therefore never invalidates a memoised child.
@code jsx The same async state, both ways
// ❌ four independent states — 16 combinations, most of them invalid
const [data, setData] = useState(null);
const [loading, setLoading] = useState(false);
const [error, setError] = useState(null);

// ✅ one reducer — only the legal transitions exist
type State = { status: 'idle' | 'loading' | 'success' | 'error'; data?: Order[]; error?: string };
type Action = { type: 'FETCH' } | { type: 'OK'; data: Order[] } | { type: 'FAIL'; error: string };

function reducer(state: State, action: Action): State {
  switch (action.type) {
    case 'FETCH': return { status: 'loading' };
    case 'OK':    return { status: 'success', data: action.data };
    case 'FAIL':  return { status: 'error', error: action.error };
  }
}
const [state, dispatch] = useReducer(reducer, { status: 'idle' });
@end
@senior
The functional-update form matters for both: `setCount(c => c + 1)` reads the current value at apply time rather than capturing it, which is the fix for stale closures in intervals and event handlers. And I would point out that `dispatch` being referentially stable is the reason `useReducer` composes better with `React.memo` and with context — passing `dispatch` down through context does not invalidate consumers the way passing a new inline setter does.
@followup
Is `useState` asynchronous?
@answer
The setter does not mutate the variable synchronously — it schedules a re-render, and the new value is visible in the *next* render, not on the next line. React 18 batches all updates including those inside promises and native event handlers (automatic batching), so three setters in one handler cause one re-render. If you need the computed value immediately, compute it into a local variable, or use the functional form so each update sees the latest state.
@red
- Reading state immediately after setting it and expecting the new value.
- Keeping derived values in state instead of computing them during render.

### What are `useMemo`, `useCallback` and `React.memo` — and when are they wrong?
@short
`useMemo` caches a computed value, `useCallback` caches a function reference, and `React.memo` skips a re-render when props are shallow-equal. All three trade memory and comparison cost for render cost, so they only pay off when the saved work is genuinely expensive or when a stable reference is required downstream.
@deep
`React.memo` compares props shallowly, so a child memoised but handed a new inline object or arrow function every render never actually skips anything — which is why `useCallback` and `React.memo` are almost always used together, or not at all.

The three legitimate reasons to memoise are: an expensive pure computation (sorting or filtering thousands of rows), a referential dependency (a value used in a `useEffect` dependency array, or a prop to a memoised child), and preventing a context value object from invalidating every consumer on every render.
@code jsx Memoisation that actually works — all three parts required
const Row = React.memo(function Row({ order, onSelect }) {
  return <tr onClick={() => onSelect(order.id)}>{order.total}</tr>;
});

function OrderTable({ orders, query }) {
  // 1. expensive derived data — recomputed only when inputs change
  const visible = useMemo(
    () => orders.filter(o => o.customer.includes(query)).sort((a, b) => b.total - a.total),
    [orders, query]
  );

  // 2. stable handler identity — without this, React.memo above never helps
  const onSelect = useCallback((id) => setSelected(id), []);

  // 3. stable context value — otherwise every consumer re-renders every render
  const ctx = useMemo(() => ({ currency, locale }), [currency, locale]);

  return <Ctx.Provider value={ctx}>{visible.map(o => <Row key={o.id} order={o} onSelect={onSelect} />)}</Ctx.Provider>;
}
@end
@senior
The senior position is that memoisation is a profiling-driven decision, not a default. Wrapping everything adds allocation, dependency-array comparison and cognitive load, and it hides the actual problem, which is usually a component doing too much or state living too high in the tree. Two structural fixes beat memoisation most of the time: move state down to the component that uses it, and pass children as a prop so the expensive subtree is created by a parent that is not re-rendering. Worth adding that the React Compiler now auto-memoises correctly written components, which makes manual `useCallback` largely obsolete going forward — showing you track where the framework is heading.
@followup
Why did adding `React.memo` change nothing in your app?
@answer
Almost always because a prop is a new reference each render — an inline arrow, an inline object, an array literal, or a context value that is rebuilt every time. Shallow comparison sees a different reference and re-renders anyway. The second common cause is that the component re-renders because of its own state or a context it consumes, which `React.memo` cannot prevent — memo only guards against parent-driven re-renders.
@red
- Wrapping every component in `React.memo` by reflex.
- Using `useMemo` for trivial arithmetic.
- Not knowing memo compares props shallowly.

### What is the Context API and what is it bad at?
@short
Context passes a value down the tree without prop drilling. It is designed for low-frequency global values — theme, locale, current user, feature flags. It is not a state manager: every consumer re-renders whenever the context value's reference changes, so putting fast-changing state in one context is a performance problem.
@deep
There is no selector mechanism. If your context holds `{ user, cart, theme }` and the cart changes, every component that consumes the context re-renders even if it only reads `theme`. The standard mitigations are splitting into several contexts by change frequency, memoising the value object, and splitting state from dispatch so components that only dispatch never re-render.
@code jsx Split contexts by update frequency, and split state from actions
const AuthStateCtx  = createContext(null);      // changes rarely
const AuthActionCtx = createContext(null);      // never changes identity

export function AuthProvider({ children }) {
  const [user, setUser] = useState(null);

  const actions = useMemo(() => ({
    login:  async (c) => setUser(await api.login(c)),
    logout: async () => { await api.logout(); setUser(null); },
  }), []);                                       // stable forever

  return (
    <AuthActionCtx.Provider value={actions}>
      <AuthStateCtx.Provider value={user}>{children}</AuthStateCtx.Provider>
    </AuthActionCtx.Provider>
  );
}

// A component that only needs `logout` never re-renders when `user` changes
export const useAuthActions = () => useContext(AuthActionCtx);
@end
@senior
The decision I would articulate is: Context for dependency injection of rarely-changing values, a real store (Zustand, Redux Toolkit) for shared mutable state that changes often, and a server-cache library (TanStack Query, SWR) for anything that came from an API. Most "we need Redux" situations are actually "we are storing server data in client state", and moving it to a query cache deletes most of the store along with all the manual loading/error/refetch code.
@followup
Why not just use Context instead of Zustand or Redux?
@answer
Because Context has no selector, no middleware and no devtools. Zustand lets a component subscribe to exactly one slice, so unrelated updates cause zero re-renders; Redux Toolkit adds time-travel debugging, a documented action log and middleware for side effects. On a small app Context is genuinely enough, and I would say so — reaching for Redux on a three-screen app is over-engineering.
@red
- Calling Context a state management library.
- Putting a high-frequency value like form input state in a global context.

### What are the Rules of Hooks and why do they exist?
@short
Hooks must be called at the top level of a component or another hook, never inside conditions, loops or nested functions, and only from React functions. The reason is that React tracks hook state by call order in a linked list per component — if the order changes between renders, the wrong state is handed to the wrong hook.
@deep
There is no name or key associated with `useState`; React simply advances a cursor. A conditional hook shifts every subsequent hook by one position, so your `useEffect` receives the state that belonged to the `useRef`, which produces bizarre bugs rather than a clean error.

The practical consequence: to make a hook conditional, move the condition *inside* the hook, or extract the conditional part into a separate component that is conditionally rendered.
@code jsx The wrong way and the two right ways
// ❌ shifts hook order when `id` is undefined
if (id) { const { data } = useQuery(['order', id], () => fetchOrder(id)); }

// ✅ 1. condition inside the hook
const { data } = useQuery({ queryKey: ['order', id], queryFn: () => fetchOrder(id), enabled: !!id });

// ✅ 2. conditional component boundary
{id ? <OrderDetail id={id} /> : <EmptyState />}      // the hook lives inside OrderDetail

// Custom hook: composes other hooks, still obeys the rules
function useDebouncedValue(value, ms = 300) {
  const [v, setV] = useState(value);
  useEffect(() => { const t = setTimeout(() => setV(value), ms); return () => clearTimeout(t); }, [value, ms]);
  return v;
}
@end
@senior
I would add that this is exactly why `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` is non-negotiable in a shared codebase — the `exhaustive-deps` rule in particular. Disabling it with a comment is occasionally correct (an effect that must genuinely run once with a ref-held callback) but it should be rare and commented with a reason. Most `// eslint-disable-next-line react-hooks/exhaustive-deps` lines I find in review are hiding a stale-closure bug that will appear later as "the value is one step behind".
@followup
Why can't you call a hook inside a callback or event handler?
@answer
Because hook state is attached to a render, and a callback runs outside the render phase — React would not know which component instance and which position the hook belongs to. If you need something on demand, use the imperative API the library provides instead: `queryClient.fetchQuery(...)` rather than `useQuery`, or a ref holding the value you need at call time.
@red
- "Just don't put hooks in `if` statements" without knowing why.
- Silencing `exhaustive-deps` routinely.

### Controlled vs uncontrolled components — which and why?
@short
A controlled input's value comes from React state and every keystroke goes through `onChange`; an uncontrolled input keeps its value in the DOM and you read it via a ref or on form submit. Controlled gives you instant validation and derived UI; uncontrolled is faster for large forms because typing does not re-render the tree.
@deep
The cost of controlled inputs is a render per keystroke of everything below the state owner. On a 40-field form with state at the top, typing becomes visibly laggy. That is why React Hook Form is the default choice in production: it keeps inputs uncontrolled by registering them with refs, subscribes only what needs to re-render, and gives you validation through a resolver.
@code jsx React Hook Form + Zod — one schema, client and server
const Schema = z.object({
  email: z.string().email(),
  amount: z.coerce.number().positive().max(100000),
});

function PaymentForm({ onPay }) {
  const { register, handleSubmit, formState: { errors, isSubmitting } } =
    useForm({ resolver: zodResolver(Schema) });        // inputs stay uncontrolled

  return (
    <form onSubmit={handleSubmit(onPay)}>
      <input {...register('email')} />
      {errors.email && <span role="alert">{errors.email.message}</span>}
      <input type="number" {...register('amount')} />
      <button disabled={isSubmitting}>Pay</button>
    </form>
  );
}
@end
@senior
The subtle bug worth mentioning is the controlled/uncontrolled switch warning: initialising with `value={user?.name}` gives `undefined` on the first render, so the input starts uncontrolled and becomes controlled when the data arrives, and React logs a warning and can lose the cursor position. The fix is `value={user?.name ?? ''}`. Beyond that, I would say the same schema should validate on both sides — the client for UX, the server because the client cannot be trusted — and Zod lets you share the exact same object.
@followup
How do you handle a very large form with cross-field validation?
@answer
Keep inputs uncontrolled with React Hook Form, use a schema with `superRefine` or `refine` for cross-field rules (confirm password, end date after start date), validate on blur rather than on change to reduce noise, and split the form into steps with per-step schemas so the user is not shown 30 errors at once. If a field must react to another field's value I subscribe narrowly with `useWatch` on that field only, rather than re-rendering the whole form.
@red
- Controlled inputs with state at the page level on a huge form.
- Not knowing why the uncontrolled-to-controlled warning appears.

### How does state management scale — local, lifted, context, store, server cache?
@short
I categorise state first: server state (came from an API), client UI state (modals, tabs), form state, and URL state. Server state goes in TanStack Query, URL state in the router, form state in React Hook Form, and only genuinely global client state goes in a store. Most apps that "need Redux" are storing server state in a store.
@deep
The escalation ladder is: keep state in the component; lift it to the nearest common parent when two siblings need it; put it in context when it is needed deep and changes rarely; use a store when it is needed widely and changes often.

Treating server data as client state is the expensive mistake — you end up hand-writing loading flags, error flags, cache invalidation, refetch-on-focus, pagination and deduplication, all of which a query library already solves and gets right.
@arch The four state categories and where each belongs
 ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │  URL state        → router (?page=2&status=paid)  shareable, back-btn│
 │  Server state     → TanStack Query / SWR          cache + invalidate │
 │  Global UI state  → Zustand / Redux Toolkit       theme, sidebar     │
 │  Form state       → React Hook Form               uncontrolled+Zod   │
 │  Local UI state   → useState                      the default        │
 └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
        ↑ prefer moving DOWN this list — less state is always cheaper
@end
@code jsx Server state with invalidation — replaces ~80 lines of manual store code
const { data: orders, isLoading, error } = useQuery({
  queryKey: ['orders', { page, status }],
  queryFn: () => api.get(`/orders?page=${page}&status=${status}`),
  staleTime: 30_000,                              // don't refetch on every mount
  placeholderData: keepPreviousData,              // no flicker while paginating
});

const { mutate: refund } = useMutation({
  mutationFn: (id) => api.post(`/orders/${id}/refund`),
  onSuccess: () => qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['orders'] }),
});
@end
@senior
The extra credit is putting filter and pagination state in the URL rather than in a store. It makes the page shareable and bookmarkable, makes the browser back button behave, survives a refresh, and gives you server-side rendering for free in Next.js because the server can read `searchParams`. It also deletes a whole slice of your store. That is a product-quality argument, not just an architectural one, and it is the kind of thing senior interviewers notice.
@followup
When would you still choose Redux Toolkit today?
@answer
When the app has genuinely complex client-side state with interdependent transitions — a multi-step workflow, a collaborative editor's local UI state, an offline queue — and you want the devtools, the action log and the middleware ecosystem for debugging. RTK Query is also a legitimate choice if you are already in Redux, since it gives you the server-cache layer inside the same store. What I would not do is add Redux for a CRUD dashboard.
@red
- Storing API responses in Redux and hand-writing cache invalidation.
- Putting everything in one global store "for consistency".

### What causes unnecessary re-renders in React and how do you find them?
@short
A component re-renders when its state changes, when its context value changes, or when its parent re-renders. The usual causes are new object or function identities passed as props, context values rebuilt every render, and state living higher in the tree than it needs to. I find them with the React DevTools Profiler and its "why did this render" highlighting.
@deep
React does not re-render *because a prop changed* — by default it re-renders every child when the parent renders, regardless of props. `React.memo` opts a subtree out of that, but only if props are referentially stable.

The most valuable structural fix is composition: if an expensive subtree is passed as `children` to a component that owns fast-changing state, the subtree's element is created by the grandparent and is referentially identical across the stateful component's renders, so it does not re-render at all — no memo needed.
@code jsx The children trick — no memoisation, and it beats memoisation
// ❌ everything below re-renders on every keystroke
function Layout() {
  const [q, setQ] = useState('');
  return (<><input value={q} onChange={e => setQ(e.target.value)} /><ExpensiveDashboard /></>);
}

// ✅ state is isolated; ExpensiveDashboard is created by App and never re-created
function Layout({ children }) {
  const [q, setQ] = useState('');
  return (<><input value={q} onChange={e => setQ(e.target.value)} />{children}</>);
}
function App() { return <Layout><ExpensiveDashboard /></Layout>; }
@end
@senior
I would describe the measurement, because that is what separates guessing from engineering: record an interaction in the Profiler, sort by actual render duration, and look at the flame graph for components rendering with identical props. Turn on "Highlight updates when components render" to see it live. Only after identifying the specific expensive component do I decide between three fixes: move state down, use the children pattern, or memoise. Blanket memoisation without measurement usually adds cost.
@followup
Does a re-render always cause a DOM update?
@answer
No — that distinction is important. A re-render means the component function runs and produces a new element tree; React then diffs it and only touches the DOM where something actually differs. So a re-render is usually cheap; it becomes a problem when the component does expensive work during render, when there are thousands of them, or when it triggers effects. This is why "reduce re-renders" is a means, not a goal.
@red
- Claiming a parent re-render is prevented by unchanged props (it is not, without memo).
- Optimising without profiling.

### How do you handle lists, keys and large data sets in React?
@short
Every list item needs a stable, unique key from the data — the entity id, not the array index. For large lists I virtualise with TanStack Virtual or react-window so only the visible rows are mounted, which turns 10,000 DOM nodes into about 20.
@deep
Keys are how React matches elements between renders. With index keys, inserting at the front shifts every key, so React reuses the wrong instances: component state, uncontrolled input values, focus and CSS transitions all attach to the wrong row. It is only safe when the list is static, never reordered, filtered or prepended.

Virtualisation solves a different problem: rendering cost and memory for long lists. It requires either fixed row heights or a measurement strategy, and it complicates find-in-page, accessibility and anchor links, which is the trade-off to state.
@code jsx Windowed list — 50,000 rows, ~20 mounted nodes
import { useVirtualizer } from '@tanstack/react-virtual';

function TransactionList({ rows }) {
  const parentRef = useRef(null);
  const v = useVirtualizer({
    count: rows.length,
    getScrollElement: () => parentRef.current,
    estimateSize: () => 56,
    overscan: 8,                                  // render a few beyond the viewport
  });

  return (
    <div ref={parentRef} style={{ height: 600, overflow: 'auto' }}>
      <div style={{ height: v.getTotalSize(), position: 'relative' }}>
        {v.getVirtualItems().map(item => (
          <div key={rows[item.index].id}
               style={{ position: 'absolute', top: 0, transform: `translateY(${item.start}px)`, height: item.size }}>
            <Row tx={rows[item.index]} />
          </div>
        ))}
      </div>
    </div>
  );
}
@end
@senior
Before virtualising I would ask whether the client should have 50,000 rows at all. Server-side pagination or cursor-based infinite scroll usually beats shipping the whole table, because it also cuts payload size, JSON parse time and memory. Virtualisation is the right answer when the user genuinely needs to scroll a large local dataset — a log viewer, a spreadsheet — and it pairs well with infinite queries so you fetch pages as the window advances.
@followup
What breaks when you virtualise a table?
@answer
Browser find-in-page stops working because unrendered rows are not in the DOM, screen readers need explicit `aria-rowcount`/`aria-rowindex` since the DOM no longer reflects the true list, sticky headers and column alignment need care, and variable row heights require dynamic measurement which can cause scroll jitter. I would call those out proactively rather than pretend virtualisation is free.
@red
- Index keys on a sortable or filterable list.
- Rendering 10,000 rows and blaming React for being slow.

### What are error boundaries and Suspense?
@short
An error boundary is a class component with `getDerivedStateFromError`/`componentDidCatch` that catches render-phase errors below it and shows a fallback instead of unmounting the whole tree. Suspense declaratively shows a fallback while a child is loading — lazily loaded code, or data in a framework that supports it.
@deep
Error boundaries only catch errors during rendering, in lifecycle methods and in constructors of the tree below. They do *not* catch errors in event handlers, in async code, in `setTimeout`, or in the boundary itself — those still need `try/catch`. There is still no hook equivalent, so a class is required, or `react-error-boundary`.

Suspense started as a code-splitting primitive (`React.lazy`) and now also covers data with frameworks like Next.js App Router and TanStack Query's `useSuspenseQuery`. Streaming SSR uses it to send HTML for the shell immediately and stream slower parts as they resolve.
@code jsx Boundaries placed for graceful degradation, not one at the root
<ErrorBoundary fallback={<PageError />}>
  <Suspense fallback={<DashboardSkeleton />}>
    <Header />                                   {/* fast, renders immediately */}
    <ErrorBoundary fallback={<WidgetError name="Revenue" />}>
      <Suspense fallback={<CardSkeleton />}>
        <RevenueChart />                         {/* slow query — streams in later */}
      </Suspense>
    </ErrorBoundary>
  </Suspense>
</ErrorBoundary>

const Reports = React.lazy(() => import('./Reports'));   // route-level code splitting
@end
@senior
The design decision is boundary placement. One boundary at the root means any failure blanks the entire application — technically "handled", practically an outage. Placing boundaries per widget means a failing revenue chart shows a small error card while the rest of the dashboard works. I pair each boundary with a reset (`resetKeys` or a "Retry" that refetches) and report the caught error to Sentry inside `componentDidCatch` with the component stack, because a silently swallowed boundary is worse than a crash — you stop hearing about the bug.
@followup
Why don't error boundaries catch errors in event handlers?
@answer
Because event handlers run outside the render phase, so React is not in the middle of rendering a tree it can replace with a fallback — and importantly, an error in a handler does not corrupt the UI, so unmounting would be an overreaction. You handle those with `try/catch` and show a toast or set error state. The same applies to rejected promises in effects, which is why data-fetching errors are surfaced through state (or a query library's `error`) rather than thrown.
@red
- One error boundary at the root and calling it done.
- Expecting a boundary to catch an async or handler error.

### What is `useRef` used for, and how is it different from state?
@short
`useRef` gives you a mutable box whose `.current` persists across renders and, crucially, changing it does not trigger a re-render. Two uses: holding a DOM node, and holding mutable values that should not drive the UI — timer ids, previous values, in-flight flags, WebSocket instances.
@deep
The rule is: if the UI must update when the value changes, it is state; if it must survive renders but not display, it is a ref. Writing to a ref during render is discouraged because it breaks concurrent rendering assumptions — refs should be written in effects or handlers.

The "latest value" ref pattern is the standard fix for stale closures in long-lived subscriptions: store the current callback in a ref, and have the subscription call `ref.current(...)` so it always invokes the newest version without re-subscribing.
@code jsx Two refs, two purposes — DOM access and stale-closure avoidance
function ChatRoom({ roomId, onMessage }) {
  const socketRef = useRef(null);                 // mutable instance, not UI
  const handlerRef = useRef(onMessage);
  useEffect(() => { handlerRef.current = onMessage; });   // always the newest closure

  useEffect(() => {
    const ws = new WebSocket(`wss://api/rooms/${roomId}`);
    socketRef.current = ws;
    ws.onmessage = (e) => handlerRef.current(JSON.parse(e.data));  // no re-subscribe needed
    return () => ws.close();
  }, [roomId]);                                   // reconnect only when the room changes

  const inputRef = useRef(null);                  // DOM ref
  useEffect(() => inputRef.current?.focus(), []);
  return <input ref={inputRef} />;
}
@end
@senior
The senior detail is that without the handler ref, including `onMessage` in the dependency array tears down and re-establishes the WebSocket on every parent render — which in a real chat app means constant reconnect storms against your server. I have seen exactly that pattern cause a 10× increase in connection churn. The ref-holds-latest-callback trick is also what `useEvent`/`useEffectEvent` formalises in newer React.
@followup
Can you mutate a ref during render?
@answer
You should not. Render must be pure so React can call it twice (StrictMode), abandon it, or restart it under concurrent features — mutating a ref during render makes those behaviours produce inconsistent results. The two accepted exceptions are lazy initialisation guarded by a null check, and the newer `useEffectEvent` pattern. Otherwise, write refs in effects and event handlers.
@red
- Using a ref for values that should re-render the UI.
- Reading `ref.current` during render and expecting it to be up to date.

### How does React handle events, and how is it different from the DOM?
@short
React attaches a single listener at the root container and delegates, wrapping native events in a SyntheticEvent that normalises browser differences. Handlers are camelCase props, you call `preventDefault()` rather than returning false, and since React 17 delegation is per-root rather than on `document`, which matters when embedding React inside another app.
@deep
Synthetic events are pooled no longer (pooling was removed in React 17), so you can hold onto the event object. The delegation model means `stopPropagation()` in a React handler stops React's propagation but a native listener attached directly to a DOM node may still fire, and ordering between the two can be surprising — a real source of bugs with third-party widgets and modal libraries.

React 18 batches all state updates inside handlers, including in async continuations, so multiple `setState` calls produce one render.
@code jsx The details that get asked: typing, propagation, and native listeners
function Row({ id, onOpen }) {
  const onDelete = (e: React.MouseEvent<HTMLButtonElement>) => {
    e.stopPropagation();                          // don't trigger the row's onClick
    remove(id);
  };
  return (
    <tr onClick={() => onOpen(id)}>
      <td><button onClick={onDelete}>Delete</button></td>
    </tr>
  );
}

// A native listener when you need `passive` or capture semantics React does not expose
useEffect(() => {
  const onScroll = () => { /* ... */ };
  window.addEventListener('scroll', onScroll, { passive: true });
  return () => window.removeEventListener('scroll', onScroll);
}, []);
@end
@senior
Two production notes. First, `{ passive: true }` on scroll and touch listeners lets the browser scroll without waiting for your handler, which is a direct improvement to input latency and Core Web Vitals — React's `onScroll` prop cannot express it, so you use a native listener. Second, the React-versus-native propagation mismatch is the usual cause of "my dropdown closes immediately when I click it": a native `document` click listener fires before React's delegated handler in some setups. Knowing that saves an hour of debugging.
@followup
Why can't you return `false` from a React handler to prevent default?
@answer
Because that only ever worked with jQuery-style and inline HTML handlers. React ignores the return value, so you must call `e.preventDefault()` explicitly. That is also more honest — `preventDefault` and `stopPropagation` are two different things, and returning `false` in jQuery did both, which caused subtle bugs.
@red
- Not knowing what event delegation is.
- Adding `document`-level listeners without removing them in cleanup.

### What are custom hooks and how do you design a good one?
@short
A custom hook is just a function starting with `use` that calls other hooks — it extracts stateful logic for reuse, not markup. A good one has a single responsibility, a clear return shape, handles its own cleanup, and does not secretly couple you to a specific component's layout.
@deep
The key insight is that custom hooks share *logic*, not *state*: two components calling `useCounter()` get two independent counters. If you want shared state you need a store or context underneath.

Design-wise, return an object rather than a tuple once you have more than two values, because destructuring by name survives future additions. Accept an options object for the same reason. And keep the hook agnostic of the UI — a hook that returns JSX is a component wearing a disguise.
@code ts Two hooks I would actually ship
export function useLocalStorage<T>(key: string, initial: T) {
  const [value, setValue] = useState<T>(() => {
    try { const raw = localStorage.getItem(key); return raw ? JSON.parse(raw) as T : initial; }
    catch { return initial; }                     // private mode / corrupt JSON
  });
  useEffect(() => {
    try { localStorage.setItem(key, JSON.stringify(value)); } catch {}
  }, [key, value]);
  // stay in sync across tabs — the detail that shows you have shipped this
  useEffect(() => {
    const onStorage = (e: StorageEvent) => { if (e.key === key && e.newValue) setValue(JSON.parse(e.newValue)); };
    window.addEventListener('storage', onStorage);
    return () => window.removeEventListener('storage', onStorage);
  }, [key]);
  return [value, setValue] as const;
}

export function useOnlineStatus() {
  const [online, setOnline] = useState(navigator.onLine);
  useEffect(() => {
    const on = () => setOnline(true), off = () => setOnline(false);
    window.addEventListener('online', on); window.addEventListener('offline', off);
    return () => { window.removeEventListener('online', on); window.removeEventListener('offline', off); };
  }, []);
  return online;
}
@end
@senior
The senior framing is about *boundaries*: a good custom hook is a seam you can test and replace. `useOrders()` that wraps a query gives you one place to change the endpoint, the cache key strategy or the error mapping, and components never import the HTTP client. I would also flag SSR safety — touching `window`, `localStorage` or `navigator` at the top level breaks server rendering in Next.js, so the initialiser must be lazy and guarded, which is exactly why `useSyncExternalStore` exists with its separate server snapshot.
@followup
How do you test a custom hook?
@answer
With `renderHook` from React Testing Library: render it, assert on `result.current`, and wrap state changes in `act()`. For anything that touches the network I test through a component with MSW intercepting HTTP, because that tests the real integration rather than a mock of my own abstraction. Testing hooks in isolation is fine; testing them *only* in isolation tends to miss the wiring bugs.
@red
- Custom hooks that return JSX.
- Hooks that assume they are used in exactly one component.

### How do you optimise React performance? Give a checklist.
@short
Measure first with the Profiler and Lighthouse, then work down: code-split routes and heavy components, virtualise long lists, move state down, stabilise references, memoise genuinely expensive work, defer non-urgent updates with `useTransition`, and cut bundle size by auditing dependencies and images.
@deep
The two categories are load performance and runtime performance, and they need different tools. Load performance is bundle size, code splitting, image optimisation, font loading and critical CSS — measured by Lighthouse and Core Web Vitals (LCP, INP, CLS). Runtime performance is render cost — measured by the React Profiler.

`useTransition` and `useDeferredValue` are the concurrency tools: they let you mark an update as interruptible so typing stays responsive while an expensive filtered list catches up.
@code jsx Concurrent rendering: keep the input at 60fps while a heavy list updates
function Search({ items }) {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const deferred = useDeferredValue(query);              // lags behind under load
  const results = useMemo(() => filterLarge(items, deferred), [items, deferred]);
  const stale = query !== deferred;

  return (
    <>
      <input value={query} onChange={e => setQuery(e.target.value)} />   {/* always instant */}
      <div style={{ opacity: stale ? 0.6 : 1 }}>
        <ResultList results={results} />
      </div>
    </>
  );
}

// Route-level splitting: the single highest-leverage load-time fix
const Analytics = lazy(() => import('./pages/Analytics'));   // 380 KB off the initial bundle
@end
@senior
The highest-leverage wins in my experience are not React-specific at all. Route-level code splitting, replacing moment.js with date-fns or `Intl`, importing icons individually instead of the whole set, serving images as AVIF/WebP at the right size, and self-hosting fonts with `font-display: swap` typically move Lighthouse more than any amount of `useMemo`. I run `rollup-plugin-visualizer` or `source-map-explorer` on every release, set a bundle-size budget in CI so a regression fails the build, and measure real users with `web-vitals` rather than trusting lab numbers.
@followup
What are Core Web Vitals and which one does JavaScript hurt most?
@answer
LCP (largest contentful paint, target under 2.5s), INP (interaction to next paint, under 200ms) and CLS (cumulative layout shift, under 0.1). JavaScript hurts INP most directly — long tasks on the main thread delay the response to a click — and it hurts LCP indirectly by delaying hydration and render. That is why splitting long tasks, deferring non-critical scripts and reducing hydration work matter more than micro-optimising render functions.
@red
- Listing `useMemo` as the whole performance strategy.
- Optimising with no measurement and no budget.

# SECTION Next.js Fundamentals | Rendering strategies, the App Router, caching and the Server/Client component split — the highest-signal frontend topic in 2026 interviews.

### What are the rendering strategies in Next.js and how do you choose?
@short
CSR renders in the browser, SSR renders per request on the server, SSG renders at build time, and ISR is SSG with background revalidation. I choose by how fresh the data must be and how personalised the page is: marketing pages SSG, product listings ISR, dashboards SSR or client-fetched, and highly interactive widgets client-side.
@deep
The trade-offs are time-to-first-byte versus freshness versus server cost. SSG has the best TTFB because it is a static file on a CDN, but the content is as old as the last build. ISR keeps the CDN benefit and refreshes a page in the background after a revalidation window, so users get stale-then-fresh. SSR is always current but pays a server render per request and cannot be cached at the edge without care. CSR gives an empty shell to crawlers and a slow LCP, but it is right for authenticated app screens where SEO is irrelevant.

In the App Router these are not separate APIs any more — a route is static by default and becomes dynamic when it reads request-time data such as `cookies()`, `headers()` or `searchParams`, or when you set `export const dynamic = 'force-dynamic'`.
@code tsx App Router: the same page, three strategies
// 1. Static (SSG) — built once, served from the CDN
export default async function Page() {
  const posts = await fetch('https://api/posts').then(r => r.json());   // cached by default
  return <List posts={posts} />;
}

// 2. ISR — static, refreshed at most every 60s in the background
export const revalidate = 60;

// 3. Dynamic (SSR) — rendered per request
export const dynamic = 'force-dynamic';
// ...or implicitly, by reading request data:
import { cookies } from 'next/headers';
export default async function Page() {
  const session = (await cookies()).get('sid');       // this makes the route dynamic
  const orders = await fetch(`https://api/orders`, { cache: 'no-store', headers: { cookie: `sid=${session?.value}` } });
  return <Orders data={await orders.json()} />;
}

// On-demand invalidation — the best of both: static speed, instant freshness
import { revalidateTag, revalidatePath } from 'next/cache';
export async function POST() { revalidateTag('products'); }   // called from a CMS webhook
@end
@senior
The pattern I would advocate is ISR with on-demand revalidation via tags rather than a short time window. A 60-second `revalidate` means you serve stale content for up to a minute and re-render on a timer whether or not anything changed; tagging fetches with `next: { tags: ['product-123'] }` and calling `revalidateTag` from a CMS or admin webhook gives you CDN-speed pages that update within seconds of an actual change, with no wasted renders. That is the answer that shows you have run this in production rather than read the docs.
@followup
How do you decide between SSR and client fetching for an authenticated dashboard?
@answer
If the first paint needs the data and SEO or a fast LCP matters, render it on the server so the HTML arrives populated. If the page is behind a login, highly interactive and the data changes as the user works, I render a shell on the server and fetch on the client with TanStack Query, because I get caching, background refetch and optimistic updates that server rendering alone does not give. Often the right answer is both: server-render the first page of data and hydrate it into the query cache so there is no loading flash and no double fetch.
@red
- Saying SSR is "always better for performance".
- Not knowing what makes a route dynamic in the App Router.

### What is the difference between Server Components and Client Components?
@short
Server Components run only on the server, never ship their code to the browser, and can await data directly — including hitting the database. Client Components are marked with `'use client'`, hydrate in the browser and are the only ones that can use state, effects, browser APIs and event handlers. The default in the App Router is server.
@deep
The mental model is a boundary, not a mode. `'use client'` marks an entry point: that component and everything it *imports* becomes client code. But a Server Component can still be passed *through* a Client Component as `children` or as a prop, and it stays on the server — that is the composition pattern that keeps bundles small.

Props crossing the boundary must be serialisable: plain objects, arrays, primitives, dates, and Server Functions. You cannot pass a function, a class instance or a Map.
@code tsx Keeping the boundary as low as possible in the tree
// app/orders/page.tsx  — Server Component (default). No JS shipped for this file.
import { db } from '@/lib/db';
import { Filters } from './filters';           // 'use client'

export default async function OrdersPage({ searchParams }) {
  const { status } = await searchParams;
  const orders = await db.order.findMany({ where: { status }, take: 50 });   // direct DB access

  return (
    <>
      <Filters />                              {/* small interactive island */}
      <OrderTable orders={orders} />           {/* stays on the server */}
    </>
  );
}

// ✅ Server content inside a Client shell — passed as children, so it stays server-rendered
'use client';
export function Collapsible({ children }) {
  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);
  return <><button onClick={() => setOpen(!open)}>Toggle</button>{open && children}</>;
}
// usage in a Server Component:
// <Collapsible><ExpensiveServerRenderedReport /></Collapsible>
@end
@senior
The measurable benefit is bundle size: moving a markdown renderer, a date library and a chart's data preparation to the server removed those from the client bundle entirely in projects I have worked on. The trap is `'use client'` at the top of a layout, which turns the entire subtree into client code and silently deletes the benefit — I audit for that with the bundle analyser. The other senior point is security: because Server Components can touch the database directly, an accidental `'use client'` on a file that imports your DB client is a build error by design, but leaking secrets through props is not, so I keep server-only code behind `import 'server-only'`.
@followup
Can a Server Component import a Client Component and vice versa?
@answer
A Server Component can import and render a Client Component freely — that is the normal direction. A Client Component cannot *import* a Server Component, because at that point it is all client code; instead you pass the server-rendered element in as `children` or a prop from a server parent. That inversion is the single most important composition rule in the App Router.
@red
- Adding `'use client'` to every file "to be safe".
- Passing a function or a class instance across the boundary.

### How does data fetching and caching work in the Next.js App Router?
@short
In Server Components you `await` directly — no `useEffect`, no loading state plumbing. Next.js extends `fetch` with caching controls: `cache: 'force-cache'` or `no-store`, `next: { revalidate: n }` for time-based ISR, and `next: { tags: [...] }` for on-demand invalidation via `revalidateTag`.
@deep
There are several caches, and knowing which one bit you is the actual skill: the request memoisation cache (dedupes identical fetches within one render pass), the Data Cache (persists fetch results across requests and deployments), the Full Route Cache (the rendered HTML/RSC payload), and the client-side Router Cache (prefetched segments in the browser).

Defaults have changed across versions — Next 15 made `fetch` and route handlers uncached by default, which was a deliberate correction of the "why is my data stale" complaints in Next 14. Being able to say that shows you have tracked the framework.
@code tsx Parallel fetching, tagging and invalidation
async function getUser(id: string) {
  return fetch(`${API}/users/${id}`, { next: { tags: [`user:${id}`], revalidate: 3600 } }).then(r => r.json());
}
async function getOrders(id: string) {
  return fetch(`${API}/users/${id}/orders`, { next: { tags: [`orders:${id}`] } }).then(r => r.json());
}

export default async function Page({ params }) {
  const { id } = await params;
  // ✅ parallel — two independent awaits in one Promise.all avoids a request waterfall
  const [user, orders] = await Promise.all([getUser(id), getOrders(id)]);
  return <Profile user={user} orders={orders} />;
}

// Server Action mutating data then invalidating exactly what changed
'use server';
export async function updateProfile(id: string, data: FormData) {
  await db.user.update({ where: { id }, data: { name: String(data.get('name')) } });
  revalidateTag(`user:${id}`);        // surgical — not the whole site
}
@end
@senior
The failure mode I would call out is the request waterfall: sequential `await`s in nested Server Components serialise your latency, so a page with four nested components each awaiting 150ms takes 600ms instead of 150. The fixes are `Promise.all` at the same level, and `<Suspense>` boundaries so slow sections stream in independently rather than blocking the shell. I would also mention that caching a `fetch` that includes a user's auth header is a data-leak risk — cached responses are shared, so anything per-user must be `no-store` or keyed carefully.
@followup
What is streaming and how does `loading.tsx` relate to it?
@answer
Streaming sends the HTML shell as soon as it is ready and pushes the rest as each Suspense boundary resolves, so time-to-first-byte does not wait for your slowest query. `loading.tsx` is sugar: Next wraps the route segment in a Suspense boundary with that file as the fallback. Manual `<Suspense>` around individual slow components gives finer control — the header and navigation appear instantly while a heavy chart streams in a second later.
@red
- Using `useEffect` to fetch in a Server Component context.
- Not knowing that fetches are deduplicated within a render.

### What are Server Actions and when would you use one?
@short
A Server Action is a function marked `'use server'` that runs on the server but can be called directly from a client component or a form's `action` prop. It removes the need to hand-write an API route for simple mutations, gives you progressive enhancement (forms work without JavaScript) and integrates with cache revalidation.
@deep
Under the hood Next.js creates an endpoint and a client stub; calling the function posts to that endpoint. That means an action is a *public HTTP endpoint* — anyone can call it with a crafted request — so it must do its own authentication, authorisation and validation exactly like a REST handler.

`useFormStatus` and `useActionState` give you pending state and returned errors without manual state management, and `useOptimistic` lets the UI update before the round trip completes.
@code tsx A Server Action with auth, validation and optimistic UI
// app/orders/actions.ts
'use server';
import { z } from 'zod';
import { auth } from '@/lib/auth';

const Refund = z.object({ orderId: z.string().uuid(), reason: z.string().min(3).max(200) });

export async function refundOrder(_prev: unknown, formData: FormData) {
  const session = await auth();                                  // 1. authenticate
  if (!session) return { error: 'UNAUTHENTICATED' };

  const parsed = Refund.safeParse(Object.fromEntries(formData));  // 2. validate
  if (!parsed.success) return { error: 'INVALID', details: parsed.error.flatten() };

  const order = await db.order.findUnique({ where: { id: parsed.data.orderId } });
  if (order?.userId !== session.user.id) return { error: 'FORBIDDEN' };   // 3. authorise

  await stripe.refunds.create({ payment_intent: order.paymentIntentId });
  revalidateTag(`orders:${session.user.id}`);
  return { ok: true };
}

// app/orders/refund-form.tsx
'use client';
export function RefundForm({ orderId }) {
  const [state, action, pending] = useActionState(refundOrder, null);
  return (
    <form action={action}>
      <input type="hidden" name="orderId" value={orderId} />
      <textarea name="reason" required />
      <button disabled={pending}>{pending ? 'Refunding…' : 'Refund'}</button>
      {state?.error && <p role="alert">{state.error}</p>}
    </form>
  );
}
@end
@senior
The security point is the one that separates answers here: "it looks like a local function call" leads people to skip auth checks because the caller is already inside an authenticated page. It is not a local call — it is a POST endpoint with a stable id, and it must be treated as untrusted input. I also avoid Server Actions for anything a mobile app or third-party client needs, because those need a documented REST or GraphQL surface; actions are a web-app convenience, not a public API. And long-running work (sending 5,000 emails, generating a report) belongs in a queue triggered by the action, not inside it.
@followup
Server Action or Route Handler — how do you choose?
@answer
Server Action for a mutation that originates from your own UI and benefits from progressive enhancement and cache revalidation — form submissions, toggles, deletes. Route Handler (`app/api/.../route.ts`) when you need a stable, documented HTTP contract: mobile clients, third-party webhooks, anything needing custom status codes, streaming responses or CORS. Payment webhooks specifically must be route handlers, because the provider posts a raw signed body that you have to verify byte-for-byte.
@red
- Assuming a Server Action is automatically authenticated.
- Doing heavy or long-running work inline in an action.

### How does routing work in the App Router?
@short
Routing is file-system based: a folder is a URL segment, `page.tsx` makes it publicly routable, `layout.tsx` wraps all children and persists across navigations, `loading.tsx` and `error.tsx` provide Suspense and error boundaries, and `[id]`, `[...slug]` and `(group)` handle dynamic, catch-all and organisational segments.
@deep
Layouts are the feature people underuse. A layout does not re-render on navigation between its children, so shared shells — sidebars, headers, providers — keep their state and do not refetch. Route groups `(marketing)` let you apply different layouts without adding a URL segment, and parallel routes `@modal` plus intercepting routes `(.)photo/[id]` are how you build a modal that has its own URL and survives a refresh as a full page.
@arch A realistic App Router tree
 app/
 ├── layout.tsx                 ← root shell: fonts, providers, <html>
 ├── (marketing)/               ← route group: no URL segment
 │   ├── layout.tsx             ← marketing header/footer
 │   └── page.tsx               ← "/"            (static, SSG)
 ├── (app)/
 │   ├── layout.tsx             ← auth guard + sidebar (persists across nav)
 │   ├── dashboard/
 │   │   ├── page.tsx           ← "/dashboard"
 │   │   ├── loading.tsx        ← Suspense fallback for the segment
 │   │   └── error.tsx          ← client error boundary ('use client')
 │   └── orders/
 │       ├── page.tsx           ← "/orders"
 │       └── [id]/page.tsx      ← "/orders/:id"
 ├── @modal/(.)orders/[id]/page.tsx  ← intercepting route: modal on soft nav,
 │                                     full page on refresh / direct link
 ├── api/webhooks/stripe/route.ts    ← POST handler, raw body for signature check
 └── middleware.ts                   ← runs at the edge before every matched request
@end
@senior
The detail worth raising is that `middleware.ts` runs on the Edge runtime, so it has no Node APIs, no database driver and a small size budget — it is right for a cheap cookie check, a redirect, a locale rewrite or setting a security header, and wrong for a full session lookup against Postgres. The correct split is: middleware does a fast optimistic check (is there a session cookie at all), and the actual authorisation happens in the layout or page where you can safely hit the database. Treating middleware as your security boundary is a real vulnerability, because a missing `matcher` entry silently leaves a route unprotected.
@followup
How do layouts differ from templates?
@answer
A layout is preserved across navigations — its state, effects and DOM persist, which is what makes a sidebar keep its scroll position. A `template.tsx` remounts on every navigation, giving each route a fresh instance, which you want for enter animations or for an effect that must re-run per page view. Layouts are the default; templates are the exception.
@red
- Doing database work in middleware.
- Not knowing layouts persist state across navigation.

### How do you handle images, fonts and SEO in Next.js?
@short
`next/image` handles responsive sizing, modern formats, lazy loading and reserved space to avoid layout shift. `next/font` self-hosts and preloads fonts with zero layout shift and no request to Google. SEO comes from the Metadata API — a `metadata` export or `generateMetadata` per route — plus `sitemap.ts`, `robots.ts` and JSON-LD.
@deep
`next/image` matters for Core Web Vitals in two specific ways: it emits `width`/`height` or `fill` with a container, which fixes CLS, and it serves AVIF/WebP at device-appropriate sizes, which usually cuts image bytes by more than half. `priority` on the LCP image disables lazy loading and preloads it, which is often the single biggest LCP win on a landing page.

`next/font` eliminates the render-blocking round trip to a font CDN and the flash of unstyled text by generating an adjusted fallback with matched metrics.
@code tsx The four things I set on every production page
import Image from 'next/image';
import { Inter } from 'next/font/google';

const inter = Inter({ subsets: ['latin'], display: 'swap', variable: '--font-inter' });

// Per-route metadata, generated from the data the page already fetched
export async function generateMetadata({ params }): Promise<Metadata> {
  const { slug } = await params;
  const p = await getProduct(slug);
  return {
    title: `${p.name} — Acme`,
    description: p.summary,
    alternates: { canonical: `https://acme.com/p/${slug}` },
    openGraph: { images: [{ url: p.image, width: 1200, height: 630 }] },
  };
}

export default function Page({ product }) {
  return (
    <Image src={product.image} alt={product.name}
           width={1200} height={630}
           priority                        /* LCP image: preload, no lazy loading */
           sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 50vw" />
  );
}
@end
@senior
The senior addition is that `next/image` optimisation runs on your server or your Vercel bill — at scale I put a CDN with an image service in front, or use `loader` to point at Cloudinary/imgix, because on-the-fly optimisation of a large catalogue is a real cost and a real cold-start latency. And the `sizes` prop is the most commonly wrong one: without it, the browser assumes `100vw` and downloads a desktop-sized image for a thumbnail, which silently undoes the optimisation.
@followup
What is the difference between `next/image` and a plain `<img>` with `loading="lazy"`?
@answer
A plain `<img>` gives you lazy loading and nothing else. `next/image` additionally generates a srcset for device widths, converts to AVIF/WebP based on the `Accept` header, reserves layout space to prevent CLS, and can produce a blur placeholder. The trade is a server-side optimisation step and some configuration for remote hosts, so for a purely static SVG icon a plain `<img>` is simpler and I would use it.
@red
- Not setting `sizes`, or omitting `priority` on the hero image.
- Loading fonts from a third-party CDN and taking the CLS hit.

### How do you do authentication in Next.js?
@short
Sessions live in an httpOnly, Secure, SameSite cookie — never in localStorage, which is readable by any injected script. Middleware does a cheap optimistic check for the cookie's presence and redirects, and the real authorisation check happens in the layout, page or Server Action where you can query the database. Auth.js (NextAuth) covers OAuth providers and session management.
@deep
The App Router changes where checks belong. A Server Component can call `auth()` and simply not render data the user cannot see — there is no client bundle to inspect, so the check is genuinely enforced. But every Server Action and Route Handler is independently reachable, so each needs its own check; a guard in a layout does not protect them.

For token-based flows I use a short-lived access token (10–15 minutes) plus a long-lived refresh token in an httpOnly cookie, with rotation and reuse detection.
@code tsx Layered auth: cheap middleware, real check at the data boundary
// middleware.ts — Edge, fast, optimistic only
export function middleware(req: NextRequest) {
  const hasSession = req.cookies.has('session');
  if (!hasSession && req.nextUrl.pathname.startsWith('/app')) {
    const url = new URL('/login', req.url);
    url.searchParams.set('next', req.nextUrl.pathname);
    return NextResponse.redirect(url);
  }
}
export const config = { matcher: ['/app/:path*'] };

// lib/auth.ts — the real check, cached per request
import { cache } from 'react';
export const requireUser = cache(async () => {
  const token = (await cookies()).get('session')?.value;
  const user = token ? await verifySession(token) : null;    // DB / JWT verify
  if (!user) redirect('/login');
  return user;
});

// Every page, layout AND server action calls it — defence in depth
export default async function Page() {
  const user = await requireUser();
  const orders = await db.order.findMany({ where: { userId: user.id } });  // scoped by user
  return <Orders data={orders} />;
}
@end
@senior
Two points I always make. First, authorisation must be *data-scoped*, not just route-scoped: `findMany({ where: { userId: user.id } })` is what stops IDOR, whereas checking "is logged in" at the route and then fetching by a URL id lets any user read any record. Second, wrapping the session lookup in React's `cache()` deduplicates it across the layout, the page and every component in one request — without it you can easily do five identical session queries per page render, which shows up as unexplained latency.
@followup
Why not store the JWT in localStorage?
@answer
Because any XSS — including one from a compromised npm dependency — can read it and exfiltrate it, and there is no way to revoke it. An httpOnly cookie is unreadable from JavaScript, so an XSS can still make requests as the user but cannot steal a token to use later from elsewhere. I pair it with `SameSite=Lax` (or `Strict` where the flow allows), the `Secure` flag, a CSRF token or double-submit cookie for state-changing requests, and a strict Content-Security-Policy to reduce XSS in the first place.
@red
- Storing tokens in localStorage.
- Relying on middleware alone as the authorisation boundary.

### What is hydration, and what causes a hydration mismatch?
@short
Hydration is React attaching event listeners and state to server-rendered HTML so it becomes interactive. A mismatch happens when the client's first render produces different markup from the server's — typically from dates, random values, `window`/`localStorage` access, browser extensions, or invalid HTML nesting.
@deep
React compares the server HTML against the first client render. If they differ, React 18 logs an error and re-renders that subtree on the client, which costs performance and can visibly flash. React 19 improved the error message to show a diff, which makes debugging far quicker.

Invalid nesting is the sneaky cause: a `<div>` inside a `<p>`, or a `<p>` inside a `<p>`, gets silently corrected by the browser's HTML parser, so the DOM no longer matches what React produced.
@code tsx The four common causes and their fixes
// ❌ time / locale — server and client render in different timezones
<span>{new Date().toLocaleString()}</span>

// ✅ render a stable value, format after mount
const [mounted, setMounted] = useState(false);
useEffect(() => setMounted(true), []);
<span suppressHydrationWarning>{mounted ? new Date().toLocaleString() : null}</span>

// ❌ browser-only API during render
const theme = localStorage.getItem('theme');           // ReferenceError on the server

// ✅ read it in an effect, or use a cookie so the server knows too
const theme = (await cookies()).get('theme')?.value ?? 'light';   // no mismatch at all

// ❌ non-deterministic
<div id={Math.random()} />        // ✅ use React's useId()
const id = useId();
@end
@senior
The senior fix for theme flicker specifically is a cookie rather than `localStorage`, because the server can read a cookie and render the correct class on the first pass — no mismatch, no flash of the wrong theme, no `suppressHydrationWarning`. That is a good example of the general principle: prefer making the server aware of the state over papering over the difference on the client. I would also note that `suppressHydrationWarning` only suppresses one level deep and is a genuine escape hatch, not a fix — using it broadly hides real bugs.
@followup
How do you avoid hydration cost entirely for a static section?
@answer
Keep it as a Server Component so it never hydrates — that is the App Router's biggest structural advantage. In the Pages Router the equivalent is dynamic import with `ssr: false` for the interactive parts only, or shipping plain HTML. The general goal is to reduce the amount of the tree that needs to become interactive, which is the same idea behind islands architecture in Astro.
@red
- Wrapping everything in `suppressHydrationWarning`.
- Accessing `window` or `localStorage` during render.

### How do you deploy and configure a Next.js app outside Vercel?
@short
`next build` then `next start` behind a reverse proxy works anywhere, and `output: 'standalone'` produces a minimal server plus only the needed `node_modules` for a small Docker image. On AWS I run it in ECS or on EC2 behind an ALB, with a CDN in front for `/_next/static`, and images either optimised by a service or served through CloudFront.
@deep
The things that need explicit handling off-Vercel are: the image optimiser (CPU-heavy, so cache aggressively or delegate), ISR's cache storage (the default file-system cache is per-instance, so with several replicas one instance revalidates and the others still serve stale — you need a shared cache handler backed by Redis or S3), and the Edge runtime for middleware (which becomes just Node in a self-hosted setup).

Environment variables split into build-time `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` (inlined into the client bundle, so never secrets) and server-only variables read at runtime.
@code dockerfile Multi-stage build with standalone output — ~150 MB instead of ~1.2 GB
FROM node:22-alpine AS deps
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
RUN npm ci

FROM node:22-alpine AS builder
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=deps /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY . .
ENV NEXT_TELEMETRY_DISABLED=1
RUN npm run build                      # next.config.js: { output: 'standalone' }

FROM node:22-alpine AS runner
WORKDIR /app
ENV NODE_ENV=production
RUN addgroup -g 1001 nodejs && adduser -S nextjs -u 1001
COPY --from=builder /app/public ./public
COPY --from=builder --chown=nextjs:nodejs /app/.next/standalone ./
COPY --from=builder --chown=nextjs:nodejs /app/.next/static ./.next/static
USER nextjs
EXPOSE 3000
CMD ["node", "server.js"]
@end
@senior
The multi-instance ISR cache is the trap most people hit and it presents as "some users see old content at random", which is very hard to debug without knowing the cause. The fix is a custom `cacheHandler` writing to Redis or S3 so all replicas share revalidation state. I would also flag that `NEXT_PUBLIC_` variables are baked in at build time, so the same image cannot be promoted from staging to production if the API URL differs — either build per environment, or read runtime config on the server and pass it down, which is the cleaner pipeline.
@followup
How do you get zero-downtime deploys?
@answer
Rolling or blue-green at the load balancer with a real readiness probe — the new task only receives traffic once `/healthz` passes, and old tasks drain in-flight requests before stopping. The Next-specific detail is asset versioning: `/_next/static` paths are content-hashed, so a client that already loaded the old HTML can still fetch its old chunks. If you delete old assets immediately, users mid-session get chunk-load errors; I keep the previous build's static assets on the CDN and handle `ChunkLoadError` by prompting a refresh.
@red
- Putting a secret in a `NEXT_PUBLIC_` variable.
- Running multiple instances with the default file-system ISR cache.

### What is the difference between the Pages Router and the App Router?
@short
The Pages Router uses `getServerSideProps`/`getStaticProps` with everything shipped as client components, and a single `_app`.
