The App Router uses React Server Components by default, nested layouts, streaming with Suspense, Server Actions and a granular caching model. New projects go App Router; large existing Pages apps can migrate route by route since both can coexist.
@deep
The conceptual shift is from "fetch data in a special function and pass props to a client page" to "components are async and fetch their own data on the server". That removes the props-drilling of `getServerSideProps` and lets each component own its data requirement, at the cost of a new mental model around caching and the client boundary.

Layouts are the other big win: the Pages Router re-rendered the whole page tree on navigation, while App Router layouts persist, which preserves state and avoids refetching shared data.
@code tsx The same page in both routers
// Pages Router
export async function getServerSideProps({ params, req }) {
  const session = await getSession(req);
  if (!session) return { redirect: { destination: '/login', permanent: false } };
  const order = await db.order.findUnique({ where: { id: params.id } });
  return { props: { order: JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(order)) } };   // must be serialisable
}
export default function OrderPage({ order }) { return <Detail order={order} />; }

// App Router — no special export, no manual serialisation dance
export default async function OrderPage({ params }) {
  const { id } = await params;
  const user = await requireUser();
  const order = await db.order.findUnique({ where: { id, userId: user.id } });
  if (!order) notFound();
  return <Detail order={order} />;
}
@end
@senior
On a migration I would not do a big-bang rewrite. Both routers run side by side, so I move one low-risk route first, learn the caching model on it, then migrate by traffic value. The two things that consistently bite are third-party libraries that assume client-side execution (they need `'use client'` wrappers) and CSS-in-JS libraries that were not built for RSC — which is why Tailwind or CSS Modules are the pragmatic choice for App Router projects. I would also be honest that the App Router's caching defaults have been a genuine source of confusion and changed in Next 15; knowing that history is more useful than pretending it was smooth.
@followup
Can you use both routers in the same application?
@answer
Yes — `app/` takes precedence for a conflicting route, and everything else falls through to `pages/`. That is the supported incremental migration path. The caveats are that they do not share layouts, a navigation between the two is a full page load rather than a client-side transition, and you effectively ship both runtimes, so the bundle is larger during the transition period.
@red
- Claiming the App Router is "just a folder rename".
- Not knowing they can coexist during migration.

# SECTION React Native Fundamentals | Your offline-first geolocation tracking app makes this a résumé-defence topic, not a trivia topic.

### How does React Native actually work under the hood?
@short
React Native runs your JavaScript in a JS engine (Hermes) and renders real native views — `UIView` on iOS, `android.view.View` on Android — rather than a WebView. The old architecture passed serialised JSON messages over an asynchronous bridge; the new architecture replaces that with JSI, which lets JavaScript hold direct references to C++ objects and call native methods synchronously.
@deep
Three threads matter: the JS thread runs your React code, the native UI thread does layout and drawing, and a shadow thread runs Yoga to compute flexbox layout. In the old architecture every update crossed the bridge as a batched, asynchronous, JSON-serialised message, which is why animations driven from JS could drop frames whenever the JS thread was busy.

The new architecture (Fabric renderer, TurboModules, Codegen) removes the serialisation: JSI exposes native objects to JS directly, TurboModules are lazily loaded instead of all initialised at startup, and Codegen generates type-safe bindings from TypeScript specs.
@arch Old bridge vs new architecture
  OLD                                        NEW (Fabric + JSI + TurboModules)
  ┌──────────┐   JSON over async bridge      ┌──────────┐  direct C++ refs (JSI)
  │ JS thread│ ⇄════════════════════════⇄   │ JS thread│ ⇄──────────────────⇄
  └──────────┘   batched, serialised         └──────────┘  sync calls possible
       ↓  every frame of a JS animation           ↓  shadow tree in C++,
       ↓  crosses the bridge                      ↓  concurrent React support
  ┌──────────────┐                           ┌──────────────┐
  │ Native/UI    │  ← dropped frames when    │ Native/UI    │  ← no serialisation,
  │ thread       │    the JS thread is busy  │ thread       │    lazy TurboModules
  └──────────────┘                           └──────────────┘
@end
@senior
The practical consequence I would give is animation strategy. Any animation driven by `setState` on the JS thread stutters the moment the JS thread does real work — a list re-render, a JSON parse, a location update. That is why Reanimated exists: it runs animation worklets on the UI thread, so a gesture stays at 60fps even while JavaScript is blocked. On the geolocation app that distinction is not academic — background location callbacks fire on the JS thread and would otherwise visibly jank any in-progress animation.
@followup
What is Hermes and why does it matter?
@answer
Hermes is the JS engine built for React Native. It precompiles JavaScript to bytecode at build time, so there is no parse-and-compile cost at startup — that typically cuts time-to-interactive substantially and reduces memory. It is the default now, and the trade-off is that some `eval`-dependent or engine-specific code behaves differently, and you need Hermes-compatible source maps for readable production stack traces in Sentry.
@red
- Saying React Native renders in a WebView.
- Not knowing the bridge has been replaced.

### How is React Native different from React for the web?
@short
The programming model is identical — components, hooks, state, props. What differs is the primitives (`View`, `Text`, `Image`, `ScrollView` instead of DOM elements), styling (a JavaScript subset of CSS with flexbox by default and no cascade), navigation (React Navigation instead of URLs), and the platform APIs you must handle: permissions, background execution, app lifecycle and store releases.
@deep
Every string must be inside a `<Text>` — there is no implicit text node. Styles are objects, there is no inheritance except partially for text, and units are density-independent pixels rather than px/rem. `flexDirection` defaults to `column`, not `row`, which trips up every web developer once.

The bigger difference is operational: you cannot just deploy. A native change requires a store review, so you plan for over-the-air updates (Expo Updates or CodePush) for JS-only changes, and you must support users on old versions for months.
@code tsx Platform-aware component with safe areas and correct list usage
import { View, Text, Platform, StyleSheet, FlatList } from 'react-native';
import { useSafeAreaInsets } from 'react-native-safe-area-context';

export function TripList({ trips }) {
  const insets = useSafeAreaInsets();
  return (
    <FlatList
      data={trips}
      keyExtractor={t => t.id}
      renderItem={({ item }) => <Row trip={item} />}
      // perf: fixed height lets FlatList skip measurement entirely
      getItemLayout={(_, i) => ({ length: 72, offset: 72 * i, index: i })}
      initialNumToRender={10}
      windowSize={7}
      removeClippedSubviews={Platform.OS === 'android'}
      contentContainerStyle={{ paddingBottom: insets.bottom }}
    />
  );
}

const styles = StyleSheet.create({
  card: {
    padding: 16,
    ...Platform.select({
      ios:     { shadowColor: '#000', shadowOpacity: 0.1, shadowRadius: 8, shadowOffset: { width: 0, height: 2 } },
      android: { elevation: 3 },          // Android has no shadow* props
    }),
  },
});
@end
@senior
The senior framing is that "write once, run anywhere" is false and pretending otherwise causes bad apps. Permissions differ (Android 10+ needs a separate background-location grant and a prominent-disclosure screen; iOS has "While Using" versus "Always"), background execution differs fundamentally, keyboard handling differs, and back-button behaviour exists only on Android. I budget for platform-specific code and test on real low-end Android devices, because the emulator hides both the performance and the battery reality.
@followup
When would you choose React Native over Flutter or native?
@answer
React Native when the team is already strong in React and TypeScript and the app is primarily product surface — forms, lists, networking, moderate animation — because you share language, tooling, validation schemas and often business logic with the web app. Native when the app is built around platform-specific capability: heavy camera or AR work, tight background processing, or sub-frame performance requirements. Flutter is a fine choice technically; I would not push a React team onto Dart without a strong reason.
@red
- Claiming full code sharing with the web app.
- Not knowing that all text must be in a `<Text>`.

### How do you handle navigation in React Native?
@short
React Navigation is the standard: a native stack for screen-to-screen, tabs and drawers for top-level structure, all typed so route params are checked at compile time. Deep links map URLs and push notifications to screens, and I keep authentication state at the navigator level so the whole navigator swaps between an auth stack and an app stack.
@deep
The auth pattern matters: rather than pushing a login screen and popping it, you conditionally render two entirely different navigators. That guarantees the back gesture cannot return to an authenticated screen and cleanly resets state on logout.

`@react-navigation/native-stack` uses the platform's native navigator, which gives you real native transitions and gestures and much better performance than the JS-based stack.
@code tsx Typed navigation with an auth switch and deep linking
type RootStackParamList = {
  Home: undefined;
  TripDetail: { tripId: string };
  Checkout: { orderId: string; amount: number };
};
declare global { namespace ReactNavigation { interface RootParamList extends RootStackParamList {} } }

const Stack = createNativeStackNavigator<RootStackParamList>();

export function Root() {
  const { user, loading } = useAuth();
  if (loading) return <Splash />;
  return (
    <NavigationContainer linking={{
      prefixes: ['acme://', 'https://acme.com'],
      config: { screens: { TripDetail: 'trips/:tripId', Checkout: 'pay/:orderId' } },
    }}>
      {user ? (
        <Stack.Navigator>
          <Stack.Screen name="Home" component={Home} />
          <Stack.Screen name="TripDetail" component={TripDetail} />
        </Stack.Navigator>
      ) : (
        <AuthStack />          {/* entire navigator swaps — no way to go "back" into the app */}
      )}
    </NavigationContainer>
  );
}

// Type-safe usage — a wrong param name is a compile error
const nav = useNavigation<NativeStackNavigationProp<RootStackParamList>>();
nav.navigate('TripDetail', { tripId: trip.id });
@end
@senior
The detail that shows production experience is handling a deep link or notification tap that arrives while the app is cold-starting and the user is not yet authenticated: you must hold the intended destination, complete the auth flow, then navigate — otherwise the link silently drops the user on the home screen. I store the pending route and consume it after auth resolves. The second detail is that `navigation.navigate` in a `useEffect` before the navigator has mounted throws, so navigation triggered from outside a screen goes through a navigation ref with a queue.
@followup
How do you handle the Android hardware back button?
@answer
`BackHandler.addEventListener('hardwareBackPress', handler)` inside a `useFocusEffect`, returning `true` to consume the event. React Navigation handles the common case automatically, but you need custom handling for things like "close the bottom sheet instead of leaving the screen" or "confirm before discarding a half-filled form". The listener must be removed when the screen loses focus or you get handlers from several screens fighting each other.
@red
- Pushing and popping login screens instead of swapping navigators.
- Untyped navigation params.

### How do you build an offline-first React Native app?
@short
Offline-first means the UI reads from a local database and writes to a local queue, and synchronisation happens in the background when connectivity allows. Every write gets a client-generated id, is stored durably, and is replayed idempotently to the server; the UI never blocks on the network.
@deep
The architecture has four pieces: a local store (SQLite via WatermelonDB or op-sqlite, or MMKV for small key-value data), an outbox table for pending mutations, a sync engine with exponential backoff, and a conflict-resolution policy. AsyncStorage is fine for preferences and wrong for a queue of thousands of records — it is slow and not transactional.

Idempotency is the critical property: the client may retry a batch it already sent successfully because the response was lost. Every queued item carries a UUID that the server uses as a natural dedup key.
@arch Offline-first geolocation pipeline
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Background location task (native, survives JS being suspended)       │
  └──────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                 │ point {uuid, lat, lng, ts, accuracy, batteryPct}
                 ▼
        ┌─────────────────┐   write-ahead, never blocks the UI
        │ SQLite outbox   │   status: pending | inflight | acked
        └────────┬────────┘
                 │  NetInfo says online + backoff timer elapsed
                 ▼
        ┌─────────────────┐  POST /v1/locations/batch  (max 200 points,
        │  Sync engine    │  Idempotency-Key: batch uuid, gzip body)
        └────────┬────────┘
                 │ 2xx → mark acked + delete    4xx → dead-letter + log
                 │ 5xx/timeout → backoff ×2 (cap 5 min) + jitter, retry
                 ▼
        ┌─────────────────┐  server dedups on point uuid (unique index)
        │  API + Postgres │  so a replayed batch is a no-op
        └─────────────────┘
@end
@code ts The outbox write path and a safe batch flush
export async function enqueuePoint(p: Omit<Point, 'uuid'>) {
  await db.write(async () => {
    await db.get('outbox').create(r => {
      r.uuid = uuid();                     // client-generated → idempotency key
      r.payload = JSON.stringify(p);
      r.status = 'pending';
      r.attempts = 0;
    });
  });
}

export async function flush() {
  if (!(await NetInfo.fetch()).isInternetReachable) return;
  const batch = await db.get('outbox').query(Q.where('status', 'pending'), Q.take(200)).fetch();
  if (!batch.length) return;

  const batchId = uuid();
  try {
    await api.post('/v1/locations/batch',
      { points: batch.map(r => JSON.parse(r.payload)) },
      { headers: { 'Idempotency-Key': batchId } });
    await db.write(() => Promise.all(batch.map(r => r.destroyPermanently())));
    backoff.reset();
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.status >= 400 && e.status < 500) await deadLetter(batch, e);   // never retry a 422 forever
    else backoff.next();                                                 // 1s,2s,4s… cap 5 min + jitter
  }
}
@end
@senior
Three things I would raise that only come from shipping this. First, battery: continuous high-accuracy GPS drains a phone in hours, so you use significant-change or distance-filtered updates, drop accuracy when stationary, and batch uploads rather than posting per point — users uninstall apps that eat their battery. Second, OS restrictions: iOS suspends apps aggressively and Android's Doze and per-vendor battery optimisers kill background work, so the location task must be a native background task with the right entitlement, not a JavaScript timer. Third, storage growth: a device offline for a week accumulates a lot of points, so the outbox needs a cap and a downsampling policy or you fill the user's disk.
@followup
How do you resolve conflicts when two devices edit the same record offline?
@answer
It depends on the data. For append-only telemetry like location points there is no conflict — dedupe on uuid and you are done. For editable records the options are last-write-wins with a server timestamp (simple, lossy), per-field merge with version vectors (better, more complex), or a CRDT if genuine concurrent editing is required. I pick the weakest model that satisfies the product: LWW with a visible "this was updated on another device" prompt is often more honest than silently merging.
@red
- Using AsyncStorage as a durable mutation queue.
- Retrying forever without backoff or a dead-letter path.
- No idempotency key, so retries duplicate data.

### How do you optimise React Native performance?
@short
Keep the JS thread free: use `FlatList`/`FlashList` with stable keys and memoised rows instead of mapping over an array, run animations through Reanimated on the UI thread, use `InteractionManager` to defer work until transitions finish, and profile with Flipper or the Hermes profiler rather than guessing.
@deep
The typical culprits are: rendering a long list with `.map` inside a `ScrollView` (which mounts everything), unmemoised `renderItem` and inline styles causing full list re-renders, large uncompressed images, and expensive synchronous work — JSON parsing, encryption, big array transforms — on the JS thread.

Startup time is its own topic: Hermes bytecode, lazy TurboModules, deferring non-critical initialisation, and RAM bundles/inline requires all help. Users judge an app by cold start.
@code tsx The three fixes that matter most
// 1. Memoised row + stable callback: without these the whole list re-renders per state change
const Row = React.memo(({ item, onPress }) => (
  <Pressable onPress={() => onPress(item.id)}><Text>{item.name}</Text></Pressable>
), (a, b) => a.item.updatedAt === b.item.updatedAt);

const onPress = useCallback((id) => nav.navigate('Detail', { id }), [nav]);

// 2. Reanimated worklet — runs on the UI thread, unaffected by a busy JS thread
const offset = useSharedValue(0);
const style = useAnimatedStyle(() => ({ transform: [{ translateX: withSpring(offset.value) }] }));

// 3. Defer heavy work until the navigation animation completes
useEffect(() => {
  const task = InteractionManager.runAfterInteractions(() => loadHeavyAnalytics());
  return () => task.cancel();
}, []);
@end
@senior
The measurement discipline is what I would emphasise: I look at JS frame rate and UI frame rate separately in the performance monitor, because they tell you different things — a low JS FPS with a healthy UI FPS means your animations are fine but interactions will lag, and the reverse means you are overdrawing or thrashing layout. For genuinely heavy work — parsing a large sync payload, hashing, image processing — I move it off the JS thread with `react-native-worklets` or a native module, the same reasoning as `worker_threads` in Node. And I always test on a low-end Android device, because that is where the majority of real users are and where the emulator lies to you.
@followup
Why is `FlashList` faster than `FlatList`?
@answer
`FlatList` recycles by unmounting and remounting cells; FlashList reuses the underlying views and re-binds data, which avoids the mount cost that dominates on long lists. It also needs an `estimatedItemSize` so it can pre-compute layout rather than measuring. The trade-off is that recycling exposes bugs when a row holds internal state, so rows must be pure functions of their item.
@red
- Rendering long lists inside a `ScrollView`.
- Animating with `setState` in a loop.
- Only testing on an iPhone simulator.

### How do you store data securely on a mobile device?
@short
Tokens and secrets go in the Keychain on iOS and the Keystore-backed EncryptedSharedPreferences on Android — via `react-native-keychain` or Expo SecureStore. AsyncStorage is unencrypted plain files and is readable on a rooted or jailbroken device, so it is for non-sensitive preferences only. Bundled API keys are never secret, because anyone can unzip the app.
@deep
The threat model differs from the web: there is no XSS, but there is device compromise, backup extraction and reverse engineering. Anything shipped in the JavaScript bundle — including `process.env` values inlined at build time — is readable with a text editor after unzipping the IPA or APK.

That means third-party secrets must live on your server, with the app calling your backend, which then calls the third party. The app authenticates as a user, never as a privileged API client.
@code ts Secure token storage plus certificate pinning for high-value traffic
import * as Keychain from 'react-native-keychain';

await Keychain.setGenericPassword('refresh', token, {
  service: 'com.acme.auth',
  accessible: Keychain.ACCESSIBLE.WHEN_UNLOCKED_THIS_DEVICE_ONLY,  // not in iCloud backups
  accessControl: Keychain.ACCESS_CONTROL.BIOMETRY_CURRENT_SET,     // Face ID / fingerprint
});

const creds = await Keychain.getGenericPassword({ service: 'com.acme.auth' });

// MMKV for fast non-secret local state (it also supports an encryption key)
const storage = new MMKV({ id: 'app', encryptionKey: await getDeviceKey() });
storage.set('lastSyncAt', Date.now());
@end
@senior
The senior points are: short-lived access tokens so a stolen device has a small window, refresh-token rotation with server-side revocation so you can kill a session remotely, and root/jailbreak detection as a signal rather than a gate (it is bypassable, so use it to raise risk scoring, not to hard-block). For a payments or location app I would also add certificate pinning to resist a user-installed proxy — with a documented rotation plan, because a pinned certificate that expires bricks every installed app until users update, which is a genuinely dangerous failure mode.
@followup
Is code obfuscation worth it?
@answer
It raises the cost of casual reverse engineering and nothing more — Hermes bytecode already makes the JS unreadable, and a determined attacker will still get through. It is worth doing because it is cheap, but it must never be part of your security model. The rule stands: if the client can decrypt it, so can an attacker, so no secret that matters should ever be on the device.
@red
- Storing JWTs in AsyncStorage.
- Shipping a third-party secret key in the app bundle.

### How do you handle push notifications and permissions?
@short
Push goes through APNs on iOS and FCM on Android, usually with Firebase Cloud Messaging as the single interface. The flow is: request permission at a moment the user understands why, get a device token, register it against the user on your server, and handle the notification in three distinct states — foreground, background and cold start from a tap.
@deep
Permissions are the part people get wrong. iOS asks once; a denial is effectively permanent short of the user going into Settings, so you show a pre-permission explanation screen first and only trigger the system prompt when they say yes. Android 13+ also requires a runtime `POST_NOTIFICATIONS` permission.

Device tokens rotate — on reinstall, on restore to a new device, sometimes on OS update — so you must refresh them and prune dead ones on your server, or your delivery rate quietly decays and you keep paying to send to tokens that no longer exist.
@code ts Token lifecycle and the three delivery states
import messaging from '@react-native-firebase/messaging';

async function registerPush(userId: string) {
  const status = await messaging().requestPermission();       // after your own rationale screen
  if (status !== messaging.AuthorizationStatus.AUTHORIZED) return;

  const token = await messaging().getToken();
  await api.post('/devices', { token, platform: Platform.OS, appVersion });

  // tokens rotate — keep the server in sync or delivery silently degrades
  return messaging().onTokenRefresh(t => api.post('/devices', { token: t, platform: Platform.OS }));
}

messaging().onMessage(handleForeground);                 // app open: show an in-app banner yourself
messaging().setBackgroundMessageHandler(handleData);     // app backgrounded: data-only messages
messaging().onNotificationOpenedApp(n => routeTo(n.data.deeplink));   // tapped while backgrounded
const initial = await messaging().getInitialNotification();           // tapped from cold start
if (initial) pendingRoute.set(initial.data.deeplink);                 // navigate after auth resolves
@end
@senior
The operational points: never put sensitive content in the payload, since notifications render on a locked screen and pass through Apple's and Google's infrastructure — send an identifier and fetch the detail after the tap. Deduplicate on the server so a retry does not double-notify. And treat push as best-effort, never as a delivery guarantee for anything transactional: FCM will drop messages for a device that has been offline too long, so a payment receipt must also exist in the app and by email. Finally, respect quiet hours and per-category preferences, because an app that spams gets its notifications disabled entirely and you lose the channel.
@followup
What is the difference between a notification message and a data message?
@answer
A notification message is rendered by the OS automatically and your handler may not run at all when the app is backgrounded. A data-only message always wakes your background handler, so it is what you use to trigger a silent sync — but delivery is throttled by the OS, especially on iOS where content-available pushes are rate-limited and can be dropped entirely. For anything that must display, send a notification payload; for background work, send data and accept that it is opportunistic.
@red
- Requesting permission on first launch with no context.
- Putting sensitive data in the push payload.
- Never pruning stale device tokens.

### How do you release and update a React Native app?
@short
JavaScript-only changes ship over the air with Expo Updates or CodePush, which lets you fix a bug in hours instead of waiting for review. Anything touching native code — a new dependency with native modules, a permission, an SDK upgrade — requires a full store build. I keep OTA and binary versions strictly compatible and gate risky changes behind a remote feature flag.
@deep
The dangerous coupling is between the JS bundle and the native binary. If an OTA update calls a native module that only exists in a newer binary, users on the old binary crash on launch — and because they crash on launch, they may not be able to receive a fixing update. Every OTA channel must therefore be scoped to a runtime version.

Rollout strategy: staged rollout on the stores (1% → 10% → 100%), crash-rate monitoring per version in Sentry, and the ability to roll back the OTA bundle instantly.
@arch Release pipeline with a safe OTA path
  git tag v2.4.0
      │
      ├─► EAS Build / Fastlane ─► TestFlight + Play internal ─► staged store rollout
      │        (native changes; runtimeVersion = "2.4.0")          1% → 10% → 100%
      │                                                            watch crash-free %
      └─► JS-only fix ─► EAS Update --channel production
               │            (published ONLY to runtimeVersion 2.4.0 clients)
               │
               ├─ crash rate rises ─► `eas update:rollback` → previous bundle in minutes
               └─ healthy ─────────► keep
@end
@senior
The senior instinct is to protect the ability to recover. That means: never publish an OTA update that cannot itself be replaced (test the update path, not just the app), always keep the previous bundle, and put every risky feature behind a server-controlled flag so you can disable it without any release at all. I would also mention store policy — both Apple and Google permit OTA JavaScript updates that do not change the app's core purpose, so the mechanism is legitimate, but shipping substantially new functionality that bypassed review is a policy violation and a real risk to the account.
@followup
How do you support users stuck on an old version?
@answer
A version check on launch against a server-provided minimum: below the soft minimum, show a dismissible prompt; below the hard minimum, block with a forced-update screen, which you need when an API contract has genuinely broken. Alongside that I version the API and keep old versions alive for a defined window, because mobile clients cannot be force-upgraded the way a web app can — some users simply will not update for months, and the backend has to tolerate that.
@red
- OTA-updating JS that depends on newer native code.
- No rollback plan and no staged rollout.

# SECTION Node.js Fundamentals | Single thread, huge concurrency — the questions here are really about knowing where the limits are.

### How does Node.js achieve concurrency with a single thread?
@short
Node runs your JavaScript on one thread but delegates I/O to the operating system through libuv, which uses epoll, kqueue or IOCP. The thread issues the operation, registers a callback and moves on; when the OS signals completion, the event loop invokes the callback. Concurrency comes from waiting efficiently, not from parallelism.
@deep
libuv gives you two mechanisms. Network I/O uses the kernel's readiness notification, so ten thousand idle sockets cost almost nothing — no thread per connection. File system operations, DNS resolution via `getaddrinfo`, and some crypto functions cannot be done that way, so they go to a thread pool that defaults to four threads.

The event loop runs in phases: timers, pending callbacks, poll (where it waits for I/O), check (`setImmediate`), and close callbacks. Between every phase it drains `process.nextTick` and then the microtask queue.
@code js Proving the thread pool exists — and why it matters
// Four parallel pbkdf2 calls finish together; the fifth waits for a free thread.
// Set UV_THREADPOOL_SIZE=8 and all five finish together.
const crypto = require('node:crypto');
const start = Date.now();
for (let i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
  crypto.pbkdf2('secret', 'salt', 200_000, 64, 'sha512', () =>
    console.log(i, Date.now() - start, 'ms'));
}
// Typical: 0..3 ≈ 190ms, 4 ≈ 380ms  ← the 4-thread pool is the bottleneck
@end
@senior
The operational insight is that CPU-bound work and thread-pool-bound work are the two ways to break a Node service, and they present identically to a user (everything gets slow) but need different fixes. Heavy `bcrypt` on a login endpoint saturates the thread pool and makes unrelated requests queue; a synchronous JSON parse of a 50MB payload blocks the loop outright. I monitor event-loop delay with `perf_hooks.monitorEventLoopDelay` and alert on p99 lag above ~100ms, because that metric moves before latency does and tells you *which* kind of problem you have.
@followup
When would you use `worker_threads` versus a separate process or a queue?
@answer
`worker_threads` for CPU-bound work that must stay in-process and can share memory via `SharedArrayBuffer` — image resizing, large parsing, compression. A child process or a separate service when you want isolation and independent scaling. A queue (BullMQ, SQS) when the work is asynchronous from the user's perspective — report generation, email sends, video transcoding — because then you also get retries, rate limiting, visibility and back-pressure for free. My default for anything over a few hundred milliseconds is the queue.
@red
- "Node is multi-threaded" or "Node is asynchronous so nothing blocks".
- Not knowing about the libuv thread pool.

### What is the difference between `process.nextTick`, `setImmediate` and `setTimeout(fn, 0)`?
@short
`process.nextTick` runs before anything else — it drains after the current operation, ahead of promises. Microtasks (promise callbacks) run next. `setImmediate` runs in the check phase, after the poll phase of the current loop iteration. `setTimeout(fn, 0)` is really `setTimeout(fn, 1)` and runs in the timers phase, so its ordering relative to `setImmediate` at the top level is non-deterministic.
@deep
Inside an I/O callback the ordering *is* deterministic: `setImmediate` always fires before `setTimeout`, because you are already past the poll phase and the check phase comes immediately after, whereas the timers phase requires another full loop iteration. That is the standard interview trick.

`process.nextTick` recursion starves the event loop entirely — the queue is drained until empty before the loop can advance, so an infinite `nextTick` chain hangs the process without ever hitting a timer.
@code js The deterministic version — put them inside an I/O callback
require('node:fs').readFile(__filename, () => {
  setTimeout(() => console.log('timeout'), 0);
  setImmediate(() => console.log('immediate'));   // ✅ always first here
  process.nextTick(() => console.log('nextTick')); // ✅ before both
  Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('promise'));
});
// nextTick, promise, immediate, timeout — every time
@end
@senior
In application code I almost never need `nextTick`; its legitimate use is deferring a callback so it runs after the current function returns, which libraries use to guarantee "always async" APIs — a function that sometimes calls back synchronously and sometimes asynchronously causes genuinely awful bugs (the Zalgo problem). If I need to yield to the event loop inside a long computation to keep the process responsive, I use `setImmediate` or `await new Promise(r => setImmediate(r))`, because `nextTick` does not yield at all.
@followup
How do you break up a long synchronous computation without blocking?
@answer
Chunk it and yield: process N items, then `await new Promise(r => setImmediate(r))` so the loop can service pending I/O, then continue. That keeps health checks and other requests responsive at the cost of a slightly longer total runtime. If the work is genuinely heavy the better answer is a worker thread or a queue — yielding just makes the blocking less bad, it does not make the work cheap.
@red
- Saying `setTimeout(fn, 0)` runs immediately.
- Using `process.nextTick` to yield to I/O.

### Explain Node streams and back-pressure.
@short
A stream processes data in chunks instead of loading everything into memory, so a 2GB file uses a few megabytes of RAM. Back-pressure is the flow-control signal: `write()` returning false means the destination's buffer is full, so the source should pause until `drain`. `pipeline()` wires this up correctly including error propagation.
@deep
The four types are Readable, Writable, Duplex and Transform. The key property is that `pipe`/`pipeline` respects the `highWaterMark` — if the consumer is slower than the producer, the producer is paused rather than buffering without limit.

Ignoring back-pressure is how you turn a streaming solution back into a memory problem: reading a file fast and writing to a slow socket without flow control just moves the whole file into Node's heap.
@code js Streaming CSV export — flat memory, correct errors, correct cleanup
import { pipeline } from 'node:stream/promises';
import { Transform } from 'node:stream';

app.get('/export/transactions', async (req, res) => {
  res.setHeader('Content-Type', 'text/csv');
  res.setHeader('Content-Disposition', 'attachment; filename="tx.csv"');

  const rows = db.query('SELECT id, amount, created_at FROM transactions').stream(); // cursor
  const toCsv = new Transform({
    objectMode: true,
    transform(row, _enc, cb) { cb(null, `${row.id},${row.amount},${row.created_at}\n`); },
  });

  try {
    // pipeline handles back-pressure, error propagation AND destroys streams on failure
    await pipeline(rows, toCsv, res);
  } catch (err) {
    if (!res.headersSent) res.status(500).end();
    else res.destroy(err);            // headers already sent — abort the response
    logger.error({ err }, 'export failed');
  }
});
@end
@senior
Two senior details. First, `.pipe()` does *not* forward errors or destroy the remaining streams, which leaks file descriptors and sockets — `stream.pipeline` exists precisely to fix that, and it is what I use every time. Second, the database side must also stream: `SELECT *` into an array and then streaming the array is pointless, you need a server-side cursor (`pg-query-stream`, MySQL's `stream()`), or you have simply moved the memory problem one layer up. The other production note is that once you have started streaming a 200 response you cannot change the status code, so validation must happen before the first byte.
@followup
How do you set `highWaterMark` and does it matter?
@answer
It is the buffer threshold at which `write()` starts returning false — 16KB for byte streams, 16 objects in object mode. Raising it trades memory for fewer pause/resume cycles and can help throughput on fast consumers; lowering it reduces memory at the cost of more signalling. I only tune it after measuring, and the default is right for almost everything. What matters far more is that you respect the signal at all.
@red
- Using `.pipe()` and assuming errors propagate.
- "Streaming" a response after loading the whole result set into an array.

### How does the `require`/module system and `module.exports` work?
@short
CommonJS wraps every module in a function that receives `exports`, `require`, `module`, `__filename` and `__dirname`, executes it once, and caches the resulting `module.exports` keyed by resolved path. Subsequent `require` calls of the same path return the cached object, which makes modules effectively singletons per process.
@deep
The caching is why a module that creates a database pool at import time gives every consumer the same pool — usually what you want, occasionally a surprise in tests where you need `jest.resetModules()`.

Resolution walks up `node_modules` directories from the requiring file, which is why two versions of the same package can coexist at different depths — and why `instanceof` across them fails. Assigning to `exports` directly (`exports = {}`) breaks the export because it rebinds the local parameter rather than `module.exports`.
@code js The wrapper, the cache, and the export gotcha
// What Node actually runs:
// (function (exports, require, module, __filename, __dirname) { ...your code... });

module.exports = { a: 1 };     // ✅ replaces the export object
exports.b = 2;                 // ✅ adds a property to the same object
exports = { c: 3 };            // ❌ silently exports nothing — rebinds the local only

// Singleton behaviour from the cache
// db.js
const pool = new Pool({ connectionString: process.env.DATABASE_URL });
module.exports = pool;         // every requiring module shares this exact pool

// Clearing the cache (tests only)
delete require.cache[require.resolve('./config')];
@end
@senior
The pattern I would flag is module-level side effects: connecting to a database, reading a file, or starting a timer at import time makes the module impossible to test in isolation and makes startup order significant. I prefer an explicit `createPool(config)` factory called once from a composition root, which is also what makes dependency injection possible. The other thing worth knowing is that circular requires in CommonJS return a *partially populated* export object rather than throwing, so you get a mysterious `undefined` at runtime instead of a clear error — ESM's TDZ failure is genuinely better here.
@followup
How do you handle configuration across environments?
@answer
Environment variables as the source of truth, validated once at startup against a schema so a missing or malformed value fails the boot rather than the first request that touches it. Secrets come from a secret manager (AWS Secrets Manager, SSM Parameter Store) injected as env vars at deploy, never committed. I keep `.env` for local development only, with `.env.example` in git listing the required keys, and I never branch on `NODE_ENV` for business logic — feature flags handle that.
@red
- Reassigning `exports` and wondering why nothing is exported.
- Side-effectful module top-level code.

### What are `Buffer` and the difference between text and binary handling?
@short
A `Buffer` is a fixed-length chunk of raw bytes outside the V8 heap, used for binary data — file contents, network packets, crypto output, image bytes. Strings in Node are UTF-16 in memory, so converting between them requires an explicit encoding, and getting that wrong is how you corrupt binary data or mangle non-ASCII text.
@deep
The classic bug is treating a binary payload as a UTF-8 string: invalid byte sequences are replaced with U+FFFD, permanently destroying the data. That is why a webhook signature check must run over the raw bytes, and why `express.json()` on a Stripe webhook route breaks the signature — the body has already been parsed and re-serialised.

`Buffer.from` allocates and copies; `Buffer.allocUnsafe` skips zero-filling for speed and can therefore expose old memory contents, which has been a real CVE class.
@code js Raw body for webhook verification — the concrete case this matters in
// ❌ express.json() consumed and re-serialised the body; the signature can never match
app.use(express.json());
app.post('/webhooks/stripe', (req, res) => stripe.webhooks.constructEvent(req.body, sig, secret));

// ✅ keep the exact bytes for this route only
app.post('/webhooks/stripe',
  express.raw({ type: 'application/json' }),          // req.body is a Buffer
  (req, res) => {
    let event;
    try {
      event = stripe.webhooks.constructEvent(req.body, req.headers['stripe-signature'], WEBHOOK_SECRET);
    } catch (err) {
      return res.status(400).send(`Webhook Error: ${err.message}`);   // reject, do not process
    }
    enqueue(event);                                    // ack fast, process async
    res.json({ received: true });
  });

// Constant-time comparison for any secret — never use ===
crypto.timingSafeEqual(Buffer.from(expected), Buffer.from(received));
@end
@senior
The two senior notes are: always use `crypto.timingSafeEqual` for signature and token comparison, because `===` short-circuits on the first differing byte and leaks information through timing; and buffers live outside the V8 heap, so a leak of buffers shows up as growing RSS while heap usage looks fine — which is exactly the confusing memory profile you get from an unbounded file-upload or image-processing path. Knowing to look at RSS versus heap is what makes that diagnosable.
@followup
How do you handle large file uploads?
@answer
Stream them, and preferably do not route them through your API at all. The pattern I use is a presigned S3 URL: the client asks the backend for a short-lived signed PUT URL, uploads directly to S3, then tells the backend the object key. That removes upload bandwidth, memory and timeout risk from your servers entirely and scales for free. When files must pass through the server, I stream with `busboy` straight to storage, enforce a size limit before buffering, and validate the real content type from the magic bytes rather than trusting the client's header.
@red
- Reading an uploaded file into memory with `readFileSync`.
- Comparing secrets with `===`.

### How do you handle environment configuration and secrets in Node?
@short
Configuration comes from environment variables, validated at startup with a schema so the process refuses to boot on a bad value. Secrets come from a managed store — AWS Secrets Manager or SSM — and are injected at deploy time. Nothing sensitive is ever committed, and `.env` exists for local development only.
@deep
Fail-fast validation is the part people skip. Without it, a missing `JWT_SECRET` means tokens are signed with `undefined` and every request works fine until someone tries to verify one, or worse, signs with an empty key. With a schema, the container simply does not start and your deploy fails visibly.

Rotation matters too: secrets should be rotatable without a code change, which means reading them at startup (or refreshing periodically) rather than baking them into an image.
@code ts Validated config with sane defaults and clear failure
import { z } from 'zod';

const Env = z.object({
  NODE_ENV: z.enum(['development', 'test', 'production']).default('development'),
  PORT: z.coerce.number().int().positive().default(3000),
  DATABASE_URL: z.string().url(),
  REDIS_URL: z.string().url(),
  JWT_SECRET: z.string().min(32, 'JWT_SECRET must be at least 32 chars'),
  STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET: z.string().startsWith('whsec_'),
  LOG_LEVEL: z.enum(['debug', 'info', 'warn', 'error']).default('info'),
});

const parsed = Env.safeParse(process.env);
if (!parsed.success) {
  console.error('❌ Invalid environment:', parsed.error.flatten().fieldErrors);
  process.exit(1);                    // crash at boot, not at 3am on one code path
}
export const env = Object.freeze(parsed.data);
@end
@senior
Two additions. First, if a secret is ever committed, rotating it is mandatory — removing the commit does not help, because it is in the git history and in every clone and probably already scraped. I would also add `gitleaks` or `trufflehog` to CI as a pre-emptive control. Second, I never log the config object; I log a redacted version, because a startup log with `DATABASE_URL` in it ends up in CloudWatch, in Sentry breadcrumbs and in a support ticket screenshot. Pino's `redact` option handles that centrally.
@followup
How do you manage config for a monorepo with several services?
@answer
Each service validates its own schema and owns its own variables, with shared parsing helpers in a common package so the validation style is consistent. I avoid one giant shared `.env`, because it couples services and makes it impossible to see what a service actually needs. In deployment, each service gets only the secrets it uses, which also limits blast radius if one is compromised.
@red
- Reading `process.env.X` scattered throughout the code with no validation.
- Committing `.env` and deleting it later without rotating.

### What is the difference between `cluster`, PM2 and containers for scaling Node?
@short
`cluster` forks one process per CPU core sharing a port, so a single machine uses all its cores. PM2 wraps that with process management — restarts, log aggregation, zero-downtime reloads, monitoring. Containers plus an orchestrator do the same job at the infrastructure level, which is where it belongs once you have more than one machine.
@deep
The reason you need any of this is that one Node process uses one core. On a 4-core box, a single process leaves 75% of the CPU idle under load.

The important consequence of multiple processes is that in-memory state stops working: sessions, rate-limit counters, caches, WebSocket connection maps and `setInterval` schedulers all become per-process. That is why they belong in Redis, and why a cron job in a clustered app runs N times unless you add a lock.
@code js Cluster with graceful shutdown — and the state that must move to Redis
import cluster from 'node:cluster';
import { availableParallelism } from 'node:os';

if (cluster.isPrimary) {
  const n = availableParallelism();
  for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) cluster.fork();
  cluster.on('exit', (worker, code) => {
    logger.warn({ pid: worker.process.pid, code }, 'worker died, restarting');
    cluster.fork();                                    // supervise
  });
} else {
  const server = app.listen(env.PORT);
  process.on('SIGTERM', async () => {
    server.close(async () => {                         // stop accepting, drain in-flight
      await Promise.all([pool.end(), redis.quit()]);
      process.exit(0);
    });
    setTimeout(() => process.exit(1), 15_000).unref(); // hard cap
  });
}
@end
@senior
In a containerised deployment I would argue *against* clustering inside the container: one process per container is simpler to reason about, gives the orchestrator accurate CPU metrics for autoscaling, and makes a crash a clean pod restart instead of a partially degraded pod. Run N containers with one process each and let ECS or Kubernetes handle the supervision. PM2 in cluster mode is the right tool when you are on plain EC2 without an orchestrator — which is a completely legitimate architecture, and one worth defending on cost grounds for a small service.
@followup
What breaks when you go from one process to four?
@answer
Anything held in process memory. Sessions need Redis or a JWT; rate limiters need a shared store; in-memory caches become four inconsistent caches with different expiry; WebSocket rooms need a Redis pub/sub adapter so a message published on instance 1 reaches a subscriber on instance 3; scheduled jobs need a distributed lock so they run once rather than four times; and sticky sessions become necessary unless the app is genuinely stateless. Enumerating that list is the answer they are looking for.
@red
- Keeping sessions in memory and adding a second instance.
- Running cron in every replica without a lock.

### How do you debug and profile a Node application?
@short
`node --inspect` with Chrome DevTools for breakpoints, `--cpu-prof` or the DevTools profiler for CPU flame graphs, heap snapshots for memory, and structured logs with a request id for production. `clinic.js` gives a quick verdict on whether a problem is CPU, I/O or event-loop related.
@deep
The diagnostic order I follow is: reproduce, measure, then change one thing. For latency I look at event-loop delay first, because it distinguishes "we are CPU-bound" from "the database is slow". For memory I take two heap snapshots minutes apart under load and diff them — the objects that grew tell you what is retained, and the retainer path tells you why.

In production, logs need to be structured JSON with a correlation id, or you cannot follow one request across services.
@code js Production-grade observability primitives
import pino from 'pino';
import { monitorEventLoopDelay } from 'node:perf_hooks';
import { randomUUID } from 'node:crypto';

const logger = pino({ level: env.LOG_LEVEL, redact: ['req.headers.authorization', '*.password'] });

const h = monitorEventLoopDelay({ resolution: 20 });
h.enable();
setInterval(() => {
  logger.info({ loopP99Ms: h.percentile(99) / 1e6, rssMb: process.memoryUsage().rss / 1e6 }, 'health');
  h.reset();
}, 30_000).unref();

// correlation id: one request, traceable across services and log lines
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  req.id = req.headers['x-request-id'] ?? randomUUID();
  res.setHeader('x-request-id', req.id);
  req.log = logger.child({ reqId: req.id, userId: req.user?.id });
  next();
});
@end
@senior
The senior habit is treating observability as a design requirement rather than something bolted on during an incident. That means: structured logs with a correlation id propagated through every downstream call, RED metrics (rate, errors, duration) per endpoint, distributed tracing with OpenTelemetry so you can see that 300ms of a 400ms request was one N+1 query, and Sentry for exceptions with release tagging so a regression is attributable to a deploy. Without those, debugging production is guesswork, and "add a log line and redeploy" is a very expensive loop.
@followup
How do you find a memory leak in production?
@answer
Confirm it first — RSS growing steadily across restarts, not just a heap that sawtooths normally under GC. Then take heap snapshots at intervals with `v8.writeHeapSnapshot()` or via the inspector, load them into DevTools and use the comparison view to find object types that keep growing. The retainer path usually points straight at the cause, and in Node it is nearly always one of four things: an unbounded `Map`/array cache, event listeners added per request without removal, closures captured by a long-lived timer, or a stream that was never destroyed on error.
@red
- Debugging production by adding `console.log` and redeploying.
- Unstructured logs with no request id.

### What is the difference between `exec`, `spawn`, `fork` and when do you shell out?
@short
`spawn` streams output and is right for long-running or large-output processes; `exec` buffers the whole output into memory and is right for short commands; `fork` is `spawn` specialised for a new Node process with an IPC channel. Shelling out at all is a last resort, and if the command includes user input it must never go through a shell.
@deep
The security issue is command injection: `exec(\`convert ${filename} out.png\`)` with a filename of `a.png; rm -rf /` executes both. `spawn('convert', [filename, 'out.png'])` passes arguments directly to `execve` with no shell, so there is nothing to inject into.

`exec`'s buffering also has a hard limit (`maxBuffer`, 1MB by default) and will kill the child when exceeded — a classic cause of "it works on small files".
@code js Safe subprocess use
import { execFile, spawn } from 'node:child_process';
import { promisify } from 'node:util';

// ❌ shell interpolation — command injection
// exec(`ffmpeg -i ${userPath} out.mp4`);

// ✅ no shell, arguments passed as an array
const run = promisify(execFile);
const { stdout } = await run('ffprobe', ['-v', 'error', '-show_format', userPath],
  { timeout: 10_000, maxBuffer: 1024 * 1024 });

// Long-running with streaming output and a hard timeout
const child = spawn('ffmpeg', ['-i', userPath, '-vf', 'scale=720:-1', outPath]);
child.stderr.on('data', d => logger.debug(d.toString()));
const kill = setTimeout(() => child.kill('SIGKILL'), 60_000);
child.on('close', code => { clearTimeout(kill); if (code !== 0) reject(new Error(`exit ${code}`)); });
@end
@senior
Beyond injection, the operational points are: always set a timeout and always handle non-zero exit codes, because an orphaned or hung child process is a slow resource leak that eventually exhausts file descriptors or memory. And a CPU-heavy subprocess spawned per request will happily consume every core and starve the Node process itself — so media processing belongs in a worker queue with a concurrency limit, not inline in a request handler. I would also validate that the input path is inside an expected directory, because path traversal is the second half of this vulnerability class.
@followup
When would you shell out instead of using a library?
@answer
When a mature native tool does the job far better than any JS binding — ffmpeg for media, ImageMagick or libvips for complex image work, pg_dump for backups. The trade is that you now have a system dependency to install in your Docker image, version-pin and monitor. For simple image resizing I would use `sharp`, which is a native binding rather than a subprocess and is both faster and safer.
@red
- Building a shell command with string interpolation of user input.
- No timeout on child processes.

### How do you write a graceful shutdown?
@short
On `SIGTERM`, stop accepting new connections, finish in-flight requests, close the database pool, Redis connections and message-queue consumers, then exit — with a hard timeout so a stuck request cannot block the shutdown forever. Without it, every deploy drops requests and can leave transactions half-finished.
@deep
The sequence matters. If you close the database pool before the server stops accepting connections, in-flight requests fail. If you never set a timeout, a hung upstream call keeps the process alive until the orchestrator sends `SIGKILL` and you lose everything in flight anyway.

There is also a readiness dimension: the load balancer should stop routing to the instance *before* the process starts draining, which means failing the readiness probe first and waiting a few seconds for propagation.
@code js Ordered, bounded shutdown
let shuttingDown = false;
app.get('/readyz', (_req, res) => res.status(shuttingDown ? 503 : 200).end());

async function shutdown(signal) {
  if (shuttingDown) return;
  shuttingDown = true;
  logger.info({ signal }, 'shutdown started');

  await sleep(5_000);                       // 1. let the LB notice /readyz is failing

  server.close(() => logger.info('http server closed'));   // 2. stop accepting new
  await once(server, 'close').catch(() => {});

  await queueWorker.close();                // 3. stop taking new jobs, finish current
  await Promise.allSettled([pool.end(), redis.quit()]);    // 4. release resources
  logger.info('shutdown complete');
  process.exit(0);
}

process.on('SIGTERM', () => shutdown('SIGTERM'));
process.on('SIGINT',  () => shutdown('SIGINT'));
setTimeout(() => { logger.error('forced exit'); process.exit(1); }, 30_000).unref();  // hard cap
@end
@senior
The detail most people miss is the deliberate pause before closing the server: Kubernetes sends `SIGTERM` and removes the pod from endpoints concurrently, not sequentially, so without the delay you can still receive requests after you have stopped listening. The same applies to an ALB with a deregistration delay. The other point is idempotency — with a bounded shutdown, some work will be interrupted, so the job or request must be safe to retry. Graceful shutdown reduces the damage; idempotency is what actually makes it correct.
@followup
What happens to in-flight database transactions when the process is killed?
@answer
On `SIGKILL` the connection drops and Postgres rolls back the open transaction, which is safe — you lose the work but not consistency. The danger is work that spans a transaction and an external side effect: if you charged a card and the process died before committing the order row, the money moved and your database does not know. That is why the outbox pattern exists — write the intent in the same transaction as the state change, and let a separate worker perform the external call idempotently.
@red
- No `SIGTERM` handler at all.
- Closing the DB pool before draining requests.

### What are the most common security mistakes in a Node/Express API?
@short
The recurring ones are: string-concatenated SQL, no input validation, secrets in the repo, JWTs in localStorage, missing rate limiting on auth endpoints, permissive CORS with credentials, leaking stack traces to clients, and outdated dependencies. Most are prevented by a small set of defaults applied consistently.
@deep
The highest-impact controls are cheap: parameterised queries everywhere, schema validation at every boundary, `helmet` for security headers, a strict CORS allow-list, rate limiting backed by Redis, bcrypt or argon2 for passwords, httpOnly cookies for tokens, and a generic error response to the client with the detail only in logs.

Authorisation deserves separate attention from authentication: checking that someone is logged in is not the same as checking that *this* user may read *this* record, and IDOR is consistently one of the most common real-world API vulnerabilities.
@code ts A hardened Express baseline
import helmet from 'helmet';
import rateLimit from 'express-rate-limit';
import { RedisStore } from 'rate-limit-redis';

app.disable('x-powered-by');
app.use(helmet({ contentSecurityPolicy: { directives: { defaultSrc: ["'self'"], scriptSrc: ["'self'"] } } }));
app.use(cors({ origin: env.ALLOWED_ORIGINS.split(','), credentials: true, maxAge: 86400 }));
app.use(express.json({ limit: '100kb' }));                 // bound the body size

app.use('/auth', rateLimit({
  windowMs: 15 * 60_000, limit: 10, standardHeaders: true,
  store: new RedisStore({ sendCommand: (...a) => redis.call(...a) }),   // shared across instances
  keyGenerator: req => `${req.ip}:${req.body?.email ?? ''}`,            // per-account, not just per-IP
}));

// Parameterised query — never string concatenation
const { rows } = await pool.query('SELECT id, email FROM users WHERE email = $1', [email]);

// Authorisation scoped to the data, not just the route
const order = await pool.query(
  'SELECT * FROM orders WHERE id = $1 AND user_id = $2', [req.params.id, req.user.id]);
if (!order.rowCount) return res.status(404).json({ error: 'NOT_FOUND' });  // 404, not 403 — don't confirm existence
@end
@senior
Two things I would emphasise. First, returning 404 rather than 403 for a record the user may not access avoids confirming that the id exists, which is a small but real information leak in an enumeration attack. Second, rate limiting keyed only on IP is easily bypassed by a botnet and unfairly punishes users behind a shared NAT — keying on account plus IP, with progressive delays and an account lockout signal, is meaningfully better. Beyond code, I would mention `npm audit` and Dependabot in CI, and pinning with a lockfile, because supply chain is now a bigger practical risk than most application-level bugs.
@followup
How do you prevent NoSQL injection in MongoDB?
@answer
Validate types before they reach the query. The attack is passing an object where a string is expected — `{ email: { $gt: '' } }` matches any user — so a Zod schema that insists `email` is a string kills it outright. Mongoose casts according to the schema which helps, and `express-mongo-sanitize` strips `$`-prefixed keys, but explicit validation at the boundary is the real defence. The same principle applies everywhere: never let user input decide the *structure* of a query, only its values.
@red
- "We use an ORM so we're safe from injection" (raw queries and `$where` still exist).
- Only checking authentication and not per-record authorisation.

### How do you write and run tests for a Node backend?
@short
A pyramid: many fast unit tests over pure logic, a solid layer of integration tests that exercise real HTTP handlers against a real database in a container, and a few end-to-end tests over critical journeys. I use Vitest or Jest with Supertest, Testcontainers for a real Postgres, and MSW or nock to stub third-party HTTP.
@deep
The decision that most affects test quality is what you mock. Mocking your own repository layer tests your mocks; running against a real Postgres in Docker catches constraint violations, transaction behaviour and SQL errors that a mock never will. What I do mock is third-party network calls, because they are slow, flaky and cost money.

Isolation between tests comes from a transaction rolled back after each test, or truncating tables between tests — both are far more reliable than trying to make tests order-independent by convention.
@code ts Integration test against a real database, with a stubbed payment provider
import request from 'supertest';
import { PostgreSqlContainer } from '@testcontainers/postgresql';

let container, app;
beforeAll(async () => {
  container = await new PostgreSqlContainer('postgres:16-alpine').start();
  process.env.DATABASE_URL = container.getConnectionUri();
  await migrate();
  app = await createApp();                    // factory, not a module side effect
}, 60_000);
afterAll(() => container.stop());

beforeEach(() => db.query('BEGIN'));
afterEach(() => db.query('ROLLBACK'));        // perfect isolation, no truncation cost

it('rejects a duplicate webhook delivery', async () => {
  nock('https://api.stripe.com').post(/.*/).reply(200, { id: 'ch_1' });

  const payload = signedStripeEvent({ id: 'evt_1', type: 'payment_intent.succeeded' });
  await request(app).post('/webhooks/stripe').set(payload.headers).send(payload.raw).expect(200);
  await request(app).post('/webhooks/stripe').set(payload.headers).send(payload.raw).expect(200);

  const { rows } = await db.query('SELECT count(*) FROM orders WHERE payment_intent = $1', ['pi_1']);
  expect(Number(rows[0].count)).toBe(1);      // idempotency actually verified
});
@end
@senior
The senior framing is that tests are a risk-management tool, so I invest where failure is expensive: payment flows, auth, and anything touching money or personal data get integration tests that exercise the real path including retries and duplicates. Coverage percentage is a weak metric — 90% coverage of getters proves nothing — so I would rather talk about which failure modes are covered. In CI, tests must be deterministic and parallel-safe; a flaky test that people retry is worse than no test, because it trains the team to ignore red builds.
@followup
How do you test code that depends on time?
@answer
Inject the clock. Pass a `now()` function or a `Clock` dependency so tests can control it directly, which is cleaner than mocking globals. When that is impractical — for example testing a library's internal timer — I use fake timers (`vi.useFakeTimers()`) and advance them explicitly. What I avoid is `await sleep(2000)` in a test, because it is slow and still flaky under CI load.
@red
- Mocking the database and calling it an integration test.
- Chasing a coverage number instead of covering risk.

# SECTION Express.js | You have shipped 40+ REST API endpoints on Express with Sequelize — these must be automatic.

### How does Express middleware work?
@short
Express keeps an ordered stack of functions with the signature `(req, res, next)`. Each one can inspect or modify the request and response, then either end the cycle by sending a response or call `next()` to pass control on. Order is everything — a middleware registered after a route never runs for that route.
@deep
There are four kinds: application-level (`app.use`), router-level, error-handling (four arguments — Express detects the arity), and built-in or third-party. Calling `next(err)` skips every remaining normal middleware and jumps to the first error handler.

Because Express 4 predates promises, a rejected promise in an async handler is *not* caught — it becomes an unhandled rejection. Express 5 fixes this by forwarding rejections to `next` automatically; on Express 4 you wrap handlers or use `express-async-errors`.
@code ts A correctly ordered stack
const app = express();

app.use(helmet());                        // 1. security headers — before anything else
app.use(cors({ origin: allowlist }));
app.use(requestId);                       // 2. correlation id for every log line
app.use(pinoHttp({ logger }));            // 3. structured request logging
app.use(express.json({ limit: '100kb' })); // 4. body parsing (but NOT before raw webhooks)

app.use('/webhooks', rawBodyRouter);      // mounted earlier with express.raw()
app.use('/api/v1', authenticate, apiRouter);

app.use((req, res) => res.status(404).json({ error: 'NOT_FOUND', path: req.path }));  // 5. 404
app.use(errorHandler);                    // 6. LAST — 4 args, catches everything above

// Router-level middleware applies only within that router
const apiRouter = Router();
apiRouter.use(requireVerifiedEmail);
apiRouter.get('/orders/:id', authorize('order:read'), ah(getOrder));
@end
@senior
Two ordering mistakes I look for in review. First, mounting `express.json()` globally before a webhook route, which destroys the raw body needed for signature verification — the webhook router must be mounted first with `express.raw()`. Second, putting the error handler before the routes, in which case it never fires and every error becomes a hung request or a default HTML stack trace. I would also mention that middleware runs per request, so anything expensive there — a database lookup for the user on every request — is a latency tax across the entire API and belongs behind a cache.
@followup
How do you share data between middleware?
@answer
Attach it to `req` (typed via module augmentation, not `as any`), which is the conventional approach and is fine for request-scoped values like `req.user` and `req.id`. For values needed deep in the call stack without threading them through every function signature — a trace id inside a repository, for example — I use `AsyncLocalStorage`, which gives you request-scoped context that survives across awaits without polluting every signature. That is the modern answer and it is what OpenTelemetry uses internally.
@red
- Registering the error handler before the routes.
- Global `express.json()` breaking webhook signature checks.

### How do you structure a large Express application?
@short
By feature, in layers: routes handle HTTP, controllers translate between HTTP and the domain, services hold business logic, and repositories own data access. Nothing above the repository knows about SQL, and nothing below the controller knows about `req` and `res`. That separation is what makes the business logic testable without a server.
@deep
The failure mode of the default Express tutorial structure is "fat controllers": validation, business rules, three database calls and a Stripe call in one 200-line handler. It cannot be unit-tested, it cannot be reused by a queue worker or a CLI, and every change risks breaking an unrelated path.

Grouping by feature rather than by technical type (`/orders/*` rather than `/controllers`, `/services`, `/models`) keeps related code together and makes deleting a feature a single directory removal.
@arch Feature-sliced layering
  src/
  ├── app.ts                 createApp() factory — no side effects at import
  ├── server.ts              listen + graceful shutdown
  ├── config/env.ts          Zod-validated environment
  ├── modules/
  │   ├── orders/
  │   │   ├── order.routes.ts      HTTP verbs, paths, middleware wiring
  │   │   ├── order.controller.ts  req/res ⇄ DTO. No business rules.
  │   │   ├── order.service.ts     business rules, transactions. NO req/res.
  │   │   ├── order.repository.ts  SQL / ORM only. NO business rules.
  │   │   ├── order.schema.ts      Zod: request + response contracts
  │   │   └── order.service.test.ts
  │   └── payments/ …
  ├── shared/
  │   ├── middleware/  auth.ts  errorHandler.ts  requestId.ts
  │   ├── errors/      AppError.ts + subclasses
  │   └── db/          pool.ts  transaction.ts
  └── jobs/            workers reusing the SAME services
@end
@code ts The layer boundary in practice
// controller — thin: parse, delegate, format. That is all.
export const createOrder: RequestHandler = async (req, res) => {
  const dto = CreateOrderSchema.parse(req.body);
  const order = await orderService.create(req.user.id, dto);   // no SQL, no Stripe here
  res.status(201).location(`/api/v1/orders/${order.id}`).json(toOrderResponse(order));
};

// service — business rules + transaction boundary. Reusable by a queue worker.
export async function create(userId: string, dto: CreateOrderDto) {
  return withTransaction(async (tx) => {
    const items = await catalog.priceItems(dto.items, tx);       // may throw OutOfStockError
    const totals = priceOrder(items, dto.coupon);                // pure function — unit tested
    const order = await orderRepo.insert({ userId, ...totals }, tx);
    await outboxRepo.insert({ type: 'order.created', payload: { orderId: order.id } }, tx);
    return order;                                                // side effects go via the outbox
  });
}
@end
@senior
The point I would make is that the layering exists to protect the *transaction boundary* and the *reusability* of business logic. Once the service owns the transaction, a queue worker, a CLI backfill and the HTTP route all get the same guarantees. And putting the external side effect (email, Stripe call, webhook) in an outbox row inside the same transaction, rather than calling it inline, is what prevents the classic "database rolled back but the customer got the email" inconsistency. That single pattern is worth more than any amount of folder structure.
@followup
When would you move to NestJS?
@answer
When the team is large enough that convention beats freedom — Nest gives you enforced modules, DI, decorators for validation and guards, and a consistent structure that new joiners recognise. The cost is a heavier framework, decorator metadata, and more indirection to trace during debugging. For a small team shipping fast, structured Express is lighter and just as maintainable if the layering is disciplined; the discipline is the actual variable, not the framework.
@red
- All logic in route handlers.
- Folder structure by technical type with 60 files in `/controllers`.

### How do you validate requests in Express?
@short
With a schema at the boundary — Zod, Joi or class-validator — applied as middleware so every route declares what it accepts for body, params and query. Validation returns 422 with field-level detail, and the parsed, typed result replaces the raw input so downstream code never touches unvalidated data.
@deep
Validation is also coercion: query strings are always strings, so `?page=2` needs to become a number, and `?active=false` must not become the truthy string `'false'`. A schema does that conversion once, in one place.

Just as important is stripping unknown fields. Without it, a client can send `{ role: 'admin' }` to a profile update endpoint and, if you spread the body into an update, escalate their own privileges — mass assignment, and it is still common.
@code ts Reusable validation middleware — body, params and query together
import { z, ZodSchema } from 'zod';

export const validate = (schemas: { body?: ZodSchema; params?: ZodSchema; query?: ZodSchema }) =>
  (req, res, next) => {
    try {
      if (schemas.params) req.params = schemas.params.parse(req.params);
      if (schemas.query)  req.query  = schemas.query.parse(req.query);
      if (schemas.body)   req.body   = schemas.body.parse(req.body);   // ✅ replaces raw input
      next();
    } catch (e) {
      if (e instanceof z.ZodError)
        return res.status(422).json({ error: 'VALIDATION_FAILED', fields: e.flatten().fieldErrors });
      next(e);
    }
  };

const ListQuery = z.object({
  page:  z.coerce.number().int().min(1).default(1),
  limit: z.coerce.number().int().min(1).max(100).default(20),   // cap it — no ?limit=100000
  status: z.enum(['pending', 'paid', 'shipped']).optional(),
});

const UpdateProfile = z.object({ name: z.string().min(1).max(80), bio: z.string().max(500).optional() })
  .strict();                       // ✅ reject unknown keys — kills mass assignment

router.get('/orders', validate({ query: ListQuery }), listOrders);
router.patch('/me', validate({ body: UpdateProfile }), updateProfile);
@end
@senior
Two details I would raise. First, always cap `limit` — an uncapped page size is both a denial-of-service vector and an accidental full-table scan. Second, `.strict()` versus `.strip()` is a real API design decision: stripping unknown fields is forgiving and good for clients, rejecting them catches typos early and prevents mass assignment. I use `.strict()` on anything that writes and `.strip()` on read filters. And the same schemas generate the OpenAPI spec, so the docs cannot drift from the implementation, which is the strongest argument for schema-first validation.
@followup
Where should validation live — middleware, controller or service?
@answer
Structural validation (types, formats, required fields, ranges) belongs at the boundary as middleware, because it is about the HTTP contract. Business validation — "this coupon has expired", "insufficient stock", "you cannot refund twice" — belongs in the service, because it needs domain state and must apply equally when the same operation comes from a queue worker or a CLI. Putting business rules in HTTP middleware is how you end up with rules that only apply to one entry point.
@red
- Trusting `req.body` shape and casting it with `as`.
- Spreading the raw body into a database update.

### How do you implement authentication middleware?
@short
Read the credential — a bearer token or an httpOnly cookie — verify it, load the minimum identity needed, attach it to `req.user`, and call `next()`. Authentication answers "who are you"; a separate authorisation layer answers "may you do this to this record", and both are needed.
@deep
For JWTs, verification means checking the signature, the expiry, the issuer and audience, and the algorithm — pinning `algorithms: ['HS256']` matters because accepting `alg: none` or allowing an algorithm switch is a well-known bypass.

Stateless JWTs cannot be revoked, so for logout or a compromised session you need either short expiry plus refresh rotation, or a denylist of revoked token ids in Redis with the TTL set to the token's remaining lifetime.
@code ts Authentication plus record-scoped authorisation
export const authenticate: RequestHandler = async (req, res, next) => {
  const token = req.cookies.access ?? req.headers.authorization?.replace(/^Bearer /, '');
  if (!token) return res.status(401).json({ error: 'UNAUTHENTICATED' });
  try {
    const claims = jwt.verify(token, env.JWT_PUBLIC_KEY, {
      algorithms: ['RS256'], issuer: 'api.acme.com', audience: 'acme-web',
    }) as Claims;

    if (await redis.exists(`revoked:${claims.jti}`))     // logout / compromise
      return res.status(401).json({ error: 'TOKEN_REVOKED' });

    req.user = { id: claims.sub, role: claims.role, tenantId: claims.tid };
    next();
  } catch (e) {
    const code = e instanceof jwt.TokenExpiredError ? 'TOKEN_EXPIRED' : 'TOKEN_INVALID';
    res.status(401).json({ error: code });               // distinct code lets the client refresh
  }
};

// Authorisation must be about the RECORD, not just the role
export const canAccessOrder: RequestHandler = async (req, res, next) => {
  const owns = await orderRepo.existsForUser(req.params.id, req.user.id, req.user.tenantId);
  if (!owns && req.user.role !== 'admin') return res.status(404).json({ error: 'NOT_FOUND' });
  next();
};
@end
@senior
The distinct `TOKEN_EXPIRED` versus `TOKEN_INVALID` codes matter operationally: the client should silently refresh on the first and force a re-login on the second, and collapsing them into a generic 401 causes either refresh loops or unnecessary logouts. The other senior point is multi-tenancy — every query must be scoped by `tenantId`, and the safest way is at the data layer (a repository that always applies the filter, or Postgres row-level security) rather than trusting every developer to remember. One missing tenant filter is a cross-customer data leak, which is a company-ending bug.
@followup
Access token in a cookie or in the Authorization header?
@answer
For a browser app I prefer an httpOnly cookie, because JavaScript cannot read it and therefore XSS cannot exfiltrate it — with `SameSite` and a CSRF token to cover the cookie's automatic-send behaviour. For mobile apps and server-to-server calls, the Authorization header is the right choice since there is no CSRF risk and no cookie jar. Many systems do both: cookie for the web client, bearer for the API clients, verified by the same middleware.
@red
- Not pinning the JWT algorithm.
- Treating a role check as sufficient authorisation.

### How do you handle file uploads in Express?
@short
`multer` for multipart parsing, with strict limits on size and count, storage to disk or memory only when necessary, and validation of the real file type from its magic bytes rather than the client-supplied MIME type or extension. For anything sizeable I skip the server entirely and use presigned S3 uploads.
@deep
The risks are: memory exhaustion from buffering large files, disk exhaustion from unbounded uploads, path traversal from using the client's filename, and serving user-uploaded content from your own origin, which turns an uploaded HTML or SVG file into stored XSS against your domain.

The presigned-URL pattern removes most of this: the client uploads directly to S3 with a short-lived signed URL constrained by content type and size, and your API only records the resulting key.
@code ts Presigned upload — the pattern I would defend in an interview
import { S3Client } from '@aws-sdk/client-s3';
import { createPresignedPost } from '@aws-sdk/s3-presigned-post';

app.post('/uploads/sign', authenticate, async (req, res) => {
  const { contentType, sizeBytes } = SignSchema.parse(req.body);
  if (!['image/jpeg', 'image/png', 'application/pdf'].includes(contentType))
