    return res.status(422).json({ error: 'UNSUPPORTED_TYPE' });

  const key = `u/${req.user.id}/${randomUUID()}`;          // never the client's filename
  const post = await createPresignedPost(s3, {
    Bucket: env.UPLOAD_BUCKET, Key: key, Expires: 60,
    Conditions: [['content-length-range', 1, 10 * 1024 * 1024], ['eq', '$Content-Type', contentType]],
    Fields: { 'Content-Type': contentType },
  });
  res.json({ ...post, key });        // client PUTs straight to S3; our server never sees the bytes
});

// If it must pass through the server:
const upload = multer({
  storage: multer.diskStorage({ destination: '/tmp/uploads', filename: () => randomUUID() }),
  limits: { fileSize: 5 * 1024 * 1024, files: 3 },
  fileFilter: (_req, file, cb) => cb(null, ALLOWED.has(file.mimetype)),   // first check only
});
// then verify the real type from magic bytes before doing anything with it
const type = await fileTypeFromFile(req.file.path);
if (!type || !ALLOWED.has(type.mime)) { await fs.unlink(req.file.path); throw new BadRequest('BAD_FILE'); }
@end
@senior
The senior points: serve user content from a separate domain or a CDN with `Content-Disposition: attachment` and a restrictive CSP so an uploaded SVG cannot execute script in your origin; scan for malware if users share files with each other; and always generate your own object key rather than trusting the filename, which kills path traversal and collisions in one move. The presigned approach also means an upload does not occupy a Node worker for 30 seconds, which directly protects your API's latency under load.
@followup
How do you handle a resumable or very large upload?
@answer
S3 multipart upload: the client requests presigned URLs per part, uploads parts in parallel, retries only failed parts, then calls your API to complete the upload. That gives resumability and parallelism without any of it touching your servers. For a browser I would use a library like Uppy over tus or S3 multipart rather than implementing chunk bookkeeping by hand.
@red
- Trusting the client's MIME type.
- Storing uploads under the user-supplied filename.
- Serving uploaded content from the app's own origin.

### What is CORS and how do you configure it correctly?
@short
CORS is a browser mechanism that lets a server declare which other origins may read its responses. It is enforced by the browser, not the server, so it protects users — it is not an access control for your API. Configure an explicit allow-list, and never combine `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` with credentials.
@deep
A "simple" request goes straight through and the browser only blocks the *response* from being read. Anything with a non-simple method, a custom header or a JSON content type triggers a preflight `OPTIONS` request, which the server must answer with the allowed origin, methods and headers.

`Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true` is what allows cookies to be sent cross-origin, and the spec forbids using it with a wildcard origin — you must echo a specific allowed origin, which means validating against a list rather than reflecting whatever the client sent.
@code ts Correct CORS with credentials, plus preflight caching
const allowlist = new Set(env.ALLOWED_ORIGINS.split(','));   // exact origins, from config

app.use(cors({
  origin: (origin, cb) => {
    if (!origin) return cb(null, true);            // curl / server-to-server: no Origin header
    cb(null, allowlist.has(origin));               // ✅ never reflect an arbitrary origin
  },
  credentials: true,                               // required for cookies
  methods: ['GET', 'POST', 'PATCH', 'DELETE'],
  allowedHeaders: ['Content-Type', 'Authorization', 'X-Request-Id', 'Idempotency-Key'],
  exposedHeaders: ['X-Request-Id', 'RateLimit-Remaining'],   // readable by the browser
  maxAge: 86400,                                   // cache preflight for 24h — fewer OPTIONS round trips
}));
@end
@senior
The mistake I have seen cause a real vulnerability is reflecting `req.headers.origin` back with `credentials: true`, which effectively allows every site to make authenticated requests as your users — it looks like it works, and it disables the protection entirely. The second detail worth mentioning is `maxAge`: without it, every cross-origin request pays an extra preflight round trip, which on a high-latency mobile connection is a visible performance cost. And CORS does nothing about CSRF for cookie-authenticated simple requests, so `SameSite` cookies plus a CSRF token are still required.
@followup
Why does my request work in Postman but fail in the browser?
@answer
Because Postman is not a browser and does not enforce CORS — there is no origin, and no preflight. That difference is the clearest demonstration that CORS is a browser-side protection: your API is perfectly reachable by any non-browser client regardless of the headers, which is exactly why CORS must never be treated as authentication or authorisation.
@red
- `origin: true` or reflecting the Origin header with credentials enabled.
- Believing CORS secures the API.

### How do you implement pagination, filtering and sorting?
@short
Offset pagination (`?page=2&limit=20`) is simple and lets you jump to a page, but it degrades on deep offsets and can skip or duplicate rows when data changes. Cursor pagination (`?after=<opaque>`) is stable and fast at any depth because it uses an indexed key predicate instead of `OFFSET`. I default to cursor for feeds and offset for admin tables.
@deep
`OFFSET 100000` makes the database read and discard 100,000 rows, so latency grows linearly with page number. A cursor turns it into `WHERE (created_at, id) < ($1, $2) ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC LIMIT 20`, which uses the index and is constant time.

The tie-breaker column matters: sorting only by `created_at` with duplicate timestamps produces unstable ordering and can skip rows, so the cursor must include a unique column.
@code ts Cursor pagination that is actually stable
const List = z.object({
  limit: z.coerce.number().int().min(1).max(100).default(20),
  after: z.string().optional(),                    // opaque base64 cursor
  status: z.enum(['pending','paid','shipped']).optional(),
});

router.get('/orders', validate({ query: List }), async (req, res) => {
  const { limit, after, status } = req.query;
  const cur = after ? JSON.parse(Buffer.from(after, 'base64url').toString()) : null;

  const { rows } = await pool.query(`
    SELECT id, total, status, created_at
      FROM orders
     WHERE user_id = $1
       AND ($2::text IS NULL OR status = $2)
       AND ($3::timestamptz IS NULL OR (created_at, id) < ($3, $4))
     ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC
     LIMIT $5`,
    [req.user.id, status ?? null, cur?.ts ?? null, cur?.id ?? null, limit + 1]);

  const hasMore = rows.length > limit;
  const page = rows.slice(0, limit);
  const next = hasMore
    ? Buffer.from(JSON.stringify({ ts: page.at(-1).created_at, id: page.at(-1).id })).toString('base64url')
    : null;

  res.json({ data: page, page: { nextCursor: next, hasMore } });
});
-- index that makes this O(log n): CREATE INDEX ON orders (user_id, created_at DESC, id DESC);
@end
@senior
Two things I would add. Sorting must be against an allow-list of columns, never interpolated from the query string, or `?sort=` becomes SQL injection. And I return `hasMore` rather than a total count by default, because `COUNT(*)` over a large filtered set is often the most expensive part of the request — if the product genuinely needs a total, I use an approximate count from `pg_class.reltuples` or a cached count, and I say so explicitly rather than pretending exactness is free.
@followup
When is offset pagination the right choice?
@answer
When the user needs to jump to an arbitrary page — admin tables, report views — and the dataset is small enough or the filters selective enough that deep offsets do not occur. It is also simpler to implement across a UI that shows page numbers. I would still cap the maximum page, because nobody legitimately browses to page 5,000 but a scraper will.
@red
- `LIMIT`/`OFFSET` on an infinite feed.
- Interpolating a sort column from the query string.

### How do you version an API and manage breaking changes?
@short
URL versioning (`/api/v1/...`) is the most explicit and the easiest to route, cache and document, which is why I default to it. The more important discipline is avoiding breaking changes at all: adding fields is safe, removing or renaming them is not, and clients — especially mobile — cannot be forced to upgrade.
@deep
The alternatives are header or media-type versioning, which keep URLs clean but are harder to test, cache and explain. Whatever you choose, you need a deprecation process: announce, add a `Deprecation` and `Sunset` header, monitor usage per version, contact remaining consumers, and only then remove.

Expand-and-contract is the practical technique for evolving without breaking: add the new field alongside the old, migrate consumers, then remove the old one in the next major version.
@code ts Versioning, deprecation signalling and a compatibility shim
app.use('/api/v1', v1Router);
app.use('/api/v2', v2Router);

// v1 kept alive but clearly deprecated — with telemetry so you know who is left
v1Router.use((req, res, next) => {
  res.setHeader('Deprecation', 'true');
  res.setHeader('Sunset', 'Wed, 31 Dec 2026 23:59:59 GMT');
  res.setHeader('Link', '<https://docs.acme.com/migrate-v2>; rel="deprecation"');
  metrics.increment('api.v1.request', { route: req.path, client: req.headers['x-client-id'] });
  next();
});

// Expand-and-contract: emit both shapes during the transition
function toOrderResponse(o, version) {
  const base = { id: o.id, status: o.status, totalMinor: o.total_minor, currency: o.currency };
  return version === 1
    ? { ...base, total: o.total_minor / 100 }      // old float field, kept for v1 clients
    : base;
}
@end
@senior
The senior framing is that versioning is a *cost*, so the goal is to need it rarely. Additive changes, tolerant readers (clients must ignore unknown fields), and never changing the meaning of an existing field cover most evolution. When a breaking change is unavoidable, the telemetry per client id is what makes the sunset possible — without it you are guessing whether anyone still uses v1, and you will keep it forever. For mobile specifically I plan for at least 6–12 months of overlap, because a meaningful share of users simply do not update.
@followup
How do you handle a breaking change in the database that both versions read?
@answer
Expand and contract at the schema level too: add the new column, dual-write to both, backfill the old data, switch reads to the new column, verify, then drop the old one — each step deployed separately and each reversible. The rule is that a schema migration and the code that depends on it never ship in the same deploy, because that combination cannot be rolled back safely.
@red
- Changing a response field's type in place.
- Removing a version with no usage data and no notice.

# SECTION Python & FastAPI | Python is on your CV as a language and FastAPI as a framework, from self-directed pipeline and RAG work rather than production. State that once, then answer at depth — listing a language is an invitation to be tested on it.

### How does Python's GIL affect concurrency, and when does it actually matter?
@short
The GIL means only one thread executes Python bytecode at a time, so threads give you no CPU parallelism. They still give you I/O concurrency, because the lock is released around blocking I/O — so for an API or an ingestion pipeline waiting on an embedding endpoint, threads and asyncio both work. For CPU-bound work you need separate processes, which is what `multiprocessing` and Gunicorn or Uvicorn workers give you.
@deep
The practical consequence is that "add threads" is the wrong instinct for CPU-bound Python and the right one for I/O-bound Python, and knowing which you have is the whole question. Profiling answers it: if the process is at 100% of one core, you are CPU-bound and threads will not help.

The other consequence is deployment shape. A Python API scales by running N worker processes behind a supervisor, one per core, rather than by threading inside one process — which is the same conclusion Node reaches by a completely different route.
@code text Which concurrency model for which workload
  WORKLOAD              GIL IMPACT            USE
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  HTTP request handling  none (I/O releases)   async def + asyncio
  calling an LLM / API   none (I/O releases)   asyncio.gather
  reading files / DB     none (I/O releases)   async, or a thread pool
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  embedding maths        FULL — serialised     multiprocessing / native
  parsing 10k documents  FULL — serialised     ProcessPoolExecutor
  image / video work     FULL — serialised     separate worker service
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  NUMPY / native libs    RELEASED inside C     threads DO parallelise
                         → this is the exception people forget:
                           numpy, pandas and most native extensions
                           drop the GIL in their C code

  DEPLOYMENT   uvicorn --workers $(nproc)   ← processes, not threads
               each worker is a separate interpreter, separate GIL
@end
@senior
The nuance worth volunteering is that the GIL is released inside native extensions, so numpy-heavy work genuinely does parallelise across threads. That is why "Python is slow and single-threaded" is too crude a statement to be useful — the interesting question is always where the time is being spent, and in a data pipeline it is usually inside C code or waiting on the network rather than in the interpreter.

I would also say that in my own pipeline work the bottleneck was never the GIL. It was the embedding API's rate limit, which no amount of local concurrency improves — the correct fix was batching and backoff, not parallelism. Reaching for concurrency before measuring is how you end up with a complicated program that is exactly as slow as the simple one.
@followup
Does free-threaded Python change your answer?
@answer
It changes the ceiling rather than the approach. The free-threaded builds remove the GIL so threads can genuinely parallelise pure-Python CPU work, which is a real shift for workloads that previously had to use processes. I would still not redesign around it today: the ecosystem support for C extensions is still maturing, single-threaded performance carries a cost in some builds, and the process-per-core model is well understood and works everywhere. I would treat it as something to evaluate for a specific CPU-bound service with a benchmark, not as a general default — and I would be honest that I have read about it rather than run it in anger.
@red
- Claiming the GIL makes Python unusable for web services.
- Reaching for threads to speed up CPU-bound Python.
- Not knowing native extensions release the GIL.

### In FastAPI, when do you write `async def` and when plain `def` — and what happens if you get it wrong?
@short
`async def` when the handler awaits I/O with async libraries; plain `def` when the work is blocking or CPU-bound. Getting it wrong in one specific direction is severe: a blocking call inside `async def` stalls the entire event loop, so one slow request freezes every concurrent request in that worker. Plain `def` is safe because FastAPI runs it in a thread pool.
@deep
This is the single most common FastAPI production bug and it is invisible in development, because with one user there is nothing to block. Under concurrency it presents as latency that scales with load in a way that makes no sense until you understand the cause.

The asymmetry is worth internalising: declaring a handler `def` when it could have been async costs you a thread and a little throughput. Declaring it `async def` and then blocking inside it costs you the whole worker.
@code python The bug, and both correct forms
# ✘ WRONG — blocking call inside async def: freezes the event loop
@app.get("/report")
async def report():
    rows = db.execute("SELECT ...")      # blocking driver — STALLS EVERYTHING
    return heavy_pandas_transform(rows)  # CPU-bound — STALLS EVERYTHING

# ✔ CORRECT (a) — plain def: FastAPI runs it in a threadpool automatically
@app.get("/report")
def report():
    rows = db.execute("SELECT ...")      # blocking is fine here
    return heavy_pandas_transform(rows)  # still holds the GIL, but not the loop

# ✔ CORRECT (b) — async all the way down: async driver, awaited
@app.get("/report")
async def report():
    rows = await async_db.fetch("SELECT ...")          # async driver
    return await run_in_threadpool(heavy_transform, rows)  # offload CPU work

# ✔ CORRECT (c) — genuinely concurrent I/O, the reason to use async at all
@app.get("/enrich")
async def enrich(ids: list[str]):
    return await asyncio.gather(*(fetch_one(i) for i in ids))
    # 20 calls at 200ms each: ~200ms concurrently, ~4s sequentially
@end
@senior
The detection story is what I would lead with, because the bug is easy to describe and hard to find. The symptom is that p99 latency degrades under concurrency while CPU sits low and the database is idle — the worker is not busy, it is blocked. `asyncio` debug mode logs callbacks that take too long, and that is usually what identifies it.

The design conclusion I draw is that mixing sync and async database drivers in one codebase is where this bug is born, so I would standardise on one and enforce it — an async endpoint that imports the sync client is a lint rule, not a code-review conversation. It is the same class of discipline as never calling a synchronous file read in a Node request handler, and it is worth being explicit that Node has exactly the same failure mode with `fs.readFileSync` or a long synchronous loop.
@followup
How does this compare with the equivalent mistake in Node?
@answer
It is the same mistake with the same mechanism — one event loop, one blocking call, everyone waits. The difference is that Node's ecosystem is async by default, so it is genuinely hard to accidentally block: nearly every library returns a promise, and the synchronous variants are named `...Sync` as a warning. Python's ecosystem is predominantly synchronous, so the blocking version of any library is usually the default one and the async version is the exception you have to seek out. That makes the mistake far easier to make in FastAPI than in Express, which is why the `def` versus `async def` choice deserves a review checklist item rather than trust.
@red
- Making every handler `async def` because it looks modern.
- Using a synchronous database driver inside `async def`.
- CPU-bound work in an async handler with no threadpool offload.

### What does Pydantic actually give you that hand-written validation does not?
@short
One declaration that is simultaneously the runtime validator, the static type, the parsing/coercion layer and the OpenAPI schema. In Express I would write a Zod or Joi schema and a TypeScript interface and keep them in sync by discipline; in FastAPI the model is the single source, so the request body cannot be validated against one shape and typed as another.
@deep
The property that matters most is parse-don't-validate: the handler receives a typed object that is already guaranteed valid, rather than a raw dict it must check. Invalid input never reaches business logic, so there is no defensive checking scattered through the code.

The generated OpenAPI document is a genuine operational benefit rather than a nicety, because it cannot drift from the implementation — it is derived from the same models the server enforces.
@code python One model, four jobs
class CreateOrder(BaseModel):
    model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid")   # reject unknown fields

    customer_id: UUID                            # parsed AND validated
    items: Annotated[list[Item], Field(min_length=1)]
    currency: Literal["INR", "USD"]              # enum in the OpenAPI schema
    coupon: str | None = None

    @field_validator("coupon")
    @classmethod
    def normalise(cls, v: str | None) -> str | None:
        return v.strip().upper() if v else None  # coercion lives WITH the type

@app.post("/orders", response_model=OrderOut, status_code=201)
async def create(order: CreateOrder) -> OrderOut:
    # order.customer_id is a UUID object, not a string.
    # items is non-empty. currency is one of two values. Guaranteed.
    ...

  THAT ONE CLASS PRODUCES:
   1  422 with a field-level error list, before your code runs
   2  a real static type for the editor and the type checker
   3  coercion: "2026-01-04" → date, "  save10 " → "SAVE10"
   4  the OpenAPI/JSON-Schema entry that documents the endpoint

  extra="forbid" IS THE ONE I ARGUE FOR:
    default behaviour silently DROPS unknown fields, so a client sending
    {"discount": 50} to an endpoint with no discount field gets a 201 and
    no discount. Forbidding turns a silent no-op into a 422.
@end
@senior
The setting I would defend explicitly is `extra="forbid"`. Silently ignoring unknown fields is the default in most validation stacks and it produces the worst kind of integration bug — the client believes it sent something meaningful, the server returns success, and nobody discovers the mismatch until a reconciliation report disagrees. Failing loudly on an unexpected field converts that into a clear error at the boundary.

The trade-off to be honest about is that strict rejection makes API evolution harder, because adding a field to a client before the server knows about it now breaks. That is a versioning discipline question rather than a reason to be permissive, and I would rather have the conversation than have the silent drop.
@followup
Where does Pydantic cost you?
@answer
Validation is not free, and on a hot path parsing large nested payloads is measurable — Pydantic v2 moved the core to Rust and is roughly an order of magnitude faster than v1, which changed this from a real concern to a minor one, but it is not zero. The place I would actually be careful is using the same model for the API boundary and the database layer: it couples your public contract to your storage schema, so a column rename becomes a breaking API change. I keep request, response and persistence models separate even though it looks like duplication, because they change for different reasons and at different times.
@red
- Treating Pydantic as only a validator and re-checking fields inside the handler.
- Leaving the default that silently drops unknown fields.
- One model shared across the API contract and the database schema.

### How would you structure a Python ingestion pipeline so a failure halfway through is recoverable?
@short
As idempotent stages with checkpointed state, not as one long script. Each document gets a content hash, each stage records what it has completed, and re-running skips finished work rather than redoing it. That way a crash at document 8,000 of 10,000 resumes rather than restarting, and re-ingesting an unchanged corpus costs almost nothing.
@deep
The property that makes this work is that the unit of work is a document and its content hash, not a position in a loop. Position-based resumption breaks the moment the input ordering changes; hash-based resumption is stable under reordering, re-runs and partial corpus updates.

The expensive stage — embedding — is the one that must never be repeated unnecessarily, because it costs money and rate limit. Caching embeddings by chunk hash means a document whose formatting changed but whose text did not is effectively free.
@code text Stages, checkpoints and the idempotency key
  discover ──► extract ──► chunk ──► embed ──► upsert
     │           │           │         │          │
     │           │           │         │          └─ ON CONFLICT (chunk_hash)
     │           │           │         │             DO UPDATE  ← idempotent
     │           │           │         └─ cache: chunk_hash → vector
     │           │           │            (the only stage that costs money)
     │           │           └─ deterministic: same text → same chunk hashes
     │           └─ per-document try/except → dead-letter, never abort the run
     └─ source listing + content hash

  documents(id, source_uri, content_hash, stage, updated_at)
    stage ∈ discovered | extracted | chunked | embedded | indexed | failed

  RESUME   WHERE stage != 'indexed' AND content_hash = <current>
           → a re-run does the remaining work and nothing else

  BATCHING  embed in batches of 96, bounded concurrency (semaphore, ~4),
            exponential backoff with jitter on 429
            ← the real bottleneck is the provider's rate limit,
              not the GIL and not your CPU

  ONE POISON DOCUMENT MUST NOT KILL THE RUN:
    a 400-page scanned PDF that OCRs to garbage → mark failed, record the
    error, continue. Then a report of failures, which is a work queue
    rather than a stack trace at 3am.
@end
@senior
The decision I would defend is doing the transactional swap at the end rather than deleting and rebuilding in place. If you delete the old vectors and the run fails, search is broken until you fix it — so I write to a new version and switch a pointer, which makes rollback instant and keeps the index queryable throughout.

The second point is that this design is not really about Python. The same reasoning applies to the Node sync backend I built for offline geolocation: idempotency keys, per-item failure isolation, and resumability rather than restartability. That is the transferable idea, and I would rather present it as a general pattern I have applied twice than as a Python trick.
@followup
How do you know the pipeline is producing good output, not just completing?
@answer
Completion metrics and quality metrics are different things and I track both. Completion is documents processed, failed and skipped, plus a failure report. Quality is measured against a golden set of questions with known correct source documents — recall@k after every ingestion change, so a chunking tweak that quietly reduces retrieval quality is caught at the pipeline rather than discovered as "the assistant got worse". Without that, a run that finishes cleanly and indexes garbage looks exactly like success.
@red
- One long script with no checkpoint.
- Resuming by loop index rather than by content hash.
- Re-embedding unchanged content on every run.
- One malformed document aborting the whole run.

### How do you manage Python dependencies and ship a Python service?
@short
A lockfile, a virtual environment, and a multi-stage container — the same discipline as `package-lock.json` and a Node image. `pyproject.toml` declares direct dependencies, the lockfile pins the full resolved tree with hashes, and the image installs from the lock so the build is reproducible. Then Uvicorn workers behind a process supervisor, which is the Python equivalent of PM2 in cluster mode.
@deep
The failure this prevents is the classic "works on my machine": a loose `requirements.txt` with unpinned transitive dependencies resolves differently on different days, so a build that succeeded last week installs a new minor version today and breaks.

Layer ordering in the Dockerfile matters for build time in exactly the way it does for Node — dependencies are copied and installed before application code, so a code change does not invalidate the dependency layer.
@code text The Python equivalents of a stack you already run
  NODE                          PYTHON
  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  package.json                  pyproject.toml
  package-lock.json             uv.lock / poetry.lock / requirements.txt
                                  compiled with hashes
  npm ci                        uv sync --frozen  /  pip install --require-hashes
  node_modules/                 .venv/
  PM2 cluster mode              uvicorn --workers N   (or gunicorn -k uvicorn)
  nodemon                       uvicorn --reload
  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  # multi-stage: build deps, then a slim runtime
  FROM python:3.12-slim AS deps
  COPY pyproject.toml uv.lock ./
  RUN uv sync --frozen --no-dev          ← cached until the lock changes

  FROM python:3.12-slim
  COPY --from=deps /app/.venv /app/.venv
  COPY . .                                ← code changes invalidate only this
  ENV PATH="/app/.venv/bin:$PATH"
  USER app                                ← never run as root
  CMD ["uvicorn","app.main:app","--host","0.0.0.0","--workers","4"]

  WORKERS   one process per core; each has its own interpreter and GIL.
            Behind Nginx exactly like the Node services — same reverse
            proxy, same TLS termination, same health check.
@end
@senior
The point I would make is that this is deliberately the same operational shape as the Node services I run in production — containerised, reverse-proxied by Nginx, process-supervised, health-checked, logging structured JSON to the same place. Introducing a second language should not mean introducing a second way of deploying, monitoring and rolling back, because that operational divergence is the actual cost of polyglot stacks and it is usually underestimated.

I would also flag the one genuine difference: Python images are large if you are careless, because scientific dependencies pull in a lot. Multi-stage builds and slim base images matter more here than in Node, and it is worth checking the image size in CI rather than discovering a 2GB image at deploy time.
@followup
Where do you put the model or embedding files?
@answer
Not in the image, in almost every case. Baking a large model file into the container makes every build slow and every rollback expensive, and it couples model updates to code deploys. I would pull it at startup from object storage with the version pinned by an environment variable, cache it on a mounted volume so restarts are fast, and verify a checksum before use. That keeps the image small and lets me change the model version without rebuilding, which is the same argument as not baking configuration into an image.
@red
- An unpinned requirements.txt.
- Installing dependencies after copying application code.
- Running the container as root.
- One process and no workers, then wondering why throughput is flat.

### Python type hints versus TypeScript — how much do they actually give you?
@short
Less, and differently. Both are erased at runtime and both need a separate checker, but TypeScript's checker is mandatory in the build while Python's is optional and bolted on, so an unchecked Python codebase can carry annotations that are simply wrong. The counter-balance is Pydantic: where TypeScript gives you nothing at runtime, Pydantic uses the same annotations to enforce the type at the boundary, which is the one place it genuinely matters more.
@deep
The practical difference is enforcement. TypeScript will not emit if you configure it not to, and CI fails; `mypy` or `pyright` has to be added deliberately, and a codebase without it gets annotations that decay into documentation.

Gradual typing is also more genuinely gradual in Python. Any unannotated function is invisible to the checker, so partial adoption is easy — and easy to stall halfway, which is the risk.
@code text What each gives you, honestly
                        TYPESCRIPT              PYTHON
  runtime enforcement   none                    none (annotations erased)
                        + Zod at boundaries     + PYDANTIC at boundaries ← real
  checker               tsc, in the build       mypy/pyright, opt-in
  strictness default    strict is a flag,       everything Any until you
                        but conventionally on   turn on strict settings
  ecosystem types       DefinitelyTyped, broad  py.typed, patchier
  null safety           strictNullChecks        `X | None` + a strict checker
  generics              structural, powerful    nominal-ish, improving fast

  THE POINT I MAKE IN AN INTERVIEW
   Neither language checks types at runtime. The difference between a
   codebase where types help and one where they lie is whether the
   checker runs in CI — that is a process decision, not a language one.
   I put `mypy --strict` on new modules and leave legacy ones excluded,
   which is exactly the incremental strategy I used migrating JS to TS.
@end
@senior
The connection I would draw is to the migration on my CV. Moving a JavaScript codebase to TypeScript taught me that the value arrives only when the checker is enforced and the strictness ratchets — allowing `any` freely produces a codebase that looks typed and catches nothing. Python is the same shape of problem: annotations without `mypy` in CI are comments.

Where I think Python is genuinely ahead is the boundary. Pydantic means the annotation on a request model is enforced at runtime with no extra declaration, whereas in TypeScript I must write a Zod schema alongside the interface and keep them aligned. So my honest summary is that TypeScript is stronger inside the codebase and Python is stronger at the edge of it.
@followup
Would you turn on strict typing for an existing untyped Python codebase?
@answer
Not globally on day one, because a large error count gets suppressed rather than fixed and the exercise loses credibility. I would enable the checker in CI with strictness scoped to new and touched modules, so the boundary of typed code expands with normal work, and put the strict settings in configuration per-package so the ratchet is visible and reviewable. That is the same approach that worked on the JavaScript to TypeScript migration, and the reason it works is that it never asks anyone to stop delivering features in order to adopt types.
@red
- Claiming Python type hints are enforced at runtime.
- Annotations with no checker in CI.
- A big-bang strict-mode conversion.

### Would you build a new service in FastAPI or Express, and why?
@short
Whichever the work is actually made of. If the service is primarily calling models, embedding, or doing anything numerical or ML-adjacent, FastAPI — because the libraries are there and rewriting them in Node is not engineering, it is stubbornness. If it is a general web API in a stack that is already TypeScript, Express or Node, because sharing types and tooling with the clients is worth more than any framework difference. I would not make the choice on framework merit; both are fine.
@deep
The real cost of adding a second language is operational rather than syntactic: another build, another dependency scanner, another runtime to patch, another set of idioms for whoever is on call. That cost is fixed and recurring, so it needs a proportionate reason.

The reason that usually justifies it is library gravity. The Python data and ML ecosystem has no equivalent in Node, so a pipeline that needs it should be Python and should talk to the rest of the system over an API or a queue.
@code text The decision, and the shape it produces
  CHOOSE PYTHON / FASTAPI WHEN
   • the work is embeddings, ML, scientific or data-heavy
   • the libraries you need only exist there
   • the team's strength is Python

  CHOOSE NODE / EXPRESS WHEN
   • the clients are TypeScript and you want ONE contract type
   • the work is I/O orchestration, webhooks, real-time
   • you already operate Node and adding a language buys nothing

  THE SHAPE I ACTUALLY BUILT (AI assistant project)
    Next.js client
        │
        ▼
    Node/TypeScript API  ← auth, sessions, streaming, tool calling,
        │   ▲               shares types with the client
        │   │
        ▼   │ (async, via the database / queue — NOT in the request path)
    Python ingestion  ← chunking, embedding, pgvector upsert
        │
        ▼
    PostgreSQL + pgvector   ← the integration point is the DATA,
                              not an RPC call between the two services

  WHY THAT SEAM   ingestion is a batch job with a different scaling
                  profile and different failure semantics from a request
                  handler. Splitting on that boundary is not a language
                  decision that happens to work — it is the right seam,
                  and the language follows it.
@end
@senior
The position I would defend is that the seam should be chosen for operational reasons first and the language assigned second. In the assistant project the ingestion pipeline is a batch workload that can fail, retry and take an hour; the API is a low-latency request handler. Those belong in separate deployables regardless of language, and once they are separate, the pipeline being Python costs almost nothing because the integration point is Postgres rather than a synchronous call.

I would also say plainly that if the whole thing were Node, that would have been a reasonable outcome too — I would have used a hosted embedding API and skipped the Python service entirely. Choosing the polyglot version was partly to work properly in Python on something real, and I would not dress that up as a purely technical necessity.
@followup
Does that not mean two services to deploy for a small project?
@answer
It does, and for a small project that is a real cost I would weigh rather than wave away. The mitigation is that they are not two services in the demanding sense — the pipeline is a scheduled job, not a highly available API, so it has no uptime requirement, no load balancer and no autoscaling. It runs in the same Docker Compose stack, ships logs to the same place and is deployed by the same pipeline. If it had needed to be a synchronous dependency of the request path I would have thought much harder, because that turns a batch job into a component whose availability I now have to care about.
@red
- Choosing a framework on benchmarks rather than on the work.
- Adding a second language without acknowledging the operational cost.
- Putting a batch pipeline in the synchronous request path.

# SECTION REST API Design | Designing the contract — the part that outlives your implementation.

### What makes an API RESTful?
@short
REST models the system as resources identified by URLs, manipulated with the standard HTTP verbs, using a uniform interface and stateless requests. In practice "RESTful" means: nouns not verbs in paths, correct methods and status codes, statelessness, and cacheability. Most real APIs are pragmatic REST rather than academically pure.
@deep
Roy Fielding's constraints are client-server, statelessness, cacheability, a layered system, a uniform interface, and optionally code-on-demand. Statelessness is the one with real architectural consequences: no server-side session affinity means any instance can serve any request, which is what makes horizontal scaling and rolling deploys simple.

The Richardson Maturity Model is a useful vocabulary — level 0 is a single endpoint tunnelling RPC, level 1 introduces resources, level 2 uses verbs and status codes correctly, and level 3 adds hypermedia. Most production APIs live at level 2, and that is a defensible engineering choice rather than a failure.
@code http Resource-oriented design — verbs in the method, not the path
GET    /api/v1/orders?status=paid&limit=20     200  list, filterable, paginated
POST   /api/v1/orders                          201 + Location: /api/v1/orders/o_9
GET    /api/v1/orders/o_9                      200  single resource
PATCH  /api/v1/orders/o_9                      200  partial update
DELETE /api/v1/orders/o_9                      204  no body
GET    /api/v1/orders/o_9/items                200  sub-resource

# Actions that are not CRUD: model them as sub-resources, not verbs in the path
POST   /api/v1/orders/o_9/refunds              201  ← creates a refund resource
POST   /api/v1/orders/o_9/cancellation         201
# ❌ POST /api/v1/cancelOrder?id=o_9
@end
@senior
The senior nuance is knowing when to stop being pure. Non-CRUD operations — "retry this payment", "recalculate this invoice" — are genuinely awkward as resources, and inventing a `POST /orders/{id}/retries` resource is usually better than a verb path because it gives the action an id, a status and a history you can query. But if the modelling gymnastics obscure the intent, a clear verb endpoint is better than a confusing noun. I would rather defend a pragmatic, consistent, well-documented API than a doctrinally pure one nobody can use.
@followup
Is REST always the right choice?
@answer
No. GraphQL fits clients with widely varying data needs — a mobile app that wants three fields and a dashboard that wants thirty — because it eliminates over-fetching and request waterfalls, at the cost of caching complexity and query-depth abuse risk. gRPC fits internal service-to-service traffic where you want binary efficiency, streaming and generated clients. REST is the right default for public APIs and web clients because it is cacheable at the HTTP layer, debuggable with curl, and every client already understands it.
@red
- Verbs in paths (`/getUser`, `/createOrder`).
- Calling an RPC-over-POST API "RESTful".

### What is idempotency and which HTTP methods are idempotent?
@short
An idempotent operation produces the same server state whether it is applied once or many times. `GET`, `PUT`, `DELETE` and `HEAD` are idempotent by specification; `POST` is not, which is why any `POST` that moves money needs an explicit idempotency key. Note that idempotent is not the same as safe — `DELETE` changes state, it just does not change it further on repeat.
@deep
The reason this matters is that networks fail after the server has acted. A client sends `POST /payments`, the server charges the card, the response is lost, the client retries — and without protection you have charged twice.

The standard solution is a client-generated `Idempotency-Key` header. The server records the key with the request fingerprint and the response, inside the same transaction as the work. A repeat with the same key returns the stored response without re-executing; a repeat with the same key but a different body is a client error.
@code ts Idempotent POST — the pattern every payment interviewer wants to hear
CREATE TABLE idempotency_keys (
  key            text PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id        uuid NOT NULL,
  request_hash   text NOT NULL,
  status         text NOT NULL,            -- in_progress | completed
  response_code  int,
  response_body  jsonb,
  created_at     timestamptz DEFAULT now()
);

app.post('/payments', authenticate, async (req, res) => {
  const key = req.header('Idempotency-Key');
  if (!key) return res.status(400).json({ error: 'IDEMPOTENCY_KEY_REQUIRED' });
  const hash = sha256(JSON.stringify(req.body));

  const result = await withTransaction(async (tx) => {
    // atomic claim — the unique PK is what makes concurrent duplicates safe
    const claimed = await tx.query(
      `INSERT INTO idempotency_keys (key, user_id, request_hash, status)
       VALUES ($1,$2,$3,'in_progress') ON CONFLICT (key) DO NOTHING RETURNING key`,
      [key, req.user.id, hash]);

    if (!claimed.rowCount) {
      const prior = await tx.query('SELECT * FROM idempotency_keys WHERE key = $1', [key]);
      if (prior.rows[0].request_hash !== hash) throw new Conflict('KEY_REUSED_WITH_DIFFERENT_BODY');
      if (prior.rows[0].status === 'in_progress') throw new Conflict('REQUEST_IN_PROGRESS'); // 409, client retries
      return { code: prior.rows[0].response_code, body: prior.rows[0].response_body };        // replay
    }

    const payment = await charge(req.user, req.body);          // the real work
    await tx.query(`UPDATE idempotency_keys SET status='completed', response_code=201,
                    response_body=$2 WHERE key=$1`, [key, payment]);
    return { code: 201, body: payment };
  });

  res.status(result.code).json(result.body);
});
@end
@senior
Three senior details. First, storing the request hash catches a client that reuses a key for a different payload — a genuine bug you want to surface loudly rather than silently return the wrong response. Second, the `in_progress` state handles concurrent duplicates: two simultaneous retries, one wins the insert and the other gets a 409 telling it to retry shortly. Third, the key record must be written in the same transaction as the effect, or a crash between them reintroduces the double-charge. And keys need a TTL — 24 hours is the industry norm — with a cleanup job, or the table grows forever.
@followup
Is `PUT` really idempotent if it has side effects like sending an email?
@answer
Idempotency is defined over server state, not over observable side effects, so strictly the method contract is about the resource. In practice, sending an email twice is a real product bug, so I treat any externally visible side effect as needing its own deduplication — usually by making the side effect flow through an outbox with a unique event id, so the consumer dedupes even if the handler runs twice. That is the same reasoning as at-least-once message delivery: assume duplicates, make the consumer idempotent.
@red
- Believing a retry is safe because "it's just a POST".
- Storing the idempotency record outside the transaction.

### How do you choose the right HTTP status code?
@short
2xx for success — 200 with a body, 201 with a `Location` for creation, 202 for accepted-but-async, 204 for no content. 4xx means the client must change something: 400 malformed, 401 not authenticated, 403 authenticated but not permitted, 404 not found, 409 conflict, 422 semantically invalid, 429 rate limited. 5xx means we broke.
@deep
The distinctions that matter operationally: 401 versus 403 tells the client whether to refresh a token or give up; 409 versus 422 tells it whether retrying later might succeed; and 429 with `Retry-After` tells it exactly when. Getting these right is what lets clients implement correct retry behaviour without guessing.

The most damaging mistake is returning 200 with `{ "error": ... }` in the body. Every layer between you and the client — load balancers, CDNs, monitoring, retry libraries — reads the status code, so a 200 error is invisible to your alerting and gets cached.
@code ts Status codes carrying real semantics
if (!token)                  return res.status(401).json({ error: 'UNAUTHENTICATED' });
if (!canRefund)              return res.status(403).json({ error: 'FORBIDDEN' });
if (!order)                  return res.status(404).json({ error: 'NOT_FOUND' });
if (order.status === 'refunded')
                             return res.status(409).json({ error: 'ALREADY_REFUNDED' });
if (amount > order.total)    return res.status(422).json({ error: 'AMOUNT_EXCEEDS_TOTAL' });

// Rate limited — tell the client exactly when to come back
res.status(429).set('Retry-After', '30').json({ error: 'RATE_LIMITED' });

// Long-running work: accept it, hand back a status URL
res.status(202).location(`/api/v1/jobs/${job.id}`).json({ jobId: job.id, status: 'queued' });

// Created: always include Location
res.status(201).location(`/api/v1/orders/${order.id}`).json(order);
@end
@senior
Two nuances worth raising. Returning 404 instead of 403 for a record that exists but is not yours prevents enumeration — the attacker cannot distinguish "does not exist" from "not yours". And 202 with a job resource is the honest answer for anything slow: a synchronous endpoint that takes 40 seconds will be killed by an ALB idle timeout, a mobile radio timeout, or a user's patience, and returning 202 plus a pollable or webhook-notified job is both more reliable and more scalable.
@followup
When do you use 400 versus 422?
@answer
400 for a request the server cannot parse or that violates the protocol — malformed JSON, a missing required header. 422 for a syntactically valid request whose content fails business or schema validation — a well-formed body with an invalid email or a negative quantity. The distinction helps clients: a 400 usually means a client bug, a 422 usually means user input to correct. Some teams use 400 for both, which is acceptable if it is consistent and documented — consistency matters more than the exact choice.
@red
- 200 with an error payload.
- Using 500 for validation failures.

### What does a good API error response look like?
@short
A stable, machine-readable shape: a code the client can branch on, a human message for developers, field-level details for validation, and a request id for support. Never a raw stack trace or database message, and never a message that changes wording between releases while clients parse it.
@deep
Clients need to distinguish "retry this", "fix the input", and "give up". A string message cannot express that reliably, so the `code` is the contract and the `message` is documentation. RFC 9457 (`application/problem+json`) is a reasonable standard to adopt if you want an off-the-shelf shape.

Field-level errors matter for forms: returning the first error only means the user fixes one field per round trip, which is a poor experience.
@code json A consistent error contract across every endpoint
// 422 — validation
{
  "error": {
    "code": "VALIDATION_FAILED",
    "message": "One or more fields are invalid.",
    "fields": {
      "email":  ["must be a valid email address"],
      "amount": ["must be greater than 0", "must be at most 100000"]
    },
    "requestId": "req_01HXYZ...",
    "docs": "https://docs.acme.com/errors/VALIDATION_FAILED"
  }
}

// 409 — conflict, with the data the client needs to recover
{ "error": { "code": "ALREADY_REFUNDED", "message": "This order was already refunded.",
             "refundId": "rf_88", "requestId": "req_01HXZA..." } }

// 500 — never leak internals
{ "error": { "code": "INTERNAL", "message": "Something went wrong.", "requestId": "req_01HXZB..." } }
@end
@senior
The request id is the piece that pays for itself. It goes in the response body and the `X-Request-Id` header, it is in every log line for that request, and it propagates to downstream services — so a user pastes it into a support ticket and you find the exact trace in seconds instead of grepping by timestamp. I would also mention that error codes are part of your public contract: once a client branches on `INSUFFICIENT_FUNDS`, renaming it is a breaking change, so they get the same versioning discipline as fields.
@followup
Should the error message be shown to end users?
@answer
No, not directly. The API message is for developers; the UI should map error codes to user-appropriate copy, localised and phrased for the audience. That separation also means you can improve internal messages without changing user-facing text, and it prevents technical detail — table names, provider errors — from reaching a user's screen or a screenshot in a public forum.
@red
- Returning `err.message` from the ORM to the client.
- Inconsistent error shapes across endpoints.

### How do you document an API?
@short
OpenAPI generated from the same schemas that validate requests, so the docs cannot drift. That gives you an interactive explorer, generated typed clients, and contract tests. Documentation that is written separately from the code is always out of date within a month.
@deep
The workflow I prefer is code-first with generation: Zod schemas define validation, `zod-to-openapi` produces the spec, and the spec produces the client SDK and the docs site. The alternative is spec-first — write OpenAPI, generate server stubs and types — which suits organisations where the contract is negotiated between teams before implementation.

Either way, the spec becomes testable: contract tests assert that responses actually match the declared schemas, which catches drift that unit tests miss.
@code ts One schema → validation, types, docs and client SDK
import { extendZodWithOpenApi, OpenAPIRegistry } from '@asteasolutions/zod-to-openapi';
extendZodWithOpenApi(z);
const registry = new OpenAPIRegistry();

const Order = z.object({
  id: z.string().uuid().openapi({ example: 'o_01HX...' }),
  totalMinor: z.number().int().openapi({ description: 'Total in the smallest currency unit' }),
  currency: z.enum(['INR', 'USD']),
  status: z.enum(['pending', 'paid', 'refunded']),
}).openapi('Order');

registry.registerPath({
  method: 'get', path: '/orders/{id}', tags: ['Orders'],
  security: [{ bearerAuth: [] }],
  request: { params: z.object({ id: z.string().uuid() }) },
  responses: {
    200: { description: 'The order', content: { 'application/json': { schema: Order } } },
    404: { description: 'Not found', content: { 'application/json': { schema: ApiError } } },
  },
});

// npx openapi-typescript openapi.json -o src/api-types.ts   → typed client, zero duplication
@end
@senior
The extra step I would advocate is contract testing in CI: replay recorded responses against the spec, or use Schemathesis/Dredd to fuzz every documented endpoint and assert that the responses validate. It catches the classic drift where someone adds a field to the database, returns it, and forgets the docs — harmless — versus removes a field and breaks every client — not harmless. For a public API I would also publish a changelog and version the spec itself, because consumers build against it.
@followup
GraphQL is self-documenting — is that an argument to use it?
@answer
Partly, and it is a genuine advantage: the schema is introspectable, and tools like GraphiQL give you an explorer for free. But it introduces problems REST does not have: HTTP caching largely stops working because everything is a POST to one endpoint, query depth and complexity need limiting to prevent abuse, and the N+1 problem moves into your resolvers and needs DataLoader. I would choose GraphQL for the client-flexibility benefit, not for documentation, since OpenAPI closes that gap.
@red
- Hand-maintained Postman collections as the only documentation.
- A spec that no test validates.

### How do you cache HTTP responses?
@short
With `Cache-Control` for freshness and `ETag`/`Last-Modified` for revalidation. Public, immutable assets get a long `max-age` with `immutable`; personalised responses get `private` or `no-store`; and semi-fresh data gets a short `max-age` with `stale-while-revalidate` so users get an instant response while the cache refreshes in the background.
@deep
`ETag` enables conditional requests: the client sends `If-None-Match`, and if the resource has not changed the server responds `304 Not Modified` with no body, saving bandwidth but not the round trip. `Cache-Control: max-age` avoids the round trip entirely.

The critical safety rule is `private` versus `public`. A shared cache — a CDN or a corporate proxy — that stores a personalised response with `public` will serve one user's data to another. That is a genuine, repeated production incident class.
@code http The four cases you need to get right
# 1. Content-hashed static asset — never revalidate
Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable

# 2. Public, semi-fresh API data — instant response, background refresh
Cache-Control: public, max-age=60, stale-while-revalidate=600
ETag: "W/\"a1b2c3\""

# 3. Personalised response — must never be stored by a shared cache
Cache-Control: private, no-cache
Vary: Authorization, Accept-Encoding

# 4. Anything sensitive
Cache-Control: no-store

# Conditional request → 304, no body
GET /api/v1/orders/o_9   If-None-Match: "a1b2c3"
HTTP/1.1 304 Not Modified
@end
@senior
`Vary: Authorization` is the header people forget, and its absence is exactly how a CDN serves user A's response to user B. I treat `private` plus a correct `Vary` as mandatory on anything authenticated. On the invalidation side, I prefer cache keys that include a version or content hash over trying to purge, because purging is eventually consistent across a CDN's edge nodes and "we purged but some POPs still served the old thing" is a miserable incident. `stale-while-revalidate` is the highest-value directive most teams are not using — it converts a slow cache miss into a fast stale hit plus a background refresh.
@followup
How does this interact with a CDN in front of your API?
@answer
The CDN honours the same headers, so `public, max-age` lets it serve responses without touching your origin — which is how you absorb a traffic spike on a product listing endpoint. The rules are: never mark authenticated responses public, set `Vary` correctly, use `s-maxage` when you want a different TTL at the shared cache than in the browser, and use surrogate keys or cache tags so you can purge precisely when the underlying data changes.
@red
- Caching an authenticated response as `public`.
- No `Vary` header on content that differs by header.

### How do you rate limit an API?
@short
Count requests per key — user id, API key, IP, or a combination — in a shared store like Redis so the limit holds across instances, and respond with 429 plus `Retry-After` and `RateLimit-*` headers. Different endpoints get different budgets: login and OTP are far stricter than a read endpoint.
@deep
The algorithms in increasing sophistication: fixed window (simple, but allows a double burst at the boundary), sliding window log (accurate, more memory), sliding window counter (a good approximation), and token bucket (allows controlled bursts, which is usually the best user experience).

Implementation must be atomic. A read-then-write from Node is racy across concurrent requests, so the increment and expiry belong in a single Lua script or a Redis pipeline.
@code lua Atomic token bucket in Redis — burst-tolerant and race-free
-- KEYS[1]=bucket  ARGV: capacity, refillPerSec, nowMs, cost
local b = redis.call('HMGET', KEYS[1], 'tokens', 'ts')
local cap, rate, now, cost = tonumber(ARGV[1]), tonumber(ARGV[2]), tonumber(ARGV[3]), tonumber(ARGV[4])
local tokens = tonumber(b[1]) or cap
local ts     = tonumber(b[2]) or now
tokens = math.min(cap, tokens + ((now - ts) / 1000) * rate)   -- refill by elapsed time
if tokens < cost then
  redis.call('HMSET', KEYS[1], 'tokens', tokens, 'ts', now)
  redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], math.ceil(cap / rate * 1000))
  return {0, math.ceil((cost - tokens) / rate)}               -- denied + Retry-After seconds
end
redis.call('HMSET', KEYS[1], 'tokens', tokens - cost, 'ts', now)
redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], math.ceil(cap / rate * 1000))
return {1, 0}
@end
@code ts Applying different budgets per endpoint class
const limits = {
  'auth:login':  { capacity: 5,   refillPerSec: 0.05 },   // 5 burst, ~3/min sustained
  'auth:otp':    { capacity: 3,   refillPerSec: 0.01 },
  'api:read':    { capacity: 100, refillPerSec: 20 },
  'ai:generate': { capacity: 5,   refillPerSec: 0.2, cost: 5 },   // expensive → costs more tokens
};

res.set({
  'RateLimit-Limit': cap, 'RateLimit-Remaining': remaining,
  'RateLimit-Reset': resetSeconds, ...(allowed ? {} : { 'Retry-After': retryAfter }),
});
@end
@senior
The senior points are the failure policy and the key choice. If Redis is down, do you fail open or closed? For a login endpoint I fail open and rely on other controls, because locking every user out is worse than the attack; for an expensive LLM endpoint I fail closed, because the cost of an unbounded spike is real money. On keys, IP-only limits punish shared NATs and are trivially evaded by a botnet, so I key on the authenticated user where possible and combine IP with the target account on login so credential stuffing against one account is throttled regardless of source. And for a paid API I return the limit headers on *every* response, not just 429s, so clients can self-pace.
@followup
Where should rate limiting live — the app, the gateway, or the CDN?
@answer
Layered. Coarse volumetric limits belong at the edge (CloudFront, WAF, API Gateway) because they absorb attacks before they cost you compute. Fine-grained, business-aware limits — per user, per plan, per endpoint cost — belong in the application, because only it knows the plan and the semantic cost. Relying only on the app means an attack still consumes your servers; relying only on the edge means you cannot express "free tier gets 100 AI generations a month".
@red
- In-memory rate limiting with multiple instances.
- Non-atomic increment-then-check.

### How do you design an API for mobile clients specifically?
@short
Assume high latency, unreliable connectivity, and clients you cannot force to upgrade. That means fewer, coarser endpoints to avoid round trips, small payloads, cursor pagination, explicit versioning with long support windows, idempotent writes, and clear cache and sync semantics.
@deep
The round-trip cost dominates on mobile: five sequential requests on a 200ms RTT connection is a full second before anything renders. So mobile-facing endpoints often aggregate — a `/home` endpoint returning everything the first screen needs — even though that is less "pure" REST. This is the backend-for-frontend pattern.

Payload size matters for both speed and the user's data plan: return only needed fields, support sparse fieldsets, compress, and prefer integers in minor units over formatted strings.
@code ts A BFF endpoint plus delta sync — the two mobile-specific patterns
// 1. One round trip for the whole home screen, fetched in parallel server-side
app.get('/api/v1/mobile/home', authenticate, async (req, res) => {
  const [profile, trips, notices, flags] = await Promise.all([
    userSvc.summary(req.user.id),
    tripSvc.recent(req.user.id, 5),
    noticeSvc.unread(req.user.id, 3),
    flagSvc.forUser(req.user.id),
  ]);
  res.set('Cache-Control', 'private, max-age=30').json({ profile, trips, notices, flags });
});

// 2. Delta sync: the client sends what it last saw, the server sends only changes
app.get('/api/v1/sync', authenticate, async (req, res) => {
  const since = z.coerce.date().parse(req.query.since);
  const [changed, deleted] = await Promise.all([
    repo.changedSince(req.user.id, since),      // needs an index on (user_id, updated_at)
    repo.tombstonesSince(req.user.id, since),   // deletions need tombstones, not silence
  ]);
  res.json({ changed, deleted, serverTime: new Date().toISOString() });  // client stores serverTime
});
@end
@senior
The two details that show mobile experience: deletions need tombstone rows, because "absent from the response" is indistinguishable from "unchanged" in a delta sync — without tombstones, deleted records live forever on the device. And the client must use the *server's* clock as the next `since` value, never its own, because device clocks are wrong often enough to silently skip records. I would also keep the mobile BFF as a separate surface from the public API so I can change it freely with each app release without a versioning ceremony.
@followup
How do you handle API responses when the app is offline?
@answer
The client reads from a local database and treats the network as a synchronisation mechanism rather than the source of truth, with writes queued in an outbox and replayed with idempotency keys. From the API side, what I owe that client is: idempotent writes, delta sync with tombstones, clear conflict semantics (a version or `updated_at` per record so the server can reject a stale write with 409), and stable ids that the client can generate — client-generated UUIDs mean an offline-created record has its final id immediately.
@red
- Chatty APIs requiring five round trips to render one screen.
- No delta sync, so the app refetches everything on every launch.

# SECTION HTTP & Networking | What actually happens between the browser and your server.

### What happens when you type a URL and press Enter?
@short
DNS resolves the hostname to an IP, TCP connects (three-way handshake), TLS negotiates encryption, the browser sends an HTTP request, the server responds, and the browser parses HTML, builds the DOM and CSSOM, fetches subresources, runs JavaScript and paints. Each of those stages is a place latency and failures come from.
@deep
The detail worth having: DNS is resolved through a cache hierarchy (browser → OS → router → resolver → root/TLD/authoritative), TLS 1.3 completes in one round trip (or zero with resumption), and HTTP/2 multiplexes many requests over one connection so head-of-line blocking at the HTTP layer disappears — though TCP-level head-of-line blocking remains, which is what HTTP/3 over QUIC solves.

On the rendering side, render-blocking CSS and synchronous scripts in `<head>` delay first paint, which is why `defer`, `async`, preloading the LCP image and inlining critical CSS matter.
@arch The full path, with where the time goes
  Browser
    │ 1. DNS       ~0–100ms (cached: 0)  ── browser → OS → resolver → authoritative
    │ 2. TCP       1 RTT                 ── SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK
    │ 3. TLS       1 RTT (TLS 1.3)       ── 0-RTT on resumption
    │ 4. Request   ───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    ▼                                                             ▼
  CDN edge ──(cache hit: return here, ~10ms)──          Load balancer (ALB)
    │ miss                                                        │
    ▼                                                             ▼
  Origin: Nginx → Node app → { Redis cache hit? → return }   → PostgreSQL
                                                              → 3rd party (Stripe…)
  Response ─► browser: parse HTML → CSSOM → render tree → layout → paint
              JS: defer/async so parsing is not blocked; hydrate if SSR
@end
@senior
The senior version of this answer is to narrate it as a *latency budget* and say where you would attack it: a CDN removes the origin round trip for cacheable content, HTTP/2 or 3 removes connection overhead per asset, `preconnect` warms DNS and TLS for a third-party origin, and server-side the win is usually a cache or a fixed query rather than anything network-level. I would also point out that TTFB tells you about the server and the network, while LCP tells you about the whole pipeline including render — confusing the two sends people optimising the wrong layer.
@followup
Where would you look first if TTFB is high?
@answer
Split it: is it DNS/TCP/TLS (connection setup, visible in the waterfall's timing breakdown) or is it server processing? If the connection phases are fast and waiting is long, it is server-side — then I look at the application trace for the slow span, which in my experience is a database query, an uncached third-party call, or a cold start. If connection setup is slow, it is DNS, a distant origin (fix with a CDN or a closer region), or TLS configuration.
@red
- Skipping DNS and TLS in the explanation.
- Not knowing what is render-blocking.

### Explain HTTP methods and their semantics.
@short
`GET` reads and must be safe with no side effects; `POST` creates or performs an action and is not idempotent; `PUT` replaces a resource entirely and is idempotent; `PATCH` applies a partial update and is generally not idempotent unless you design it to be; `DELETE` removes and is idempotent; `HEAD` and `OPTIONS` are metadata.
@deep
"Safe" and "idempotent" have real consequences: browsers, proxies and crawlers will prefetch and retry `GET` freely, so a `GET /deleteUser?id=5` can be triggered by a link prefetcher or a security scanner. Only `GET` and `HEAD` are cacheable by default.

The `PUT` versus `PATCH` distinction is often blurred in practice. `PUT` with a partial body technically means "set the resource to exactly this", which would null the omitted fields — most APIs do not implement it that strictly, which is fine as long as it is documented.
@code http Semantics in practice
GET    /orders/o_9            safe, idempotent, cacheable
HEAD   /orders/o_9            same headers, no body — useful for existence/size checks
POST   /orders                not idempotent → needs Idempotency-Key for money
PUT    /orders/o_9            full replacement; sending it twice = same final state
PATCH  /orders/o_9            partial; { "status": "shipped" } — idempotent here by design
DELETE /orders/o_9            idempotent: second call returns 204 or 404, state unchanged
OPTIONS /orders               CORS preflight; Allow header

# A PATCH that is NOT idempotent — avoid unless intentional
PATCH /counters/c_1  { "increment": 1 }      ← applying twice gives a different result
@end
@senior
The practical rules I enforce: never mutate state on `GET` (a prefetcher will find it), always support `HEAD` implicitly, and make `DELETE` idempotent by returning success or 404 rather than erroring on a second call — clients retrying a lost response should not see a failure. For `PATCH`, I prefer explicit field semantics over JSON Merge Patch's `null`-means-delete convention unless clients understand it, because "did they mean set to null or not provided?" is a genuine ambiguity that causes data loss.
@followup
How do you delete something safely when the client might retry?
@answer
Make it idempotent and observable: the first `DELETE` marks the row deleted and returns 204; a repeat returns 204 again (or 404, documented either way) without erroring. For anything auditable I use a soft delete — a `deleted_at` timestamp — so the operation is reversible and the record remains for audit and for foreign keys. Hard deletion then happens on a retention schedule, which also matters for GDPR-style erasure requests where you must prove the data is genuinely gone.
@red
- State-changing GET endpoints.
- Second DELETE returning 500.

### What is the difference between HTTP/1.1, HTTP/2 and HTTP/3?
@short
HTTP/1.1 sends one request at a time per connection, so browsers open six connections per origin. HTTP/2 multiplexes many streams over one TCP connection with header compression and binary framing. HTTP/3 runs over QUIC on UDP, which removes TCP head-of-line blocking and makes connection migration possible when a phone switches networks.
@deep
With HTTP/1.1, a slow response blocks the connection behind it, which is why bundling, spriting and domain sharding were standard practice. HTTP/2 makes those anti-patterns: many small cacheable files are now better than one big bundle, because one changed file no longer invalidates everything.

HTTP/2 still suffers TCP head-of-line blocking — a lost packet stalls every stream on that connection, which is worst on lossy mobile networks. QUIC gives each stream its own delivery ordering, so one lost packet only stalls one stream, and it moves the TLS handshake into the transport for a 1-RTT (or 0-RTT) connection.
@code nginx Enabling HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 at the edge
server {
  listen 443 ssl;
  listen 443 quic reuseport;              # HTTP/3
  http2 on;

  ssl_protocols TLSv1.2 TLSv1.3;
  ssl_early_data on;                       # 0-RTT resumption (careful: replayable — GET only)
  add_header Alt-Svc 'h3=":443"; ma=86400';   # tells clients HTTP/3 is available

  # HTTP/2+ makes these obsolete:
  #  - domain sharding (now harmful: extra DNS/TLS handshakes)
  #  - concatenating all JS into one bundle (finer chunks cache better)
  gzip on; brotli on;
}
@end
@senior
The senior nuance is that HTTP/2 changes your bundling strategy but does not eliminate bundling — thousands of tiny modules still cost per-request overhead and hurt compression ratios, so the sweet spot is a moderate number of well-split chunks aligned with your route structure. I would also flag that 0-RTT data is replayable by an attacker, so it must be restricted to safe, idempotent requests; enabling `ssl_early_data` for POST endpoints is a real vulnerability. And in practice the biggest wins on mobile come from HTTP/3's resilience to packet loss and network switching, which does not show up in a fast-office benchmark at all.
@followup
Does HTTP/2 make a CDN unnecessary?
@answer
No — they solve different problems. HTTP/2 reduces per-request overhead on an existing connection; a CDN reduces the physical distance and therefore the round-trip time, and absorbs load at the edge. Even a perfectly multiplexed connection to a server 200ms away is slower than a cached response 10ms away. They compose: the CDN terminates HTTP/3 near the user and keeps a warm connection to your origin.
@red
- Still recommending domain sharding.
- Not knowing what head-of-line blocking means.

### How does HTTPS/TLS work?
@short
TLS gives you encryption, integrity and server authentication. The client and server negotiate a cipher suite, the server proves its identity with a certificate chained to a trusted CA, they derive a shared symmetric key (via ECDHE, which gives forward secrecy), and all subsequent traffic is encrypted with that symmetric key.
@deep
Asymmetric cryptography is used only for the handshake — authentication and key agreement — because it is slow; the bulk data uses a fast symmetric cipher like AES-GCM or ChaCha20-Poly1305. Forward secrecy means a compromise of the server's private key later cannot decrypt previously recorded traffic, because the ephemeral keys are discarded.

TLS 1.3 removed the vulnerable legacy options, reduced the handshake to one round trip, and made forward secrecy mandatory.
@arch TLS 1.3 handshake — one round trip
  Client                                              Server
    │── ClientHello ────────────────────────────────────►│  supported ciphers,
    │   + key_share (ephemeral ECDHE public key)         │  ALPN (h2/h3), SNI
    │                                                    │
    │◄── ServerHello + key_share ────────────────────────│  chosen cipher
    │◄── {Certificate, CertificateVerify, Finished} ─────│  (already encrypted)
    │                                                    │
    │── {Finished} ─────────────────────────────────────►│
    │══ application data (AES-GCM, symmetric) ══════════►│
  Client verifies: signature chain to a trusted root, hostname match (SAN),
  validity dates, and revocation (OCSP stapling).
@end
@senior
The operationally important parts are certificate lifecycle and configuration. Expired certificates are a top cause of self-inflicted outages, so automated renewal (ACME/Let's Encrypt or ACM) with monitoring on days-to-expiry is non-negotiable. I disable TLS 1.0/1.1, enable HSTS with `includeSubDomains` and a long max-age once I am confident, and use OCSP stapling so clients do not need a separate round trip to check revocation. For internal service-to-service traffic in a zero-trust setup, mutual TLS authenticates both ends rather than just the server.
@followup
What is certificate pinning and when is it dangerous?
@answer
Pinning means the client only accepts a specific certificate or public key rather than anything signed by a trusted CA — it defeats a user-installed proxy or a compromised CA. It is dangerous because certificate rotation becomes a release event: if the pinned cert expires or is replaced and clients have not updated, every installed app breaks and cannot be fixed remotely. If I pin, I pin the intermediate or a backup key as well, keep the pin set rotating ahead of expiry, and ship a remote kill-switch.
@red
- Saying HTTPS uses RSA for all traffic.
- No monitoring on certificate expiry.

### What are cookies, and what do the security flags do?
@short
A cookie is a key-value pair the browser stores and sends automatically with matching requests. `HttpOnly` hides it from JavaScript, `Secure` restricts it to HTTPS, `SameSite` controls whether it is sent on cross-site requests, and `Domain`/`Path`/`Max-Age` control scope and lifetime. Session cookies need all of the first three.
@deep
`SameSite=Lax` (now the browser default) sends the cookie on top-level navigations but not on cross-site subresource requests or POSTs, which blocks the classic CSRF vector. `Strict` blocks even the navigation case, which breaks "click a link in an email and land logged in". `None` allows everything and requires `Secure` — necessary for genuine third-party contexts like embedded widgets or an SSO iframe.

Cookie scope is a common source of bugs: setting `Domain=.acme.com` shares the cookie with every subdomain including untrusted ones, which is a real risk if you host user content on a subdomain.
@code ts Session cookie configuration I would defend
res.cookie('session', token, {
  httpOnly: true,                       // JS cannot read it → XSS cannot exfiltrate it
  secure: env.NODE_ENV === 'production',// HTTPS only
  sameSite: 'lax',                      // blocks the classic cross-site POST CSRF
  path: '/',
  maxAge: 15 * 60 * 1000,               // short-lived access token
  // domain: undefined  ← host-only by default; do NOT widen unless you need subdomains
});

res.cookie('refresh', refreshToken, {
  httpOnly: true, secure: true, sameSite: 'strict',
  path: '/api/v1/auth/refresh',         // sent ONLY to the refresh endpoint — limits exposure
  maxAge: 30 * 24 * 3600 * 1000,
});
@end
@senior
Scoping the refresh token's `Path` to just the refresh endpoint is the detail that shows care: it means the long-lived credential is not attached to every API request, so it is never in a log, a proxy trace or an error report from an unrelated endpoint. I would also mention the `__Host-` prefix, which browsers enforce as "secure, host-only, path=/", making it impossible for a subdomain to overwrite the cookie — a cheap defence against subdomain-takeover cookie injection. And with `SameSite=Lax` covering most CSRF, I still add a double-submit token for state-changing requests, because `Lax` does not cover every case and older browsers vary.
@followup
Cookies or localStorage for auth tokens?
@answer
Cookies with `HttpOnly`, for the web. localStorage is readable by any script running on the page, so a single XSS — including one from a compromised dependency — steals a token the attacker can use from anywhere, and you cannot revoke it. With an httpOnly cookie, XSS can still act as the user while the page is open, which is bad, but it cannot exfiltrate a reusable credential. For native mobile apps the calculus is different: there is no cookie jar and no CSRF, so a token in the Keychain with a bearer header is correct.
@red
- Not setting `HttpOnly` on a session cookie.
- `SameSite=None` without understanding the CSRF exposure.

### What is the difference between TCP and UDP, and where does each appear in your stack?
@short
TCP is connection-oriented and reliable — ordered delivery, retransmission, flow and congestion control — at the cost of handshakes and head-of-line blocking. UDP is fire-and-forget with no guarantees but no overhead. HTTP/1.1 and 2 use TCP; HTTP/3 uses UDP via QUIC, which rebuilds reliability per stream in userspace.
@deep
The relevant property for application engineers is head-of-line blocking. In TCP, a lost packet stalls everything behind it in the byte stream, even if the later data belongs to an unrelated HTTP/2 stream. QUIC keeps per-stream ordering, so loss affects only the stream it hit.

UDP is also what DNS, most VoIP and video, and many game protocols use, because retransmitting a stale audio frame is worse than dropping it.
@code text Where each shows up in a typical full-stack deployment
  TCP : HTTP/1.1, HTTP/2, WebSocket, PostgreSQL wire protocol, Redis (RESP),
        MySQL, SMTP, SSH, TLS  ── anything where every byte must arrive in order
  UDP : DNS (usually), QUIC/HTTP3, NTP, WebRTC media, syslog, StatsD metrics
        ── anything where latency beats completeness, or reliability is rebuilt above

  Practical consequence in your stack:
   • A Postgres connection is a TCP connection → a pool exists because handshakes are expensive
   • A WebSocket is a long-lived TCP connection → load balancer idle timeouts kill it
     unless you send heartbeats (ping/pong) more often than the timeout
   • StatsD over UDP → metrics can be silently lost; never use it for billing counters
@end
@senior
