The insight I would offer is that connection cost is why pooling exists everywhere: a TCP handshake plus TLS plus a database authentication round trip is milliseconds you pay per connection, so the pool amortises it. That is also why a serverless function opening a fresh Postgres connection per invocation is a performance and connection-limit disaster, and why PgBouncer or RDS Proxy exists. And on the WebSocket side, understanding that it is a long-lived TCP connection immediately explains why ALB idle timeouts, NAT timeouts and mobile network switches drop them, and therefore why heartbeats and reconnection with backoff are mandatory rather than optional polish.
@followup
Why does a WebSocket connection drop on mobile networks?
@answer
Several reasons stack up: NAT gateways drop idle mappings after 30–120 seconds, carriers switch the device between towers or between cellular and Wi-Fi which changes the IP, and the OS suspends the app in the background and tears down sockets. The mitigations are application-level heartbeats well inside the shortest timeout, exponential backoff with jitter on reconnect, resuming from a sequence number rather than replaying everything, and accepting that push notifications — not WebSockets — are the correct channel for reaching a backgrounded mobile app.
@red
- "UDP is unreliable so we never use it" while running DNS and QUIC.
- Not connecting connection cost to pooling.

### WebSockets vs Server-Sent Events vs polling — how do you choose?
@short
Polling is simplest and fine for low-frequency updates. SSE is a one-way server-to-client stream over plain HTTP with automatic reconnection and event ids — ideal for notifications, progress and LLM token streaming. WebSockets are full-duplex and the right choice when the client also sends frequently: chat, collaborative editing, live cursors.
@deep
SSE's advantages are underrated: it is just HTTP, so it works through proxies and with existing auth headers and compression, it reconnects automatically with `Last-Event-ID` for resumption, and it needs no separate protocol upgrade. Its limits are one-way communication, a browser cap of six connections per origin on HTTP/1.1 (not an issue over HTTP/2), and no binary frames.

WebSockets give you bidirectional, low-overhead frames after the upgrade handshake, at the cost of a stateful connection that complicates load balancing, scaling and authentication.
@code ts SSE for AI token streaming — the case where it clearly wins
app.get('/api/chat/stream', authenticate, async (req, res) => {
  res.writeHead(200, {
    'Content-Type': 'text/event-stream',
    'Cache-Control': 'no-cache, no-transform',   // no-transform stops proxies buffering
    'Connection': 'keep-alive',
    'X-Accel-Buffering': 'no',                   // tell Nginx not to buffer
  });

  const heartbeat = setInterval(() => res.write(': ping\n\n'), 15_000);  // keep the LB happy
  req.on('close', () => { clearInterval(heartbeat); abort.abort(); });   // client left → stop billing

  let id = 0;
  for await (const token of llm.stream(req.query.q, { signal: abort.signal })) {
    res.write(`id: ${++id}\n`);                  // client resumes with Last-Event-ID
    res.write(`data: ${JSON.stringify({ token })}\n\n`);
  }
  res.write('event: done\ndata: {}\n\n');
  res.end();
});
@end
@senior
Two production details. First, `X-Accel-Buffering: no` and `Cache-Control: no-transform`: without them Nginx or a CDN buffers the stream and the user sees nothing for thirty seconds and then the whole answer at once — which looks like a hang. Second, aborting the upstream LLM call when the client disconnects is a direct cost control; without it you keep generating and paying for tokens nobody will read. For choosing between the three, my rule is: if the client only receives, use SSE — it is simpler to operate, easier to scale and free to authenticate; reach for WebSockets when you genuinely need the upstream channel.
@followup
How do you scale WebSockets across multiple servers?
@answer
Keep the servers stateless and put the fan-out in Redis pub/sub (or a dedicated broker): each instance holds its own client sockets, subscribes to the relevant channels, and publishing a message on any instance delivers it to subscribers on all of them. The load balancer needs sticky sessions or at least connection-level affinity, and you need presence tracking with TTL keys so a crashed instance's users are eventually cleaned up. That architecture is exactly the stateless WebSocket fan-out pattern — and it is what makes horizontal scaling possible without every server knowing every client.
@red
- Using WebSockets for one-way notifications when SSE is simpler.
- Long-polling with no backoff, hammering the server.

### What is a CDN and what should you put behind one?
@short
A CDN is a globally distributed cache that serves content from a point of presence near the user. Static assets, images, fonts and video always belong on it; cacheable API responses and full HTML pages can too. It reduces latency, absorbs traffic spikes and cuts origin bandwidth cost.
@deep
Beyond caching, modern CDNs terminate TLS at the edge (removing several round trips of handshake latency), provide DDoS absorption and a WAF, compress and convert images, and can run edge functions for redirects, A/B splits and auth checks.

The cache key is the thing to get right: by default it is the URL, but query strings, cookies and headers can be included or excluded. Including a random analytics query parameter in the key means every visitor gets a cache miss — a very common and very expensive misconfiguration.
@code text Cache strategy per content type
  /_next/static/*.js|css   hashed filename   → Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable
  /images/*                                   → public, max-age=86400, stale-while-revalidate=604800
  /                        marketing HTML     → public, s-maxage=60, stale-while-revalidate=600
  /api/v1/products         public catalogue   → public, s-maxage=30 + surrogate-key: products
  /api/v1/me               personalised       → private, no-store   (NEVER cache at the edge)

  Cache key: strip utm_*, fbclid, gclid   → otherwise every campaign link is a MISS
  Vary: Accept-Encoding (+ Authorization on anything auth-aware)
  Invalidate by surrogate key / cache tag, not by purging paths one at a time
@end
@senior
The failure I would call out is caching an authenticated response at the edge — one misconfigured `Cache-Control: public` on a `/me` endpoint and the CDN serves one customer's profile to the next visitor. It is a catastrophic, headline-grade bug and it is a one-word mistake, which is why I treat "is this response user-specific?" as a required review question on any caching change. On the positive side, `stale-while-revalidate` at the edge is how you survive an origin outage gracefully: users keep getting slightly stale content instead of a 502, and `stale-if-error` makes that explicit.
@followup
How do you invalidate CDN content?
@answer
Prefer immutable, content-hashed URLs so invalidation is never needed — a new deploy simply references new URLs. Where content must be updated in place, use surrogate keys or cache tags so you can purge everything related to one entity in a single call, rather than enumerating paths. Purges are eventually consistent across POPs, which takes seconds to minutes, so anything that must be instantly correct should not be edge-cached in the first place.
@red
- Caching personalised responses publicly.
- Including tracking query parameters in the cache key.

### How do load balancers work and what algorithms do they use?
@short
A load balancer distributes requests across healthy backends using an algorithm — round robin, least connections, or hashing on a key for stickiness — and removes instances that fail health checks. Layer 4 balances at the TCP level; Layer 7 understands HTTP and can route by path, host or header.
@deep
Health checks are the part that determines behaviour during an incident. A shallow check (`GET /` returns 200) keeps sending traffic to an instance whose database connection is dead; a deep check that verifies dependencies can cause a cascading removal of every instance when the shared database blips, taking down the whole service.

The usual resolution is two endpoints: a liveness check that only says "the process is running" (used to restart), and a readiness check that reflects the ability to serve (used to route), with the readiness check degrading gracefully rather than failing hard on a non-critical dependency.
@code ts Liveness vs readiness — and why they differ
// Liveness: is the process wedged? If this fails, RESTART me.
app.get('/healthz', (_req, res) => res.status(200).json({ ok: true }));

// Readiness: should I receive traffic? If this fails, ROUTE AWAY (but don't restart).
app.get('/readyz', async (_req, res) => {
  const checks = await Promise.allSettled([
    pool.query('SELECT 1'),                 // critical → not ready without it
    redis.ping(),                           // degraded, but still servable
  ]);
  const dbOk = checks[0].status === 'fulfilled';
  const cacheOk = checks[1].status === 'fulfilled';
  if (shuttingDown || !dbOk) return res.status(503).json({ db: dbOk, cache: cacheOk });
  res.status(200).json({ db: true, cache: cacheOk, degraded: !cacheOk });
});
@end
@senior
The senior point is the failure-correlation trap: if every instance's readiness depends on one database, a database hiccup removes 100% of your fleet and turns a partial degradation into a total outage, and then the stampede of reconnects on recovery keeps it down. So I make readiness depend only on what is genuinely required to serve *some* traffic, use `Promise.allSettled` with per-dependency reporting, and add circuit breakers so a failing dependency degrades a feature rather than failing the instance. I would also mention connection draining on deregistration, without which every deploy drops in-flight requests.
@followup
What is sticky session affinity and when do you need it?
@answer
Affinity routes a given client consistently to the same backend, usually via a cookie or a hash of the source IP. You need it when the server holds per-connection state — WebSockets are the main legitimate case. It is a smell for HTTP APIs, because it undermines even load distribution, complicates deploys (draining a node disconnects its users) and hides the real fix, which is moving session state to Redis or a JWT so any instance can serve any request.
@red
- One health endpoint used for both liveness and readiness.
- Deep health checks that take the whole fleet down together.

### What are the main web security risks you design against?
@short
The recurring ones are injection (SQL, NoSQL, command), broken access control (IDOR, missing authorisation), XSS, CSRF, sensitive data exposure, misconfigured CORS or headers, and vulnerable dependencies. Broken access control is consistently the number one category in the OWASP Top 10 and the one automated tools catch least well.
@deep
Each has a structural defence rather than a per-case fix: parameterised queries for injection; server-side authorisation checks scoped to the record for access control; contextual output encoding plus CSP for XSS; SameSite cookies plus anti-CSRF tokens for CSRF; and encryption at rest and in transit with least-privilege access for data exposure.

The pattern worth stating is defence in depth: no single control is assumed to hold. A CSP limits the damage of an XSS that slips through; httpOnly cookies limit the damage further; short token lifetimes limit it again.
@code ts The controls, in code
// Injection — parameterised, always
await pool.query('SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = $1', [email]);           // ✅
// NoSQL — validate the TYPE, not just the value
z.object({ email: z.string().email() }).parse(req.body);   // kills { $gt: '' } attacks

// Broken access control — scope by owner AND tenant, at the data layer
const order = await repo.findOne({ id, userId: req.user.id, tenantId: req.user.tenantId });

// XSS — React escapes by default; the danger is the escape hatch
<div dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: DOMPurify.sanitize(userHtml) }} />   // sanitise, always

// CSRF — SameSite + double-submit token for state-changing requests
app.use(csrf({ cookie: { httpOnly: true, sameSite: 'lax', secure: true } }));

// Headers
app.use(helmet({ hsts: { maxAge: 31536000, includeSubDomains: true, preload: true } }));
@end
@senior
What I would emphasise is that authorisation is the hardest one to get right because it cannot be solved by a library — it depends on your domain. The failure is always the same shape: an endpoint takes an id from the URL, the developer checks that the user is logged in, and forgets to check that the record belongs to them. My structural answer is to make it impossible by construction: repositories that require an owner or tenant scope in their signature, or Postgres row-level security so the database itself refuses. Relying on every developer remembering, on every endpoint, forever, is not a control.
@followup
How do you protect against XSS in a React app?
@answer
React escapes interpolated values by default, so the main risks are `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`, `href={userInput}` allowing `javascript:` URLs, and injecting into `<script>` or style contexts. I sanitise any HTML with DOMPurify on the server side of the boundary, validate URL schemes against an allow-list, and deploy a strict Content-Security-Policy with nonces so that even if something slips through, the injected script has no permission to run or to exfiltrate. Then httpOnly cookies mean a successful XSS still cannot steal a reusable token.
@red
- Assuming an ORM or framework makes you immune.
- Sanitising on the client only.

# SECTION SQL & Database Fundamentals | The layer where most real performance problems actually live.

### What are the different types of JOIN?
@short
`INNER JOIN` returns only matching rows. `LEFT JOIN` returns all rows from the left table with NULLs where there is no match, and `RIGHT JOIN` is its mirror. `FULL OUTER JOIN` returns everything from both sides. `CROSS JOIN` is the Cartesian product, and a self-join joins a table to itself for hierarchies.
@deep
The trap is filtering a `LEFT JOIN` in the `WHERE` clause: `WHERE b.status = 'x'` discards the NULL rows and silently turns it into an inner join. The condition has to go in the `ON` clause to preserve the outer semantics.

`FULL OUTER JOIN` is rare in application code but useful for reconciliation — finding rows present on one side and not the other, which is exactly what a payment reconciliation job does.
@code sql The LEFT JOIN filter trap, and the anti-join pattern
-- ❌ silently becomes an INNER JOIN: users with no paid orders are dropped
SELECT u.id, o.total FROM users u
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.user_id = u.id
WHERE o.status = 'paid';

-- ✅ condition belongs in ON; users with no paid orders keep a NULL total
SELECT u.id, o.total FROM users u
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.user_id = u.id AND o.status = 'paid';

-- Anti-join: users who have never ordered (usually faster than NOT IN)
SELECT u.id FROM users u
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.user_id = u.id
WHERE o.id IS NULL;
-- or:  WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.user_id = u.id)
@end
@senior
The senior detail is `NOT IN` versus `NOT EXISTS`: if the subquery returns even one NULL, `NOT IN` returns no rows at all, because `x NOT IN (1, NULL)` evaluates to UNKNOWN. That has caused silent, total data loss in reports. `NOT EXISTS` has no such problem and usually plans better as an anti-join. I would also mention that joining on unindexed foreign keys is one of the most common causes of a slow query — Postgres does not create an index on the referencing side of a foreign key automatically, which surprises people coming from MySQL.
@followup
Which join type is fastest?
@answer
The question is really about the join *algorithm*, which the planner chooses: nested loop (great when one side is tiny and the other is indexed), hash join (great for large unsorted joins with enough work_mem), and merge join (great when both inputs are already sorted). You do not pick these directly; you influence them by providing indexes, accurate statistics and sufficient `work_mem`. If the planner picks a nested loop over a million rows, the fix is almost always a missing index or a stale `ANALYZE`, not a join hint.
@red
- Filtering a LEFT JOIN in WHERE and not knowing it changes semantics.
- Using `NOT IN` with a nullable column.

### What is an index and how does it work?
@short
An index is a separate data structure — usually a B-tree — that maps column values to row locations, turning a full table scan into a logarithmic lookup. It makes reads fast and writes slightly slower, because every insert, update and delete must maintain it, and it consumes disk space.
@deep
A B-tree index supports equality, range and prefix queries, and can serve `ORDER BY` without a sort if the order matches. Column order in a composite index matters enormously: an index on `(a, b)` serves `WHERE a = ?`, `WHERE a = ? AND b = ?` and `ORDER BY a, b`, but not `WHERE b = ?` alone.

An index is only usable if the column appears unwrapped: `WHERE lower(email) = ?` cannot use an index on `email`, but it can use an expression index on `lower(email)`. Similarly, a leading wildcard `LIKE '%foo'` cannot use a B-tree.
@code sql Composite index design and the covering-index trick
-- The query we need to serve
SELECT id, total, status FROM orders
 WHERE user_id = $1 AND status = 'paid'
 ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20;

-- Rule: equality columns first, then the range/sort column
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_user_status_created
    ON orders (user_id, status, created_at DESC);

-- Covering index: INCLUDE the selected columns so the heap is never touched
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_cover
    ON orders (user_id, status, created_at DESC) INCLUDE (total);
-- → EXPLAIN shows "Index Only Scan", which is dramatically faster

-- Partial index: index only the rows you actually query, much smaller
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_pending ON orders (created_at) WHERE status = 'pending';

-- Expression index for case-insensitive lookup
CREATE INDEX idx_users_lower_email ON users (lower(email));
@end
@senior
Partial indexes are the underused one: if 98% of your orders are `completed` and you only ever query `pending`, a partial index is 2% of the size, fits in memory and is far cheaper to maintain. Covering indexes with `INCLUDE` are the other big win, because an index-only scan avoids the heap fetch entirely. Against that, I audit for unused indexes with `pg_stat_user_indexes` — every index is a tax on every write, and I have seen tables with twelve indexes where three were used. Index design is a query-driven exercise: you write the query first, then the index that serves it.
@followup
Why is my index not being used?
@answer
Common causes: the column is wrapped in a function or has an implicit type cast (`WHERE id = '123'` against a bigint); a leading wildcard in `LIKE`; the query is not selective enough, so a sequential scan is genuinely cheaper and the planner is right; statistics are stale so the planner's row estimate is wrong (fix with `ANALYZE`); or the index's leading column is not in the predicate. `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)` tells you which — comparing the estimated rows to the actual rows immediately reveals a statistics problem.
@red
- "Add an index to every column."
- Not knowing composite index column order matters.

### What are ACID properties and transaction isolation levels?
@short
Atomicity means all-or-nothing; Consistency means constraints hold before and after; Isolation means concurrent transactions do not see each other's intermediate state; Durability means a committed transaction survives a crash. Isolation is tunable — Read Committed, Repeatable Read and Serializable trade concurrency for correctness.
@deep
The anomalies define the levels: dirty read (seeing uncommitted data), non-repeatable read (the same row changes between two reads), and phantom read (new rows appear matching your predicate). Read Uncommitted allows all three, Read Committed (Postgres's default) prevents dirty reads, Repeatable Read (MySQL InnoDB's default) also prevents non-repeatable reads, and Serializable prevents all of them.

Postgres implements this with MVCC: readers never block writers and writers never block readers, because each transaction sees a snapshot. The cost is dead tuples that `VACUUM` must reclaim.
@code sql The lost-update problem and three correct fixes
-- ❌ read-modify-write: two concurrent requests both read 10, both write 9 → one sale lost
SELECT stock FROM products WHERE id = 1;         -- 10
UPDATE products SET stock = 9 WHERE id = 1;

-- ✅ 1. atomic update — let the database do the arithmetic
UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 1 AND stock > 0;
-- rowCount = 0 means it was out of stock; no race possible

-- ✅ 2. pessimistic lock — when you must read, decide, then write
BEGIN;
SELECT stock FROM products WHERE id = 1 FOR UPDATE;    -- blocks other writers
UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 1;
COMMIT;

-- ✅ 3. optimistic lock — best for low contention, no lock held across think-time
UPDATE products SET stock = $newStock, version = version + 1
 WHERE id = 1 AND version = $readVersion;               -- 0 rows → retry
@end
@senior
The choice between these is a real engineering decision. The atomic update is best when the logic fits in SQL. `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` is correct but serialises access to that row, which becomes the bottleneck on a hot product during a flash sale, and it risks deadlocks if two transactions lock rows in different orders — so I always lock in a consistent order. Optimistic locking is best when conflicts are rare and you want no lock held while a user thinks. The wrong answer is doing the arithmetic in application code without any of the three, which is the single most common concurrency bug I find in code review.
@followup
What is a deadlock and how do you avoid it?
@answer
Two transactions each hold a lock the other needs, so neither can proceed; the database detects the cycle and kills one with a serialization or deadlock error. The main prevention is acquiring locks in a consistent order everywhere — for example always ordering by primary key when updating multiple rows. Beyond that: keep transactions short, do not hold a transaction open across a network call or user think-time, and make the application retry on the deadlock error code, because at high concurrency some deadlocks are unavoidable and a retry is the correct response.
@red
- Doing `SELECT` then `UPDATE` in application code with no lock.
- Holding a transaction open across an HTTP call to a payment provider.

### What is normalisation, and when do you denormalise?
@short
Normalisation removes redundancy by splitting data into related tables — 1NF atomic values, 2NF no partial dependency on a composite key, 3NF no transitive dependency. It prevents update anomalies. You denormalise deliberately, for read performance, when the join cost is measured and the duplication is kept correct.
@deep
The everyday practical form is: store an entity once and reference it by foreign key. The cost is joins on read, and at some scale — analytics dashboards, feeds, search results — those joins become the bottleneck.

Legitimate denormalisation includes: caching a computed aggregate (`orders.item_count`), duplicating a rarely changing attribute to avoid a join (`order_items.product_name` at time of purchase), and materialised views for reporting.
@code sql Deliberate denormalisation, with the correctness mechanism
-- Immutable snapshot: the price at purchase time must NOT follow the product's current price
CREATE TABLE order_items (
  order_id     uuid REFERENCES orders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  product_id   uuid REFERENCES products(id),
  product_name text        NOT NULL,     -- denormalised on purpose: historical accuracy
  unit_price_minor int     NOT NULL,     -- NOT a join to products.price
  qty          int         NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id)
);

-- Cached aggregate kept correct by a trigger (or by the application, transactionally)
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN item_count int NOT NULL DEFAULT 0;

CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION sync_item_count() RETURNS trigger AS $$
BEGIN
  UPDATE orders SET item_count = (SELECT count(*) FROM order_items WHERE order_id = NEW.order_id)
   WHERE id = NEW.order_id;
  RETURN NULL;
END $$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;

CREATE TRIGGER trg_item_count AFTER INSERT OR DELETE ON order_items
FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION sync_item_count();

-- Reporting: a materialised view refreshed on a schedule, not queried live
CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW daily_revenue AS
SELECT date_trunc('day', created_at) d, currency, sum(total_minor) rev
FROM orders WHERE status = 'paid' GROUP BY 1, 2;
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON daily_revenue (d, currency);   -- enables REFRESH CONCURRENTLY
@end
@senior
The distinction I would draw is between denormalisation for *performance* and denormalisation for *correctness*. Storing `unit_price_minor` on the order item is not an optimisation — it is required, because the order must reflect what the customer actually agreed to pay, and joining to the live product price would rewrite history when prices change. That is a modelling decision people get wrong and it shows up as customer disputes. Performance denormalisation, by contrast, needs an explicit mechanism to stay correct (trigger, transactional update, or scheduled refresh) and an accepted staleness window, both of which should be written down.
@followup
Should you use triggers for this?
@answer
Triggers guarantee the invariant regardless of which code path writes, which is their real strength — a backfill script or a manual fix cannot bypass them. The cost is hidden behaviour: a developer reading the application code has no idea a trigger fired, and debugging is harder. My rule is triggers for invariants that must never be violated (audit logs, denormalised counters, `updated_at`), and application code for anything with business branching. Either way it should be documented in the schema migration, not discovered during an incident.
@red
- Normalising to 5NF in an OLTP app and joining eight tables per request.
- Denormalising with no mechanism to keep the copy correct.

### What is the N+1 query problem?
@short
It is issuing one query to fetch a list and then one more per item to fetch a relation — 1 + N round trips where 2 would do. It is the single most common performance bug in ORM-based code, and it usually appears only in production because the development dataset has ten rows.
@deep
The fix is to fetch the related data in one query: an eager-loading `include`/`JOIN`, a second query with `WHERE id IN (...)` and an in-memory join, or a batching layer like DataLoader that collects ids within one tick and issues a single query.

The `IN (...)` approach is often better than a join for one-to-many relations, because a join multiplies rows and transfers the parent columns repeatedly, whereas two queries transfer each row once.
@code ts N+1 in three ORMs, and the fix in each
// ❌ Sequelize: 1 + N queries
const orders = await Order.findAll({ where: { userId } });
for (const o of orders) o.items = await OrderItem.findAll({ where: { orderId: o.id } });

// ✅ eager load — one query with a join
const orders = await Order.findAll({ where: { userId }, include: [{ model: OrderItem, as: 'items' }] });

// ✅ or two queries + in-memory grouping (often faster for one-to-many)
const orders = await Order.findAll({ where: { userId } });
const items  = await OrderItem.findAll({ where: { orderId: orders.map(o => o.id) } });
const byOrder = Map.groupBy(items, i => i.orderId);
orders.forEach(o => o.items = byOrder.get(o.id) ?? []);

// ✅ GraphQL resolvers: DataLoader batches per tick automatically
const itemLoader = new DataLoader(async (orderIds) => {
  const rows = await db.orderItems.whereIn('order_id', orderIds);   // ONE query
  const g = Map.groupBy(rows, r => r.order_id);
  return orderIds.map(id => g.get(id) ?? []);                       // must preserve order
});
@end
@senior
Two senior additions. First, detection: I enable query logging with a per-request counter in development and fail a test if a single request issues more than a threshold of queries — that catches N+1 before review rather than after a customer complains. In production, an APM trace showing 200 identical spans makes it obvious. Second, eager loading is not free: `include` on a deep tree can produce a huge cartesian result set, so the answer "just add include everywhere" causes its own problem. The correct instinct is to fetch exactly the data the endpoint returns, which is also why I keep response DTOs explicit rather than dumping whole model trees.
@followup
Is GraphQL more prone to N+1?
@answer
Yes, structurally — each resolver is written independently and knows nothing about the batch it is part of, so a query returning 50 orders each with a user triggers 50 user lookups by default. DataLoader is the standard fix: it defers loads to the end of the tick, batches the keys, issues one query and distributes the results. It is effectively mandatory in any non-trivial GraphQL server, along with query depth and complexity limits to prevent a client from requesting a pathologically expensive tree.
@red
- Looping over results with an await inside.
- Not knowing how to detect it.

### How do transactions work in application code?
@short
Begin, do the work, commit — or roll back on any error. The critical rules are: keep the transaction as short as possible, never perform a network call to a third party inside it, and pass the transaction handle through every query in the unit of work so they actually participate.
@deep
The most common bug is a "transaction" where some queries use the transaction client and others use the pool, so half the work is outside the transaction and does not roll back. That is why a `withTransaction(cb)` helper that hands you the client is much safer than manual BEGIN/COMMIT scattered through a service.

Holding a transaction across an external HTTP call is the other serious mistake: a slow Stripe call holds locks and a pooled connection for seconds, which under load exhausts the pool and stalls unrelated requests.
@code ts A transaction helper, and the outbox pattern for external effects
export async function withTransaction<T>(fn: (tx: PoolClient) => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
  const client = await pool.connect();
  try {
    await client.query('BEGIN');
    const result = await fn(client);
    await client.query('COMMIT');
    return result;
  } catch (e) {
    await client.query('ROLLBACK').catch(() => {});
    throw e;
  } finally {
    client.release();                          // ALWAYS — a leaked client kills the pool
  }
}

// ✅ external side effects go through an outbox row committed atomically with the state change
await withTransaction(async (tx) => {
  const order = await orderRepo.insert(dto, tx);
  await tx.query(
    `INSERT INTO outbox (id, topic, payload) VALUES ($1,$2,$3)`,
    [randomUUID(), 'order.created', JSON.stringify({ orderId: order.id })]);
  return order;
});
// a separate worker polls outbox → calls Stripe / sends email → marks sent (idempotently)
@end
@senior
The outbox pattern is the answer to the dual-write problem, and it is worth naming explicitly: you cannot atomically commit to a database and call an external API, so instead you commit the *intent* alongside the state change and let a worker perform the effect with at-least-once delivery. That converts "we charged the card but the order rolled back" into "the order exists and the charge will be attempted, idempotently, until it succeeds". I would also mention statement and idle-in-transaction timeouts at the database level as a safety net — `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` kills a transaction someone left open, which otherwise blocks VACUUM and bloats the table.
@followup
What is a distributed transaction and would you use one?
@answer
A two-phase commit across multiple resources — a coordinator asks every participant to prepare, then to commit. In practice I avoid it: it is slow, it blocks if the coordinator fails, and most modern datastores and SaaS APIs do not support it. The alternative is a saga — a sequence of local transactions with compensating actions on failure — coordinated by an orchestrator or by events. For payments, the compensation is a refund or a void; you accept temporary inconsistency and converge, rather than pretending you can have distributed atomicity.
@red
- Calling Stripe inside a transaction.
- Forgetting to release the client in `finally`.

### What is the difference between `WHERE` and `HAVING`, and how does GROUP BY work?
@short
`WHERE` filters individual rows before grouping; `HAVING` filters groups after aggregation. `GROUP BY` collapses rows into groups, and every selected column must either be in the `GROUP BY` or wrapped in an aggregate function.
@deep
Logical evaluation order is: FROM → JOIN → WHERE → GROUP BY → HAVING → SELECT → DISTINCT → ORDER BY → LIMIT. That explains two common confusions: you cannot reference a `SELECT` alias in `WHERE` (it does not exist yet) but you can in `ORDER BY`, and filtering in `WHERE` before grouping is far cheaper than filtering in `HAVING` after.

Window functions are the other half of this topic — they aggregate without collapsing rows, which is what you want for running totals, rankings and per-group top-N.
@code sql Aggregation and window functions side by side
-- GROUP BY: one row per customer. Filter rows in WHERE, groups in HAVING.
SELECT u.id, u.email, count(*) AS orders, sum(o.total_minor) AS revenue
  FROM users u JOIN orders o ON o.user_id = u.id
 WHERE o.created_at >= now() - interval '90 days'      -- cheap: filters rows first
   AND o.status = 'paid'
 GROUP BY u.id, u.email
HAVING sum(o.total_minor) > 100000                      -- filters GROUPS
 ORDER BY revenue DESC LIMIT 50;

-- Window function: keeps every row AND adds the aggregate
SELECT id, user_id, total_minor, created_at,
       sum(total_minor) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY created_at) AS running_total,
       row_number()     OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY created_at DESC) AS rn
  FROM orders;

-- Top 3 orders per user — the classic use of row_number()
SELECT * FROM (
  SELECT *, row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY total_minor DESC) rn FROM orders
) t WHERE rn <= 3;
@end
@senior
Window functions are the highest-leverage SQL skill for a full-stack engineer, because they replace loops in application code. Computing a running total or a per-user ranking in Node means pulling every row into memory; `row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY …)` does it in the database with an index-backed sort. The performance caveat is that a window function usually requires a sort, so an index matching `PARTITION BY … ORDER BY …` is what keeps it fast. I would also mention `FILTER (WHERE …)` inside aggregates as a cleaner alternative to `sum(CASE WHEN … THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)`.
@followup
How would you get a count and a page of results in one round trip?
@answer
`count(*) OVER ()` as an extra column gives the total for the filtered set alongside the paginated rows, in one query. It is convenient but not free — the database still has to evaluate the full result set to count it. On a large table I prefer to skip the exact total entirely and return `hasMore`, or use an approximate count, because an exact `COUNT` over millions of filtered rows is often the slowest part of a listing endpoint.
@red
- Putting an aggregate condition in `WHERE`.
- Selecting a column that is neither grouped nor aggregated (MySQL allows it and gives arbitrary values).

### What are primary keys, foreign keys and constraints — and do you use them?
@short
Yes, always. A primary key uniquely identifies a row, foreign keys enforce referential integrity, and `UNIQUE`, `CHECK` and `NOT NULL` constraints make invalid states impossible to store. Application-level validation is a user-experience layer; the database is the only place a guarantee actually holds.
@deep
The argument against constraints is usually performance or migration friction. Both are real but small compared to the cost of corrupt data: an orphaned `order_items` row with no order, or two users with the same email because a race slipped past an application check.

`ON DELETE` behaviour is a design decision, not a default: `CASCADE` for genuinely owned children (order items), `RESTRICT` for things that should block deletion (a product with orders), and `SET NULL` for optional references.
@code sql A schema that makes invalid states unrepresentable
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id            uuid PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  user_id       uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  status        text NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('pending','paid','shipped','refunded')),
  total_minor   bigint NOT NULL CHECK (total_minor >= 0),   -- integer minor units, never float
  currency      char(3) NOT NULL,
  payment_intent text UNIQUE,                                -- one order per payment intent
  created_at    timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  updated_at    timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);

-- Partial unique index: only ONE active cart per user, but many completed orders
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_open_cart ON orders (user_id) WHERE status = 'pending';

-- Prevent double refunds structurally, not with an if-statement
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_refund_per_order ON refunds (order_id) WHERE status <> 'failed';

-- Exclusion constraint: no overlapping bookings for the same room
CREATE EXTENSION btree_gist;
ALTER TABLE bookings ADD CONSTRAINT no_overlap
  EXCLUDE USING gist (room_id WITH =, during WITH &&);
@end
@senior
The `payment_intent text UNIQUE` line is worth calling out in a payments interview: it makes double-charging structurally impossible even if the webhook is delivered twice and the application logic has a race, because the second insert simply violates the constraint. That is the pattern I advocate generally — express invariants as constraints so a bug becomes a caught error rather than corrupt data. On money specifically, always integer minor units, never floating point: `0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3` in binary floating point, and rounding drift in financial totals is a genuine, auditable problem.
@followup
Should IDs be UUIDs or auto-increment integers?
@answer
Integers are smaller, faster to index and naturally ordered, but they leak volume information and require a server round trip to generate. Random UUIDv4 primary keys hurt B-tree locality, causing page splits and index bloat on high insert rates. My default now is UUIDv7 or ULID — time-ordered, so they keep insert locality like an integer while being globally unique and safe to generate on the client, which is exactly what an offline-first mobile app needs. If I must use v4, I keep it out of the clustering key.
@red
- "We enforce it in the application so we don't need constraints."
- Storing money as `float` or `double`.

### How do you write and manage database migrations?
@short
Migrations are versioned, ordered, reviewed SQL files checked into git and applied automatically by CI/CD. Every migration is forward-only in production practice, backwards-compatible with the currently deployed code, and tested on a copy of production-like data before it runs against production.
@deep
The rule that prevents most incidents is: never deploy a schema change and the code that requires it at the same time. Expand and contract — add the new column, deploy code that writes both, backfill, deploy code that reads the new one, then drop the old column in a later release. Each step is independently reversible.

Locking is the other production concern: on a large table, adding a column with a volatile default, adding a NOT NULL constraint, or creating an index without `CONCURRENTLY` takes a lock that stalls every query against that table.
@code sql Safe versus unsafe migrations on a large table
-- ❌ blocks all reads and writes on a 50M-row table for minutes
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_status ON orders (status);
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN region text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'IN';   -- (safe in PG11+, not before)
ALTER TABLE orders ALTER COLUMN email SET NOT NULL;                -- full table scan under lock

-- ✅ safe equivalents
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_orders_status ON orders (status);    -- no write lock (cannot be in a txn)

ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN region text;                          -- instant, nullable
-- backfill in batches so no single statement holds a long lock
UPDATE orders SET region = 'IN' WHERE region IS NULL AND id IN (
  SELECT id FROM orders WHERE region IS NULL LIMIT 10000);          -- repeat until done

ALTER TABLE orders ADD CONSTRAINT orders_email_nn CHECK (email IS NOT NULL) NOT VALID;
ALTER TABLE orders VALIDATE CONSTRAINT orders_email_nn;             -- validates without a write lock

-- Always set a lock timeout so a migration fails fast instead of queueing every query behind it
SET lock_timeout = '3s';
SET statement_timeout = '5min';
@end
@senior
The `lock_timeout` line is the one I would insist on. Without it, an `ALTER TABLE` that cannot get its lock waits — and every subsequent query on that table queues behind it, so a migration that "should be instant" takes the whole application down within seconds. With a short lock timeout the migration simply fails and you retry at a quieter moment. I would also mention testing migrations against a restored production snapshot, because a migration that runs in 40ms on 200 development rows can run for 40 minutes on 50 million.
@followup
How do you roll back a bad migration?
@answer
For additive changes, roll back the code and leave the schema — an unused column is harmless. For destructive changes there is often no true rollback, which is exactly why destructive steps are deferred: drop a column only after the code that used it has been gone for a release. If data was actually lost, recovery means point-in-time restore from backups, which is why I test restores rather than just taking backups. The real answer is that safety comes from sequencing, not from a `down` migration you have never run.
@red
- Creating indexes on large tables without `CONCURRENTLY`.
- Deploying a schema change and dependent code together.

### What is connection pooling and why does it matter?
@short
Opening a database connection costs a TCP handshake, TLS, authentication and in Postgres a whole new backend process — tens of milliseconds and several megabytes each. A pool keeps a small set of connections open and hands them out, so requests reuse them. Without a pool, connection setup dominates your latency and you exhaust `max_connections`.
@deep
The pool size is not "bigger is better". Each Postgres connection is an OS process with its own memory; a few hundred connections cause heavy context switching and can be slower than fifty. The usual guidance is a modest pool per instance, sized so that `instances × poolSize` stays well under `max_connections` with headroom for admin access.

Serverless breaks this model entirely: every concurrent invocation wants its own connection, so you need an external pooler — PgBouncer or RDS Proxy — in transaction mode.
@code ts Pool configuration and the maths that must hold
import { Pool } from 'pg';

export const pool = new Pool({
  connectionString: env.DATABASE_URL,
  max: 20,                         // per process
  min: 2,
  idleTimeoutMillis: 30_000,
  connectionTimeoutMillis: 5_000,  // fail fast rather than queue forever
  statement_timeout: 10_000,       // no runaway query holds a connection
  application_name: 'orders-api',  // shows in pg_stat_activity — invaluable during an incident
});

pool.on('error', (err) => logger.error({ err }, 'idle client error'));

// The constraint that must hold:
//   instances × max  +  background workers  +  migrations  +  5 for humans  <  max_connections
//   e.g.  6 × 20 = 120  + 10 + 2 + 5 = 137   <   200 ✅
@end
@senior
The failure mode to describe is pool exhaustion, because it is the most common "the API is down" cause I have seen: one slow query or one leaked client means every request queues waiting for a connection, health checks time out, the orchestrator restarts instances, the restarts reconnect en masse, and the database is now the bottleneck for a problem that started elsewhere. The defences are a connection timeout so requests fail fast instead of queueing, a statement timeout so no query can hold a connection indefinitely, releasing clients in `finally`, and monitoring `pg_stat_activity` for `idle in transaction` sessions — which is the signature of a forgotten `COMMIT`.
@followup
Why does PgBouncer transaction mode break some features?
@answer
Because in transaction pooling a client gets a server connection only for the duration of a transaction, so anything with session state does not survive: prepared statements, `SET` session variables, advisory locks, `LISTEN`/`NOTIFY` and temporary tables. Drivers usually need prepared statements disabled or a compatible protocol setting. It is still the right mode for serverless and high-concurrency workloads — you just have to know which features you are giving up.
@red
- Creating a new client per request.
- Setting pool max to 100 per instance across 10 instances.

### What is the difference between SQL and NoSQL, and how do you choose?
@short
Relational databases give you a fixed schema, joins, and strong transactional guarantees across rows and tables. Document stores give you flexible schemas and horizontal scale with data modelled around access patterns. I default to Postgres because it handles relational, JSON, full-text and vector workloads well, and I reach for a specialised store when there is a specific reason.
@deep
The honest comparison in 2026 is narrower than it used to be. Postgres has JSONB with indexing, full-text search, `pgvector` for embeddings, logical replication and partitioning; MongoDB has multi-document transactions. So the choice is less "SQL versus NoSQL" and more "does my access pattern need joins and constraints, or does it need a schema that varies per document and a sharded write path".

Where document stores genuinely win: deeply nested documents always read as a unit, extremely high write throughput with a natural shard key, and schemas that vary meaningfully per record.
@code sql Postgres doing the "NoSQL" job — JSONB with a GIN index
CREATE TABLE events (
  id         bigserial PRIMARY KEY,
  type       text NOT NULL,
  payload    jsonb NOT NULL,                 -- flexible, per-type shape
  created_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);

CREATE INDEX idx_events_payload ON events USING gin (payload jsonb_path_ops);
CREATE INDEX idx_events_type_time ON events (type, created_at DESC);

-- containment query, index-backed
SELECT * FROM events
 WHERE type = 'payment.failed'
   AND payload @> '{"provider":"stripe","code":"card_declined"}'
 ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 50;

-- extract typed values for reporting, still one database
SELECT payload->>'provider' AS provider, count(*)
  FROM events WHERE type = 'payment.failed' GROUP BY 1;
@end
@senior
The pragmatic position I would argue is "one database until it hurts". Every additional datastore is another thing to back up, monitor, patch, secure and reason about during an incident, and cross-store consistency becomes your problem. Postgres with JSONB covers the flexible-schema case, and the specialised stores earn their place when there is a measured need: Redis for sub-millisecond ephemeral state, Elasticsearch when Postgres full-text search stops being enough, ClickHouse when analytical scans dominate. Choosing MongoDB because "the schema might change" is usually choosing to defer schema decisions until they are expensive.
@followup
When has a document model genuinely been the right choice?
@answer
When the aggregate is always read and written whole and the shape varies — a CMS where each page type has different blocks, an audit log of heterogeneous events, or a product catalogue where categories have wildly different attributes. In those cases modelling relationally means either a sparse wide table or an entity-attribute-value schema, both of which are worse. The trade you accept is that cross-document consistency and ad-hoc joins become application concerns.
@red
- "NoSQL is faster" with no qualification.
- Choosing a document store to avoid designing a schema.

### How do you find and fix a slow query?
@short
Find it with `pg_stat_statements` or the slow query log, ranked by total time rather than by worst single execution. Explain it with `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)`, compare estimated versus actual rows to spot bad statistics, and look for sequential scans, expensive sorts and nested loops over large sets. Then fix the index, the query, or the data model.
@deep
Total time matters more than per-call time: a 40ms query run 200,000 times an hour costs far more than a 3-second report run twice a day, and it is usually easier to fix.

The reading order in a plan is inside-out and bottom-up. The numbers that matter are `actual rows` versus `rows` (a large gap means stale statistics or a correlation the planner cannot see), `loops` (a nested loop running 50,000 times is your N+1 in SQL form), and `Buffers: shared read` (actual disk I/O rather than cache hits).
@code sql The diagnosis workflow, end to end
-- 1. What is actually expensive? Rank by TOTAL time.
SELECT substring(query, 1, 90) AS q, calls,
       round(total_exec_time::numeric, 1) AS total_ms,
       round(mean_exec_time::numeric, 2)  AS mean_ms,
       rows
  FROM pg_stat_statements
 ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC LIMIT 20;

-- 2. Explain the offender with real execution data
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, FORMAT TEXT)
SELECT o.* FROM orders o
 WHERE o.user_id = '…' AND o.status = 'paid'
 ORDER BY o.created_at DESC LIMIT 20;

/* Seq Scan on orders  (cost=0..48210 rows=1 width=128)
                       (actual time=0.4..812.6 rows=18 loops=1)
     Filter: ((user_id = '…') AND (status = 'paid'))
     Rows Removed by Filter: 2431982          ← reading 2.4M rows to return 18
     Buffers: shared read=38210               ← 300MB of disk I/O per call     */

-- 3. Fix: an index that serves the predicate AND the sort
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_orders_user_status_created
    ON orders (user_id, status, created_at DESC);

-- 4. Refresh statistics and verify the new plan
ANALYZE orders;
-- Index Scan using idx_orders_user_status_created … actual time=0.05..0.21 rows=18
@end
@senior
Beyond indexes, the fixes I look for in order are: is the query returning columns nobody uses (`SELECT *` on a table with a large text or JSONB column is a hidden I/O cost); is it running N times when it could run once; can it be answered from a cache or a materialised view; and is the data model wrong for the access pattern. I would also say explicitly that not every slow query should be fixed — a report that runs nightly and takes 20 seconds is fine. The metric I optimise is user-visible p99 latency and database CPU, not the list of slow queries.
@followup
What does a high `Rows Removed by Filter` tell you?
@answer
That the database is reading far more rows than it returns, which means the index is either missing or not selective enough for this predicate. If an index exists but is not being used, the planner may believe the filter is unselective — check estimated versus actual rows, run `ANALYZE`, and consider a partial or composite index that matches the predicate more precisely. It is the single most useful line in a plan for deciding whether to add an index.
@red
- Optimising a query nobody runs often.
- Using `EXPLAIN` without `ANALYZE` and treating estimates as facts.

# SECTION PostgreSQL & MySQL | MySQL is your production engine, PostgreSQL your project engine — know exactly where they differ, and never answer for one in the other's syntax.

### What are the main differences between PostgreSQL and MySQL?
@short
Postgres is stricter and more feature-rich — real check constraints, transactional DDL, advanced index types (GIN, GiST, BRIN), window functions everywhere, JSONB, extensions like PostGIS and pgvector. MySQL with InnoDB is simpler, historically faster for straightforward read-heavy workloads, and clusters index data around the primary key.
@deep
The clustering difference has real consequences. InnoDB stores rows physically in primary-key order and every secondary index stores the primary key as its pointer — so a large or random primary key makes every secondary index bigger and slower. Postgres uses a heap plus separate indexes with a physical tuple id, so primary-key choice matters less for index size but more for insert locality.

Postgres's MVCC keeps old row versions in the table, which is why `VACUUM` exists; InnoDB keeps them in an undo log. Postgres also has transactional DDL — you can roll back a migration that includes `ALTER TABLE`, which MySQL cannot.
@code sql Behavioural differences that bite in practice
-- MySQL (non-strict historic modes) silently truncates; Postgres errors.
INSERT INTO users (name) VALUES (repeat('x', 500));   -- name varchar(255)
-- MySQL (old sql_mode): stored as 255 chars, warning only
-- Postgres: ERROR: value too long for type character varying(255)

-- Case sensitivity: MySQL's default collation is case-insensitive, Postgres is not
SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = 'A@B.COM';
-- MySQL: matches 'a@b.com'.  Postgres: does not — use citext or lower() + expression index.

-- Upsert syntax differs
-- Postgres
INSERT INTO counters (k, v) VALUES ($1, 1)
ON CONFLICT (k) DO UPDATE SET v = counters.v + 1;
-- MySQL
INSERT INTO counters (k, v) VALUES (?, 1)
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE v = v + 1;

-- Postgres-only power that often decides the choice
CREATE INDEX ON docs USING gin (to_tsvector('english', body));      -- full-text
CREATE INDEX ON items USING ivfflat (embedding vector_cosine_ops);  -- pgvector
@end
@senior
The reason I default to Postgres is consolidation: full-text search, JSONB, geospatial via PostGIS, vector similarity via pgvector, and LISTEN/NOTIFY all live in one database I already back up and monitor. On a RAG project that meant no separate vector database at all, which removed an entire operational surface. Where MySQL still makes sense is an existing MySQL estate with team expertise, or a managed platform where it is the cheaper tier — and that is a perfectly good reason. What I would not do is claim one is universally faster; the workload and the schema decide that.
@followup
What is `VACUUM` and why does it matter?
@answer
Postgres's MVCC leaves dead tuples behind on every update and delete; `VACUUM` reclaims that space and updates visibility maps, and `ANALYZE` refreshes planner statistics. Autovacuum handles this normally, but on a high-churn table it can fall behind, causing bloat — the table grows, indexes grow, and scans get slower even though the row count is flat. The signs are a growing `n_dead_tup` in `pg_stat_user_tables` and a table size far larger than its data. The fixes are tuning autovacuum thresholds per table, and avoiding long-running transactions, which prevent vacuuming of anything newer than their snapshot.
@red
- "Postgres is always better" with no workload reasoning.
- Not knowing about VACUUM or bloat.

### How do you design a schema for a multi-tenant application?
@short
Three models: a shared schema with a `tenant_id` column on every table, a schema per tenant, or a database per tenant. Shared schema scales to many tenants and is cheapest to operate; database-per-tenant gives the strongest isolation and easiest per-tenant restore. I default to shared schema with row-level security enforcing the boundary.
@deep
The risk in a shared schema is a missing `tenant_id` filter — one forgotten `WHERE` clause leaks another customer's data. That is why the filter should not depend on developer discipline: Postgres row-level security applies it in the database, so even a raw query or an ad-hoc psql session is constrained.

Database-per-tenant becomes operationally painful past a few dozen tenants: migrations must run N times, connection pools multiply, and monitoring fragments. Schema-per-tenant sits in between and has similar migration costs.
@code sql Row-level security — the tenant filter the application cannot forget
ALTER TABLE orders ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
ALTER TABLE orders FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;      -- applies to the table owner too

CREATE POLICY tenant_isolation ON orders
  USING (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id')::uuid);

-- The application sets the tenant once per request, on the pooled connection
await client.query('SET LOCAL app.tenant_id = $1', [req.user.tenantId]);   -- LOCAL = per transaction
const { rows } = await client.query('SELECT * FROM orders WHERE id = $1', [id]);
-- ↑ even without a tenant_id predicate, another tenant's row is invisible

-- Every tenant-scoped index leads with tenant_id
CREATE INDEX ON orders (tenant_id, created_at DESC);
@end
@senior
`SET LOCAL` inside a transaction is the detail that matters with connection pooling: a plain `SET` persists on the pooled connection and would leak the previous request's tenant to the next one — which is exactly the bug RLS was supposed to prevent. I would also mention noisy-neighbour management: one tenant running a huge export can starve everyone in a shared database, so per-tenant rate limits, statement timeouts and, for large customers, the option to move them to a dedicated database are part of the design. The migration path from shared to dedicated should exist before you need it.
@followup
How do you handle a tenant that wants their data in a specific region?
@answer
That is a data-residency requirement and it usually forces a per-region deployment: a separate database (often a separate stack) in that region, with a routing layer that resolves a tenant to their home region — typically at the DNS or gateway level using the tenant's identifier. Shared-schema-single-region cannot satisfy it. I would design the tenant lookup to be region-aware from the start, because retrofitting it means changing every entry point.
@red
- Relying on developers remembering the tenant filter.
- `SET` instead of `SET LOCAL` with a connection pool.

### How do you model and query time-series or event data?
@short
Append-only table, partitioned by time, indexed on `(entity_id, time DESC)`, with old partitions dropped or archived on a retention schedule. Pre-aggregate into rollup tables for dashboards rather than scanning raw events, and consider TimescaleDB or a column store once volume outgrows plain Postgres.
@deep
Partitioning is the key technique: `DROP TABLE` on an old partition is instant, whereas `DELETE FROM events WHERE created_at < …` on millions of rows is slow, bloats the table and generates enormous WAL. Partition pruning also means a query for last week only touches one or two partitions.

For dashboards, querying raw events per page load does not scale. Continuous aggregation into hourly and daily rollups turns a 50-million-row scan into a 700-row scan.
@code sql Declarative partitioning with rollups
CREATE TABLE location_points (
  id          bigserial,
  user_id     uuid        NOT NULL,
  recorded_at timestamptz NOT NULL,
  lat         double precision NOT NULL,
  lng         double precision NOT NULL,
  accuracy_m  real,
  PRIMARY KEY (id, recorded_at)
) PARTITION BY RANGE (recorded_at);

CREATE TABLE location_points_2026_08 PARTITION OF location_points
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-08-01') TO ('2026-09-01');
CREATE INDEX ON location_points_2026_08 (user_id, recorded_at DESC);

-- retention: instant, no bloat, no WAL storm
DROP TABLE location_points_2026_02;

-- hourly rollup the dashboard actually queries
CREATE TABLE trip_hourly (
  user_id uuid, hour timestamptz, points int, distance_m double precision,
  PRIMARY KEY (user_id, hour)
);
INSERT INTO trip_hourly
SELECT user_id, date_trunc('hour', recorded_at), count(*), sum(step_distance)
  FROM location_points WHERE recorded_at >= $1 AND recorded_at < $2
 GROUP BY 1, 2
ON CONFLICT (user_id, hour) DO UPDATE
   SET points = EXCLUDED.points, distance_m = EXCLUDED.distance_m;
@end
@senior
For a geolocation product the other design decision is write amplification: a device sending a point every five seconds is 17,000 rows per user per day, so I batch inserts (`COPY` or multi-row `INSERT`) rather than one statement per point, and I downsample — full resolution for 7 days, one point per minute for 90 days, hourly rollups forever. Storage cost and query cost both fall by orders of magnitude and no product requirement is lost. That downsampling policy is the kind of concrete answer that separates someone who has run this from someone who has designed it on a whiteboard.
@followup
When would you move off Postgres for this?
@answer
When ingest rate or retention makes it uneconomical — sustained hundreds of thousands of points per second, or years of raw retention with heavy analytical scans. Then TimescaleDB (still Postgres, with automatic partitioning, compression and continuous aggregates) is the smallest step, and ClickHouse is the right answer for genuinely analytical workloads because columnar storage and compression change the economics entirely. I would move only with measurements, since partitioned Postgres with rollups handles more than most people expect.
@red
- `DELETE` for retention on a huge table.
- Querying raw events for every dashboard load.

### How does replication work and what is read/write splitting?
@short
A primary accepts writes and streams its WAL to replicas that apply it. Reads can be routed to replicas to scale read capacity, but replicas lag — typically milliseconds, occasionally seconds — so a read immediately after a write may not see it. That read-after-write inconsistency is the main thing to design around.
@deep
Postgres streaming replication is asynchronous by default: the primary commits without waiting for replicas, so a failover can lose the last few transactions. Synchronous replication removes that risk at the cost of write latency, and it means a replica outage can block writes unless you configure quorum carefully.

Read/write splitting is implemented in the application or by a proxy. The application-level approach is explicit and predictable: writes and any read that must be consistent go to the primary; everything else goes to a replica.
@code ts Read/write split with an explicit consistency escape hatch
const primary = new Pool({ connectionString: env.DATABASE_URL });
const replica = new Pool({ connectionString: env.DATABASE_REPLICA_URL });

export const db = {
  write: primary,
  read:  (opts?: { consistent?: boolean }) => (opts?.consistent ? primary : replica),
};

// ✅ after a write, read from the primary within the same request
const order = await db.write.query('INSERT INTO orders … RETURNING *', […]);
const fresh = await db.read({ consistent: true }).query('SELECT … WHERE id = $1', [order.id]);

// ✅ dashboards, reports, search: replica is fine and takes load off the primary
const report = await db.read().query('SELECT … heavy aggregate …');

// Monitor lag — alert before users notice
SELECT client_addr, state,
       pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), replay_lsn) AS lag_bytes
  FROM pg_stat_replication;
@end
@senior
The pattern I would describe is "sticky reads after write": for a short window after a user writes, route their reads to the primary — either per-request as above, or by recording the write LSN in the session and only using a replica that has replayed past it. Without that, the user creates an order, is redirected to the order page, and gets a 404 — which is a support ticket, not a theoretical problem. I would also point out that read replicas do not help write-heavy workloads at all; for that you need sharding, batching or a different data model, and reaching for a replica is treating the wrong symptom.
@followup
How does failover work and what do you lose?
@answer
A managed service like RDS Multi-AZ promotes a standby automatically, usually within a minute or two, and the DNS endpoint moves. With asynchronous replication you can lose transactions that had committed on the primary but not yet reached the standby, which is your RPO. Applications must handle the connection break — pooled connections die, so the driver needs retry and the code needs to tolerate a failed in-flight transaction. I test failover deliberately rather than discovering the application's behaviour during a real one.
@red
- Assuming replicas are perfectly consistent.
- Sending all reads to a replica including read-after-write.

### What is `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` telling you?
@short
It runs the query and reports the actual plan with real timings, row counts and buffer usage per node. The most valuable signals are estimated versus actual rows (a large mismatch means bad statistics), the scan types, the loop counts, and whether sorts spilled to disk.
@deep
Read it inside-out: the deepest nodes execute first. `cost` is the planner's estimate in arbitrary units, `actual time` is real milliseconds, and `loops` multiplies — a node showing `actual time=0.02..0.05 rows=1 loops=50000` cost 2.5 seconds in total.

`BUFFERS` distinguishes cache hits from disk reads, which tells you whether the fix is an index or more memory. `Sort Method: external merge Disk: 82MB` means `work_mem` is too small for this query.
@code sql A plan, annotated
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT u.email, count(o.id)
  FROM users u LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.user_id = u.id
 WHERE u.created_at > now() - interval '30 days'
 GROUP BY u.email;

/*
 HashAggregate  (cost=…  rows=9800)  (actual time=1240.1..1250.4 rows=9812 loops=1)
   ->  Hash Right Join  (actual time=310.2..1080.5 rows=214883 loops=1)
         Hash Cond: (o.user_id = u.id)
         ->  Seq Scan on orders o  (actual time=0.01..640.2 rows=2431982 loops=1)
               Buffers: shared read=38210        ← 300MB read from DISK, not cache
         ->  Hash  (actual time=308.9..308.9 rows=9812 loops=1)
               ->  Index Scan using idx_users_created on users u
                     (actual time=0.03..298.1 rows=9812 loops=1)
 Planning Time: 0.4 ms
 Execution Time: 1251.9 ms
*/
-- Diagnosis: the full scan of `orders` dominates. It only needs user_id + id.
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_orders_user_id ON orders (user_id) INCLUDE (id);
-- → Index Only Scan; execution time drops to ~90ms
@end
@senior
The habit worth describing is comparing estimates to reality before changing anything. If the planner estimated 1 row and got 2.4 million, the plan choice was rational given wrong information — the fix is `ANALYZE`, raising the statistics target, or an extended statistics object for correlated columns, not forcing a different plan. Forcing plans with `enable_seqscan = off` is a debugging tool, never a production fix. I would also mention `auto_explain` for capturing plans of slow queries in production, since the plan you get in a quiet console often differs from the one that ran under load.
@followup
What is `work_mem` and how do you set it?
@answer
It is the memory available per sort or hash operation, per node, per connection — so a query with three sorts across 20 connections can use 60× `work_mem`. Setting it too high risks OOM under concurrency; too low forces spills to disk. I keep the global default modest and raise it per session for known heavy queries (`SET LOCAL work_mem = '256MB'` inside the reporting transaction), which is far safer than raising it globally.
@red
- Reading `EXPLAIN` without `ANALYZE` and trusting estimates.
- Ignoring the `loops` multiplier.

### How do you handle backups and disaster recovery?
@short
Automated daily snapshots plus continuous WAL archiving for point-in-time recovery, retained according to a stated policy, stored in a different account or region, encrypted — and restored regularly to prove they work. A backup you have never restored is a hypothesis, not a backup.
@deep
Two numbers define the design: RPO, how much data you can afford to lose, and RTO, how long you can be down. Daily snapshots alone give an RPO of up to 24 hours; adding WAL archiving brings it to seconds. RTO depends on restore speed, which for a large database can be hours — so if your RTO is 15 minutes you need a warm standby, not a restore.

Logical backups (`pg_dump`) are useful for single-table recovery and cross-version moves; physical snapshots are what you use for full recovery at scale.
@code bash A DR posture you can defend
# Continuous archiving (RPO ≈ seconds)
archive_mode = on
archive_command = 'aws s3 cp %p s3://acme-wal/%f --sse aws:kms'

# Point-in-time restore — the capability that saves you from a bad migration
pg_basebackup -D /restore -h primary -X stream
cat > /restore/recovery.signal <<'CONF'
restore_command = 'aws s3 cp s3://acme-wal/%f %p'
recovery_target_time = '2026-08-18 09:14:00+05:30'   # just before the bad DELETE
CONF

# Prove it: an automated monthly restore drill that asserts row counts and app boot
0 3 1 * *  /opt/dr/restore-drill.sh --assert-tables --assert-rowcount --notify-slack
@end
@senior
The point I always make is that point-in-time recovery is what protects you from *your own* mistakes, which are far more likely than hardware failure — an `UPDATE` without a `WHERE`, a migration that dropped the wrong column, a bad backfill. Snapshots alone force you back to midnight; PITR lets you restore to the second before. I would also mention that backups must live in a separate account with different credentials, because ransomware and a compromised deploy key both target the backups, and that per-tenant logical exports are worth having for the "one customer deleted their data" case, which is far more common than a full restore.
@followup
How do you restore just one table without a full restore?
@answer
Restore the snapshot into a temporary instance, `pg_dump` the single table from it, and load it back into production — usually into a staging table first, then reconcile. For a large database that is slow, which is the argument for logical per-table dumps of critical tables alongside physical backups. Doing this under pressure for the first time is where the drill pays off: the procedure should be a documented runbook, not improvisation.
@red
- Backups that have never been restored.
- Backups in the same account with the same credentials as production.

### How do you secure a production database?
@short
Never publicly reachable — private subnet, security group restricted to the application's security group, access via a bastion or SSM. TLS in transit, encryption at rest, least-privilege roles rather than a superuser for the app, secrets in a manager with rotation, and audit logging of access.
@deep
The application role should be able to do exactly what the application needs: `SELECT`, `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE` on its tables, and nothing else — no `DROP`, no superuser, no access to other schemas. Migrations run as a separate, higher-privileged role in CI, not as the runtime user.

Personal data adds requirements: column-level encryption or tokenisation for things like card details (which you should not store at all — that is what the payment provider's vault is for), and a documented retention and erasure process.
@code sql Least-privilege roles
CREATE ROLE app_runtime LOGIN PASSWORD :'pw';
GRANT CONNECT ON DATABASE acme TO app_runtime;
GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA public TO app_runtime;
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA public TO app_runtime;
GRANT USAGE ON ALL SEQUENCES IN SCHEMA public TO app_runtime;
ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES IN SCHEMA public
  GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON TABLES TO app_runtime;   -- future tables too

-- Migrations use a different role, only in CI
CREATE ROLE app_migrator LOGIN PASSWORD :'pw2';
GRANT ALL ON SCHEMA public TO app_migrator;

-- Read-only analytics role pointed at a replica
CREATE ROLE analytics LOGIN PASSWORD :'pw3' IN ROLE pg_read_all_data;

-- Enforce TLS
ALTER SYSTEM SET ssl = on;
-- pg_hba.conf:  hostssl  all  app_runtime  10.0.0.0/16  scram-sha-256
@end
@senior
Two additions. First, separating the migrator role means a compromised application cannot drop tables — it is a meaningful blast-radius reduction for a two-line change. Second, the highest-value control is usually not at the database at all: it is not storing the data. Card numbers belong in Stripe's vault, not your database; you store a token. Similarly, hashing rather than encrypting where you only need comparison, and truncating logs of personal data, reduce what an incident can expose. I would also enable `pgaudit` or at least connection logging, because "who accessed this table" is a question you will eventually be asked by a customer or a regulator.
@followup
What do you do if a database credential leaks?
@answer
Rotate immediately and assume it was used: revoke the old credential, issue a new one through the secret manager, redeploy, and then investigate — check `pg_stat_activity` and connection logs for unfamiliar source addresses, review audit logs for unusual queries, and check whether data was exfiltrated. Then fix the cause: how did a credential end up somewhere it could leak? Usually it is a `.env` in git, a log line, or an over-broad IAM role. And note the credential must be rotated even if the leak looks internal, because you cannot prove it was not copied.
@red
- One superuser credential used by the app, migrations and analysts.
- A publicly accessible RDS instance "temporarily".

# SECTION MongoDB & Redis | The non-relational stores on your résumé — Redis you run in production, so expect that one to go deeper.

### How do you model data in MongoDB — embed or reference?
@short
Embed when the child data is owned by the parent, always read with it, and bounded in size — order line items, an address on a user. Reference when the data is shared, unbounded, or updated independently — a product referenced by thousands of orders. The rule is "model around your access patterns", not around entity relationships.
@deep
Embedding gives you a single-document read with no join and atomic updates within the document. It breaks down when the array is unbounded — MongoDB has a 16MB document limit and, well before that, updating a document with a 50,000-element array is slow and lock-heavy.

The hybrid pattern is the extended reference: store the referenced id plus the two or three fields you display, so the common read needs no lookup, and accept that a rename requires a background update.
@code js Embed, reference, and the extended-reference hybrid
// ✅ embed: bounded, owned, always read together
{ _id: 'o_9', userId: 'u_1', status: 'paid',
  items: [ { productId: 'p_3', name: 'Keyboard', unitPriceMinor: 249900, qty: 1 } ],
  shipping: { line1: '…', city: 'Kochi', pin: '682030' } }

// ❌ unbounded embed — this document grows forever and will hit 16MB
{ _id: 'u_1', email: '…', locationPoints: [ /* 4 million points */ ] }

// ✅ reference for unbounded / high-churn children
{ _id: 'lp_1', userId: 'u_1', at: ISODate('…'), loc: { type: 'Point', coordinates: [76.3, 9.9] } }
db.locationPoints.createIndex({ userId: 1, at: -1 });
db.locationPoints.createIndex({ loc: '2dsphere' });          // geospatial queries

// ✅ extended reference: denormalise the display fields only
{ _id: 'o_9', product: { _id: 'p_3', name: 'Keyboard', slug: 'keyboard' } }
@end
@senior
The senior framing is that MongoDB rewards you for knowing the access pattern in advance and punishes you when it changes — the schema flexibility is at the field level, not at the relationship level, and reshaping a billion embedded documents is a migration project. That is the honest trade against a relational model where a new join is just a new query. I would also point out that MongoDB has had multi-document ACID transactions since 4.0, so "no transactions" is out of date, but they are more expensive than a single-document update, so the idiomatic answer is still to model so that one document is one unit of consistency.
@followup
What does the aggregation pipeline give you?
@answer
A staged transformation — `$match`, `$group`, `$lookup`, `$project`, `$sort`, `$facet` — that runs in the database rather than in application code. The performance rules are: `$match` and `$sort` as early as possible so indexes are used and less data flows downstream, avoid `$lookup` on large collections since it is effectively a nested loop join, and watch the 100MB per-stage memory limit — exceeded stages need `allowDiskUse`, which is much slower. `explain('executionStats')` is the equivalent of `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`.
@red
- Embedding an unbounded array.
- Designing the schema before knowing the queries.

### What is Redis and what do you actually use it for?
@short
An in-memory data structure server — strings, hashes, lists, sets, sorted sets, streams, bitmaps, HyperLogLog — with sub-millisecond latency. I use it for caching, session storage, rate limiting, distributed locks, pub/sub fan-out, job queues via BullMQ, and leaderboards or feeds via sorted sets.
@deep
The reason it is fast is that it is single-threaded and in-memory: every command is atomic with no locking, and there is no disk in the hot path. That single thread is also the constraint — one `KEYS *` or a big `SORT` blocks every other client, which is why `SCAN` exists.

Persistence is optional and configurable: RDB snapshots (fast restart, can lose the window between snapshots) and AOF (append-only log, better durability, larger and slower). Redis is a cache first; treating it as a source of truth requires deliberately choosing and testing the durability settings.
@code ts The patterns worth knowing, in one place
// 1. Cache-aside with a TTL and jitter (jitter prevents synchronised expiry stampedes)
const cached = await redis.get(key);
if (cached) return JSON.parse(cached);
const fresh = await loadFromDb();
await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(fresh), 'EX', 300 + Math.floor(Math.random() * 60));

// 2. Distributed lock — token-guarded so you can only release YOUR lock
const token = randomUUID();
const ok = await redis.set(`lock:${job}`, token, 'NX', 'PX', 30_000);
if (ok) {
  try { await runJob(); }
  finally {
    await redis.eval(`if redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1]
                      then return redis.call('DEL', KEYS[1]) else return 0 end`,
                     1, `lock:${job}`, token);
  }
}

// 3. Sorted set: leaderboard / recent feed, O(log n) insert, O(log n + k) range
await redis.zadd('feed:u_1', Date.now(), postId);
await redis.zremrangebyrank('feed:u_1', 0, -501);         // keep the newest 500
const feed = await redis.zrevrange('feed:u_1', 0, 19);

// 4. Pub/sub fan-out for WebSocket servers
await pub.publish(`room:${roomId}`, JSON.stringify(msg));
sub.subscribe(`room:${roomId}`);                          // every instance receives it
@end
@senior
Two things I would raise. The lock example uses a random token and a Lua compare-and-delete — without that, a slow process whose lock has already expired can delete a lock now held by someone else, which silently breaks mutual exclusion. And Redis pub/sub is fire-and-forget with no persistence: a subscriber that is disconnected for two seconds loses those messages permanently. For WebSocket fan-out that is usually acceptable; for anything that must not be lost, Redis Streams with consumer groups (or a real broker) gives you acknowledgement and replay. Knowing that boundary is the difference between using Redis and misusing it.
@followup
