What happens when Redis runs out of memory?
@answer
It applies the configured `maxmemory-policy`. `noeviction` (the default) starts rejecting writes, which will break your application in a confusing way; `allkeys-lru` evicts the least recently used key, which is what you want for a pure cache; `volatile-lru` only evicts keys that have a TTL, which is right when you mix cache data with data that must not disappear. Choosing this deliberately is important — I have seen a "cache" outage that was really a `noeviction` Redis refusing writes while looking healthy on every dashboard.
@red
- Using `KEYS *` in production.
- Treating Redis pub/sub as a reliable queue.

### How do you design a cache, and what are the invalidation strategies?
@short
Cache-aside is the default: check the cache, on a miss read the database and populate it with a TTL. Invalidate by writing through, by deleting the key on write, or by letting the TTL expire. The two hard problems are staleness and stampedes, and both need explicit handling.
@deep
Write-through keeps the cache correct at the cost of write latency; write-behind is fast but can lose data; cache-aside with delete-on-write is the pragmatic middle and what most systems use. The subtlety is ordering: update the database first, then delete the cache key — deleting first leaves a window where a concurrent read repopulates the stale value.

A stampede happens when a hot key expires and a thousand concurrent requests all miss and hit the database simultaneously. The fixes are a short lock so only one request recomputes, or serving stale data while one request refreshes in the background.
@code ts Cache-aside with single-flight protection against a stampede
async function getProduct(id: string) {
  const key = `product:${id}`;
  const hit = await redis.get(key);
  if (hit) return JSON.parse(hit);

  // single-flight: only one caller recomputes; others wait briefly and re-read
  const lock = await redis.set(`${key}:lock`, '1', 'NX', 'PX', 5_000);
  if (!lock) {
    await sleep(50);
    const retry = await redis.get(key);
    if (retry) return JSON.parse(retry);
  }

  const fresh = await db.product.findUnique({ where: { id } });
  if (!fresh) {
    await redis.set(key, 'null', 'EX', 30);       // negative caching: stop repeated misses
    return null;
  }
  await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(fresh), 'EX', 600 + jitter());
  await redis.del(`${key}:lock`);
  return fresh;
}

// Invalidate on write — DB first, then cache
await db.product.update({ where: { id }, data });
await redis.del(`product:${id}`);
await redis.del(`products:list`);                 // and any derived keys
@end
@senior
Negative caching is the detail people miss: without it, a request for a non-existent id misses the cache every time and hits the database every time, which is a trivially exploitable amplification vector. The other senior point is cache key design — derived keys like list pages are the hard part of invalidation, and my preference is to include a version in the key namespace (`products:v3:list:…`) so bumping the version invalidates everything derived in one operation rather than trying to enumerate keys. And I always ask what happens when the cache is empty: if the system cannot survive a cold cache, the cache is load-bearing infrastructure, not an optimisation, and needs the same availability treatment as the database.
@followup
How do you decide what to cache?
@answer
Read-heavy, expensive to compute, and tolerant of some staleness — that combination is where caching pays. I look at the slow-query list crossed with call frequency: a 300ms query called 5,000 times a minute is the obvious candidate. What I do not cache is anything where staleness has a business cost measured in money or trust — a payment status, an inventory count at checkout, a permission check — because a stale answer there is worse than a slow one.
@red
- Caching with no TTL and no invalidation path.
- Deleting the cache key before committing the write.

### What are Redis Streams and how do they compare to a queue?
@short
A Stream is an append-only log with consumer groups: messages persist, each consumer group tracks its own position, and messages must be acknowledged. Compared with pub/sub it gives durability, replay and at-least-once delivery; compared with a full broker like Kafka or SQS it is simpler but has less tooling and weaker retention guarantees.
@deep
The consumer group model means several workers can process a stream in parallel, each getting distinct messages, with unacknowledged messages visible in the pending entries list so a crashed consumer's work can be claimed by another. That is the property a plain list-based queue (`LPUSH`/`BRPOP`) lacks — with a list, a worker that dies mid-job loses the message.

BullMQ builds on Redis and gives you retries with backoff, delayed jobs, priorities, rate limiting and a dashboard, which is usually what you actually want rather than raw streams.
@code ts BullMQ for background work — the practical choice
import { Queue, Worker } from 'bullmq';

const emails = new Queue('emails', { connection });

await emails.add('receipt', { orderId }, {
  jobId: `receipt:${orderId}`,          // ✅ dedupe key — enqueueing twice is a no-op
  attempts: 5,
  backoff: { type: 'exponential', delay: 2000 },
  removeOnComplete: 1000,
  removeOnFail: 5000,
});

new Worker('emails', async (job) => {
  await sendReceipt(job.data.orderId);   // MUST be idempotent — at-least-once delivery
}, { connection, concurrency: 10, limiter: { max: 100, duration: 1000 } });

// Failed beyond attempts → inspect and replay from the dead-letter view
@end
@senior
The two things I would emphasise are the `jobId` deduplication key and idempotent handlers. At-least-once delivery means your handler *will* run twice eventually — a retry after a timeout that actually succeeded, or a duplicate enqueue from a retried API call — so the handler must tolerate it, usually by checking or recording a unique effect id. The other point is that Redis-backed queues are only as durable as your Redis persistence and failover configuration; for anything where losing a job means losing money, I would use SQS or a broker with stronger guarantees, or at minimum write the job intent to Postgres via the outbox and treat Redis as the delivery mechanism.
@followup
When would you choose Kafka over Redis Streams or SQS?
@answer
When you need high-throughput event streaming with long retention and multiple independent consumers replaying the same log — event sourcing, analytics pipelines, change data capture. Kafka's partitioned log with per-consumer-group offsets is designed for that. SQS is the right choice for simple decoupled task queues where you want zero operational burden. Redis Streams sit in between and make most sense when Redis is already in your stack and the volume is moderate. Kafka is a significant operational commitment, so I would not introduce it for a job queue.
@red
- Using a plain Redis list as a queue and losing jobs on worker crashes.
- Assuming exactly-once delivery.

### How do you scale Redis?
@short
Vertically first, because a single Redis instance handles enormous throughput. Then replicas for read scaling and failover via Sentinel, and Redis Cluster for sharding when the dataset exceeds one machine's memory. Cluster mode constrains you: multi-key operations must be in the same hash slot, which you control with hash tags.
@deep
Redis Cluster shards keys across nodes by CRC16 of the key modulo 16,384 slots. A multi-key command like `MGET a b c` only works if all keys are in the same slot, which is why you use hash tags — `user:{u_1}:profile` and `user:{u_1}:settings` hash on the braced part and therefore land together.

Replication is asynchronous, so a failover can lose the most recent writes. That is acceptable for cache data and unacceptable for a counter you bill on.
@code ts Cluster-safe key design and pipelining
// ❌ in cluster mode these are on different nodes — MGET / MULTI will fail
redis.mget('user:u_1:profile', 'user:u_1:settings');

// ✅ hash tag forces them into the same slot
redis.mget('user:{u_1}:profile', 'user:{u_1}:settings');

// Pipelining: one round trip instead of N — the single biggest client-side win
const pipe = redis.pipeline();
ids.forEach(id => pipe.get(`product:${id}`));
const results = await pipe.exec();

// Lua for atomic multi-step logic — runs on the server, no round trips, no races
await redis.eval(SCRIPT, 1, `bucket:{${userId}}`, capacity, refill, Date.now());
@end
@senior
The practical advice is that most teams reach for Cluster far too early. A single Redis node with 32GB and pipelining handles well over 100,000 operations per second, which is more than most applications will ever need; the usual real problem is a slow command blocking the single thread or an unbounded key space, not raw capacity. So my order of operations is: measure with `SLOWLOG` and `INFO commandstats`, remove `KEYS`/big `SORT`/large `HGETALL`, set TTLs and an eviction policy, pipeline aggressively, then add replicas, and only shard when memory genuinely exceeds one machine. Introducing Cluster costs you multi-key atomicity, which is a real functionality loss.
@followup
How do you handle a Redis failover in the application?
@answer
Use a client with Sentinel or Cluster support so it rediscovers the new primary automatically, keep retries with backoff on transient errors, and — most importantly — decide the fallback behaviour per use case. For a cache, fall through to the database and log; the site is slower but works. For a rate limiter on an expensive endpoint, fail closed. For session storage, a Redis outage logs everyone out, which is why I prefer a signed JWT for the access path and Redis only for revocation, so a Redis blip degrades rather than breaks authentication.
@red
- Sharding before measuring.
- No plan for what the app does when Redis is unavailable.

# SECTION Git & Version Control | Asked briefly, but a wrong answer about rebase or reset is memorable for the wrong reason.

### What is the difference between merge and rebase?
@short
`merge` creates a commit joining two histories and preserves exactly what happened. `rebase` replays your commits on top of another branch, producing a linear history but rewriting commit hashes. My rule: rebase your own local feature branch to keep it current, merge to integrate into a shared branch, and never rebase anything already pushed and used by others.
@deep
Rebase rewrites history — every replayed commit gets a new hash, so anyone who pulled the old commits now has a divergent branch and will produce duplicate commits when they merge. That is why the "golden rule" is do not rebase public branches.

Squash-merging a feature branch is a middle path many teams use: the branch keeps its messy history for review, and `main` gets one clean, revertible commit per feature.
@code bash Everyday workflow, including the recovery command
# Keep a feature branch current without a merge commit
git fetch origin
git rebase origin/main               # replay my commits on the latest main
# conflict → fix files → git add . → git rebase --continue
# gone wrong → git rebase --abort

# Rewrote something you regret? reflog is the undo button for almost everything.
git reflog                           # every HEAD position for ~90 days
git reset --hard HEAD@{4}            # go back to before the bad rebase

# Integrate: squash for a clean, revertible main
git switch main && git merge --squash feature/payments && git commit

# Force-push a rebased branch SAFELY (fails if someone else pushed meanwhile)
git push --force-with-lease
@end
@senior
`--force-with-lease` instead of `--force` is the detail worth knowing: it refuses the push if the remote has commits you have not seen, which prevents you from silently destroying a colleague's work. And `git reflog` is the answer to almost every "I destroyed my work" panic — commits are not deleted immediately, so a bad reset or rebase is recoverable for weeks. I would also state a team position: history is a communication tool, so I care that `main` is readable and each commit is revertible, and I am not precious about how messy a feature branch was before review.
@followup
What does `git cherry-pick` do and when is it appropriate?
@answer
It applies a single commit's changes onto the current branch as a new commit. It is right for hotfixes — fix on `main`, cherry-pick onto the release branch — and it is a smell when used routinely to move work between branches, because you end up with the same change under two hashes and confusing merge behaviour later. If I find myself cherry-picking a lot, the branching strategy is wrong.
@red
- Rebasing a shared branch.
- Using `--force` habitually.

### Explain `git reset` soft, mixed and hard.
@short
All three move the branch pointer. `--soft` keeps the changes staged, `--mixed` (the default) keeps them in the working directory but unstaged, and `--hard` discards them entirely. `--hard` is the only destructive one and it is how people lose uncommitted work.
@deep
The three-tree model makes this clear: there is HEAD (the last commit), the index (staged), and the working tree. `--soft` moves only HEAD; `--mixed` moves HEAD and resets the index; `--hard` moves all three.

`git revert` is the safe alternative for anything already pushed: it creates a new commit that undoes the change, so history is preserved and collaborators are unaffected.
@code bash Which command for which situation
git reset --soft HEAD~1     # undo the commit, keep everything staged — fix the message or re-split
git reset HEAD~1            # undo the commit, keep changes unstaged  (--mixed, the default)
git reset --hard HEAD~1     # ⚠️ undo the commit AND throw the changes away
git restore --staged file   # unstage one file, keep the edit
git restore file            # ⚠️ discard local edits to one file

# Already pushed? Do NOT reset — revert.
git revert <sha>            # new commit that undoes it; safe on shared branches
git revert -m 1 <merge-sha> # revert a merge commit, keeping mainline

# Park work without committing
git stash push -m "wip: refund flow"
git stash pop
@end
@senior
The rule I apply is: rewrite history only where it is private, revert where it is public. In an incident that distinction matters — reverting a bad deploy commit on `main` is instant, reviewable and leaves an audit trail of what happened and why, whereas force-pushing a reset breaks everyone's clone and erases the evidence. I would also mention that `git revert` on a merge commit needs `-m 1` to say which parent is mainline, which is exactly the kind of thing you do not want to be looking up for the first time at 2am.
@followup
How do you undo a commit that has already been deployed?
@answer
`git revert` it, let CI build and deploy the revert, and confirm recovery — that is the fastest safe path and it is why every commit on `main` should be independently revertible. If the change included a database migration, the revert must be planned separately, because you usually cannot un-drop a column; that is the reason destructive migrations are deferred to a later release than the code that stops using them.
@red
- `git reset --hard` on shared work.
- Not knowing `revert` exists.

### What branching strategy do you use?
@short
Trunk-based development with short-lived feature branches and pull requests, protected `main`, and feature flags for anything not ready to be visible. Long-lived branches accumulate merge pain; small branches merged daily keep integration cheap.
@deep
GitFlow's `develop`/`release`/`hotfix` structure suits products with scheduled versioned releases, but for a continuously deployed web service it adds ceremony and delays integration. Trunk-based with flags means the deployment and the release become separate events — you can deploy code all day and turn the feature on when it is ready.

Branch protection is the enforcement layer: required review, required status checks, no force-push, and a linear history requirement if that is the team's convention.
@code yaml Branch protection plus a CI gate that actually blocks bad merges
# .github/workflows/ci.yml
on: pull_request
jobs:
  verify:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with: { node-version: 22, cache: npm }
      - run: npm ci
      - run: npm run lint
      - run: npx tsc --noEmit                 # types checked separately from the build
      - run: npm run test -- --coverage
      - run: npm audit --audit-level=high
      - run: npx size-limit                   # bundle budget — fails on regression

# Required on main: verify ✅ · 1 approval · no force-push · up-to-date before merge
@end
@senior
The point I would make is that branching strategy is downstream of deployment strategy. If you can deploy safely many times a day — good tests, feature flags, fast rollback — you do not need release branches, and trunk-based is strictly better. If deployment is risky and infrequent, GitFlow's structure is compensating for that, and the real improvement is making deployment safer rather than managing branches more elaborately. Feature flags are what make this work, and they need a lifecycle: every flag gets an owner and a removal date, or you accumulate permanent dead branches in the code.
@followup
How do you handle a hotfix in production?
@answer
Branch from the deployed tag, make the smallest possible change, get an expedited review (still a review), deploy, verify, and then merge back so `main` contains the fix. If the problem is a recent deploy, rolling back is usually faster and safer than fixing forward, so my first question in an incident is "can we revert?" — fixing forward under pressure is how a small incident becomes a large one.
@red
- Long-lived branches merged after weeks.
- Committing directly to `main` without review.

### How do you write good commits and pull requests?
@short
One logical change per commit, an imperative subject under about 60 characters, and a body explaining *why* rather than what. A pull request should be small enough to review carefully — a few hundred lines — with a description covering the problem, the approach, the testing done and any risk.
@deep
Commit quality has direct operational value: `git bisect` finds the commit that introduced a bug in logarithmic time, but only if each commit builds and is a coherent change. A commit that mixes a refactor with a behavioural fix makes bisect useless and makes the fix impossible to revert cleanly.

Conventional Commits (`feat:`, `fix:`, `chore:`) add machine readability, which lets you generate changelogs and derive semantic version bumps automatically.
@code text A commit message worth writing
fix(payments): dedupe Stripe webhooks by event id

Stripe retries a webhook until it receives a 2xx. Our handler was
processing each delivery, so a slow response produced duplicate
order.paid transitions and, in two cases, duplicate fulfilment.

Store event.id in processed_webhook_events with a unique index and
insert it inside the same transaction as the order update, so a
replay is a no-op rather than a second write.

Verified with a replayed delivery in staging (evt_1P2x…): first
delivery 201, second returns 200 with no state change.

Refs: INC-482
Co-authored-by: …
@end
@senior
The sentence I would highlight is "explaining why": six months later, `git blame` on a strange-looking line should tell you the reason it exists, and that is the difference between a codebase you can change confidently and one where nobody dares touch anything. On pull requests, I would say the review is about correctness and comprehensibility, not style — formatting belongs to Prettier and ESLint in CI so humans never discuss it. And large PRs get worse reviews, empirically; splitting a 2,000-line change into five reviewable ones catches more real problems.
@followup
How do you review a pull request well?
@answer
Start from the description and the tests to understand the intent, then read the diff for correctness — error paths, concurrency, boundary conditions, and what happens on retry or partial failure. I check for security (authorisation, injection, secrets) and for operational impact (migrations, N+1s, unbounded queries). I leave the nitpicks as explicit suggestions rather than blockers, and I ask questions rather than issue instructions when I am not sure — a review is a conversation, and the author usually knows something I do not.
@red
- "fix stuff" commit messages.
- 3,000-line pull requests.

### How do you resolve merge conflicts confidently?
@short
Understand both sides before editing: what was the intent of each change? Then edit the file to the correct combined result, run the tests, and commit. Tooling helps — a three-way merge view, `git rerere` for repeated conflicts, and `git merge --abort` when you want to start again.
@deep
Conflicts are a symptom of parallel edits to the same lines, so the structural fix is smaller branches and more frequent integration. When they do happen, the danger is resolving by picking one side wholesale, which silently drops the other change — the file compiles, the tests may pass, and a feature quietly disappears.

`git checkout --ours`/`--theirs` is a blunt instrument that is right for generated files (lockfiles, snapshots) and wrong for hand-written code.
@code bash Working through a conflict
git rebase origin/main
# CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in src/orders/service.ts

git diff --name-only --diff-filter=U       # which files are conflicted
git log --merge -p src/orders/service.ts   # the commits from BOTH sides that touched it

# For a lockfile, regenerate rather than hand-merge
git checkout --theirs package-lock.json && npm install

# After editing to the correct combined behaviour
npm test
git add src/orders/service.ts && git rebase --continue

git config rerere.enabled true    # remembers resolutions; repeated conflicts auto-resolve
@end
@senior
The habit I would describe is running the tests after every non-trivial conflict resolution and, for anything touching business logic, reading the resulting function end-to-end rather than just the conflicted hunk — conflicts are exactly where subtle logic loss happens because the diff view hides context. `git rerere` is genuinely useful on a long-running rebase where the same conflict recurs on each replayed commit. And the meta-point stands: if conflicts are frequent, the fix is architectural or process-level, not a better merge tool.
@followup
Two people renamed the same file differently — how do you resolve it?
@answer
Git tracks content, not renames, so it reports this as an add/add or a rename/rename conflict. I pick one name, `git rm` the other, verify every import across the codebase (this is where a typed language earns its keep — `tsc --noEmit` finds every broken import immediately), and run the tests. The prevention is communication: renames and large refactors get their own small, quickly merged pull request rather than riding along with a feature.
@red
- Blindly taking `--ours` on source files.
- Not running tests after resolving.

### What is `.gitignore`, and what do you do if a secret was committed?
@short
`.gitignore` prevents untracked files from being staged — `node_modules`, build output, `.env`, local editor files. It does nothing for files already tracked. If a secret was committed, the only correct first step is to rotate it, because it is in the history, in every clone, and very likely already scraped by an automated crawler.
@deep
Removing it from history with `git filter-repo` or BFG rewrites every affected commit, which forces everyone to re-clone and does not recall the copies that already exist — on forks, on CI caches, in GitHub's own dangling-commit storage that remains reachable by SHA.

Prevention is the actual answer: secret scanning in CI, pre-commit hooks, and `.env.example` with placeholder values committed instead of the real file.
@code bash Response and prevention
# 1. ROTATE FIRST. Always. Assume it is compromised.
aws secretsmanager rotate-secret --secret-id prod/db

# 2. Stop tracking it (this alone does NOT remove it from history)
git rm --cached .env && echo ".env" >> .gitignore && git commit -m "chore: untrack .env"

# 3. Only if genuinely required, scrub history (coordinate with the whole team)
git filter-repo --path .env --invert-paths
git push --force-with-lease --all

# 4. Prevent recurrence
# .pre-commit-config.yaml → gitleaks protect --staged
# CI: gitleaks detect --redact --exit-code 1
@end
@senior
The judgement to demonstrate is prioritising rotation over history rewriting. Rewriting feels like fixing it and is mostly theatre — GitHub keeps unreachable commits accessible by SHA, forks retain them, and a crawler on a public repository will have found an AWS key within minutes. So: rotate, check for unauthorised use in CloudTrail or the provider's audit log, then decide whether the history rewrite is worth the disruption. I would also treat it as a process failure and add scanning, rather than as an individual mistake.
@followup
What belongs in `.gitignore` versus a global ignore?
@answer
Project-specific artefacts — `node_modules`, `dist`, `.next`, `coverage`, `.env` — go in the repository's `.gitignore`, because they apply to everyone. Personal tooling noise — `.DS_Store`, `.idea/`, editor swap files — belongs in a global ignore (`~/.gitignore_global`), because it is about your machine, not the project. Putting personal preferences in the shared file is a small thing that adds up to noise for everyone else.
@red
- Deleting the commit and not rotating the secret.
- `node_modules` committed to the repository.

### How do you use tags, releases and semantic versioning?
@short
Annotated tags mark released commits; semantic versioning communicates the nature of a change — major for breaking, minor for additive, patch for fixes. For a deployed service the version mainly serves traceability: knowing exactly which commit is running is essential during an incident.
@deep
The most operationally valuable use is embedding the version and commit SHA into the build and reporting it — in a `/version` endpoint, in log lines and in Sentry release tagging. That turns "when did this start?" into a single query, and it lets error tracking attribute a regression to a specific deploy.

For libraries, semver is a contract with consumers and breaking it erodes trust quickly; for applications, date-based or build-number versioning is often more honest.
@code bash Version traceability from tag to running process
git tag -a v2.4.0 -m "Refund flow + Razorpay webhooks"
git push origin v2.4.0

# Bake identity into the image
docker build \
  --build-arg GIT_SHA=$(git rev-parse --short HEAD) \
  --build-arg VERSION=$(git describe --tags) -t acme/api:v2.4.0 .

# Expose it — the first thing you check in an incident
app.get('/version', (_req, res) => res.json({
  version: process.env.VERSION, commit: process.env.GIT_SHA,
  builtAt: process.env.BUILD_TIME, node: process.version,
}));

Sentry.init({ release: process.env.VERSION });    // errors attributed to a deploy
@end
@senior
The `/version` endpoint sounds trivial and is disproportionately useful: during an incident the first question is always "what is actually running where", and a canary or a stuck rolling deploy means different instances answer differently. Combined with Sentry release tagging and source maps uploaded per release, you get from a production stack trace to the exact line in the exact commit in seconds. I would also mention that tags should be created by CI on merge rather than by hand, so they cannot drift from what was built.
@followup
How do you automate releases and changelogs?
@answer
Conventional Commits plus a tool like semantic-release or changesets: CI parses commit messages since the last tag, determines the version bump, generates the changelog, creates the tag and publishes. It removes a manual step that people forget and makes the changelog a by-product of work already done. The prerequisite is commit discipline, which is a good forcing function anyway.
@red
- Deploying untagged commits with no way to identify what is running.
- Bumping the major version for a bug fix.

# SECTION Linux, Docker & DevOps Basics | Enough to be dangerous on a production box — and enough to not be dangerous.

### Which Linux commands do you use when a server is misbehaving?
@short
`top`/`htop` for CPU and memory, `df -h` and `du -sh` for disk, `free -h` for memory pressure, `ss -tlnp` for listening ports, `journalctl -u service -f` or `tail -f` for logs, `lsof` for file descriptors, and `strace`/`ps` when I need to know what a process is actually doing.
@deep
The first three questions in any server incident are: is it CPU, is it memory, or is it disk? Load average above the core count means CPU or I/O saturation; high `wa` in `top` means the process is blocked on disk; a full disk causes bizarre failures because logs, temp files and database writes all fail at once.

File descriptor exhaustion is the sneaky one for Node services — every socket and file is an fd, and the default limit of 1024 is hit quickly by a leak.
@code bash The triage sequence I actually run
uptime                       # load average vs core count — 1/5/15 min trend
top -o %CPU                  # who is burning CPU? high %wa = I/O bound, not CPU bound
free -h                      # is swap being used? swapping = latency cliff
df -h && df -i               # ⚠️ check INODES too — a full inode table looks like free disk
du -sh /var/log/* | sort -h | tail    # what filled the disk
ss -tlnp                     # what is listening, and which pid owns it
ss -s                        # socket summary — TIME_WAIT explosion?
lsof -p $(pgrep -f node) | wc -l      # fd count vs `ulimit -n`
journalctl -u api -n 200 --no-pager   # recent service logs
dmesg -T | tail              # OOM killer? "Out of memory: Killed process"
@end
@senior
`dmesg | grep -i oom` is the one that resolves the most confusing incidents: a container that "randomly restarts" is usually the kernel OOM killer terminating the process because it exceeded its memory limit, and nothing in the application logs will say so — the process is killed with SIGKILL and gets no chance to log. Similarly `df -i` catches inode exhaustion, which presents as "no space left on device" while `df -h` shows plenty free, typically caused by millions of small session or cache files. Knowing those two saves hours.
@followup
A process is using 100% CPU — how do you find out why?
@answer
Identify the thread with `top -H -p <pid>`, then for a Node process attach a profiler: send `SIGUSR1` to open the inspector and capture a CPU profile, or run with `--cpu-prof`. The flame graph shows the hot stack directly. For a non-Node process, `perf top -p <pid>` gives the same picture at the native level. What I avoid is guessing — a CPU profile takes two minutes and removes the guesswork entirely.
@red
- Only checking `df -h` and missing inodes.
- Not knowing about the OOM killer.

### What is Docker and how is it different from a virtual machine?
@short
A container is an isolated process on the host kernel, using namespaces for isolation and cgroups for resource limits. A VM virtualises hardware and runs its own kernel. Containers start in milliseconds and are megabytes; VMs take seconds to minutes and are gigabytes, but give stronger isolation.
@deep
Because containers share the host kernel, a Linux container cannot run on a Windows kernel natively — Docker Desktop runs a Linux VM underneath. It also means a kernel vulnerability is a shared risk, which is why multi-tenant untrusted workloads use VMs or lightweight sandboxes like Firecracker.

The image is a stack of read-only layers with a thin writable layer on top; identical layers are shared between images and cached between builds, which is what makes builds and pulls fast when you order your Dockerfile well.
@code dockerfile A production Node image — small, cached, non-root
# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1
FROM node:22-alpine AS deps
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.npm npm ci --omit=dev

FROM node:22-alpine AS build
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
RUN npm ci
COPY . .
RUN npm run build

FROM node:22-alpine AS runtime
WORKDIR /app
ENV NODE_ENV=production
RUN addgroup -S app && adduser -S app -G app
COPY --from=deps  /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY --from=build /app/dist ./dist
USER app                                    # ✅ never run as root
EXPOSE 3000
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s --start-period=20s \
  CMD node -e "fetch('http://127.0.0.1:3000/healthz').then(r=>process.exit(r.ok?0:1)).catch(()=>process.exit(1))"
CMD ["node", "dist/index.js"]
@end
@senior
The two details I would defend are layer ordering and the multi-stage split. Copying `package*.json` and installing before copying source means a code change reuses the cached dependency layer — that alone often turns a three-minute build into fifteen seconds. The multi-stage build keeps dev dependencies and build tools out of the final image, which both shrinks it and removes attack surface. Running as a non-root user is the other non-negotiable: a container escape from root is a far worse day than from an unprivileged user, and it costs two lines.
@followup
Why is my container image 1.5GB?
@answer
Usually: a full `node:22` base instead of `alpine` or `slim`, dev dependencies included, the build context copying `node_modules` and `.git` (fix with `.dockerignore`), and each `RUN` adding a layer whose deleted files still occupy the earlier layer. `docker history` shows which layer is responsible, and `dive` makes it visual. Multi-stage builds plus a `.dockerignore` typically take an image from over a gigabyte to under 200MB, which directly improves deploy and scale-up time.
@red
- Running the container as root.
- `COPY . .` before `npm install`, destroying layer caching.

### How do you use Docker Compose for local development?
@short
Compose defines the whole local stack — app, Postgres, Redis, maybe MinIO and a mail catcher — in one file, with a single command to start it. The value is that a new developer gets a working environment in minutes and everyone runs the same dependency versions.
@deep
The details that make it actually pleasant: named volumes so database data survives restarts, `depends_on` with health conditions so the app does not start before Postgres is ready, and bind-mounting source with a node_modules exclusion so hot reload works without the container's dependencies being shadowed by the host's.

Compose is a development and small-deployment tool. For production I would use ECS, Kubernetes or a PaaS, because Compose has no scheduling, no rolling deploys and no self-healing across hosts.
@code yaml A local stack that just works
services:
  api:
    build: { context: ., target: dev }
    command: npm run dev
    environment:
      DATABASE_URL: postgres://app:app@db:5432/acme
      REDIS_URL: redis://cache:6379
    ports: ["3000:3000"]
    volumes:
      - .:/app
      - /app/node_modules          # ← keep the container's deps, not the host's
    depends_on:
      db:    { condition: service_healthy }
      cache: { condition: service_started }

  db:
    image: postgres:16-alpine
    environment: { POSTGRES_USER: app, POSTGRES_PASSWORD: app, POSTGRES_DB: acme }
    ports: ["5432:5432"]
    volumes: [pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data]
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U app"]
      interval: 5s
      retries: 10

  cache:
    image: redis:7-alpine
    command: ["redis-server", "--maxmemory", "256mb", "--maxmemory-policy", "allkeys-lru"]

volumes: { pgdata: }
@end
@senior
The `/app/node_modules` anonymous volume is the line that saves hours: without it, the host bind mount hides the container's installed dependencies and you get confusing native-module errors, especially across macOS and Linux architectures. The `service_healthy` condition matters too — `depends_on` alone only waits for the container to start, not for Postgres to accept connections, so the app crashes on first boot and people add a `sleep` instead of a health check. For CI I would use Testcontainers rather than Compose, because it gives per-test-run isolation and cleans up automatically.
@followup
How do you keep development and production environments consistent?
@answer
Same base image and same dependency versions via a lockfile, environment differences expressed only as configuration, and the same migrations run in both. Perfect parity is impossible — production has managed RDS, an ALB, real network latency and real data volumes — so I close the gap where bugs actually come from: database version and extensions, Node version, and timezone/locale settings. Then I rely on a staging environment that mirrors production topology for anything the local stack cannot represent.
@red
- No health check dependency, so the app crashes on first start.
- Different Postgres major versions locally and in production.

### What is CI/CD and what does a good pipeline look like?
@short
Continuous integration runs lint, type-check, tests, security scans and a build on every pull request so `main` is always releasable. Continuous delivery takes that artefact through environments automatically with gates. A good pipeline is fast, deterministic, and blocks merges when it fails.
@deep
The key principle is build once, deploy everywhere: produce one immutable artefact (a container image tagged with the commit SHA) and promote that same artefact through staging to production. Rebuilding per environment means you deploy something that was never tested.

Speed matters because a slow pipeline changes behaviour — people batch changes, skip local checks and merge without waiting. Caching dependencies, running jobs in parallel, and only running expensive checks where relevant keeps it under about ten minutes.
@code yaml Build once, promote the same image
name: deploy
on: { push: { branches: [main] } }

jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    outputs: { image: ${{ steps.meta.outputs.image }} }
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: docker/setup-buildx-action@v3
      - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with: { role-to-assume: ${{ secrets.DEPLOY_ROLE }}, aws-region: ap-south-1 }   # OIDC, no static keys
      - uses: aws-actions/amazon-ecr-login@v2
      - id: meta
        run: echo "image=$ECR/api:${GITHUB_SHA::7}" >> $GITHUB_OUTPUT
      - uses: docker/build-push-action@v5
        with: { push: true, tags: '${{ steps.meta.outputs.image }}', cache-from: type=gha, cache-to: type=gha,mode=max }

  staging:
    needs: build
    environment: staging
    steps:
      - run: ./deploy.sh staging ${{ needs.build.outputs.image }}
      - run: npm run test:smoke -- --base-url https://staging.acme.com

  production:
    needs: staging
    environment: production          # ← requires a manual approval in GitHub
    steps:
      - run: ./deploy.sh production ${{ needs.build.outputs.image }}   # SAME image, promoted
      - run: ./verify.sh || ./rollback.sh
@end
@senior
Two things I would highlight. OIDC federation instead of long-lived AWS access keys in secrets — the pipeline assumes a role with a short-lived token, so there is no static credential to leak, and this is now the expected practice. And a deploy that verifies itself: a smoke test after deployment plus automatic rollback on failure, because the value of CD is not that it deploys, it is that it recovers. A pipeline that can deploy but requires a human to roll back at 2am is only half the system.
@followup
How do you handle database migrations in CI/CD?
@answer
Migrations run as a separate step before the application deploy, with a higher-privileged role, and they must be backwards-compatible with the currently running code — expand-and-contract, never a breaking change deployed with its code. For rollout I run migrations once (a dedicated job, not per-container, or a lock so concurrent instances do not race), and destructive steps are deferred to a later release once the old code is gone. That sequencing is what makes rollback possible.
@red
- Rebuilding the image separately for each environment.
- Long-lived cloud credentials stored as CI secrets.

### How do you configure Nginx as a reverse proxy?
@short
Nginx terminates TLS, serves static files directly, and proxies dynamic requests to the Node upstream. The essentials are correct proxy headers so the app sees the real client IP and protocol, sensible timeouts, gzip or brotli compression, and WebSocket upgrade handling if you need it.
@deep
`X-Forwarded-For` and `X-Forwarded-Proto` matter because without them your application logs the proxy's IP for every request and generates `http://` redirect URLs behind an HTTPS terminator. Express needs `app.set('trust proxy', 1)` to read them, and it must be set correctly or rate limiting keyed on IP limits everyone as one client.

Buffering is the other common surprise: Nginx buffers responses by default, which breaks Server-Sent Events and streaming unless you disable it for those locations.
@code nginx A reverse proxy configuration that covers the real cases
upstream api { server 127.0.0.1:3000; keepalive 32; }

server {
  listen 443 ssl; http2 on;
  server_name api.acme.com;

  ssl_certificate     /etc/letsencrypt/live/api.acme.com/fullchain.pem;
  ssl_certificate_key /etc/letsencrypt/live/api.acme.com/privkey.pem;
  ssl_protocols TLSv1.2 TLSv1.3;

  client_max_body_size 10m;                 # match your upload limit
  gzip on; gzip_types application/json text/css application/javascript;

  location / {
    proxy_pass http://api;
    proxy_http_version 1.1;
    proxy_set_header Host              $host;
    proxy_set_header X-Real-IP         $remote_addr;
    proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For   $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
    proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
    proxy_connect_timeout 5s;
    proxy_read_timeout    60s;
  }

  location /api/chat/stream {               # SSE / streaming: no buffering, long timeout
    proxy_pass http://api;
    proxy_buffering off;
    proxy_cache off;
    proxy_read_timeout 3600s;
  }

  location /ws {                            # WebSocket upgrade
    proxy_pass http://api;
    proxy_http_version 1.1;
    proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade;
    proxy_set_header Connection "upgrade";
    proxy_read_timeout 3600s;               # otherwise idle sockets are cut at 60s
  }
}
@end
@senior
The two lines that cause the most confusing bugs are `proxy_buffering off` for streaming and `proxy_read_timeout` for WebSockets. A streaming AI response through a default Nginx config appears to hang and then delivers everything at once, which looks like an application bug and is not. And a WebSocket silently dropping every 60 seconds is the default `proxy_read_timeout` doing its job. I would also mention `trust proxy` on the Express side, because getting it wrong means every rate limit and every audit log records the load balancer's IP — which makes your abuse controls useless.
@followup
Do you still need Nginx if you have an ALB?
@answer
Often not. An ALB terminates TLS, does health checks, load balances and sets the forwarded headers, so a second proxy adds a hop for little benefit. I keep Nginx when I want features the ALB does not provide cheaply — serving static files from the instance, fine-grained caching, complex rewrites, or per-path rate limiting close to the app. In a container setup on ECS or Kubernetes I usually drop it and let the ingress do that work.
@red
- Forgetting `trust proxy` and rate limiting the load balancer.
- Default buffering breaking SSE and streaming.

### What AWS services would you use for a typical full-stack app, and why?
@short
For a standard deployment: Route 53 for DNS, CloudFront for the CDN, an ALB in front of ECS Fargate for the API, RDS Postgres Multi-AZ, ElastiCache Redis, S3 for uploads, Secrets Manager for credentials, CloudWatch for logs and metrics, and SQS for background work. That covers the stack without operating servers.
@deep
The choice between EC2, ECS/Fargate and Lambda is about operational burden versus control and cost. EC2 gives control and is cheapest at steady high utilisation but you patch and scale it. Fargate removes host management at a premium per vCPU. Lambda is ideal for spiky, short workloads and awkward for long-lived connections, cold-start-sensitive latency, and database connection limits.

Networking is the part people underestimate: a proper VPC with public subnets for the ALB and private subnets for compute and database, security groups referencing each other rather than CIDR ranges, and NAT for outbound access.
@arch A sane, boring, defensible AWS topology
  Route 53 ─► CloudFront ─► S3 (static/Next assets, OAC, no public bucket)
                  │
                  └─► ALB (public subnets, ACM cert, WAF)
                          │  target group :3000, health check /readyz
                          ▼
                  ECS Fargate service (private subnets, 2+ tasks across AZs)
                     │            │                │
                     ▼            ▼                ▼
              RDS Postgres   ElastiCache      SQS ──► worker service
              Multi-AZ        Redis            │        (same image, different command)
              (private)       (private)        └─► DLQ + alarm
                     ▲
                     └── Secrets Manager (DB creds, rotated) · IAM task role (no static keys)
  Observability: CloudWatch Logs + metrics + alarms → SNS → PagerDuty/Slack; X-Ray or OTel traces
@end
@senior
The design choices I would defend: nothing except the ALB and CloudFront is publicly reachable, security groups reference each other so there are no IP allow-lists to maintain, the tasks use an IAM role rather than any static credentials, and the worker runs the same image as the API with a different command so there is one artefact to build and test. On cost, the biggest levers are usually right-sizing RDS, using Graviton instances, S3 lifecycle policies to move old objects to cheaper storage, and avoiding cross-AZ data transfer — and I would mention that cost is an engineering concern, not just finance's.
@followup
When would you use Lambda instead of a container?
@answer
For event-driven, bursty, short-lived work — S3 triggers, scheduled jobs, webhook receivers, image thumbnailing — where per-request billing and zero idle cost win, and where cold starts are tolerable. I avoid it for latency-sensitive user-facing APIs unless provisioned concurrency justifies its cost, and for anything holding a database connection per invocation, which needs RDS Proxy to avoid exhausting connections. WebSockets on Lambda via API Gateway are possible but awkward compared with a long-running container.
@red
- Putting the database in a public subnet.
- Static IAM access keys in environment variables.

### How do you monitor and alert on a production system?
@short
Three pillars: metrics (RED — rate, errors, duration — per endpoint plus system resources), structured logs with correlation ids, and distributed traces. Alerts fire on user-visible symptoms with a clear runbook, not on every anomaly, and every alert should be actionable or it gets deleted.
@deep
The distinction that matters is symptom versus cause alerting. Alerting on "p99 latency above 2s" or "5xx rate above 1%" tells you users are affected; alerting on "CPU above 80%" often does not, because a service can be healthy at 90% CPU and broken at 20%.

SLOs formalise this: define what "working" means (99.9% of requests succeed within 500ms), measure it, and alert on burn rate — how fast you are consuming the error budget — rather than on instantaneous spikes.
@code ts Instrumenting what you will actually be asked about at 3am
import client from 'prom-client';

const httpDuration = new client.Histogram({
  name: 'http_request_duration_seconds',
  help: 'Request duration',
  labelNames: ['method', 'route', 'status'],
  buckets: [0.01, 0.05, 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 1, 2, 5],
});

app.use((req, res, next) => {
  const end = httpDuration.startTimer();
  res.on('finish', () => end({ method: req.method, route: req.route?.path ?? 'unknown', status: res.statusCode }));
  next();
});

// Business metrics matter as much as technical ones
const paymentsFailed = new client.Counter({ name: 'payments_failed_total', labelNames: ['provider', 'reason'] });
const webhookLag = new client.Gauge({ name: 'webhook_processing_lag_seconds' });
const queueDepth = new client.Gauge({ name: 'queue_depth', labelNames: ['queue'] });

// Alerts that are worth waking someone for:
//   • 5xx rate > 1% for 5m                      (users are broken)
//   • p99 latency > 2s for 10m                  (users are suffering)
//   • payments_failed_total rate spike          (money is being lost)
//   • queue_depth growing for 15m               (we are falling behind)
//   • DLQ messages > 0                          (work is being dropped)
//   • cert expiry < 14 days, disk > 85%         (predictable self-inflicted outages)
@end
@senior
The business metrics are what distinguish a senior answer. Technical dashboards can be entirely green while payments quietly fail for one provider because of a certificate change on their side — so I instrument payment success rate by provider, webhook processing lag, and queue depth, and alert on those. I would also insist on alert hygiene: every alert has a runbook link and an owner, alerts that fire without action get tuned or deleted, and paging alerts are separated from informational ones. Alert fatigue is how real incidents get missed, and it is a design failure, not a people failure.
@followup
What is the difference between logs, metrics and traces?
@answer
Metrics are cheap aggregated numbers over time — good for alerting and dashboards, bad for explaining a single request. Logs are detailed per-event records — good for investigating a specific case, expensive at volume. Traces connect one request's path across services with timing per span — the only one that answers "where did the 800ms go". You need all three: metrics tell you something is wrong, traces tell you where, logs tell you why.
@red
- Alerting on CPU and calling it monitoring.
- Logs with no request id in a multi-service system.

# SECTION Authentication & Authorization | Where a wrong answer is not just a lost point — it is a breach.

### How does JWT authentication work, and what are its trade-offs?
@short
A JWT is a signed, base64url-encoded token with a header, a payload of claims and a signature. The server verifies the signature and trusts the claims without a database lookup, which makes it stateless and horizontally scalable. The trade-off is that you cannot revoke it before it expires, so tokens must be short-lived.
@deep
It is signed, not encrypted — anyone can decode the payload, so it must never contain secrets. Verification checks the signature, `exp`, `nbf`, `iss` and `aud`, and the algorithm must be pinned; accepting `alg: none` or allowing an RS256 token to be verified as HS256 with the public key as the secret are both classic bypasses.

The stateless benefit is real for scale but it is also the weakness: a stolen token works until expiry, and a role change does not take effect until the token refreshes.
@code ts Access + refresh with rotation and reuse detection
// Short-lived access token: no DB lookup on the hot path
const access = jwt.sign(
  { sub: user.id, role: user.role, tid: user.tenantId },
  privateKey, { algorithm: 'RS256', expiresIn: '15m', issuer: 'api.acme.com', audience: 'acme-web', jwtid: randomUUID() });

// Long-lived refresh token: opaque, stored hashed, rotated on every use
const refresh = randomBytes(32).toString('base64url');
await db.refreshTokens.insert({
  userId: user.id, tokenHash: sha256(refresh), family: familyId,
  expiresAt: addDays(new Date(), 30), usedAt: null,
});

// Rotation with reuse detection — the important part
export async function rotate(presented: string) {
  const row = await db.refreshTokens.findByHash(sha256(presented));
  if (!row || row.expiresAt < new Date()) throw new Unauthorized('INVALID_REFRESH');

  if (row.usedAt) {
    // This token was already exchanged → it was stolen and replayed.
    await db.refreshTokens.revokeFamily(row.family);      // kill every session in the family
    logger.warn({ userId: row.userId }, 'refresh token reuse detected');
    throw new Unauthorized('TOKEN_REUSE_DETECTED');
  }

  await db.refreshTokens.markUsed(row.id);
  return issuePair(row.userId, row.family);
}
@end
@senior
Refresh-token reuse detection is the detail that shows you have thought about theft rather than just about expiry. Because each refresh token is single-use, a second presentation means either the legitimate client or an attacker is replaying an old one — either way, revoking the whole token family logs out the attacker at the cost of one re-login for the user. I would also mention the practical middle ground: a JWT for the fast path plus a Redis denylist keyed on `jti` with a TTL equal to the remaining token lifetime, which gives you immediate revocation on logout or compromise without a database lookup per request.
@followup
Sessions or JWTs — which do you actually prefer?
@answer
For a single web application, server-side sessions in Redis with an opaque cookie are simpler and strictly better on revocation: logout is instant, a compromised session is one `DEL` away, and you can list and terminate a user's active sessions. JWTs earn their place when several services must verify identity without sharing a session store, or when a mobile and a third-party client are involved. Choosing JWT for a single monolith because it is "modern" is usually choosing a harder revocation story for no benefit — and saying that is a stronger answer than reciting the JWT structure.
@red
- Storing sensitive data in the JWT payload.
- Not pinning the algorithm.
- Access tokens valid for 30 days.

### How does OAuth 2.0 and "Login with Google" work?
@short
OAuth 2.0 is an authorisation-delegation protocol: the user authenticates with the provider, the provider returns an authorisation code to your redirect URI, and your server exchanges that code plus your client secret for tokens. OpenID Connect layers identity on top by adding an ID token. The Authorization Code flow with PKCE is the only flow to use today.
@deep
The code exchange happens server-to-server, so the access token never appears in a URL or in browser history — that is what the implicit flow got wrong and why it is deprecated. PKCE adds a code verifier and challenge so an intercepted authorisation code cannot be redeemed by an attacker, which matters for mobile and single-page apps that cannot hold a client secret.

The `state` parameter is a CSRF defence for the redirect: you generate it, store it in the session, and reject the callback if it does not match. `nonce` does the equivalent for the ID token.
@arch Authorization Code + PKCE, step by step
  1. App generates: code_verifier (random), code_challenge = S256(verifier), state, nonce
  2. Redirect → https://accounts.google.com/o/oauth2/v2/auth
        ?client_id=…&redirect_uri=…&response_type=code&scope=openid email profile
        &state=…&nonce=…&code_challenge=…&code_challenge_method=S256
  3. User authenticates and consents at Google (your app never sees the password)
  4. Google redirects → https://acme.com/auth/callback?code=…&state=…
  5. Server verifies `state` matches the session  ← CSRF protection
  6. Server POSTs to the token endpoint: code + code_verifier + client_secret
  7. Google returns { access_token, id_token (JWT), refresh_token? }
  8. Server VERIFIES the id_token: signature against Google's JWKS, iss, aud,
     exp, and nonce  ← never trust an unverified id_token
  9. Server finds or creates the local user, issues ITS OWN session/JWT
@end
@senior
Step 8 is where implementations go wrong: decoding the ID token without verifying its signature and audience means anyone can forge one. Also worth stating is step 9 — you issue your own session rather than passing Google's token around your services, because your authorisation model, roles and tenancy are yours, not Google's. On account linking, matching purely on email address is risky unless the provider asserts `email_verified`, and I would require an explicit linking step when an account with that email already exists, or you have an account-takeover path.
@followup
What is the difference between OAuth and OpenID Connect?
@answer
OAuth 2.0 is about authorisation — granting an application limited access to a resource on the user's behalf, expressed as an access token with scopes. It says nothing about who the user is. OpenID Connect is a thin identity layer on top: it adds the ID token, a JWT with verified claims about the user, plus a standard `/userinfo` endpoint and discovery document. Using a raw OAuth access token as proof of identity is a known anti-pattern, because the token was issued for a different audience and may not have been issued to you at all.
@red
- Using the implicit flow.
- Skipping `state` or not verifying the ID token signature.

### How do you store passwords?
@short
With a slow, salted, memory-hard hash — argon2id preferred, bcrypt acceptable — never a fast general-purpose hash like SHA-256 and never encryption. Each password gets a unique salt (handled automatically by both algorithms), and the work factor is tuned so hashing takes on the order of 100–250ms on your hardware.
@deep
The point of a slow hash is to make offline brute-force expensive if the database leaks. SHA-256 can be computed billions of times per second on a GPU; argon2id is deliberately memory-hard, which defeats GPU and ASIC parallelism far better than bcrypt's CPU-only cost.

Comparison must be constant-time — both libraries' `verify` functions handle this — and the same code path must run whether or not the user exists, or response timing reveals which emails are registered.
@code ts Registration, login and transparent rehashing
import argon2 from 'argon2';

const OPTS = { type: argon2.argon2id, memoryCost: 19456, timeCost: 2, parallelism: 1 };  // OWASP baseline

export const hashPassword = (pw: string) => argon2.hash(pw, OPTS);

export async function login(email: string, password: string) {
  const user = await db.users.findByEmail(email);

  // ✅ constant work whether or not the user exists — no timing oracle
  const hash = user?.passwordHash ?? DUMMY_HASH;
  const ok = await argon2.verify(hash, password).catch(() => false);
  if (!user || !ok) throw new Unauthorized('INVALID_CREDENTIALS');   // same message either way

  // Transparent upgrade when parameters are strengthened
  if (argon2.needsRehash(user.passwordHash, OPTS)) {
    await db.users.updateHash(user.id, await argon2.hash(password, OPTS));
  }
  return issueSession(user);
}
@end
@senior
Three details separate a real implementation. The dummy-hash comparison removes the user-enumeration timing oracle, and the identical error message removes the explicit one. `needsRehash` lets you raise the work factor over time without forcing a password reset — users upgrade silently as they log in. And I would add that the bigger wins today are not in the hashing at all: checking passwords against the Have I Been Pwned k-anonymity API blocks the credential-stuffing lists that cause most account takeovers, and offering TOTP or passkeys matters more than another round of bcrypt.
@followup
How do you implement password reset securely?
@answer
Generate a cryptographically random token, store only its hash with a short expiry (15–60 minutes) and a single-use flag, and email a link containing the raw token. On use, verify, invalidate the token, update the password, and invalidate every existing session and refresh token for that user — because a reset is often a response to compromise. The request endpoint must return the same response whether or not the email exists, and it must be rate limited per email and per IP.
@red
- SHA-256 or MD5 for passwords.
- Different error messages for "no such user" and "wrong password".

### What is the difference between authentication and authorization, and how do you model roles?
@short
Authentication establishes identity; authorisation decides what that identity may do. I model authorisation with roles for coarse capability and explicit permission checks scoped to the specific record, because role-only checks are exactly how IDOR vulnerabilities happen.
@deep
RBAC assigns permissions to roles and roles to users — simple, readable, and sufficient for most products. ABAC evaluates attributes of the user, the resource and the context ("a manager may approve expenses under 50,000 in their own department"), which is more expressive and harder to reason about.

Whichever you choose, the check must happen on the server, close to the data, and must include ownership or tenancy, not just role.
@code ts Permission checks that include the record, not just the role
type Permission = 'order:read' | 'order:refund' | 'user:manage';

const ROLE_PERMISSIONS: Record<Role, Permission[]> = {
  admin:   ['order:read', 'order:refund', 'user:manage'],
  support: ['order:read', 'order:refund'],
  user:    ['order:read'],
};

// Coarse gate
export const can = (user: User, p: Permission) => ROLE_PERMISSIONS[user.role].includes(p);

// ✅ Fine-grained: the policy takes the ACTUAL RESOURCE
export function canRefund(user: User, order: Order): Result<true, string> {
  if (order.tenantId !== user.tenantId)        return { ok: false, error: 'NOT_FOUND' };
  if (!can(user, 'order:refund'))              return { ok: false, error: 'FORBIDDEN' };
  if (user.role === 'support' && order.totalMinor > 5_000_00)
                                               return { ok: false, error: 'APPROVAL_REQUIRED' };
  if (order.status !== 'paid')                 return { ok: false, error: 'NOT_REFUNDABLE' };
  return { ok: true, value: true };
}

router.post('/orders/:id/refunds', authenticate, ah(async (req, res) => {
  const order = await repo.findScoped(req.params.id, req.user.tenantId);   // scoped fetch
  if (!order) return res.status(404).json({ error: 'NOT_FOUND' });
  const decision = canRefund(req.user, order);
  if (!decision.ok) return res.status(decision.error === 'FORBIDDEN' ? 403 : 422).json({ error: decision.error });
  res.json(await refundService.execute(order, req.user));
}));
@end
@senior
The structural point is that authorisation logic should be a pure function of (user, resource, action) that you can unit-test exhaustively, rather than scattered `if` statements across controllers. That gives you a single place to audit, and you can write a test matrix over every role and every state. I would also say the strongest defence is making the unsafe path unavailable: a repository whose `find` requires a tenant scope in its signature means the insecure version does not compile. Relying on reviewers to notice a missing check on the fortieth endpoint is not a control.
@followup
How do you handle permission changes taking effect immediately with JWTs?
@answer
Keep the access token short (10–15 minutes) so changes propagate on the next refresh, and for changes that must be immediate — a revoked admin, a suspended account — push a revocation entry into Redis keyed by user id with a timestamp, and have the token check reject any token issued before that timestamp. That is one fast Redis lookup rather than a full database check, and it gives you instant effect where it matters while keeping the stateless benefit elsewhere.
@red
- Checking only the role and not the record's owner.
- Doing authorisation checks on the client.

### What is CSRF and how do you prevent it?
@short
Cross-site request forgery tricks a logged-in user's browser into making a state-changing request to your site from another site, relying on cookies being sent automatically. The defences are `SameSite` cookies, an anti-CSRF token that the attacker's site cannot read, and checking the Origin or Referer header on state-changing requests.
@deep
CSRF only affects credentials the browser attaches automatically — cookies and HTTP Basic auth. A bearer token in an `Authorization` header is immune, because a cross-site page cannot add that header without your JavaScript.

`SameSite=Lax`, now the default in major browsers, blocks cookies on cross-site POSTs and subresource requests, which eliminates the classic form-submission attack. It does not cover every case, so a token is still worth having for sensitive operations.
@code ts Double-submit token, plus origin checking
// Issue a random token in a readable cookie AND require it in a header
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  if (!req.cookies.csrf) {
    res.cookie('csrf', randomBytes(32).toString('base64url'),
      { sameSite: 'lax', secure: true, httpOnly: false });   // readable by same-origin JS only
  }
  next();
});

app.use((req, res, next) => {
  if (['GET', 'HEAD', 'OPTIONS'].includes(req.method)) return next();

  // 1. Origin check — cheap and effective
  const origin = req.headers.origin ?? req.headers.referer;
  if (origin && !allowlist.has(new URL(origin).origin))
    return res.status(403).json({ error: 'CSRF_ORIGIN' });

  // 2. Double-submit: the attacker's site cannot read our cookie to echo it back
  const header = req.get('X-CSRF-Token');
  if (!header || !req.cookies.csrf ||
      !crypto.timingSafeEqual(Buffer.from(header), Buffer.from(req.cookies.csrf)))
    return res.status(403).json({ error: 'CSRF_TOKEN' });

  next();
});
@end
@senior
The reason double-submit works is the same-origin policy: a malicious site can *cause* your cookie to be sent, but it cannot *read* it to place the matching header. That distinction is the whole mechanism, and being able to state it clearly is what the question is really testing. I would add that a successful XSS defeats every CSRF defence — the attacker's script is same-origin and can read the token — so CSRF protection assumes you do not have XSS, which is why CSP and output encoding are the more fundamental controls.
@followup
Does a REST API using bearer tokens need CSRF protection?
@answer
No, provided it *only* accepts bearer tokens and never falls back to cookie authentication. The browser will not attach an `Authorization` header to a cross-site request. The danger is a hybrid API that accepts either — then the cookie path is CSRF-vulnerable even though the documented path is not, and attackers will find it. If you support both, apply CSRF protection to the cookie path specifically.
@red
- Believing CORS prevents CSRF.
- Adding CSRF tokens to a pure bearer-token API and calling it done.

### How do you implement multi-factor authentication?
@short
TOTP is the practical default: a shared secret, a QR code for the authenticator app, and a 6-digit code derived from the secret and the current 30-second window. Store the secret encrypted, allow a small time-window skew, rate limit verification attempts, and issue single-use recovery codes. Passkeys/WebAuthn are the stronger, phishing-resistant option.
@deep
TOTP is vulnerable to real-time phishing — a fake site can relay the code — whereas WebAuthn binds the credential to the origin, so a phishing site simply cannot produce a valid assertion. That makes passkeys meaningfully stronger, and browser and platform support is now good enough to offer them as the primary option with TOTP as fallback.

SMS is the weakest factor because of SIM-swap attacks, but it is still far better than no second factor and is what many users will actually use.
@code ts TOTP enrolment and verification with replay protection
import { authenticator } from 'otplib';

// Enrolment
const secret = authenticator.generateSecret();
const uri = authenticator.keyuri(user.email, 'Acme', secret);   // → QR code
await db.users.setPendingMfa(user.id, encrypt(secret));          // encrypted at rest
// Only activate after the user proves they can generate a valid code:
if (!authenticator.verify({ token: submitted, secret })) throw new BadRequest('INVALID_CODE');
await db.users.activateMfa(user.id, await generateRecoveryCodes(user.id));  // hashed, single-use

// Verification at login
authenticator.options = { window: 1 };            // ±30s tolerance for clock drift
const ok = authenticator.verify({ token, secret: decrypt(user.mfaSecret) });
if (!ok) { await recordFailedMfa(user.id); throw new Unauthorized('INVALID_CODE'); }

// ✅ replay protection — a code is valid for 30s, so an attacker who sees it could reuse it
const used = await redis.set(`mfa:${user.id}:${token}`, '1', 'NX', 'EX', 90);
if (!used) throw new Unauthorized('CODE_ALREADY_USED');
@end
@senior
The replay guard is the detail most implementations miss: a TOTP code is valid for the whole window, so if it is intercepted it can be reused within that window. Marking it consumed in Redis closes that. The other operational point is recovery: recovery codes must be hashed like passwords, single-use, and regenerable, and there must be a documented human process for a user who loses both device and codes — otherwise your support team will invent an insecure one under pressure, which is how MFA gets socially engineered.
@followup
Where in the login flow does MFA belong?
@answer
After the password is verified and before a full session is issued. The intermediate state is a short-lived, limited-scope token that can only be exchanged for a session by completing the second factor — never a full session that is "waiting" for MFA, because that is bypassable by simply not completing the step. I also require re-authentication or MFA for sensitive actions (changing email, disabling MFA, adding a payout account), because session hijacking should not be enough to take the account over permanently.
@red
- Storing the TOTP secret in plaintext.
- Issuing a full session before the second factor is verified.

### How do you secure API keys and service-to-service authentication?
@short
API keys are for identifying a client application, not a user. I generate them with high entropy, store only a hash, show the raw key exactly once, scope them to specific permissions, support rotation with an overlap period, and rate limit per key. For internal service-to-service traffic I prefer mTLS or short-lived signed tokens over static keys.
@deep
The key must be verifiable without a plaintext lookup, so store a hash — but a fast hash is acceptable here (unlike passwords) because the key is high-entropy, so brute force is infeasible regardless. To make lookup efficient, include a non-secret prefix in the key that identifies the record.

Rotation needs overlap: the customer creates a second key, deploys it, then revokes the first. Forcing an instant swap causes an outage and so people never rotate.
@code ts Key issuance, lookup and scoped verification
// Issue: prefix identifies the record; the secret half is never stored
const id     = 'ak_' + randomBytes(6).toString('hex');
const secret = randomBytes(32).toString('base64url');
const key    = `${id}.${secret}`;                          // shown ONCE
await db.apiKeys.insert({
  id, hash: sha256(secret), userId, scopes: ['orders:read'],
  lastUsedAt: null, expiresAt: addYears(new Date(), 1),
});

// Verify: O(1) lookup by prefix, constant-time compare of the secret
export async function verifyKey(presented: string) {
  const [id, secret] = presented.split('.');
  const row = await db.apiKeys.findById(id);
  if (!row || row.revokedAt || row.expiresAt < new Date()) throw new Unauthorized('INVALID_KEY');
  if (!crypto.timingSafeEqual(Buffer.from(row.hash), Buffer.from(sha256(secret))))
    throw new Unauthorized('INVALID_KEY');
  void db.apiKeys.touch(id);                                // last-used, for rotation nudges
  return row;
}
@end
@senior
Two operational points. Recording `lastUsedAt` is what makes revocation politically possible — you can tell a customer "this key has not been used in 90 days" instead of guessing, and you can detect a leaked key still in use after rotation. And publishing your key format with a distinctive prefix lets GitHub's secret scanning detect and notify you when a customer commits one, which is a genuinely valuable free control. For internal traffic I would push toward mTLS or a workload identity (IAM roles, SPIFFE) rather than shared static keys, because static secrets between services are the ones nobody ever rotates.
@followup
How do you handle a leaked API key?
@answer
Revoke it immediately, issue a replacement, and notify the customer with the timeline. Then investigate: check the access logs for that key id for unfamiliar source IPs and unusual endpoints, determine what data was reachable within its scopes, and decide on disclosure obligations. The design lesson is scoping — a key limited to `orders:read` for one tenant contains the damage, whereas a single all-powerful key means a leak is a full breach. Least privilege is what makes an incident survivable.
@red
- Storing API keys in plaintext.
- One key with full access, never rotated.

### What is Single Sign-On and how does SAML compare to OIDC?
@short
SSO lets a user authenticate once with an identity provider and access many applications. SAML is the older XML-based standard, still dominant in enterprise; OIDC is the modern JSON/JWT-based layer on OAuth 2.0 and is what you would choose for anything new. Both delegate authentication to the IdP and return signed assertions about the user.
@deep
The mechanics are analogous: a redirect to the IdP, authentication there, and a signed assertion posted back. SAML sends an XML assertion, usually via HTTP POST binding, and verification requires careful XML signature validation — historically a source of serious vulnerabilities because XML canonicalisation is subtle. OIDC's JWT verification is simpler and harder to get wrong.

In B2B SaaS you will often need both: enterprise customers ask for SAML with their existing IdP (Okta, Azure AD), while everyone else uses OIDC social login.
@code text What you must verify on any SSO assertion
  SAML                                   OIDC
  ────                                   ────
  ✔ XML signature valid (IdP cert)       ✔ JWT signature valid (JWKS, kid)
  ✔ Signature covers the ASSERTION,      ✔ iss matches the expected issuer
    not just the response  ← CVE class   ✔ aud == your client_id
  ✔ Audience == your entity ID           ✔ exp / iat within tolerance
  ✔ NotBefore / NotOnOrAfter             ✔ nonce matches the one you sent
  ✔ InResponseTo matches your request    ✔ at_hash if you received an access token
  ✔ Assertion ID not replayed (cache it) ✔ Replay protection on the code
  ✔ IdP certificate not expired/rotated  ✔ JWKS cached with rotation support

  Then, in BOTH cases: map to a local user, apply YOUR authorisation,
  issue YOUR session. Never trust IdP-supplied roles without mapping.
@end
@senior
The two things I would emphasise: certificate and key rotation is where SSO integrations break in production — an IdP rotates its signing certificate and every login fails at once, so you cache JWKS with a refresh on unknown `kid` and monitor certificate expiry for SAML. And never trust IdP-supplied group or role claims blindly; map them explicitly to your own roles, because an IdP administrator on the customer side would otherwise be able to grant themselves privileges in your system by editing a group name. Just-in-time provisioning needs the same care — creating users automatically is convenient and needs a domain allow-list.
@followup
How do you handle SSO-initiated logout?
@answer
Single logout is genuinely hard and often not worth implementing fully. The IdP can notify relying parties via front-channel iframes or back-channel calls, but delivery is unreliable and browsers increasingly block third-party iframes. The pragmatic approach is short session lifetimes plus a session check against the IdP for sensitive operations, and clear communication that closing the browser does not end every session. Where the customer requires true SLO, back-channel logout with OIDC is the more reliable of the options.
@red
- Trusting IdP-supplied roles without mapping.
- No plan for IdP certificate rotation.

# SECTION Testing Fundamentals | What you test, why, and how you keep the suite from becoming the bottleneck.

### What is the testing pyramid and how do you apply it?
@short
Many fast unit tests over pure logic, fewer integration tests over real wiring, and a small number of end-to-end tests over critical user journeys. The shape follows from cost: unit tests are milliseconds, E2E tests are seconds and flaky. Inverting the pyramid gives you a slow, unreliable suite that people start ignoring.
@deep
The modern refinement is the "testing trophy", which puts the most weight on integration tests, because the highest-value bugs live in the wiring between units, not inside them. A unit test of a mocked service proves little; a test that runs the real handler against a real database catches SQL errors, constraint violations and transaction bugs.

End-to-end tests are for journeys that must never break: signup, login, checkout, payment. Everything else is better covered lower down.
@code ts What each level actually looks like
// Unit — pure function, no mocks, microseconds
expect(priceOrder(items, expiredCoupon).discount).toBe(0);

// Integration — real HTTP handler + real Postgres, third party stubbed
const res = await request(app).post('/orders')
  .set('Authorization', `Bearer ${token}`)
  .send({ items: [{ sku: 'KB-1', qty: 2 }] });
expect(res.status).toBe(201);
expect(await db.orders.count()).toBe(1);           // it really wrote

// E2E — real browser, real stack, the journey that must never break
test('user can pay and see the receipt', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('/checkout');
  await page.getByLabel('Card number').fill('4242424242424242');
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Pay' }).click();
  await expect(page.getByText('Payment successful')).toBeVisible({ timeout: 15_000 });
});
@end
@senior
The framing I would give is risk-based rather than ratio-based: I ask what would be most expensive if it broke silently, and put the strongest test there. For a payments system that means integration tests over the webhook path including duplicates and out-of-order delivery — a scenario no unit test can express. I would also point out that coverage percentage is a weak proxy; a suite with 90% coverage that mocks the database proves the mocks work. What I track instead is whether known failure modes have a test, and whether the suite runs fast enough that people run it before pushing.
@followup
How do you keep tests from becoming flaky?
@answer
Remove the sources of nondeterminism: no fixed `sleep` — wait for a condition; no shared mutable state between tests — isolate with a transaction rollback or a fresh schema; no reliance on ordering; inject the clock rather than using real time; and stub the network so third-party latency cannot fail a build. When a test does go flaky, I quarantine and fix it rather than adding a retry, because a retried flaky test trains everyone to ignore red builds, and eventually a real failure gets ignored too.
@red
- Mostly E2E tests, few unit tests.
- Adding retries instead of fixing flakiness.

### How do you test React components?
@short
With React Testing Library, asserting on what the user sees and does rather than on implementation details — query by role, label and text, not by class name or component internals. Network calls are intercepted with MSW so the component exercises its real fetching code against controlled responses.
@deep
