The guiding principle is that tests should not break when you refactor. Asserting on state variables or on a `data-testid` for every element couples the test to the implementation; asserting that "after clicking Submit, an error message appears" survives a rewrite.

`userEvent` rather than `fireEvent` matters because it simulates the full interaction sequence — focus, keydown, keypress, input — which catches bugs that a synthetic change event does not.
@code tsx A test that survives refactoring
import { render, screen } from '@testing-library/react';
import userEvent from '@testing-library/user-event';
import { http, HttpResponse } from 'msw';

const server = setupServer(
  http.post('/api/refunds', () => HttpResponse.json({ error: 'ALREADY_REFUNDED' }, { status: 409 }))
);
beforeAll(() => server.listen()); afterEach(() => server.resetHandlers()); afterAll(() => server.close());

it('shows a clear message when the order was already refunded', async () => {
  render(<RefundForm orderId="o_9" />, { wrapper: Providers });

  await userEvent.type(screen.getByLabelText(/reason/i), 'Damaged in transit');
  await userEvent.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: /refund/i }));

  expect(await screen.findByRole('alert')).toHaveTextContent(/already refunded/i);
  expect(screen.getByRole('button', { name: /refund/i })).toBeEnabled();   // recovered, not stuck
});
@end
@senior
Querying by role is not just a style preference — it doubles as an accessibility check. If `getByRole('button', { name: /refund/i })` cannot find your element, a screen reader cannot either, so the test suite quietly enforces accessible markup. I would also mention that I test the error and loading states as deliberately as the happy path, because those are the paths that get shipped untested and then encountered by real users at the worst moment. For visual regressions I would use Playwright screenshots or Chromatic rather than trying to assert on styles in unit tests.
@followup
Should you test custom hooks in isolation?
@answer
Sometimes — `renderHook` is useful for a hook with non-trivial logic, like a debounce or a reducer-backed state machine. But most hooks are better tested through a component that uses them, because that is how they will actually be used and it catches integration issues like missing dependencies or effects that do not clean up. Testing a hook in isolation and never through a component is how you end up with a hook that passes its tests and breaks in the app.
@red
- Querying by CSS class or internal state.
- Mocking `fetch` by hand instead of intercepting at the network layer.

### How do you test an API endpoint end to end?
@short
Spin up the real application with a real database (Testcontainers or a dedicated test schema), send real HTTP requests with Supertest, and assert on both the response and the resulting database state. Third-party HTTP is intercepted. Each test is isolated by a transaction rollback or truncation.
@deep
Asserting on the database state, not just the response body, is what catches the interesting bugs: the endpoint returns 201 but wrote the wrong tenant id, or created two rows, or did not write the outbox entry. The response alone cannot tell you that.

Authentication should be exercised, not bypassed: generate a real token for a test user rather than injecting `req.user`, so the auth middleware is covered too.
@code ts A test that covers the failure paths people skip
describe('POST /orders', () => {
  it('rejects an unauthenticated request', async () => {
    await request(app).post('/orders').send(valid).expect(401);
  });

  it('rejects a body with unknown fields (mass assignment)', async () => {
    const res = await request(app).post('/orders').set(auth(user))
      .send({ ...valid, tenantId: otherTenant.id, status: 'paid' });
    expect(res.status).toBe(422);
  });

  it('cannot create an order in another tenant', async () => {
    const res = await request(app).post('/orders').set(auth(userOfTenantA))
      .send({ ...valid, warehouseId: warehouseOfTenantB.id });
    expect(res.status).toBe(404);                     // not 403 — do not confirm existence
  });

  it('is idempotent under retry', async () => {
    const key = randomUUID();
    const a = await request(app).post('/orders').set(auth(user)).set('Idempotency-Key', key).send(valid);
    const b = await request(app).post('/orders').set(auth(user)).set('Idempotency-Key', key).send(valid);
    expect(b.body.id).toBe(a.body.id);
    expect(await db.orders.count()).toBe(1);          // ← the assertion that matters
  });
});
@end
@senior
The tests I insist on are the negative and adversarial ones: unauthenticated, wrong tenant, unknown fields, replayed request, malformed input. Those are the paths attackers use and the paths developers forget, and they are cheap to write once the harness exists. The final assertion in the idempotency test — counting rows — is the pattern I would highlight generally: assert on the *effect*, not on the response, because a handler can return the right JSON while writing the wrong data.
@followup
How do you test something that depends on a third-party API like Stripe?
@answer
Three layers. Unit-level, stub the client and test my own logic. Integration-level, intercept HTTP with nock or MSW and replay realistic payloads including error cases — declined cards, rate limits, malformed webhooks. And for the provider's own behaviour, use their test mode and sandbox in a staging environment, plus their CLI to replay webhook deliveries. What I never do is call the live API in CI: it is slow, rate limited, and it makes the build depend on someone else's uptime.
@red
- Bypassing auth middleware in tests.
- Asserting only on the response status.

### What is test-driven development and do you use it?
@short
Write a failing test, write the minimum code to pass it, then refactor. I use it selectively — it is excellent for pure logic with clear rules (pricing, validation, parsers, state machines) and awkward for exploratory UI work or when I do not yet know the shape of the solution.
@deep
The real benefit is design pressure: code that is hard to test is usually hard to use, so writing the test first surfaces bad dependencies immediately. It also guarantees the test actually fails without the code, which is not true of tests written afterwards — a surprising number of retrofitted tests pass even when the implementation is removed.

Where it fits poorly is when the requirement is genuinely unclear, or when the cost of the test harness exceeds the value at that stage.
@code ts TDD on a business rule — the case where it clearly pays
// 1. RED — write the rule as a test first
it('applies the coupon cap, not the raw percentage', () => {
  const coupon = { pct: 0.2, maxOff: 500_00 };
  const totals = priceOrder([{ price: 10_000_00, qty: 1 }], coupon);
  expect(totals.discount).toBe(500_00);          // 20% would be 2000_00 — capped
});

// 2. GREEN — minimum implementation
const discount = coupon ? Math.min(subtotal * coupon.pct, coupon.maxOff) : 0;

// 3. REFACTOR — with the test as a safety net
//    ...then add the next rule as the next failing test:
it('never lets the discount exceed the subtotal', () => { … });
it('applies tax after the discount, not before', () => { … });
@end
@senior
The honest position I would take is that strict TDD on everything is dogma, but "write the test before you consider it done" is discipline worth defending. The place I always write the test first is a bug fix: reproduce the bug as a failing test, then fix it. That proves you understood the bug, proves the fix works, and prevents regression — three benefits for one test. It also makes the pull request self-explanatory, because the test is the specification of what was wrong.
@followup
How do you decide what not to test?
@answer
I do not test framework behaviour, third-party library internals, or trivial getters — those tests cost maintenance and prove nothing. I also do not chase coverage into code that is exercised thoroughly by integration tests already. The question I ask is: if this broke, would I find out quickly some other way? If a broken value would be obvious on the first page load, a test adds less than it costs; if it would silently mis-charge a customer for a month, it needs a test.
@red
- Claiming 100% TDD when the codebase says otherwise.
- Testing implementation details to raise coverage.

### How do you test asynchronous and concurrent code?
@short
Await the result rather than sleeping, use fake timers for anything timer-driven, and for concurrency actually run the operations in parallel and assert the invariant — for example that only one of ten concurrent purchases succeeded against one unit of stock. Assertions on final state are more reliable than assertions on timing.
@deep
Race conditions do not reproduce reliably in a single-threaded test, so the useful approach is to exercise the real concurrency: fire N parallel requests with `Promise.all` against the real database and assert the invariant held. That catches missing locks and non-atomic updates, which no amount of sequential testing will.

For time-based logic, injecting a clock beats mocking globals, because it works with real async and does not interact badly with timers inside libraries.
@code ts A concurrency test that actually catches the bug
it('never oversells the last unit', async () => {
  await db.products.update({ id: 'p_1' }, { stock: 1 });

  // 20 concurrent purchase attempts against 1 unit of stock
  const results = await Promise.allSettled(
    Array.from({ length: 20 }, () =>
      request(app).post('/orders').set(auth(user)).send({ items: [{ sku: 'p_1', qty: 1 }] }))
  );

  const created = results.filter(r => r.status === 'fulfilled' && r.value.status === 201);
  expect(created).toHaveLength(1);                                  // exactly one winner
  expect((await db.products.findById('p_1')).stock).toBe(0);        // never negative
});

// Timer-driven logic: fake timers, no real waiting
vi.useFakeTimers();
const flush = createBatcher({ intervalMs: 5_000 });
flush.add(point);
await vi.advanceTimersByTimeAsync(5_000);
expect(api.post).toHaveBeenCalledOnce();
@end
@senior
That oversell test is the one I would show, because it is the difference between testing that the code runs and testing that the invariant holds under the conditions that actually break it. It fails immediately against a naive read-modify-write and passes against an atomic `UPDATE … WHERE stock > 0`. The general technique — assert the invariant after real parallel execution — applies to idempotency, rate limiting, distributed locks and queue consumers, and it is one of the most valuable test patterns for backend work.
@followup
How do you test retry and failure-handling logic?
@answer
By making the dependency fail deliberately and deterministically: an interceptor that returns 500 for the first two calls and 200 for the third, and then asserting both the eventual success and the number of attempts. I also test the give-up path — that after the maximum attempts the job lands in the dead-letter queue and an alert fires — because that path is what runs during a real outage and it is almost never exercised otherwise.
@red
- `await sleep(1000)` as a synchronisation mechanism.
- Never testing concurrent access to shared state.

### What is contract testing and when would you use it?
@short
Contract testing verifies that a consumer's expectations of an API match what the provider actually delivers, without running both together. The consumer records expectations, the provider verifies it can satisfy them in its own CI. It is most valuable when several teams own services that must interoperate.
@deep
The problem it solves is that integration testing every pair of services is combinatorially expensive and slow, while mocking each other independently lets the two drift apart silently. Pact or a shared OpenAPI spec with schema validation on both sides gives you the safety without the coupling.

For a smaller system, validating responses against the OpenAPI schema in the provider's tests and generating the client types from that same spec achieves most of the benefit with far less machinery.
@code ts Lightweight contract testing from an OpenAPI schema
import { OpenAPIBackend } from 'openapi-backend';
const api = new OpenAPIBackend({ definition: './openapi.json' });
await api.init();

it.each(ENDPOINTS)('%s response matches the published schema', async ({ method, path, req }) => {
  const res = await request(app)[method](path).set(auth(user)).send(req);
  const valid = api.validateResponse(res.body, method, path, res.status);
  expect(valid.errors ?? []).toEqual([]);       // response drifted from the spec → fail the build
});

// And on the client side, types are GENERATED from the same file:
//   npx openapi-typescript openapi.json -o src/api-types.ts
// so a removed field is a compile error in the consumer, not a runtime surprise.
@end
@senior
The judgement call is proportionality. Full Pact-style contract testing with a broker is worth it when independent teams deploy independently and a breaking change would be discovered in production. For a single team owning both the API and the web client in a monorepo, generating types from one schema gives you compile-time safety that is stronger and simpler than any contract test. I would recommend the lightest mechanism that makes drift impossible to ship, and be explicit that the goal is preventing drift rather than adopting a particular tool.
@followup
How do you know a change is backwards-compatible?
@answer
Diff the schema, not the code. Tools like `oasdiff` classify changes as breaking or non-breaking — removing a field, narrowing a type, adding a required request field are breaking; adding an optional field or a new endpoint is not. I run that in CI against the published spec and fail the build on a breaking change unless it is accompanied by a version bump. That turns "we think it is compatible" into a mechanical check.
@red
- Mocking a partner service by hand and never verifying the mock is accurate.
- Adding a required request field and calling it non-breaking.

# SECTION Computer Science Fundamentals | DSA, OOP, OS and networking — asked in screening rounds and in every MNC loop.

### What are Big-O complexities and where do they show up in your code?
@short
Big-O describes how work grows with input size. The ones that matter daily are O(1) hash lookup, O(log n) binary search and B-tree index seek, O(n) a single pass, O(n log n) sorting, and O(n²) nested loops — which is where accidental performance bugs live. In web code the highest-impact case is turning a nested lookup into a hash map.
@deep
The reason it matters is that a difference invisible on 100 rows is fatal on 100,000. A `find()` inside a `map()` is O(n·m) and looks perfectly innocent in review, but at 5,000 users and 5,000 orders it is 25 million comparisons per request.

The same reasoning applies to database work: an index turns an O(n) sequential scan into an O(log n) seek, and an N+1 query pattern is O(n) network round trips where 1 would do — and network round trips dominate everything.
@code ts The accidental quadratic, and the fix
// ❌ O(n × m) — 5,000 orders × 5,000 users = 25,000,000 comparisons
const enriched = orders.map(o => ({
  ...o, user: users.find(u => u.id === o.userId)          // O(m) inside an O(n) loop
}));

// ✅ O(n + m) — build the index once, then O(1) lookups
const byId = new Map(users.map(u => [u.id, u]));
const enriched = orders.map(o => ({ ...o, user: byId.get(o.userId) }));

// Common complexities in practice
// O(1)       map.get, set.has, array index, Redis GET
// O(log n)   binary search, B-tree index seek, sorted-set operations
// O(n)       one pass: filter/map/reduce, table scan
// O(n log n) Array.sort, database sort without a matching index
// O(n²)      nested loop, naive dedup with includes, N+1 in a loop
@end
@senior
The point I would add is that constants and I/O often dominate at real-world sizes. An O(n²) loop over 50 items is irrelevant; a single unindexed database query is not. So my ranking of what to look at is: network round trips first (N+1, sequential awaits), then database plans, then algorithmic complexity in hot loops, then micro-optimisation — which almost never matters in application code. Big-O is a tool for spotting the cases that will not survive growth, not a licence to optimise everything.
@followup
What is the complexity of `Array.prototype.includes` versus `Set.has`?
@answer
`includes` is O(n) — it scans linearly. `Set.has` is O(1) on average, backed by a hash table. So a de-duplication loop using `includes` is O(n²), while the same loop with a `Set` is O(n). On 10,000 items that is roughly 50 million comparisons versus 10,000, which is the difference between 200ms and under a millisecond. It is the most common quadratic I find in real code.
@red
- Reciting complexities with no example from your own work.
- Optimising algorithmic complexity while ignoring an N+1 query.

### Explain the core data structures and when you use each.
@short
Arrays for ordered data with index access; hash maps for O(1) keyed lookup; sets for membership and de-duplication; stacks for LIFO (undo, call stack, DFS); queues for FIFO (job processing, BFS); heaps for priority (schedulers, top-K); trees for hierarchy and ordering; graphs for relationships.
@deep
In web work the four that come up constantly are hash map, set, queue and heap. The hash map is the workhorse for indexing and caching. The set is for de-duplication and membership. The queue is what your background job system is. The heap is what a priority queue or a "top 10 by score" query is doing under the hood — and in Redis, a sorted set gives you that with O(log n) insert.

Trees appear as B-trees in database indexes and as the DOM and React's fibre tree; graphs appear in permission hierarchies, dependency resolution and social features.
@code ts Choosing the structure the problem actually needs
// Top-K without sorting everything: a min-heap of size k → O(n log k), not O(n log n)
class MinHeap { /* push, pop, peek — O(log n) */ }
function topK(items: Score[], k: number) {
  const heap = new MinHeap();
  for (const it of items) {
    heap.push(it);
    if (heap.size > k) heap.pop();          // keep only the k largest
  }
  return heap.toSortedArray();
}
// In Redis the same thing is one line: ZREVRANGE leaderboard 0 9

// LRU cache: Map preserves insertion order, so re-insert on access = O(1) LRU
class LRU<K, V> {
  private m = new Map<K, V>();
  constructor(private max: number) {}
  get(k: K) { const v = this.m.get(k); if (v !== undefined) { this.m.delete(k); this.m.set(k, v); } return v; }
  set(k: K, v: V) {
    if (this.m.has(k)) this.m.delete(k);
    this.m.set(k, v);
    if (this.m.size > this.max) this.m.delete(this.m.keys().next().value);
  }
}
@end
@senior
The framing that lands in an interview is connecting the structure to something you have actually built: "the WebSocket connection registry is a Map from user id to a Set of sockets, and per-socket metadata is a WeakMap so a dropped connection cannot leak"; "the offline sync queue is a FIFO backed by SQLite, not an in-memory array, because it must survive an app restart". That shows the data structure was a decision, not a coincidence. I would also mention that the right answer is often "the database or Redis already has this structure" — a sorted set beats implementing a heap.
@followup
How does a hash map handle collisions?
@answer
Two common strategies: separate chaining, where each bucket holds a list or tree of entries, and open addressing, where a collision probes for the next free slot. Average lookup stays O(1) as long as the load factor is controlled by resizing, but worst case degrades to O(n) if every key collides. That is the basis of hash-flooding denial-of-service attacks, which is why modern runtimes randomise the hash seed per process — a detail worth knowing because it is why you should never rely on object key iteration order for security-relevant logic.
@red
- Listing structures with no application to real work.
- Not knowing that JavaScript `Map` preserves insertion order.

### Explain the four pillars of OOP with real examples.
@short
Encapsulation hides internal state behind an interface; abstraction exposes only what matters; inheritance shares behaviour through an "is-a" relationship; polymorphism lets different implementations be used through one interface. In practice I lean heavily on encapsulation and polymorphism, and use inheritance sparingly.
@deep
Polymorphism is the one with the most architectural value: programming to an interface lets you swap Stripe for Razorpay, Postgres for an in-memory fake in tests, or S3 for local disk in development, without touching the calling code. That is the dependency inversion principle in practice.

Inheritance is the one most often misused. Deep hierarchies couple unrelated concerns and make behaviour hard to trace; composition — building objects from collaborators — is more flexible and easier to test.
@code ts Polymorphism doing real work: one interface, three payment providers
interface PaymentProvider {
  readonly name: 'stripe' | 'razorpay' | 'paypal';
  createIntent(order: Order): Promise<{ intentId: string; clientSecret: string }>;
  verifyWebhook(rawBody: Buffer, signature: string): PaymentEvent;   // ← each differs entirely
  refund(intentId: string, amountMinor: number): Promise<Refund>;
}

class StripeProvider implements PaymentProvider { /* … */ }
class RazorpayProvider implements PaymentProvider { /* … */ }

// The service knows nothing about any specific provider — open for extension,
// closed for modification. Adding a fourth provider touches no existing code.
class CheckoutService {
  constructor(private providers: Map<string, PaymentProvider>) {}
  async start(order: Order, provider: string) {
    const p = this.providers.get(provider) ?? throwErr('UNSUPPORTED_PROVIDER');
    return p.createIntent(order);
  }
}
// Tests inject a FakeProvider — no network, deterministic, fast.
@end
@senior
The senior version of this answer connects it to SOLID: the interface is dependency inversion, the provider map is open/closed, and each provider class is single-responsibility. That is not academic — it is exactly how supporting three payment providers stays maintainable, because the differences between them (signature verification schemes, refund semantics, currency handling) are contained in one class each rather than smeared across the checkout flow as `if (provider === 'stripe')` branches. When someone asks why OOP matters, that is the concrete answer.
@followup
Composition or inheritance?
@answer
Composition by default. Inheritance couples the child to the parent's implementation, and changing the parent can break children in non-obvious ways — the fragile base class problem. Composition builds behaviour by delegating to collaborators, which is more flexible, easier to test in isolation, and easier to change. I use inheritance for genuine "is-a" specialisation with a stable contract — a base `AppError` that error middleware can `instanceof` — and for framework extension points where it is the required idiom.
@red
- Reciting the four pillars with textbook animal examples.
- Deep inheritance hierarchies in application code.

### What are the SOLID principles and do they matter in JavaScript?
@short
Single responsibility, open/closed, Liskov substitution, interface segregation, dependency inversion. They matter in any language — they are about coupling and change, not about classes. In a Node codebase they show up as thin controllers, injected dependencies, small focused modules and interfaces rather than concrete imports.
@deep
The two with the most day-to-day value are single responsibility and dependency inversion. SRP is why a controller should not contain SQL and a Stripe call — each reason to change should touch one place. DIP is why a service should receive a repository rather than importing the database module directly, which is what makes it testable and swappable.

Liskov and interface segregation are more relevant when you are designing libraries or class hierarchies; in application code they mostly show up as "do not make an interface that forces implementers to throw NotImplemented".
@code ts SRP + DIP in a Node service — testable with no mocks of your own code
// ❌ everything in one place: HTTP, SQL, email, business rules
app.post('/orders', async (req, res) => {
  const r = await pool.query('INSERT INTO orders …');
  await sgMail.send({ to: req.body.email, … });
  res.json(r.rows[0]);
});

// ✅ each piece has one reason to change, and dependencies are injected
export function createOrderService(deps: {
  orders: OrderRepository;          // interface, not the Postgres implementation
  mailer: Mailer;
  clock: () => Date;
}) {
  return {
    async create(userId: string, dto: CreateOrderDto) {
      const totals = priceOrder(dto.items, dto.coupon);        // pure
      const order = await deps.orders.insert({ userId, ...totals, createdAt: deps.clock() });
      await deps.mailer.send(orderConfirmation(order));
      return order;
    },
  };
}

// Test: real logic, fake collaborators — no database, no network, milliseconds
const svc = createOrderService({ orders: new InMemoryOrders(), mailer: fakeMailer, clock: () => FIXED });
@end
@senior
The nuance I would add is that SOLID is a heuristic, not a rulebook, and over-applying it produces an interface for every class and a factory for every interface — indirection with no benefit. The test I use is whether the abstraction has more than one real implementation, or would plausibly get one: a `PaymentProvider` interface is justified because there are three; a `UserRepository` interface with exactly one implementation forever is often just ceremony, though it still buys testability. Judgement about when *not* to abstract is what distinguishes a senior engineer from someone applying patterns by rote.
@followup
Which design patterns do you actually use?
@answer
Repository and service layer for structure; strategy for interchangeable behaviour like payment providers; factory for constructing configured clients; adapter when wrapping a third-party SDK behind my own interface; observer in the form of event emitters and pub/sub; and singleton for connection pools — though in Node the module cache gives you that implicitly. What I avoid is applying patterns pre-emptively; most of these emerged in my code because a specific pain appeared, which is the right order.
@red
- Reciting SOLID without an example.
- Creating an interface for every single class.

### What is a process versus a thread, and why does it matter for Node?
@short
A process has its own memory space and is isolated; threads share memory within a process and are cheaper to create and switch between. Node runs your JavaScript on one thread, so a CPU-bound task blocks everything — which is why you scale with multiple processes (cluster, containers) and offload CPU work to worker threads.
@deep
Context switching between processes is more expensive than between threads because the memory mapping changes. Threads share memory, which makes communication cheap and data races possible — JavaScript avoids the race problem by not sharing mutable state between workers except through `SharedArrayBuffer`.

The scheduling detail that matters operationally: when your container has a CPU limit of 0.5 cores, the kernel throttles your process, which shows up as latency spikes rather than as high CPU in your metrics.
@code js Worker threads for genuinely CPU-bound work
// main.js — the event loop stays free
import { Worker } from 'node:worker_threads';

function hashInWorker(payload) {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const w = new Worker('./hash-worker.js', { workerData: payload });
    w.on('message', resolve);
    w.on('error', reject);
    w.on('exit', (code) => code !== 0 && reject(new Error(`worker exited ${code}`)));
  });
}

// hash-worker.js — heavy CPU work, isolated from the request path
import { parentPort, workerData } from 'node:worker_threads';
parentPort.postMessage(expensivePureComputation(workerData));

// In practice: use a pool (piscina) rather than a worker per request —
// worker startup is ~30ms and unbounded workers will exhaust memory.
@end
@senior
The practical guidance is a decision tree: if the work is I/O, Node already handles it — do nothing. If it is CPU-bound and short, a worker pool keeps the event loop responsive. If it is CPU-bound and long, it belongs in a queue and a separate service, so it can scale and fail independently of the API. I would also mention that `worker_threads` shares the process's memory limit, so ten workers each holding a large buffer will OOM the container — which is why a bounded pool matters more than the threading model itself.
@followup
What is a race condition and how do you prevent one in Node?
@answer
Two operations interleaving in an order that produces an incorrect result. Node's single thread does not save you, because an `await` is a yield point — between reading a value and writing it, another request can run. The fixes are the same as anywhere: make the operation atomic (a single SQL `UPDATE … WHERE`, a Redis `INCR`, a Lua script), or take a lock (`SELECT … FOR UPDATE`, or a Redis lock with a token), or use optimistic concurrency with a version column and retry.
@red
- "Node is single-threaded so there are no race conditions."
- Spawning a worker per request.

### How does memory management and garbage collection work in V8?
@short
V8 uses a generational collector: most objects die young, so the small "new space" is collected frequently with a fast scavenger, and survivors are promoted to the "old space", collected by a slower mark-sweep-compact process that runs incrementally and concurrently to limit pauses. You do not free memory; you avoid retaining it.
@deep
A leak in JavaScript is unintentional retention — something still references an object that is no longer needed. The four usual sources in Node are: an unbounded cache (a Map or array that only grows), event listeners added and never removed, closures captured by long-lived timers or subscriptions, and streams or sockets never destroyed on error.

Heap limits matter operationally: Node's default old-space limit is often lower than the container's memory limit, so you either raise `--max-old-space-size` or the process OOMs while the container looks half-empty. Conversely, buffers live outside the heap, so a buffer leak grows RSS while heap metrics look fine.
@code js Finding and fixing retention
// Symptom: RSS climbs steadily and never returns after GC
node --max-old-space-size=1024 --heapsnapshot-signal=SIGUSR2 dist/index.js
kill -USR2 <pid>          # snapshot 1, then again 10 minutes later → diff in DevTools

// The four classic leaks
const cache = new Map();            // ❌ never evicted → use an LRU or Redis with TTL
emitter.on('data', handler);        // ❌ per request, never removed → emitter.off in cleanup
setInterval(() => use(bigObject));  // ❌ the closure pins bigObject forever
stream.on('error', () => {});       // ❌ not destroyed → fd + buffer leak; use stream.pipeline

// Expose the numbers so you can alert on the trend, not discover it at 3am
setInterval(() => {
  const m = process.memoryUsage();
  logger.info({ rssMb: m.rss / 1e6, heapUsedMb: m.heapUsed / 1e6, externalMb: m.external / 1e6 });
}, 60_000).unref();
@end
@senior
The distinction I would draw is between a leak and legitimate growth. A heap that sawtooths — rising then dropping after GC — is healthy; one where the post-GC floor climbs every cycle is a leak. Watching RSS alone misleads, because it includes buffers and native memory. The other senior point is that manual optimisation like `global.gc()` or object pooling is almost never the answer in application code; the answer is finding what is retained, and the heap snapshot diff with its retainer path tells you directly.
@followup
Why did my container restart with no error in the logs?
@answer
Almost always the OOM killer. The kernel sends SIGKILL, which cannot be caught, so the process gets no chance to log anything — you find it in `dmesg`, in the container runtime's exit reason (`OOMKilled`), or in ECS/Kubernetes events. The two fixes are opposite in nature: either the memory limit is genuinely too low for the workload, or there is a leak. Setting `--max-old-space-size` below the container limit at least converts a silent kill into a catchable JavaScript heap-out-of-memory error with a stack.
@red
- Not knowing about the OOM killer.
- Blaming "a Node memory leak" without a heap snapshot.

### What happens in the OS when your server handles a request?
@short
The kernel accepts the TCP connection into a listen queue, your process's event loop is notified via epoll that the socket is readable, it reads bytes into user space, your code runs, and the response is written back through the socket buffer. File descriptors, socket buffers and the listen backlog are all finite resources you can exhaust.
@deep
The limits that bite: `ulimit -n` (open file descriptors per process — every socket counts), the listen backlog (`somaxconn`, the queue of accepted-but-not-yet-processed connections), and ephemeral port exhaustion on the client side when making many outbound connections.

`TIME_WAIT` sockets accumulate when your server closes many short-lived connections; keep-alive connections avoid that entirely, which is one reason HTTP keep-alive and connection pooling matter so much.
@code bash The OS-level limits behind "the server stopped accepting connections"
ulimit -n                     # file descriptors — default 1024 is low for a busy server
cat /proc/sys/net/core/somaxconn        # listen backlog
ss -s                         # socket summary: how many in TIME_WAIT / ESTABLISHED
lsof -p $(pgrep -f node) | wc -l        # actual fds in use by the process

# Symptoms and causes
#  EMFILE: too many open files       → fd leak (unclosed sockets/streams) or ulimit too low
#  ECONNREFUSED                      → nothing listening, or backlog full
#  EADDRINUSE                        → previous process still holding the port
#  ETIMEDOUT on outbound             → ephemeral port exhaustion or a firewall

# Node side: reuse outbound connections instead of creating one per request
const agent = new https.Agent({ keepAlive: true, maxSockets: 50 });
@end
@senior
The connection-reuse point is the one with the biggest practical impact: a Node service calling a third-party API without a keep-alive agent opens a new TCP and TLS connection per request, which adds tens of milliseconds each and can exhaust ephemeral ports under load. Setting a keep-alive agent is a one-line change that has measurably cut p99 latency in services I have worked on — and it is exactly the kind of unglamorous fix behind a meaningful API response-time reduction. Similarly, raising `ulimit -n` in the container spec is routine for anything holding many concurrent connections, such as a WebSocket server.
@followup
What is the difference between blocking and non-blocking I/O at the OS level?
@answer
A blocking read suspends the calling thread until data is available; a non-blocking read returns immediately with whatever is there, or `EAGAIN`. Non-blocking I/O combined with an event notification mechanism — epoll on Linux, kqueue on BSD, IOCP on Windows — lets one thread manage thousands of sockets, which is exactly what libuv does for Node. That is why Node handles high concurrency with one thread while a thread-per-connection server needs thousands of threads for the same load.
@red
- Not knowing what EMFILE means.
- Making outbound HTTP calls without connection reuse.

### Explain the OSI model or the layers of the network stack — briefly.
@short
Practically, four layers matter: link (Ethernet/Wi-Fi), network (IP — addressing and routing), transport (TCP/UDP — ports, reliability), and application (HTTP, DNS, TLS). The seven-layer OSI model adds session, presentation and application distinctions that are mostly academic; the TCP/IP model is what real systems implement.
@deep
The layering explains where problems live. "Cannot resolve host" is DNS at the application layer; "connection refused" is transport — something reached the host but nothing was listening; "connection timed out" usually means a firewall or routing problem at the network layer, because a refusal would have come back faster; "certificate error" is TLS between transport and application.

That diagnostic mapping is the actual reason to know the model.
@code bash Diagnosing by layer, top to bottom
dig api.acme.com                    # DNS resolving? correct IP? (application layer)
ping 10.0.3.14                      # host reachable? (network layer — often blocked, so not conclusive)
traceroute api.acme.com             # where does the path stop? (routing)
nc -vz api.acme.com 443             # is the port open? (transport layer)
openssl s_client -connect api.acme.com:443 -servername api.acme.com   # TLS: cert chain, expiry, SNI
curl -v https://api.acme.com/healthz # full application-layer request with headers

# Reading the failure:
#   NXDOMAIN                  → DNS / record missing
#   Connection refused        → reached the host, nothing listening (wrong port, crashed process)
#   Connection timed out      → firewall / security group / routing black hole
#   certificate verify failed → expired cert, wrong SNI, missing intermediate
@end
@senior
The distinction between "connection refused" and "connection timed out" is the single most useful piece of network diagnosis in cloud work: refused means you reached the machine and nothing was listening — usually a crashed process or the wrong port — while timed out means the packets were dropped, which in AWS almost always means a security group or NACL. Knowing that lets you go straight to the right place instead of restarting services at random. I would also mention that a missing intermediate certificate is a classic cause of "works in my browser, fails from the server", because browsers fetch missing intermediates and most clients do not.
@followup
How does DNS resolution actually work?
@answer
The resolver checks its cache, then queries the root servers for the TLD nameservers, the TLD servers for the domain's authoritative nameservers, and the authoritative server for the record — each result cached according to its TTL. The practical consequences are that a DNS change propagates as fast as the shortest TTL in the chain, so lowering TTL *before* a planned migration is what makes cutover fast, and that DNS-based failover is only as quick as clients honour the TTL, which many do not.
@red
- Reciting all seven OSI layers with no diagnostic value.
- Not knowing the difference between refused and timed out.

### What are the classic algorithms you should be able to write?
@short
For a full-stack role the realistic set is: binary search, two pointers, sliding window, hash-map frequency counting, BFS and DFS on a graph or tree, and a basic sort plus merge. Beyond that, most interview problems reduce to "use the right data structure", which is usually a map or a set.
@deep
The patterns matter more than memorised solutions. Sliding window solves "longest substring with a constraint" and also "rate limit over a time window". Two pointers solves pair-sum problems and also merging sorted result sets. BFS gives shortest path in an unweighted graph and also level-order traversal of a tree or a comment thread.

Being able to state the complexity and the trade-off of your solution matters more than producing the optimal one immediately.
@code ts Three patterns that cover a large share of screening questions
// 1. Sliding window — longest substring without repeating characters, O(n)
function longestUnique(s: string) {
  const seen = new Map<string, number>();
  let start = 0, best = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < s.length; i++) {
    const prev = seen.get(s[i]);
    if (prev !== undefined && prev >= start) start = prev + 1;   // shrink the window
    seen.set(s[i], i);
    best = Math.max(best, i - start + 1);
  }
  return best;
}

// 2. Hash map counting — two-sum in O(n) instead of O(n²)
function twoSum(nums: number[], target: number) {
  const seen = new Map<number, number>();
  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    const need = target - nums[i];
    if (seen.has(need)) return [seen.get(need)!, i];
    seen.set(nums[i], i);
  }
  return null;
}

// 3. BFS — shortest path / level order, O(V + E)
function bfs(graph: Map<string, string[]>, start: string, goal: string) {
  const q = [[start]], seen = new Set([start]);
  while (q.length) {
    const path = q.shift()!;
    const node = path.at(-1)!;
    if (node === goal) return path;
    for (const n of graph.get(node) ?? []) {
      if (!seen.has(n)) { seen.add(n); q.push([...path, n]); }
    }
  }
  return null;
}
@end
@senior
In a senior interview the algorithm question is often a proxy for communication: state your assumptions, name the brute-force solution and its complexity, then explain what you would improve and why, then write it and test it against an edge case. Interviewers are watching how you decompose and how you respond to a hint, more than whether you recall the optimal trick. I would also be honest that in day-to-day work these appear rarely — but the underlying skill, recognising that a nested loop can become a map lookup, appears constantly.
@followup
How would you approach a problem you have never seen?
@answer
Clarify the input, output and constraints first — the constraints usually tell you the intended complexity, since n up to a million rules out O(n²). Then state a brute-force solution so there is something correct on the table, identify the redundant work, and choose a structure that eliminates it. I talk through the trade-offs out loud and test with an edge case — empty input, one element, duplicates — because that is what I would do in real code, and it is what the interviewer is actually assessing.
@red
- Silently writing code without stating assumptions.
- Claiming you have memorised hundreds of problems.

### How do you approach system design at a junior-to-mid level?
@short
Clarify requirements and scale, define the API, sketch the data model, draw the components, then discuss bottlenecks and failure modes. The most common mistake is jumping straight to a diagram with a cache and a queue before knowing what the system actually needs to do.
@deep
The order that works: functional requirements (what must it do), non-functional (how many users, read/write ratio, latency target, consistency needs), then the API contract, then the data model, then the architecture, then scaling and failure. Numbers matter — 1,000 requests per second against 10 million rows leads to different decisions than 10 requests per second against 10,000 rows.

Explicitly stating trade-offs is what is being assessed: every choice costs something, and naming the cost is the signal.
@arch The template to follow every time
  1. REQUIREMENTS      What must it do? What is out of scope?
                       Users? RPS? Read:write ratio? Data size? Latency target? Consistency?
  2. API               The 4–6 endpoints or events that define the contract
  3. DATA MODEL        Tables/collections, keys, indexes. What are the access patterns?
  4. HIGH LEVEL        Client → CDN → LB → service(s) → cache → database → queue → workers
  5. DEEP DIVE         The one or two hard parts: idempotency? fan-out? hot keys? ordering?
  6. SCALE             What breaks first at 10×? Read replicas, sharding, caching, async
  7. FAILURE           What happens when each component dies? Retries, backoff, DLQ, degraded mode
  8. OBSERVABILITY     What do you measure? What alerts? What is the SLO?
@end
@senior
The habit that most improves a design answer is estimating before deciding. "One million daily active users, five requests each, is about 60 requests per second average and maybe 300 at peak — that is comfortably one database" prevents you from proposing sharding for a workload a single Postgres instance handles easily. Conversely, "500 writes per second times 200 bytes is 8GB a day, so retention and partitioning are the real design problem" points you at what matters. Doing arithmetic out loud is the clearest signal of engineering judgement.
@followup
How do you handle a design question about something you have never built?
@answer
Reason from first principles and from analogy: identify what the system fundamentally is — a write-heavy log, a read-heavy catalogue, a fan-out problem, a coordination problem — and apply the patterns for that shape. I say plainly which parts I have direct experience with and which I am reasoning about, then work through the trade-offs. Interviewers respond well to honest reasoning and badly to confident invented detail, and admitting the boundary of your experience while still making progress is a strong signal rather than a weak one.
@red
- Drawing microservices, Kafka and Kubernetes before understanding the requirements.
- Never estimating any numbers.
